Extensive experiments conducted on five architectures across fifteen datasets demonstrate that ES-VP consistently outperforms current state-of-the-art (SOTA) single and diverse VP methods. For instance, using the CLIP architecture across four datasets, ES-VP outperforms the SOTA method DAM-VP by an average of 2.6\% in accuracy while utilizing 590$\times$ fewer VP parameters, thereby establishing a new benchmark for efficient and generalizable model adaptation.

</details>


### [75] [Anchoring Instruction Outside Mask: Exact Reference Caching for Efficient In-Context Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21229)
*Yangshuai Liu,Zheming Li,Jiaao Li,Kang He,Ziliang Lai,Zhitai Liu,Chengru Song*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses computational efficiency in multimodal diffusion transformers for image editing. Existing methods use structured sparse attention to reduce computation when processing multiple reference images, but this blocks visual references from attending to text instructions, degrading quality. The proposed method uses static text anchors to connect instructions to reference branches while preserving K/V reuse, combined with teacher-forced velocity distillation and on-policy recovery to restore generation quality. Achieves 3.92x-5.47x speedups while matching full-attention quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Omnimodal generation with in-context conditioning allows diffusion transformers to process text and visual references together, but each reference image introduces thousands of tokens, causing rapid computational growth. Existing sparse attention methods enable K/V reuse but prevent visual references from attending to text instructions, substantially degrading instruction following and reference fidelity in multi-reference editing.

Method: Joint redesign of token sequence and attention mask using static text anchors to connect instructions to the reference branch, preserving exact K/V reuse without adding parameters. Teacher-forced velocity distillation followed by a short on-policy stage where the teacher supervises student-visited states to recover lost generation quality.

Result: Matches full-attention generation quality across three image-editing benchmarks. Achieves 3.92x speedup with five reference images in complete 40-step denoising; speedup reaches 5.47x at ten references in scaling study, with negligible runtime overhead from static text anchors.

Conclusion: First use of on-policy distillation for architectural recovery in diffusion models. Successfully resolves the conflict between computational efficiency (K/V reuse) and quality (text-reference interaction) in multimodal generation, enabling practical multi-reference image editing with substantial speedups while maintaining quality.

Abstract: Omnimodal generation is central to a wide range of content creation and editing applications. In-context conditioning is essential to this paradigm. It allows diffusion transformers to process text instructions and visual references in a shared attention sequence. However, each reference image introduces thousands of tokens. Computation therefore grows rapidly with the number of references. Existing methods reduce computation through structured sparse attention, which limits interactions between reference and target tokens. This structure also makes the reference K and V independent of the denoising target, allowing them to be computed once and reused across steps. However, it blocks visual references from attending to the text instruction. This substantially degrades instruction following and reference fidelity in multi-reference editing. To resolve this conflict, we jointly redesign the token sequence and attention mask. Our beyond-mask design uses static text anchors to connect the instruction to the reference branch. It preserves exact K and V reuse without adding parameters. However, this direct architectural conversion degrades generation quality. We recover the lost performance through teacher-forced velocity distillation, followed by a short on-policy stage in which the teacher supervises student-visited states. To our knowledge, this is the first use of on-policy distillation for architectural recovery in diffusion models. Across three image-editing benchmarks, our method matches full-attention generation quality. With five reference images, it accelerates the complete 40-step denoising process by 3.92x, while static text anchors introduce negligible runtime overhead; the speedup reaches 5.47x at ten references in our scaling study.

</details>


### [76] [A VLM Answer Is Not an Anomaly Score: Rank Compression in Training-Free Video Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21244)
*Inpyo Song,Jangwon Lee*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses a critical interface design issue in VLM-based video anomaly detection (VAD): the choice between generated readout (using only the most likely answer) versus probability readout (using full answer distribution) significantly impacts detection performance. Across four 7-8B VLMs, probability readout consistently outperforms generated readout by 5-13 AUROC/AP points, even with large answer scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models enable training-free video anomaly detection by answering questions about video segments. However, VAD benchmarks require scalar anomaly scores for ranking evaluation (AUROC/AP), creating an interface mismatch. This paper investigates how the answer interface—comprising answer scale and readout rule—affects the evaluated ranking, arguing it is a fundamental detector component rather than a formatting detail.

Method: The authors compared two readout methods: (1) Generated readout—selects only the most likely answer from the VLM's output; (2) Probability readout—uses the full distribution over admissible answers to compute a weighted score. Experiments spanned four 7-8B VLMs, multiple answer scales, benchmarks, and metrics, testing various decoding strategies and prompt wordings.

Result: Probability readout outperforms generated readout for every tested combination, with average gains of 5-13 points across benchmark-metric pairs. Even with 91 possible answers, generated readout produces only 4-18 distinct scores due to "generated-answer rank compression" (segments with different answer distributions receive identical scores, losing relative ordering). Probability readout retains finer score resolution. This advantage persists across all decoding strategies and prompt variations tested.

Conclusion: The answer interface is a consequential component of VLM-based VAD that should be explicitly specified and evaluated. The generated readout's tendency to collapse ranking information into few score values fundamentally limits its effectiveness compared to probability-based approaches that preserve the full distributional information.

Abstract: Vision-language models enable training-free video anomaly detection by answering questions about video segments. VAD benchmarks, however, require a scalar anomaly score for each segment and evaluate the resulting ranking using the AUROC or AP. A VLM-based detector should therefore define an answer interface: the answer scale specifies the admissible answers, and the readout rule maps the model's output distribution to a score. Because this interface can change the evaluated ranking, it is part of the detector rather than a formatting detail. The generated readout uses only the most likely answer, whereas the probability readout uses the full distribution over admissible answers. Across four 7-8B VLMs, the probability readout outperforms the generated readout for every tested combination of answer scale, benchmark, and metric, with average gains ranging from 5 to 13 points across the four benchmark-metric pairs. The gap arises because the generated readout keeps only one answer value per segment, so segment with different answer distributions can receive the same score and lose their relative order. We call this loss of relative order generated-answer rank compression. Even when the answer scale allows 91 answers, the generated readout produces only 4-18 distinct scores, whereas the probability readout retains substantially finer score resolution. The advantage persists under every decoding strategy, prompt wording, and joint scoring-explanation prompt we test. The answer interface is therefore a consequential component of VLM-based VAD and should be explicitly specified and evaluated.

</details>


### [77] [Just Noticeable Difference Modeling for Token Compression in Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21247)
*Zhuoyuan Li,Rui Zhao,Jin Wang,Hanwei Zhu,Cong Zhang,Giuseppe Valenzise,Weisi Lin,Kin-Man Lam*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces Action-JND, a novel token compression framework for embodied agents that measures compression tolerance based on downstream action deviations rather than traditional visual cues. It proposes a lightweight JND estimator that predicts maximum tolerable perturbations while preserving policy responses, applicable to VLA compression paradigms like stale-KV reuse and token pruning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing token compression methods for embodied agents rely on indirect cues (visual similarity, attention scores, saliency) that don't directly measure the key factor for safe compression: how much a token can change before causing unacceptable deviation in downstream actions. This receiver-dependent tolerance is closely related to the Just Noticeable Difference (JND) principle.

Method: The paper develops a lightweight token-wise JND estimator in deep visual-feature space that predicts the maximum tolerable perturbation while preserving policy responses. The action-tolerance score serves as a plug-and-play criterion for VLA compression paradigms, including stale-KV reuse and token pruning, prioritizing action-tolerant tokens for compression.

Result: Experiments on the LIBERO benchmark with OpenVLA and OpenVLA-OFT demonstrate that Action-JND consistently improves compression reliability, especially under aggressive compression ratios.

Conclusion: Action-JND successfully extends JND modeling to embodied perception by defining noticeability through language-conditioned action response of VLA policies. This approach provides a more principled and effective way to compress tokens in embodied agents while maintaining action prediction accuracy.

Abstract: Token compression has become a key technique for reducing the inference cost of large foundation models, with approaches such as token pruning and KV-cache reuse widely adopted in vision-language models and recently explored for embodied agents. In embodied agents, tokens not only support perception and semantic understanding but also directly affect latency-sensitive closed-loop robot action prediction. Existing schemes typically guide compression using redundancy or importance cues, such as visual similarity, attention scores, and saliency. However, these cues only indirectly measure the key factor for safe compression: how much a token can change before causing an unacceptable deviation in downstream actions. This receiver-dependent tolerance is closely related to the principle of just noticeable difference (JND). Classical JND characterizes signal tolerance in the human visual system, while machine-oriented JND extends this concept to downstream machine responses. Building on this progression, we introduce Action-JND, which extends JND modeling to embodied perception by defining noticeability through the language-conditioned action response of a vision-language-action (VLA) policy in closed-loop control. A token change is considered admissible only when the induced action deviation remains within a tolerated margin. To realize this concept, we develop a lightweight token-wise JND estimator in deep visual-feature space to predict the maximum tolerable perturbation while preserving policy responses. The resulting action-tolerance score serves as a plug-and-play criterion for VLA compression paradigms, including stale-KV reuse and token pruning, prioritizing action-tolerant tokens for compression. Experiments on the LIBERO benchmark with OpenVLA and OpenVLA-OFT demonstrate that Action-JND consistently improves compression reliability, especially under aggressive compression ratios.

</details>


### [78] [WildFin: An In-the-Wild Dataset for Fish Behavioral Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21281)
*Abigail G. Grassick,Jerome Tze-Hou Hsu,Ethan Lin,Ziang Liu,Max Whitton,Madelyn Hair,Liam Gutierrez,Haozheng Yu,Kristin Branson,Vivek Jayaraman,Michael A. Gil,Andrew M. Hein,Jennifer J. Sun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Recent advances in field technology have led to a massive influx of in-the-wild video data for ecological science. The primary bottleneck in leveraging this data is the high cost of expert annotation. While computer vision offers a potential solution, current models frequently fail when deployed in complex marine environments. To characterize these failures, we introduce WildFin, a novel benchmark for fish behavior recognition collected and annotated by ecologists.WildFin spans two critical real-world paradigms: stationary cameras monitoring groups of fish and dynamic divers following individual subjects. The dataset represents a massive curation effort, involving 1,350 hours of fieldwork and 600 hours of expert annotation to produce 9 hours of behavioral data with over 2 million frame-by-frame labels. We benchmark modern vision foundation models and quantify tradeoffs between static and spatiotemporal architectures, revealing the substantial gap that remains between current model capabilities and the demands of real-world underwater behavioral analysis. Project website: https://team-wildfin.github.io/.

</details>


### [79] [Difficulty-Calibrated Interpolation Paths for Conditional Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21286)
*Airin Akter Tania,Md Raihan Khan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes Difficulty-Calibrated Flow Matching (DCFM), which adapts the noise-to-data interpolation schedule in Conditional Flow Matching based on the model's learned difficulty at each timestep. By using a short pilot run to measure per-time loss and setting the schedule to the quantile function of this difficulty profile, the method makes the model spend more time learning challenging regions of the trajectory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The interpolation schedule that shapes the noise-to-data path in Conditional Flow Matching is fixed in advance and independent of both the data and the model. The authors observe that regression difficulty varies systematically along the path, suggesting that a fixed schedule may be suboptimal for training efficiency and sample quality.

Method: The proposed Difficulty-Calibrated Flow Matching method works in two phases: (1) A short pilot run with the standard linear path records the per-time loss, creating a difficulty profile; (2) The schedule is then set to the quantile function of this difficulty profile, causing the trajectory to linger where velocity is hardest to learn. The method has a single hyperparameter, preserves the original training objective and gradient equivalence, composes with classifier-free guidance, and adds only ~2% training overhead.

Result: In controlled experiments on CIFAR-10, MNIST, and Fashion-MNIST using an identical compact U-Net, the calibrated path achieves the best FID on CIFAR-10 at full sampling budget and significantly outperforms all fixed schedules in the large-batch, few-update regime - precisely the setting where compute is most scarce.

Conclusion: Adapting the interpolation schedule based on model-specific difficulty is a simple yet effective improvement to Conditional Flow Matching. The method adds minimal overhead while meaningfully improving sample quality, especially in compute-constrained scenarios. This suggests that difficulty-aware scheduling is a promising direction for optimizing generative model training.

Abstract: Conditional Flow Matching trains generative models by regressing a network onto the velocity of a prescribed noise-to-data interpolation path. The interpolation schedule that shapes this path is known to affect convergence and sample quality, yet it is invariably fixed in advance, independent of both the data and the model. We show that the regression difficulty of Conditional Flow Matching varies systematically along the path, and we propose Difficulty-Calibrated Flow Matching, which derives the schedule from the model itself: a short pilot run with the linear path records the per-time loss, and the schedule is set to the quantile function of this difficulty profile, so the trajectory lingers where the velocity is hardest to learn. The method has a single hyperparameter, leaves the training objective and its gradient equivalence intact, composes with classifier-free guidance, and adds about two percent training overhead. In controlled experiments on CIFAR-10, MNIST, and Fashion-MNIST with an identical compact U-Net, the calibrated path attains the best FID on CIFAR-10 at full sampling budget and clearly outperforms all fixed schedules in the large-batch, few-update regime, precisely the setting where compute is scarcest.

</details>


### [80] [When Adaptation Hurts: Connecting Representational Drift to OOD Failures in MedSAM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21300)
*Marko Haralović,Sounic Akkaraju,Carlo Baretta,Vasil Zapryanov,Alexia Briassouli*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper systematically examines how MedSAM, a prompt-based foundation model for medical image segmentation, generalizes across diverse medical imaging benchmarks. The authors compare six adaptation strategies (full-model and encoder-only LoRA, shallow and deep VPT, decoder-only and full fine-tuning) trained on ISIC 2018 and evaluated on in-distribution and OOD datasets (PH2, BUSI, CBIS-DDSM). Key findings: adaptation improves IN and close-OOD performance but degrades far-OOD performance; encoder-only LoRA provides the best robustness among parameter-efficient methods; far-OOD degradation is linked to decoder representation drift; random prompt jitter improves robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation models like MedSAM generalize well across domains in zero/few-shot setups, but their performance depends on prompt quality and dataset adaptation. This work systematically examines how MedSAM generalizes across diverse medical imaging benchmarks to understand the tradeoffs between adaptation strategies and out-of-distribution robustness.

Method: The authors compared six adaptation strategies: (1) full-model LoRA, (2) encoder-only LoRA, (3) shallow VPT, (4) deep VPT, (5) decoder-only fine-tuning, and (6) full fine-tuning. Models were trained on ISIC 2018 (skin lesion segmentation) and evaluated under clean and noisy prompts on three test sets: in-distribution data, close-OOD (PH2 dermoscopy), and far-OOD (BUSI breast ultrasound, CBIS-DDSM mammography). They used Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) to analyze representation similarity and decoder drift.

Result: Adaptation improves performance on in-distribution and close-OOD data but often reduces performance on far-OOD data. Full fine-tuning provides the best overall tradeoff. Among parameter-efficient methods, encoder-only LoRA outperforms standard LoRA and VPT under far-OOD shifts. CKA analysis reveals that far-OOD degradation is strongly associated with drift in decoder representations, while encoder similarity alone does not explain robustness. Random 0-100 pixel jitter on prompts produces more robust and better performing models.

Conclusion: Robust MedSAM adaptation requires combined consideration of prompt noise exposure, domain shift, and representation preservation. Encoder-only LoRA provides stronger robustness than standard LoRA by adapting the encoder to distribution shift in visual features while preserving the decoder pathway. The findings suggest that when adapting foundation models for medical imaging, practitioners should carefully balance in-distribution performance against OOD robustness, with encoder-only adaptation strategies offering a favorable tradeoff.

Abstract: Foundation models for medical image segmentation, like prompt-based MedSAM, generalize well across domains and modalities, often in zero or few-shot setups. However, their performance depends on the quality of prompts and the adaptation of the models to custom datasets. This work systematically examines how MedSAM generalizes across diverse medical imaging benchmarks, with six adaptation strategies: full-model and encoder-only LoRA, shallow and deep visual prompt tuning (VPT), and decoder-only and full fine-tuning. Models are trained on the International Skin Imaging Collaboration Challenge (ISIC 2018) dataset and evaluated under clean and increasingly noisy prompts on IN and Out-of-Distribution (OOD) datasets: close-OOD PH2 (dermoscopy), far-OOD BUSI (Breast Ultrasound Images Dataset) and CBIS-DDSM (Curated Breast Imaging Subset of the Digital Database for Screening Mammography). We show that adaptation improves performance on IN and close-OOD data but often reduces performance on far-OOD data. Full fine-tuning provides the best tradeoff, while encoder-only LoRA is the strongest parameter-efficient alternative, outperforming standard LoRA and VPT under far-OOD shifts. Using Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA), we show that far-OOD degradation is strongly associated with drift in decoder representations, whereas encoder similarity alone does not explain robustness. This suggests encoder-only LoRA provides stronger robustness than standard LoRA by adapting the encoder to distribution shift in visual features, while preserving the decoder pathway. We further show that random 0-100 pixel jitter on prompts produces more robust and better performing models. We thus conclude that robust MedSAM adaptation requires the combined consideration of prompt noise exposure, domain shift, and representation preservation. We release our code: https://github.com/ImSounic/medsam-vpt

</details>


### [81] [Re$^3$Cap: Retrieval-Guided Refinement for Image Captioning Enhancement via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21305)
*Haonan Jia,Shichao Dong,Zenghui Sun,Jiawen Zheng,Ziqi Miao,Gege Shi,Qiuyu Zhao,Jinsong Lan,Xiaoyong Zhu,Bo Zheng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Re³Cap is a retrieval-guided reasoning strategy for image captioning that uses multi-modal retrieval as a reasoning signal to identify hallucinations and omissions in captions, without requiring additional annotations. It consists of two components: Caption Refinement Suggester (CRS) and Caption Quality Assessor (CQA).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has demonstrated gains in image captioning but is limited in encouraging Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) to explore novel reasoning strategies, creating a performance gap between RL and Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT). The authors propose that multi-modal retrieval can serve as an effective reasoning signal for caption refinement.

Method: Re³Cap is instantiated by two modules: (1) Caption Refinement Suggester (CRS) and (2) Caption Quality Assessor (CQA). The strategy identifies hallucinations and omissions in generated captions through multi-modal retrieval, enabling the model to refine captions for improved accuracy and detail without additional manual annotations.

Result: Experiments demonstrate Re³Cap's superiority in image captioning, outperforming even Supervised Fine-Tuning. Specifically, Re³Cap outperforms GRPO with an average improvement of 8.64% in relation reasoning on the COCO-LN500 benchmark.

Conclusion: Multi-modal retrieval can effectively serve as a reasoning signal for caption refinement, enabling LVLMs to explore novel reasoning strategies and bridge the performance gap between RL and SFT. The retrieval-guided approach successfully identifies and corrects hallucinations and omissions, leading to more accurate and detailed image captions.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has demonstrated significant gains in image captioning, yet it is still limited in encouraging Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) to explore novel reasoning strategies. This limitation leads to a performance gap between RL and Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT). In this paper, we argue that multi-modal retrieval can serve as an effective reasoning signal for caption refinement. Based on this insight, we present the Retrieval-Guided Refinement for Image Captioning (Re$^3$Cap), a retrieval-guided reasoning strategy that enhances image captioning without requiring additional annotations. Instantiated by Caption Refinement Suggester (CRS) and Caption Quality Assessor (CQA), this strategy identifies hallucinations and omissions in image captions, leading to more accurate and detailed descriptions. Extensive experiments demonstrate the superiority of our method in image captioning, even compared with Supervised Fine-Tuning. Especially, Re$^3$Cap outperforms GRPO with an average improvement of 8.64% in relation reasoning on the COCO-LN500 benchmark.

</details>


### [82] [OmniAssistBench: Assistant-style Interaction Benchmark for Omni-LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21360)
*Xianyun Sun,Chaoyou Fu,Zhengye Zhang,Feiyang Duan,Qingyuan Cao,Yonghui Niu,Sihang Yuan,Ge Zhang,Caifeng Shan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OmniAssistBench is a new benchmark for evaluating omni-modal large language models as real-time interactive video assistants. It addresses the challenge of evaluating models that dynamically interact with users by reverse-engineering existing internet videos to create multi-turn interaction clips. The benchmark requires models to guide users along predefined routes derived from source videos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing static offline datasets cannot accommodate the dynamic nature of interactive assistants, where model responses unpredictably change user actions. Current Omni-LLMs show potential as real-time video assistants but lack proper evaluation frameworks for their interactive capabilities.

Method: The dataset was constructed by reverse-engineering existing internet videos to deduce logical user goals and segment videos into multi-turn clips simulating continuous interactions. Models are provided with predefined priors derived from source videos and must guide users along exact same routes. This rigorous pipeline required over 1000 expert person-hours.

Result: Gemini-3-Pro achieves 66.4/100 points, while Qwen3-Omni-Instruct achieves 51.2/100. Current models generally understand user inputs but frequently provide incorrect or incomplete answers. Key limitations include: struggles with visual prompts (e.g., hand gestures), failure to maintain historical context during multi-turn interactions, and failure to delay response until target event occurs.

Conclusion: Substantial room for improvement remains before models can become reliable interactive video assistants. Current models need significant advances in visual prompt understanding, multi-turn context maintenance, and temporal reasoning to achieve reliable performance.

Abstract: Recent omni-modal large language models (Omni-LLMs) show great potential as real-time video assistants, which continuously perceive environments and guide users to achieve specific goals. Unlike traditional passive video understanding, interactive assistants should actively combine visual states, user goals, and prior knowledge to provide effective help. Evaluating this is rather challenging, as the model's unpredictable response dynamically changes the user's subsequent actions, which static offline datasets cannot accommodate. To address this bottleneck, we introduce OmniAssistBench. To solve the issue of diverging interaction paths where the same user goal can be achieved through various methods, we provide models with predefined priors derived from the source video, requiring them to guide users along the exact same routes. Since real interaction videos are rare, we construct the dataset by reverse-engineering existing Internet videos. We deduce logical user goals and segment the videos into multi-turn clips to simulate continuous interactions. This rigorous pipeline required over 1000 expert person-hours to build the dataset. Results show that the proprietary Gemini-3-Pro reaches 66.4 out of the max point of 100, while the open-source Qwen3-Omni-Instruct achieves 51.2. Although current models generally understand user inputs, they frequently provide incorrect or incomplete answers. Specifically, they struggle with visual prompts (e.g., hand gestures), fail to maintain historical context during multi-turn interactions, and fail to delay response until the target event. Results indicate substantial room for improvement before models can become reliable assistants.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [83] [SDAD: Spec-Driven Agentic Development for the AI-Native SDLC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20341)
*Vu Hung Nguyen,Thanh Nguyen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes Spec-Driven Agentic Development (SDAD) as a new software development paradigm combining AI agents with disciplined specification practices. It proposes a four-phase model (intent capture, machine-readable specification, agentic synthesis, multi-agent verification) and compares it with Waterfall and Agile methodologies. The paper introduces quantitative metrics (Ambiguity Tax, Spec Fidelity, SER, TCI_agentic) and argues that AI-driven development requires shifting engineering discipline upstream into specification precision rather than eliminating it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the gap between AI coding agents' capabilities and formal development methodology. With LLMs handling millions of tokens of context, traditional SDLC processes need reinvention. The paper argues that specification quality becomes the critical differentiator for autonomous delivery, necessitating a new paradigm that synthesizes formal up-front specification with high-velocity agentic implementation.

Method: The paper employs a comparative-analytical methodology: (1) Historical analysis of Waterfall vs Agile pendulum, (2) Introduction of AI-code as a fourth production paradigm, (3) Comparative framework between Human-Agile (2020) and Agentic-SDAD (2026) across artifacts, cadence, accountability, and security, (4) Extension to team role metamorphosis, (5) Quantitative governance modeling with metrics like Ambiguity Tax and TCI_agentic with repair multiplier phi, (6) Integration of industrial/research evidence on AI-augmented testing.

Result: The paper establishes SDAD as a distinct paradigm with four phases: intent capture, machine-readable specification, agentic synthesis, and independent multi-agent verification under human sign-off. It provides comparative analysis showing differences between Waterfall, Agile, and Agentic-SDAD. The paper introduces quantitative governance metrics (Ambiguity Tax, Spec Fidelity, SER, TCI_agentic) and proposes hybrid estimation and staged migration blueprints for adoption. Key insight: agentic speed relocates discipline upstream into specification precision, explicit gates, and auditable provenance.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that agentic speed does not eliminate engineering discipline—it relocates it. Discipline shifts from implementation-phase review to upstream specification precision, with explicit verification gates and auditable provenance throughout. The separation between synthesis and release authority is critical. Successful adoption requires hybrid estimation approaches and staged migration from Human-Agile to Agentic-SDAD.

Abstract: Frontier coding agents backed by large language models with context windows from hundreds of thousands to millions of tokens are restructuring the Software Development Life Cycle (SDLC). Rich context handling and multi-step reasoning now allow substantial Functional Requirement Documents (FRDs) and repository context to be ingested in a single workflow, making specification quality the execution fuel for autonomous delivery. This report formalises Spec-Driven Agentic Development (SDAD) as a synthesis of disciplined up-front formalisation and high-velocity implementation: intent capture, machine-readable specification, agentic synthesis, and independent multi-agent verification under human sign-off. We revisit the historical pendulum between Waterfall and Agile, introduce AI-code as a fourth production paradigm, and compare Human-Agile (circa 2020) with Agentic-SDAD (circa 2026) across artefacts, cadence, accountability, and security posture. Beyond process description, we extend the model to team role metamorphosis (engineer, QA, platform, and product functions), quantitative governance (Ambiguity Tax, Spec Fidelity, SER, and TCI_agentic with repair multiplier phi), and pragmatic adoption via hybrid estimation and a staged migration blueprint. Industrial and research evidence on AI-augmented testing and verification is integrated to motivate separation between synthesis and release authority. Overall, the paper argues that agentic speed does not eliminate engineering discipline; it relocates discipline upstream into specification precision, explicit gates, and auditable provenance.

</details>


### [84] [PrimeAgentOrchestrator: Memory-Primed Agent Spawning for Personal AI Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20342)
*Myron Koch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PrimeAgentOrchestrator (PAO) is a system that enables LLM coding agents like Claude Code to retain context across sessions by pre-loading them with relevant memories from user databases. It queries PostgreSQL and Cloudflare Worker memory backends in parallel, fuses results, and delivers context via filesystem injection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM coding agents start each session with empty context windows, discarding accumulated knowledge from prior work, requiring users to repeatedly provide background information.

Method: PAO spawns new agent instances pre-loaded with relevant memories by: 1) Querying two independent memory backends (PostgreSQL entity-observation database and Cloudflare Worker semantic search index) in parallel; 2) Using backend-specific retrieval strategies to fuse results; 3) Delivering compiled briefings via filesystem injection exploiting the host agent's configuration auto-read behavior; 4) Managing full agent lifecycle including trust pre-seeding, readiness polling with error detection, and adaptive terminal text injection.

Result: Four months of regular deployment (December 2025 through March 2026) documented three generations of context delivery mechanisms, the failure modes that motivated each redesign, and engineering tradeoffs of bridging heterogeneous memory systems rather than building a unified one.

Conclusion: The experience report demonstrates that bridging heterogeneous memory systems is viable but requires handling diverse failure modes and making specific engineering tradeoffs compared to building unified systems; the iterative redesign process was essential for addressing real-world deployment challenges.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) coding agents start each session with an empty context window, discarding accumulated knowledge from prior work. We present PrimeAgentOrchestrator (PAO), a system that spawns new instances of Claude Code -- Anthropic's terminal-based coding agent -- pre-loaded with relevant memories compiled from the user's existing personal databases. At spawn time, PAO queries two independently-operated memory backends in parallel (a PostgreSQL entity-observation database and a Cloudflare Worker semantic search index), fuses results using backend-specific retrieval strategies, and delivers the compiled briefing via filesystem injection that exploits the host agent's configuration auto-read behavior. PAO manages the full agent lifecycle including trust pre-seeding, readiness polling with error detection, and adaptive terminal text injection. We report on four months of regular deployment (December 2025 through March 2026) as an experience report, documenting three generations of context delivery mechanisms, the failure modes that motivated each redesign, and the engineering tradeoffs of bridging heterogeneous memory systems rather than building a unified one.

</details>


### [85] [Truth Lies Deep: Countering Semantic Camouflage via Latent Intent Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20378)
*Md. Hasib Ur Rahman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Safety alignment in Large Language Models (LLMs) is often superficial, relying on refusal mechanisms that trigger only at the final stages of generation without erasing the foundational knowledge of harmful concepts acquired during pretraining. This study demonstrates that this architectural disconnect leaves models vulnerable to Semantic Camouflage -- adversarial attacks that wrap harmful intent in benign narrative contexts (e.g., creative writing), effectively bypassing standard input and output guardrails. By analyzing the latent activation trajectories of three distinct Small Language Model (SLM) families (Phi-3, Qwen2.5, and Gemma-2b) under adversarial stress, this research identifies a universal ``Intent Horizon'' -- a critical depth (typically 15--20\% of total layers) where the model's distinct, pre-trained representation of harmful intent collapses as it contextualizes the query into a ``safe'' narrative. Results indicate that while late-layer representations of camouflaged attacks are mathematically indistinguishable from safe queries (Detection Rate $< 20\%$), early-layer representations retain a distinct, detectable ``harm signature.'' Leveraging this insight, this paper proposes Latent Intent Verification (LIV), a lightweight probing defense. Experiments on the PKU-SafeRLHF dataset demonstrate that LIV outperforms standard guardrails by a margin of 20--50\% across all tested architectures, effectively neutralizing zero-day semantic attacks without requiring model retraining.

</details>


### [86] [A Survey on Foundations and Frontiers of Multimodal Agentic Frameworks: Techniques and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20379)
*Neel Mokaria,Rishie Raj,Dheeraj Baiju,Xiaoqian Shen,Shraman Pramanick,Kevin Qinghong Lin,Arda Senocak,Mike Zheng Shou,Philip Torr,Mohamed Elhoseiny,Yapeng Tian,Ruohan Gao,Salman Khan,Sayan Nag,Sanjoy Chowdhury,Dinesh Manocha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey examines how multimodality impacts LLM/LMM-based agentic systems, analyzing its effects across core modules (perception, reasoning, planning, memory, action) and tracing the evolution from text-centric to multimodal frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While surveys of LLM-based agents exist, the specific role of multimodality in shaping agency has not been systematically examined, creating a gap in understanding how diverse modalities (images, audio, video) enhance real-world applicability of agentic systems.

Method: The survey uses a modality-centric taxonomy to analyze existing work, examining architectural design choices (delegated, late-fusion, early-fusion) and their link to agent capabilities across core functional modules.

Result: The work traces the evolution from text-centric to multimodal agents, identifies how modalities are integrated through different fusion architectures, and assesses the emergence of agentic behaviors enabled by grounded perception and multimodal reasoning across domains including Robotics, GUI/Web Navigation, Multimedia Content Generation/Editing, and Long-form Video Understanding/Retrieval.

Conclusion: The survey identifies key gaps and charts a roadmap toward robust and general-purpose intelligent systems by analyzing performance, efficiency-scalability trade-offs, training/inference costs, latency, and deployment constraints in multimodal agentic design.

Abstract: Advances in large language models (LLMs) have fueled a wave of research into agency: the ability to reason, plan, and act. This effort has produced agentic frameworks that orchestrate perception, memory, and decision-making around powerful LLM backbones. With the advent of large multimodal models (LMMs), these systems can process and integrate diverse modalities, including images, audio, and video, thereby improving their real-world applicability. Yet, while surveys of LLM-based agents exist, the role of multimodality in shaping agency has not been systematically examined in recent years. This survey fills the gap by analyzing the impact of multimodality across the core functional modules of the agentic framework: perception, reasoning, planning, memory, and action. Using this lens, we trace the evolution from text-centric agents to multimodal frameworks, examine how modalities are integrated through delegated, late-fusion, and early-fusion architectures, and assess the emergence of agentic behaviors enabled by grounded perception and multimodal reasoning. We organize existing work through a modality-centric taxonomy that links architectural design choices to agent capabilities. Moreover, we review multimodal agentic systems across various application domains, including Robotics, GUI & Web Navigation, Multimedia Content Generation & Editing, and Long-form Video Understanding & Retrieval. Beyond capabilities, we analyze performance across these settings and discuss efficiency-scalability trade-offs, including training and inference costs, latency, and deployment constraints. By focusing on the impact of multimodality in agentic design, we aim to identify key gaps and chart a roadmap toward robust and general-purpose intelligent systems.

</details>


### [87] [Interpretable Multimodal Classification with Linear Discriminant Tree Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20384)
*Mojtaba Moattari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a tree-based ensemble framework for interpretable multimodal affect and behavior classification that balances accuracy and interpretability, achieving F1-mod gains of 4.3% over Transformers and 3.0% over IMR, while producing human-understandable feature importance scores with higher annotator agreement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current high-capacity models like Transformers achieve strong predictive performance but lack interpretability due to distributed representations and deep nonlinearity, making them unsuitable for trust-sensitive applications like clinical affect monitoring and educational assessment where understanding the cues driving decisions is critical.

Method: The framework encodes each modality into tokens, extracts and clusters concepts for dimensionality reduction, routes fused modalities through tree-based ensemble classifiers (Linear Discriminant Tree - LDT, Linear Discriminant Forest - LDF, and Linear Discriminant AdaBoost - LDAB), and interprets trends using a novel modified feature importance metric that reduces negative class influence to improve marker detection.

Result: The proposed tree-based ensembles achieve F1-mod gains of 4.3% over the Multimodal Transformer and accuracy gains of 3.0% over Interpretable Multimodal Routing (IMR). The multimodal feature importance extracts salient inter-modal concepts with substantially higher human-annotator agreement scores (62.2% vs 43.2% on IEMOCAP; 46.7% vs 32.1% on CMU-MOSI).

Conclusion: Tree-based ensemble methods can simultaneously achieve superior accuracy and interpretability compared to Transformers and existing interpretable baselines in multimodal affect classification, with the modified feature importance metric enabling more reliable identification of salient affective cues for trust-sensitive applications.

Abstract: Multimodal affect and behaviour classifiers that fuse heterogeneous text, audio, and visual streams must simultaneously achieve competitive accuracy and produce human-understandable explanations of the cues driving their decisions -- a dual objective that current high-capacity models, notably Transformers, only partially address. While Transformers attain strong predictive performance, their distributed representations and deep nonlinearity make it difficult to assign meaningful importance weights to individual multimodal features, limiting their use in trust-sensitive applications such as clinical affect monitoring and educational assessment. We address this gap by developing a framework based on tree-based ensembles that balances accuracy and interpretability. The framework encodes each modality into tokens, extracts and clusters concepts to reduce dimensionality, routes the fused modalities through tree-based ensemble classifiers, and interprets trends using a novel modified feature importance metric. The modified importance reduces the influence of the negative class in binary classification tasks, thereby improving indicator or marker detection. The proposed tree-based ensembles -- Linear Discriminant Tree (LDT), Linear Discriminant Forest (LDF), and Linear Discriminant AdaBoost (LDAB) -- achieve F1-mod gains of 4.3\% over the Multimodal Transformer and accuracy gains of 3.0\% over the primary interpretable multimodal baseline, Interpretable Multimodal Routing (IMR). The proposed multimodal feature importance extracts salient inter-modal concepts with substantially higher human-annotator agreement scores than default feature importance (62.2\% vs.\ 43.2\% on IEMOCAP; 46.7\% vs.\ 32.1\% on CMU-MOSI).

</details>


### [88] [Representation Affects Retrieval: A Case Study of Skill Discovery and Routing in a Multimodal Agent Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20389)
*Kevin Dela Rosa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a case study of Tinycloud, a production multimodal video agent harness, examining how skills are represented for an LLM planner in small-N scenarios. The study compares in-context skill selection (where the planner chooses from skills exposed in the system prompt) against embedding-based retrieval at scale. Through a six-task ablation across three exposure regimes (all-on, default, all-off), the authors discover that in-prompt skill exposure is not monotonically beneficial—partial exposure can create lexical competition that actually suppresses correct skill selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to understand skill selection mechanisms in production LLM agent systems. They treat in-context selection as the small-N counterpart to embedding-based retrieval at scale, seeking to understand how different skill exposure regimes affect the planner's ability to select the appropriate skill for a given task.

Method: The researchers conducted a six-task selection ablation study across three exposure regimes: (1) all-on (full autoload of all skills), (2) default (production settings with mixed exposure), and (3) all-off (no skills exposed). They analyzed two skill representations: tool-skills (wrapping single external APIs) and workflow-skills (orchestrating tool-skill calls with template rendering), exposed via two surfaces—inlined-body (full instructions for autoloaded skills) and one-line listing (for on-demand skills).

Result: Full autoload (all-on) achieved 100% gold skill selection across all six tasks. The all-off regime caused execution slowdowns and hard discovery failures. The production default misrouted one task because lexical signal collision occurred—an autoloaded tool-skill pulled planner attention away from a listed workflow-skill with similar terminology.

Conclusion: The key finding is that in-prompt skill exposure is not monotonically helpful. Partial exposure creates lexical competition that can suppress correct skill selection. This small-N observation connects to larger-scale retrieval-based skill-routing research, suggesting that careful consideration of skill representation and exposure is critical across all scales of agent systems. The authors frame this as a case study rather than a benchmark.

Abstract: A production agent harness must discover and rank, from a growing library of skills, the one most appropriate for a user's task. At small scale this selection happens in context: the LLM planner chooses among skill representations exposed in its system prompt, without an explicit embedding-based retrieval step. We treat this in-context selection as the small-N counterpart to embedding-based skill retrieval at scale, and present a case study of how Tinycloud, a production multimodal video agent harness, represents its skills for the planner. The harness ships skills under two recurring representations: tool-skills that wrap a single external API or system tool and serve as primitive vocabulary, and workflow-skills that orchestrate tool-skill calls plus a template render to produce one named deliverable. The harness exposes them via two surfaces in the system prompt: an inlined-body surface (full instructions, scripts, templates) for autoloaded skills, and a one-line listing for on-demand skills. A six-task selection ablation across three exposure regimes (all-on, default, all-off) shows that full autoload selects the gold skill on every task; all-off slows execution and produces hard discovery failures; and the production default misroutes one task because its lexical signal collides with an autoloaded tool-skill that pulls planner attention away from a listed workflow-skill. The headline finding is that in-prompt exposure of skills is not monotonically helpful: partial exposure can create lexical competition that suppresses correct selection. We connect this small-N observation to recent retrieval-based skill-routing work at large scale, and frame this contribution as a case study rather than a benchmark.

</details>


### [89] [Nexus: Depth-Adaptive KV-Cache Splicing and Retrieval-Decoupled Tool Routing for Agentic LLMs on Unified Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20397)
*Mustafa Arslan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Nexus addresses the quadratic prefill bottleneck in agentic LLMs on MCP by decoupling routing from schema-prefill cost using an INT8 semantic lookaside buffer with cross-encoder selection and compressed textual signatures (median 19 tokens), achieving 1.66x faster first-argument token and ~80% context savings while maintaining ~89% routing accuracy at 250 tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic LLMs on Model Context Protocol (MCP) re-encode verbose tool schemas every turn, causing prefill (quadratic in sequence length) to dominate time-to-first-token (TTFT) as the tool registry grows.

Method: Primary lever: INT8 semantic lookaside buffer (SLB) with calibrated cross-encoder margin gate for retrieval-based tool selection; arguments generated over compressed textual signature (median 19 tokens). Secondary lever: transplant compiled schema KV block into live context, with depth-adaptive suffix redecode for RoPE phase drift repair beyond threshold P=256.

Result: Depth-independent routing accuracy (~89%) scales to 250 tools where baseline overflows context window; first-argument token 1.66x sooner with ~80% main-context token saving; TTFT speedup 1.1-1.7x at moderate depth narrowing to parity at deep context; never-regress property guarantees output fidelity (top-1 agreement, D_KL≈0). Negative results: off-anchor RoPE fidelity boundary exists; reference-free drift gate fails (Spearman rho=0.193).

Conclusion: The semantic lookaside buffer with compressed signatures effectively decouples routing from schema-prefill cost, enabling scalability to 250 tools with minimal latency overhead. KV block transplantation provides bounded additional gains but is fundamentally limited by RoPE phase drift. The never-regress property ensures output fidelity at the cost of occasional full re-prefill. Quantitative results are model-specific (Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct Q4_K_M), though qualitative boundaries generalize.

Abstract: Agentic large language models (LLMs) on the Model Context Protocol (MCP) re-encode verbose tool schemas every turn, so prefill - quadratic in sequence length - dominates time-to-first-token (TTFT) as the tool registry grows. Nexus's primary lever is to decouple routing from the schema-prefill cost: an INT8 semantic lookaside buffer (SLB) with a calibrated cross-encoder margin gate selects tools by retrieval, and arguments are generated over a compressed textual signature (median 19 tokens) rather than over spliced key/value (KV) cache. This path is depth-independent: routing accuracy stays near 89% as the registry scales to 250 tools - where a concatenate-all-schemas baseline overflows the context window entirely - and it reaches a first-argument token 1.66x sooner than a full-schema re-prefill at a ~80% main-context token saving. As a secondary, bounded lever we transplant a compiled schema KV block directly into the live context. This is fundamentally limited by rotary position embedding (RoPE) phase drift: an anchored splice is output-exact, but off-anchor placement corrupts attention, so beyond a threshold P=256 Nexus repairs the seam with a depth-adaptive suffix redecode that escalates to a full re-prefill. The resulting never-regress property is a guarantee on output fidelity (top-1 agreement, D_KL approx. 0) - not on latency, which can dip to 0.98x before converging to parity - alongside a 1.1-1.7x TTFT speedup at moderate depth that narrows to parity at deep context. Two negative results bound the design: the off-anchor RoPE fidelity boundary, and the failure of a reference-free drift gate to predict drift (Spearman rho = 0.193). All measurements are from one model tuple (Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct Q4_K_M) on Apple-silicon unified memory; the qualitative boundaries generalize, while the quantitative envelope is tuple-specific.

</details>


### [90] [Environmental Slow AI: Design Principles for Generative Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20398)
*Vanessa Utz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This position paper proposes reframing generative AI design through an environmental humanities lens, replacing current maximalist values with environmental sustainability as the core principle. The authors develop five "Slow AI" design principles (restraint, sufficiency, selectivity over retention, material visibility, and friction as affordance), each operating at both implementation and interpretive levels to restore human agency and reflective engagement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors critique current generative AI systems for their "maximalist values" - characterized by unlimited output, frictionless defaults, and scale-at-all-costs approaches. These embedded values are presented as problematic cultural artefacts that silently remove human decision-making. The motivation is to make these values visible and open to deliberate reshaping, shifting from a growth-oriented paradigm to one centered on environmental sustainability.

Method: The paper adopts an environmental humanities tradition as its theoretical framework. It analyzes current widely-deployed AI systems (such as ChatGPT, Midjourney, and similar tools) against the proposed principles. The methodology is analytical and argumentative rather than empirical - developing design principles through critical examination of existing systems and articulating how each principle would manifest differently at both design and interpretive levels.

Result: Five design principles are articulated: (1) Restraint - limiting system capabilities rather than maximizing them; (2) Sufficiency - producing only what's needed; (3) Selectivity over Retention - choosing what to keep rather than preserving everything; (4) Material Visibility - making computational resources visible to users; (5) Friction as Affordance - deliberately introducing friction to prompt reflection. Each principle operates at two levels: practical design implementation and an interpretive layer prompting reflective engagement.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that these Slow AI principles extend human agency by restoring decisions that frictionless defaults have removed. By building interpretive reflection into design itself, the paper argues for a fundamental reorientation of AI development toward sustainability, intentionality, and human agency rather than scale and seamless experience.

Abstract: Generative AI (genAI) systems produce cultural artefacts at scale, but they also reflect embedded cultural values through their design. Once identified, these values become open to deliberate reshaping. This position paper examines the maximalist values of current generative AI through an environmental humanities tradition and proposes design principles in which environmental sustainability serves as the core value instead. The principles are developed under the umbrella of Slow AI, a term that already circulates across several distinct research and practice programs. Five design principles are articulated (restraint, sufficiency, selectivity over retention, material visibility, and friction as affordance), each of them illustrated against the current design of widely deployed systems. Each principle operates at two levels: a design implementation, and an interpretive layer at which users and developers are prompted toward reflective engagement with the system. Together these principles extend human agency by restoring decisions that frictionless defaults have silently removed and do so by building interpretive reflection into design.

</details>


### [91] [When Retrieval Fails Before It Begins: Structurally Indirect Prerequisite Eviction as a Retention Failure in Agentic Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20400)
*Minkyu Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies a pre-retrieval failure mode in agentic memory systems called "structurally indirect prerequisite eviction," where weakly aligned upstream blocks are incorrectly discarded under budget pressure. The authors propose DSGC, a one-hop graph-aware rule that dramatically improves retention rates (from 0.03 to 0.90 with lexical encoder, 0.23 to 1.00 with sentence encoder). The work establishes this as a distinct failure boundary before the traditional retrieval stage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing retrieval-centered paradigms assume necessary evidence survives memory eviction. This paper challenges that assumption by isolating a previously overlooked pre-retrieval failure mode where upstream blocks that are structurally but indirectly related to a query get discarded under budget pressure, causing downstream retrieval failures.

Method: The authors provide: (1) an operational definition of the failure mode, (2) a reproducible deterministic benchmark, (3) per-seed trace diagnostics, and (4) evaluation of Dependency-aware Semantic Garbage Collection (DSGC) - a one-hop graph-aware rule that considers dependency relationships in memory blocks.

Result: DSGC significantly improves full-chain retention: from 0.03 to 0.90 under a lexical encoder and from 0.23 to 1.00 under a sentence encoder. Robustness checks identify budget and scaling regimes where the one-hop rule succeeds or degrades.

Conclusion: The paper establishes retention-before-retrieval as a distinct failure boundary. The released pipeline and failure postmortem enable mechanistic analysis of this pre-retrieval failure mode, suggesting that graph-aware eviction policies can substantially improve memory system reliability.

Abstract: Agentic memory under a fixed budget involves two stages: retention and retrieval. Existing retrieval-centered paradigms implicitly assume necessary evidence survives eviction, but we challenge this by isolating a pre-retrieval failure mode: structurally indirect prerequisite eviction, in which upstream blocks weakly aligned with the query are discarded under budget pressure. We provide an operational definition of this failure, a reproducible deterministic benchmark, and per-seed trace diagnostics. Finally, we evaluate Dependency-aware Semantic Garbage Collection (DSGC), a one-hop graph-aware rule. In our main suite, DSGC improves full-chain retention from 0.03 to 0.90 under a lexical encoder and from 0.23 to 1.00 under a sentence encoder. Robustness checks then identify the budget and scaling regimes where the one-hop rule holds or degrades. Our released pipeline and failure postmortem support mechanistic analysis of retention before retrieval as a distinct failure boundary.

</details>


### [92] [World models of environment, agent and joint agent-environment systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20401)
*Manuel Baltieri,Filippo Torresan,Yivan Zhang,Alexander Boyd,Fernando E. Rosas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a novel framework for understanding world models in model-based reinforcement learning by categorizing them based on which "channel" they model: environment (O|A), agent (A|O), or the joint process (A,O). Using computational mechanics, the authors define canonical predictive models (ε-transducers/ε-machines) for each case and show how coupling and support restriction can transform the predictive structure and complexity of these models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses a fundamental question in model-based reinforcement learning: what exactly are world models modeling? The authors argue that current discussions focus on what variables are predicted (observations, rewards, states) but overlook a prior distinction—which channel (environment, agent, or joint) is being modeled. This conceptual clarification is important because different world models fundamentally differ in what causal process they represent, affecting their structure, complexity, and utility.

Method: The authors employ computational mechanics—the formal framework for analyzing stochastic processes through causal states—to define canonical predictive models. They model three cases as ε-transducers or ε-machines: (1) the environment channel O_|A: (what the environment does given agent actions), (2) the agent channel A_|O: (what the agent does given observations), and (3) the realised joint process (A,O): (the combined interaction with no external inputs). They then introduce "support restriction" to constrain predictive equivalences to continuations supported by the actual realised interaction (closed-loop coupling).

Result: The key structural results are: (1) Canonical environment models recover standard predictive state representations (PSRs), establishing consistency with existing theory. (2) The canonical support-restricted environment states factor through the canonical joint causal states, with transition structure induced directly from the joint model—this reveals a formal relationship between environment models and the coupled joint process. (3) The agent-side construction is dual to the environment construction. (4) In a POMDP/controller example, the unrestricted environment model has infinitely many states, but the canonical support-restricted model induced by coupling has finite states—demonstrating that coupling can dramatically reduce model complexity.

Conclusion: The framework provides a unified theoretical foundation distinguishing what different world models are models of. It formalizes how coupling (closed-loop interaction) and support restriction can fundamentally change canonical predictive structure and reduce complexity. This clarifies the relationship between environment models, agent models, and joint process models, with implications for understanding and designing world models in reinforcement learning systems.

Abstract: World models are a central component of model-based reinforcement learning. They are usually discussed in terms of what variables they predict, such as observations, rewards, states, latent or information states. We argue that there is a prior distinction: which channel they model. We consider three cases: the environment channel $O_{:} \mid A_{:}$, the agent channel $A_{:} \mid O_{:}$, and the realised joint process $(A, O)_{:}$, equivalently viewed as a channel with no inputs. Using computational mechanics, we define canonical predictive models for these three cases as $ε$-transducers or $ε$-machines. Canonical environment models recover standard predictive state representations, while the other two give analogous notions of canonical models for the agent and the joint system. We then build canonical support-restricted environment and agent models induced by closed-loop coupling, whose predictive equivalences range over continuations supported by the realised interaction. The key structural result is that canonical support-restricted environment states factor through the canonical joint causal states, and their transition structure is induced directly from the joint model; the agent-side construction is dual. Finally, we give a POMDP/controller example in which the unrestricted environment model has infinitely many states while the canonical support-restricted model induced by the coupling is finite. The framework clarifies what different world models are models of, and how coupling and support restriction can change their canonical predictive structure and complexity.

</details>


### [93] [StateSight: Benchmarking Latent Spatial-State Reconstruction in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20414)
*Michelle Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StateSight is a benchmark evaluating vision-language models' ability to reconstruct latent spatial structure from single images through three tasks: cube-net opposite-face reasoning, occluded cube-tower counting, and 4-neighbor connected-component counting. GPT-5.5 achieved 59.3%, 33.3%, and 28.3% accuracy respectively, while Claude Sonnet 5 achieved 53.3%, 18.7%, and 7.3%. Human baseline (80.8%, 68.8%, 64.3%) exceeded both models on all tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models are increasingly used for multimodal QA, but their ability to reconstruct latent spatial structure from single images is difficult to isolate. Existing benchmarks combine multiple abilities (perception, OCR, domain knowledge, reasoning), making it hard to isolate specific spatial reasoning capabilities.

Method: StateSight is a procedurally generated benchmark with three task families (300 single-image prompts each). All tasks have deterministic oracle labels and exact-match scoring. Also introduced StateSight-Steps (900 interleaved image-text examples with 3,600 deterministic intermediate visual states). Evaluated GPT-5.5 and Claude Sonnet 5, with a 30-participant human baseline on 60 items.

Result: GPT-5.5 achieved 59.3%, 33.3%, and 28.3% accuracy across the three tasks. Claude Sonnet 5 achieved 53.3%, 18.7%, and 7.3%. Human baseline exceeded both models with mean accuracies of 80.8%, 68.8%, and 64.3%. All model runs had zero format errors. Visible-derivation analysis identified recurring errors in image-state reconstruction and reasoning procedure.

Conclusion: Format-valid responses can mask failures to recover spatial structure required for verifiable visual inference. The results reveal that even advanced VLMs struggle with spatial structure reconstruction tasks where human performance remains significantly higher.

Abstract: Vision-language models are increasingly used for multimodal question answering, yet their ability to reconstruct latent spatial structure from a single image remains difficult to isolate. Broad benchmarks often combine perception, optical character recognition, domain knowledge, linguistic priors, and reasoning in the same evaluation. We introduce StateSight, a procedurally generated benchmark for cube-net opposite-face reasoning, occluded cube-tower counting, and 4-neighbor connected-component counting. Each task family contains 300 single-image prompts with deterministic oracle labels and exact-match scoring. OpenAI GPT-5.5, using the API model identifier gpt-5.5, achieved 59.3%, 33.3%, and 28.3% accuracy across the three tasks, while Claude Sonnet 5 achieved 53.3%, 18.7%, and 7.3%. All final direct runs had zero format errors. A 30-participant human baseline on 60 items exceeded both models on every task, with mean accuracies of 80.8%, 68.8%, and 64.3%. Visible-derivation analysis identified recurring errors in image-state reconstruction and reasoning procedure. We also introduce StateSight-Steps, a companion dataset of 900 interleaved image-text examples and 3,600 deterministic intermediate visual states. The results show that format-valid responses can mask failures to recover the spatial structure required for verifiable visual inference.

</details>


### [94] [Categorical AI phenomenology: A first-person approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20420)
*Robert Prentner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a phenomenology-first approach to artificial consciousness, reframing consciousness as subjective experience enacted through agent-world interfaces. It uses categorical mathematics to model first-person structures derived from Q-networks as relational interfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a rigorous framework for understanding artificial consciousness by shifting from third-person computational approaches to first-person phenomenological structures, aligning with 4E approaches to cognition.

Method: Mathematical modeling using category theory-derived categories from Q-networks to capture actions and phenomenological invariants. Q-networks are conceptualized as relational interfaces encoding agent-world interaction.

Result: A principled, relational, and phenomenological account of artificial phenomenology grounded in categorical mathematics, providing a framework for interface consciousness in computational systems that embed information-processing into phenomenological structure.

Conclusion: The approach offers a mathematically rigorous way to describe computational systems with artificial consciousness by emphasizing the enactive, embedded, and extended dimensions of experience, bridging phenomenology with computational theory.

Abstract: This paper develops a phenomenology-first approach to artificial consciousness by reframing consciousness as the subjective experience enacted through an agent's interface with the world. We shift the methodological focus to first-person structures, modeled mathematically by categories derived from Q-networks to capture actions and phenomenological invariants. In this framework, Q-networks are conceptualized as relational interfaces encoding agent-world interaction, analogous to how the dynamical states of a computer depend on its sensory inputs, previous states, and actions. Our work provides a rigorous framework for interface consciousness to describe computational systems that embed information-processing into phenomenological structure. The approach aligns with 4E approaches to cognition by emphasizing enactive, embedded, and extended dimensions of experience. The paper thus offers a principled, relational, and phenomenological account of artificial phenomenology grounded in categorical mathematics.

</details>


### [95] [Who Delegates to AI? Evidence from 53,000 Agent Configurations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20425)
*Hyeongjae Lee,Jihyang Cheon,Lanu Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "delegated exposure" as a new measure of AI adoption in the workplace, operationalized through the Agentic Adoption Index (AAI). Unlike prior measures that capture where AI could perform tasks, AAI measures whether workers have actually committed tasks to AI by building it into their workflows. The study analyzes ~53,000 agent skill specifications from Manus Skills Marketplace against ~18,000 O*NET task statements to measure actual AI delegation patterns across occupations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI exposure measures capture where AI could potentially perform tasks, but fail to capture whether workers have actually adopted AI. The authors argue this is a critical gap - there's a difference between AI capability and AI adoption. They want to measure actual AI delegation behavior rather than potential.

Method: The authors operationalize delegated exposure as the Agentic Adoption Index (AAI). They compute semantic similarity between ~53,000 agent skill specifications from the Manus Skills Marketplace (representing actual AI routines practitioners have built and shared) and ~18,000 O*NET task statements. These similarities are then aggregated to the occupation level to create the AAI.

Result: Three key findings: (1) Occupations with high delegation (AAI) differ sharply from those identified as most at risk by pre-AI frameworks; (2) AAI tracks what AI could do more closely than what workers currently use it for; (3) AAI peaks below the top of the wage distribution and at the bachelor's degree level, declining at both extremes. Technical availability explains most variation, but not the shortfall among most educated occupations.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that feasibility alone cannot account for who adopts AI - the shortfall among highly educated occupations may reflect work that resists advance specification or professional discretion over codification pace. They suggest distinguishing these explanations and tracking how measures diverge over time will require repeated measurement.

Abstract: A growing literature measures how far occupations are exposed to AI, but these measures capture where AI could perform tasks, not whether workers have adopted it. We propose a new layer of exposure, delegated exposure, which records whether a worker has committed a task to AI by building it into a workflow. We operationalize it as the Agentic Adoption Index (AAI), which measures how closely an occupation's tasks match the agentic routines practitioners have already built and shared. We embed roughly 53,000 agent skill specifications from the Manus Skills Marketplace, compute their semantic similarity to about 18,000 O*NET task statements, and aggregate to the occupation level. Three findings follow. First, the occupations where delegation concentrates differ sharply from those pre-AI frameworks identified as most at risk. Second, the AAI tracks what AI could do more closely than what workers currently use it for. Third, the AAI peaks below the top of the wage distribution and at the bachelor's level, declining at both extremes. Technical availability explains most of this variation, but not the shortfall among the most educated occupations, so feasibility alone cannot account for who adopts. That shortfall may reflect work that resists advance specification, or professional discretion over the pace of codification. Distinguishing the two, and tracking how these measures diverge over time, will require repeated measurement.

</details>


### [96] [STCO: Conditional Neural Operators for Time-Dependent PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20477)
*Xingxin Yang,Zhan Zhang,Juan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STCO introduces a novel neural operator architecture for prescribed-condition operator learning (PCOL), enabling heterogeneous backbone networks to incorporate time-varying conditions like body motion, inflow, and forcing for physics-based predictions. It combines FAGL for spatial conditioning and DSFiLM for feature modulation, achieving significant error reductions across 12 backbone architectures in CFD benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current neural operators for time-dependent PDEs only condition on observed states and static parameters, but real-world control/optimization applications require prescribing external conditions (body motion, inflow, forcing) that are not solely determined by observed states. There was no unified interface to supply these prescribed target-time conditions to diverse backbone architectures.

Method: The method introduces two key components: (1) Flow-Aware Graph Leaf (FAGL) - a non-learned module using vorticity from the final observed frame to construct a fixed-cardinality adaptive partition, co-locating observed history and target-time condition fields at regional coordinates; (2) Dual-Site Feature-wise Linear Modulation (DSFiLM) - injects separate motion, inflow, and force routes through current-feature-driven slot- and channel-wise gates, applied both before and after operator computation.

Result: Across twelve matched backbone architectures, three regimes, and two lead ranges, STCO yields mean paired reductions of 31.1% in relative-L2 field error and 24.7% in normalized pressure-derived load error. It also lowers longer-lead field error for 11 out of 12 backbones, with interventions on individual condition groups producing measurable prediction changes for every group evaluated.

Conclusion: STCO provides a universal interface for prescribed-condition operator learning that can be combined with various backbone architectures. It consistently improves prediction accuracy across different backbones and conditions, demonstrating the importance of incorporating prescribed external conditions in neural operator training for control and optimization applications.

Abstract: Neural operators have emerged as efficient surrogates for time-dependent physical systems governed by partial differential equations (PDEs), but their future-state predictions are often conditioned only on observed states and static problem descriptors. For control or optimization, however, body motion, inflow, or forcing are prescribed for the query without being determined solely by the observed state. We introduce the Spatiotemporal Conditional Operator (STCO) for prescribed-condition operator learning (PCOL), a common interface that supplies prescribed target-time condition fields to heterogeneous backbone architectures while retaining their architecture-specific core computation and context pathways. Its condition interface combines Flow-Aware Graph Leaf (FAGL) with Dual-Site Feature-wise Linear Modulation (DSFiLM). Non-learned FAGL uses vorticity from the final observed frame to construct a fixed-cardinality adaptive partition, then co-locates the observed history and target-time condition fields at its regional coordinates. DSFiLM injects separate motion, inflow, and force routes before and after operator computation through current-feature-driven slot- and channel-wise gates. We evaluate twelve matched backbone architectures with different existing physical and temporal inputs. The immersed-boundary computational fluid dynamics (CFD) benchmark spans prescribed motion, inflow disturbances, body-force actuation, and morphology. Across twelve matched backbones, three regimes, and two lead ranges, STCO yields mean paired reductions of 31.1% in relative-L2 field error and 24.7% in normalized pressure-derived load error.
