It also lowers longer-lead field error for 11 backbones, while interventions on individual condition groups produce measurable prediction changes for every group evaluated.

</details>


### [97] [Terminal Agents: A Survey of AI Agents in Command-Line Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20485)
*Yi Bin,Xiaoyang Yuan,Haoxi Zeng,Wencheng Ye,Wenqi Shao,Chen Qian,Wei Ye,Yujuan Ding,Zheng Wang,Pengpeng Zeng,Jingkuan Song,Heng Tao Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey examines LLM agents that operate through terminal interfaces, proposing a seven-dimensional terminal competence profile framework. It synthesizes how model, interface, harness, runtime, and environment jointly shape agent behavior, and highlights gaps in evaluation methodologies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing surveys scatter terminal-mediated behaviors across disparate research areas (software engineering, tool use, computer-use). This creates a fragmented understanding of terminal agents—systems where the dominant action-observation loop is mediated by terminal command execution, textual feedback, and stateful environment interaction. The field needs a unified framework to study these systems systematically.

Method: The authors use terminal-mediated execution as an organizing lens to establish workload-level boundaries and connect three key areas: system architecture, competence acquisition, and evaluation. They propose a seven-dimensional terminal competence profile and conduct bounded fixed-condition diagnostics to compare benchmark families and system components.

Result: The synthesis reveals that realized agent behavior is jointly shaped by five components: model, interface, harness, runtime, and environment. Executable trajectories ground learning in action consequences, verification, and recovery. Current evaluations emphasize final outcomes while underexposing process quality, recovery capabilities, and governance. Benchmark comparisons show that different benchmark families expose different process signals, and matched system comparisons reveal benchmark-dependent performance with limited component attribution.

Conclusion: The findings motivate explicit reporting of system and runtime conditions, supported by replayable traces and process-level evidence. The seven-dimensional framework provides a unified basis for studying terminal-mediated agency across software engineering and emerging application domains.

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly act through terminals, yet existing surveys disperse terminal-mediated behavior across software engineering, tool use, and computer-use research. We regard terminal agents as systems whose dominant progress-bearing action--observation loop is mediated by terminal command execution, textual feedback, and stateful environment interaction. Using terminal-mediated execution as an organizing lens, this survey establishes workload-level boundaries and connects system architecture, competence acquisition, and evaluation through a seven-dimensional terminal competence profile. Our synthesis shows that realized behavior is jointly shaped by the model, interface, harness, runtime, and environment. Executable trajectories ground learning in action consequences, verification, and recovery, whereas prevailing evaluations emphasize final outcomes and expose process quality, recovery, and governance unevenly. Bounded fixed-condition diagnostics illustrate two implications: benchmark families expose different process signals, and matched system comparisons reveal benchmark-dependent performance and limits of component attribution. These findings motivate explicit reporting of system and runtime conditions, supported by replayable traces and process-level evidence. The framework provides a unified basis for studying terminal-mediated agency across software engineering and emerging application domains.

</details>


### [98] [Lost in Translation: How Universal Ethical Values Fail to Translate Across Global Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20490)
*Ozioma C. Oguine,Munachimso B. Oguine,Cesar Cervera,Jenny Yang,Pooja Voladoddi,Mario Rodriguez,Saif Eddin Bani Malhem,Karla Badillo-Urquiola,Daricia Wilkinson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This qualitative study examines how 14 AI ethics experts from 10 countries reinterpret universal AI ethics values (fairness, transparency, accountability) in local contexts. The research reveals significant "translation gaps" between global frameworks and situated practices, with experts adapting values to fit local moral logics. The paper proposes plural governance as an alternative that redistributes epistemic authority.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study challenges the assumption that AI ethics frameworks treat values as universal and uniformly operationalizable across contexts. The researchers seek to understand how experts actually make sense of AI ethics in practice and how global frameworks are interpreted in diverse local settings.

Method: Qualitative interview-based study with 14 AI ethics experts across 10 countries, using sensemaking approaches to understand how experts reinterpret core values and envision governance alternatives.

Result: The study finds that AI deployment is characterized by structurally unequal conditions including infrastructural constraints, extractive practices, and technology "mystification." Experts reinterpret key values to fit local moral logics: (1) privacy as collective and relational rather than individual; (2) transparency as trust-building accountability rather than technical disclosure; and (3) fairness as equity in access and representation rather than parity in outcomes. These reinterpretations represent "translation gaps" between encoded global frameworks and situated local practices.

Conclusion: The paper proposes pathways toward plural governance that redistributes epistemic authority and treats ethical negotiation as an ongoing, context-sensitive process rather than a settled technical standard. This represents a significant contribution to AI ethics by challenging universalist assumptions and offering a more situated, pluralistic approach to AI governance.

Abstract: AI ethics frameworks treat values such as fairness, transparency, and accountability as universal and uniformly operationalizable across contexts. We examined how 14 experts across 10 countries made sense of AI in practice, reinterpreted core values, and envisioned governance alternatives. We found that AI deployment is characterized by structurally unequal conditions, marked by infrastructural constraints, extractive practices, and a "mystification" of technology, which fundamentally shape perceptions of risks and opportunities. Our findings reveal that experts reinterpret values to fit local moral logics: privacy as collective and relational rather than individual; transparency as trust-building accountability rather than technical disclosure; and fairness as equity in access and representation rather than parity in outcomes. We identify these as translation gaps between encoded global frameworks and situated local practices. Finally, we propose pathways toward plural governance that redistributes epistemic authority and treats ethical negotiation as an ongoing, context-sensitive process rather than a settled technical standard.

</details>


### [99] [A Temporal Planning Approach for Intelligent Flood Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20510)
*Fazlul Hasan Siddiqui,Md. Monjurul Islam,Sabah Binte Noor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an intelligent flood-response framework using temporal planning to coordinate actions for multiple simultaneous flood events. The framework models the complete operational lifecycle including triage, routing, resource allocation, and supply management, with support for mid-execution re-planning. It is formalized in both ANML and PDDL 2.1, and experimental results demonstrate its feasibility and scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Effective flood response requires coordinating multiple simultaneous flooded areas with appropriate actions in correct temporal order under severe resource constraints. Automated planning offers a solution by generating time-aware schedules given formal descriptions of resources, constraints, and goals.

Method: The framework incorporates: (1) priority-driven triage, (2) route accessibility and travel costs, (3) resource allocation, and (4) supply management. It supports mid-execution re-planning for unexpected environmental changes. The framework is formalized in both Action Notation Modeling Language (ANML) and Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) 2.1 for compatibility with various temporal planners.

Result: Experimental results establish the feasibility and scalability of the proposed framework, demonstrating that flood response scenarios can be effectively modeled and solved using temporal planning. The results also provide guidance on planner selection.

Conclusion: Temporal planning is a viable approach for flood response coordination, capable of handling the complex operational lifecycle of emergency response. The dual formalization (ANML and PDDL 2.1) enables compatibility with a wider range of temporal planners, making the framework practical for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Effective response to multiple, simultaneously flooded areas requires coordinating appropriate actions in the correct temporal order, under severe resource constraints. Automated planning provides a foundation for addressing this challenge by generating time-aware schedules, given a formal description of available resources, constraints, and goals. This work presents an intelligent flood-response framework that exploits temporal planning and models the complete operational life cycle of flood response. The framework incorporates priority-driven triage, route accessibility and travel costs, resource allocation, and supply management, while also supporting mid-execution re-planning in response to unexpected environmental changes. The framework is formulated both in the Action Notation Modeling Language (ANML) and the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) 2.1, facilitating compatibility with a wider range of temporal planners. Experimental results establish the feasibility and scalability of the proposed framework, showing that flood response scenarios can be effectively modeled and solved using temporal planning, while providing guidance on planner selection.

</details>


### [100] [FL-MAESTRO: Multi-Agent LLM Orchestration for Resource-Constrained Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20518)
*Jiajun Wu,Zirui Wang,Jiayu Zhou,Qiang Ye,Steve Drew*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FL-MAESTRO is a multi-agent LLM orchestrator for Federated Learning that jointly optimizes communication topology, per-client resource allocation, and aggregation rules through three specialist agents. It addresses the challenge of volatile edge networks where clients drop in and out during training, reducing wasted round energy from over 33% to near zero while matching baseline accuracy on non-IID CIFAR-10.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In Federated Learning, the communication topology is a runtime variable as edge devices drop in and out during training. Existing LLM-based FL systems only handle single runtime dimensions or operate at setup time. The key problem is that classical FL wastes significant energy aggregating updates from clients that will ultimately be excluded, which is the dominant source of wasted round energy on volatile edge networks.

Method: FL-MAESTRO uses three specialist LLM agents (one per decision dimension: topology, resource allocation, aggregation) that analyze client natural-text profiles and a server's predicted-failure list. A coordinator combines their analyses into a single joint decision, followed by a non-LLM feasibility check before execution. The system withholds clients whose updates would never be aggregated based on the failure prediction.

Result: On non-IID CIFAR-10 benchmark, FL-MAESTRO matches the accuracy of the strongest energy-aware baseline while cutting wasted round energy from over one-third (33%+) to near zero. The approach extends to heterogeneous device classes without requiring per-class energy models.

Conclusion: FL-MAESTRO demonstrates that LLM-based multi-agent systems can effectively optimize the three coupled runtime decisions in FL. By leveraging natural-text client profiles and predicted-failure lists, it eliminates the dominant source of wasted energy in volatile edge networks while maintaining accuracy, offering a scalable solution for heterogeneous edge environments.

Abstract: In Federated Learning (FL), the communication topology is a runtime variable rather than a fixed design choice, since links and edge devices drop in and out during training. Each round, the server must commit three coupled decisions, namely the communication topology, per-client resource allocation, and the aggregation rule for combining local updates. Recent agentic systems have begun bringing large language models (LLM) into FL, but the existing line of work either operates at setup time or handles a single runtime dimension such as client selection. We propose FL-MAESTRO, a multi-agent orchestrator that makes the joint runtime FL decision directly through three specialist LLM agents, one per decision dimension. A coordinator combines their analyses into a single decision, and a non-LLM feasibility check confirms it before the round executes. Because the orchestrator consumes the server's predicted-failure list, it withholds clients whose updates would never be aggregated, which removes the dominant source of wasted round energy in classical FL on volatile edge networks. Because client state is read as natural-text profiles, the same orchestrator extends to heterogeneous device classes without per-class energy models. On a non-IID CIFAR-10 benchmark, FL-MAESTRO matches the accuracy of the strongest energy-aware baseline while cutting wasted round energy from over a third to near zero. Code is available at https://github.com/denoslab/FL-MAESTRO.

</details>


### [101] [Volumetric Radiology AI in the Era of Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20549)
*Zanting Ye,Shengyuan Liu,Xin Liu,Chenhui Wang,Zhisong Wang,Jiashuai Liu,Zipei Wang,Cheng Wang,Wentao Pan,Mengjie Fang,Di Dong,Mohammad Salmanpour,Arman Rahmim,Yu Gu,Yong Xia,Hongming Shan,Yixuan Yuan,Yefeng Zheng,Lijun Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This review paper examines how to advance multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for volumetric radiology AI, addressing the fundamental mismatch between current 2D-based MLLMs and the need for 3D spatial context and quantitative information in clinical radiology interpretation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MLLMs are limited to 2D images or compressed representations, but clinical radiology requires full-volume spatial context and acquisition-dependent quantitative information. This creates a representational mismatch that hinders reliable volumetric radiology AI.

Method: Review of over 200 publications through July 2026, organized around: (1) volumetric representation and multimodal understanding at model level, (2) agentic orchestration at system level, and (3) links to clinical applications and evaluation. Introduces a Claim-Design-Validation framework to assess technical, workflow, and clinical claims.

Result: The review distinguishes settings where 2D views or report-mediated reasoning may suffice from those requiring native volumetric modeling. It covers volumetric foundation models, language alignment and compression strategies, and agentic systems with planning, tools, memory, and workflow interaction capabilities.

Conclusion: Clinical credibility in volumetric radiology AI requires faithful volumetric representation, traceable system behavior, claim-aligned validation, and clearly defined human oversight in realistic workflows. The appropriate approach depends on spatial, quantitative, contextual, and workflow requirements of the intended task.

Abstract: Advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are extending radiological artificial intelligence (AI) beyond task-specific image analysis toward multimodal understanding and reasoning. Volumetric radiology, however, presents a fundamental representational mismatch: clinical interpretation often requires full-volume spatial context and acquisition-dependent quantitative information, whereas current MLLMs are commonly conditioned on selected two-dimensional (2D) images, compressed visual representations, or report-derived text. Reliable volumetric radiology AI therefore requires representations that preserve task-relevant three-dimensional (3D) information and systems that can access, verify, and integrate this information across clinical workflows. In this Review, we examine more than 200 publications through July 2026. We organize the literature around volumetric representation and multimodal understanding at the model level, agentic orchestration at the system level, and their links to clinical applications and evaluation. We review volumetric foundation models, language alignment and compression strategies, and agentic systems that extend MLLMs through planning, tools, memory, and workflow interaction. We distinguish settings in which selected 2D views or report-mediated reasoning may suffice from those that warrant native volumetric modeling. We also introduce a Claim-Design-Validation framework to assess whether technical, workflow, and clinical claims are matched by appropriate design and validation. Across the literature, native volumetric modeling and agentic capabilities depend on the spatial, quantitative, contextual, and workflow requirements of the intended task. Clinical credibility requires faithful volumetric representation, traceable system behavior, claim-aligned validation, and clearly defined human oversight in realistic workflows.

</details>


### [102] [Consilience: Conformally Calibrated Communication Control for Hidden-Profile Multi-Agent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20564)
*Abhijith Babu,Ramneet Kaur,Vishal Pramanik,Olivera Kotevska,Nathaniel D. Bastian,Susmit Jha,Sunny Raj,Yanzhao Wu,Sumit Kumar Jha,Anirban Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Consilience is a novel inference-time orchestration framework for multi-agent LLM systems that addresses coordination challenges in hidden-profile settings where agents hold partial information. It uses a compact state representation to capture discussion dynamics and selects appropriate communication interventions (challenge, clarify, seek evidence, or route) with a speaker. A round-wise conformal calibration procedure provides finite-sample guarantees on action quality, improving decision accuracy and communication efficiency over fixed and unstructured protocols, sometimes exceeding full-information baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LLM systems can improve reasoning by pooling diverse perspectives, but their effectiveness depends on coordinating communication. Existing protocols (fixed schedules, round-robin, unstructured debate) fail to ensure appropriate conversational actions in hidden-profile settings where each agent holds only partial evidence required for correct decisions.

Method: Consilience uses: (1) A compact state representation capturing uncertainty, disagreement, evidence gain, redundancy, and premature consensus; (2) A controller that selects communication interventions and speakers based on this state; (3) Round-wise conformal calibration procedure providing distribution-free, finite-sample guarantees on one-step regret (probability at least 1-α); (4) An acceptance mechanism enforcing guarantees for executed actions by replacing inadmissible proposals.

Result: On HiddenBench-style hidden-profile tasks across 12 open and closed weight language models, Consilience improves decision accuracy and communication efficiency over fixed and unstructured discussion protocols. It sometimes surpasses a full-information baseline where every agent observes all evidence.

Conclusion: Certified adaptive communication control can be more valuable than increasing information availability. Consilience provides a practical mechanism for reliable multi-agent LLM coordination, demonstrating that adaptive orchestration outperforms both fixed protocols and simply providing more information to agents.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems can improve reasoning by pooling diverse perspectives, but their effectiveness depends on coordinating communication, particularly in hidden-profile settings where each agent holds only part of the evidence required for a correct decision. Existing protocols, including fixed schedules, round-robin exchange, and unstructured debate, provide no guarantee that a conversational action is appropriate. We propose Consilience, an inference-time orchestration framework that both steers and certifies multi-agent communication under distributed private information. At each turn, Consilience summarizes the discussion using a compact state capturing uncertainty, disagreement, evidence gain, redundancy, and premature consensus, then selects both a communication intervention (challenge, clarify, seek evidence, or route) and an appropriate speaker. Its central contribution is a round-wise conformal calibration procedure that provides a distribution-free, finite-sample guarantee: at each discussion round, conditional on reaching that round, the one-step regret of a controller's proposed action is bounded by a calibrated threshold with marginal probability at least 1 - alpha; an acceptance mechanism enforces the same guarantee for the executed action by replacing inadmissible proposals. On HiddenBench-style hidden-profile tasks spanning 12 open and closed weight language models, Consilience improves decision accuracy and communication efficiency over fixed and unstructured discussion protocols, sometimes surpassing a full-information baseline where every agent observes all evidence. These results demonstrate that certified adaptive communication control can be more valuable than increasing information availability, providing a practical mechanism for reliable multi-agent LLM coordination.

</details>


### [103] [Open-Weight Masked Introspection: Measuring What Language Models Can Report About Their Own Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20569)
*Emilio Ferrara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper tests whether frontier models can introspect about changes to their own internal states. The authors built OWMI, a framework that intervenes on various model components (residual-stream sites, attention heads, SAE features) and asks models to detect these changes. Across 78,000+ measurements on 8 open-weight models from 7 families, no model performed better than chance at discriminating real interventions from sham controls. However, the information needed for detection IS present in the models—fine-tuned models achieve near-perfect recovery and linear probes achieve 75-95.8% accuracy. The failure lies in translating internal state to verbal report.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to test a bold claim from recent work: that sufficiently complex models can audit their own internals, detect changes, and report them confidently. This has implications for AI safety and oversight—if models can self-monitor, they could potentially flag problematic internal states. The authors seek to rigorously verify this capability across multiple open-weight models.

Method: The authors built Open-Weight Masked Introspection (OWMI), a comprehensive framework that: (1) Intervenes on residual-stream sites, attention heads, and sparse-autoencoder features; (2) Interrogates models about whether their computation was altered; (3) Uses multiple null conditions to establish chance performance: sham runs (no alteration), impact-matched random perturbations, and a text-only observer that only sees visible output. They tested 8 open-weight models from 7 families across over 78,000 measurements.

Result: No model could discriminate real interventions from sham beyond chance (AUROC ~0.5007). An equivalence test bounds the effect below 0.15 percentage points of AUROC. However, the information exists in the models: a fine-tuned model achieved near-perfect recovery on held-out directions, and a linear probe recovered intervention presence from activations at 75-95.8% accuracy (sharpening to no held-out error at the last layer). In one model, the signal appeared in confidence rather than words—its yes/no answers never varied, but confidence separated intervention from sham at AUROC 0.647.

Conclusion: Current open-weight models cannot introspect about changes to their internal states. The failure occurs in the path from internal state to verbal report—the information is present but not accessible to the model's own self-reporting. This means oversight mechanisms that rely on a model's own testimony need validation against an internal reference. The debate remains open for future, potentially more sophisticated models.

Abstract: Are frontier models able to introspect about their internal states? Recent work suggests that under certain conditions a complex enough model can audit its own internals, call out what changed, and report back confidently about it. We tested that claim on eight open-weight models from seven families and found no such ability: asked whether their own computation had been altered, none answered better than chance. To test it we built Open-Weight Masked Introspection (OWMI), a framework that intervenes on residual-stream sites, attention heads and sparse-autoencoder features, then interrogates the model about the change against the null conditions an answer has to beat: sham runs where nothing was altered, impact-matched random perturbations, and a text-only observer that sees only the visible output.
  Over 78,000 measurements, no model's report discriminates a real intervention from a sham beyond chance (AUROC ~0.5007), and an equivalence test bounds the effect below 0.15 percentage points of AUROC. Surprisingly, all the information needed is in the models. A model fine-tuned to report this class of intervention reaches near-perfect recovery on held-out directions, and a linear probe recovers intervention presence from the same activations at 75% to 95.8% accuracy, sharpening to no held-out error at the last layer before the model speaks. In one model the signal surfaces in the confidence rather than the words: its yes-or-no report never varies, while the confidence attached to it separates intervention from sham at AUROC 0.647. The failure sits in the path from internal state to verbal report, so oversight that reads a model's own testimony needs validating against an internal reference.
  While our results show the inability of current open-weight models to introspect, the debate is not settled for future models.

</details>


### [104] [FlavourBench: Ranking Frontier Language Models with Executable Culinary Ground Truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20574)
*Josef Chen,Erim Hayretci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlavourBench is an automated benchmark for evaluating open-ended language models using a versioned culinary system as executable ground truth. Tasks present 8 ingredients and ask for 3-ingredient portfolios, with all 56 possible portfolios pre-scored by Epicure. 27 frontier endpoints were evaluated on 534 tasks, with Grok 4.6 achieving the highest score of 65.1 (95% CI 61.0-69.2).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-ended language-model benchmarks typically rely on human preference panels, other models, or brittle exact-match keys as judges, which can be inconsistent, expensive, or prone to gaming. This paper introduces an automated benchmark with dense, executable ground truth from a culinary system to provide more reliable and consistent evaluation.

Method: FlavourBench uses a versioned culinary system where each task presents eight ingredients and asks for a three-ingredient portfolio. Epicure scores all 56 possible portfolios before model execution. The benchmark evaluates 27 frontier endpoints on 534 tasks spanning substitution, pairing, and constrained composition. Every ranked model has exactly 89 valid responses per panel and family (14,418 model-task cells total). FlavourBench Score is the equal-family mean of frozen task scores. Statistical analysis uses 50,000 anchor-cluster bootstrap replicates for 95% score bands and 100,000 sign-flip draws for 351 paired model contrasts with Holm correction.

Result: Grok 4.6 has the largest point estimate at 65.1 (simultaneous 95% CI 61.0-69.2). Two independently compiled panels correlate at r = 0.89 (rank rho = 0.80). 101 of 351 model pairs are statistically resolved. The benchmark eliminates differential missingness from the leaderboard.

Conclusion: FlavourBench provides a novel automated benchmark with executable ground truth from a culinary system, offering more reliable evaluation of open-ended language models. The release includes prompts, portfolio score maps, raw responses, exact routes, content hashes, and an offline verifier for reproducibility.

Abstract: Open-ended language-model benchmarks usually inherit a judge: a human preference panel, another model, or a brittle exact-match key. We introduce FlavourBench, an automated benchmark in which a versioned culinary system supplies dense, executable ground truth. Each task presents eight ingredients and asks for a three-ingredient portfolio; before model execution, Epicure scores all 56 possible portfolios. We evaluate 27 frontier endpoints on an identical 534-task core spanning substitution, pairing, and constrained composition. Every ranked model has exactly 89 valid responses per panel and family (14,418 model-task cells total), eliminating differential missingness from the leaderboard. The FlavourBench Score is the equal-family mean of the frozen task scores. We use 50,000 anchor-cluster bootstrap replicates for simultaneous 95% score bands and 100,000 sign-flip draws for all 351 paired model contrasts, with Holm control. The two independently compiled panels correlate at r = 0.89 (rank rho = 0.80). Grok 4.6 has the largest point estimate at 65.1 (simultaneous 95% CI 61.0-69.2); 101 of 351 model pairs are resolved. The release includes the prompts, all portfolio score maps, raw responses, exact routes, content hashes, and an offline verifier that reconstructs every result.

</details>


### [105] [Difficulty-Aware Semantic-ID Optimization for Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20611)
*Xin Yu,Stephen Li,Sina Aghaei,Zifan Zhu,Jiamu Bai,Guanjie Huang,Bo Peng,Yiyao Liu,Lingzhou Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DASO is a tree-aware post-training method for Semantic-ID-based generative recommendation that addresses GRPO's failure in tree-structured tasks by profiling rollout groups by prefix-match depth and reallocating samples to prefix-guided completions at bottleneck SID levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vanilla GRPO poorly matches tree-structured Semantic-ID tasks because the exact target is often absent from the first 16 candidates of 50-beam constrained ranking, and in harder cases no candidates enter the target SID branch. When on-policy GRPO groups lack targets, item-level rewards produce weak or degenerate reward variation.

Method: DASO profiles each rollout group by prefix-match depth to locate bottleneck SID levels where candidates leave the target path. It reallocates a bounded portion of the group to prefix-guided completions while retaining raw rollouts for contrast. A SID-prefix reward provides graded credit, while an auxiliary SFT anchor mitigates regression on examples already solved by the SFT checkpoint.

Result: DASO improves over MiniOneRec-style GRPO on 11 of 12 metrics and achieves the best result on 9 of 12 metrics on public benchmarks; it also improves most level-wise recall metrics on the internal recommendation task.

Conclusion: DASO effectively addresses the target-missing problem in GRPO for tree-structured semantic ID recommendation by using difficulty-aware rollout allocation and prefix-guided completions, achieving state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: Semantic-ID-based generative recommendation casts retrieval and ranking as autoregressive generation over hierarchical item identifiers. A common recipe is SFT followed by GRPO, yet vanilla GRPO is poorly matched to this tree-structured task. Under the frozen SFT checkpoint, the exact target is absent from the first 16 candidates of the 50-beam constrained ranking for many prompts, and in harder cases none of these candidates enters the target SID branch. This prompt-level diagnostic motivates a training concern: when on-policy GRPO groups are similarly target-missing, item-level rewards may produce weak or degenerate reward variation even if some candidates follow part of the target path. We propose Difficulty-Aware Semantic-ID Optimization (DASO), a tree-aware post-training method that addresses this failure mode as an online rollout-allocation problem. Instead of using fixed difficulty buckets or uniformly injecting ground-truth completions, DASO profiles each current rollout group by prefix-match depth, locates the bottleneck SID levels where candidates leave the target path, and reallocates a bounded portion of the group to prefix-guided completions while retaining raw rollouts for contrast. A SID-prefix reward provides graded credit, while an auxiliary SFT anchor mitigates regression on examples already solved by the SFT checkpoint. On the public benchmarks, DASO improves over MiniOneRec-style GRPO on 11 of 12 metrics and achieves the best result on 9 of 12 metrics; it also improves most level-wise recall metrics on the internal recommendation task.

</details>


### [106] [Applying Anthropic Primitives at Large Enterprises: Harness Paradigm for Knowledge Work](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20622)
*George Juraj Salapa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Frontier models have collapsed the cost of writing custom code: a niche problem a specialist sees in their own domain now costs an afternoon. The cost of reviewing and maintaining that code hasn't collapsed. Each solution drifts from the next; understanding one means reading its codebase from scratch. Large enterprises build something centrally governed instead: at worst an off-the-shelf product, at best a graph-orchestration framework wired bespoke per use case, or a low-code platform used as the orchestrator. These are custom every time and limited in scope. Enterprises don't weigh a third option that escapes both constraints: the harness paradigm.
  Recent work treats the coding-agent harness as enterprise infrastructure rather than a coding tool, converging on three findings: harnesses suffice at the task level and outperform more elaborate architectures on enterprise work (arXiv:2604.00073, arXiv:2604.13107); harness choice accounts for most of the variance in agent benchmark results, more than model choice does (arXiv:2605.23950); and the gap between that finding and enterprise adoption is governance (arXiv:2605.10223, arXiv:2605.18747).
  We propose an architecture that closes that gap. One harness runs unmodified as the backbone; the code stays identical across every deployment, so reviewing what gets built collapses to reading its instructions file. Section 4 gives four mechanisms: credential-scoped tooling, where each backend gets one generic request tool and a scoped credential instead of a hand-built method; authorization logic outside the harness, so one artifact runs as a cron backbone, a chat-surface engine, and a terminal tool; registration is a side effect of pushing code, collapsing an audit a review of a text file.
  Built on microcc (<https://pypi.org/project/micro-cc/>), our reference harness.

</details>


### [107] [Evaluating Skills, Not Just Agents: Agentic Continuous Evaluation of Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20614)
*Christopher Kevin,Narendran Raghavan,Jean-Francois Puget,Roshni Malani,Meghana Puvvadi,Moshe Abramovitch,Mohit Gupta,Rama Akkiraju,Subodh Prabhu,Yogesh Dangi,Wei Luo,Seong Hee Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ACES is a repository-native framework for evaluating enterprise agent skills as executable artifacts through paired live trials, measuring Skill Lift (the added value of a target skill) across six runtime metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current deployment gates only scan artifacts for structure, style, and security but don't answer whether capability packages actually help live agents complete enterprise tasks under real conditions.

Method: Runs paired live trials with/without target skills, normalizes trajectories into ATIF format, grades six default runtime metrics, and reports Skill Lift. Tested on 145 real skills from internal and public catalogs across 947 paired cases.

Result: Mean composite Skill Lift = 0.2134 (95% CI [0.1967, 0.2301]); mean outcome-only lift = 0.1799. Composite lift positive in 72.8% of cases. Largest gains in skill execution, behavior check, and skill efficiency. Scan-only gates show weak correlation with LLM-judge (Spearman ρ=0.14).

Conclusion: Process-metric gains document capabilities that scans cannot observe. ACES provides evidence-based evaluation for agent skill deployment decisions, with an open-source implementation in NVIDIA SkillEvaluator.

Abstract: Enterprise agent programs are moving from prototypes into production, where reusable skills, tools, and workflow packages must be reviewed with evidence rather than prose. Current gates often scan these artifacts for structure, style, and security, but they do not answer the deployment question: does the capability package help a live agent complete enterprise tasks under the same model, sandbox, and grading policy?
  We present ACES (Agentic Continuous Evaluation of Skills), a repository-native framework for evaluating skills and product capability packages as executable agent artifacts. ACES runs paired live trials with and without a target skill, normalizes trajectories into the Agent Trajectory Interchange Format (ATIF), grades six default runtime metrics, and reports Skill Lift: the target skill's added value for a fixed task, harness, workspace, and scorer. The same protocol supports product-owned task suites that compare baseline, skill, bundle, team-skill, and plugin targets.
  On 145 real skills from internal enterprise repositories and public catalogs, scan-only gates surface useful authoring issues but measure complementary facets (structural versus LLM-judge Spearman $ρ= 0.14$). Across 947 scored paired cases from 58 of 64 production skills and four primary harnesses, mean composite Skill Lift is 0.2134 (95\% paired-case CI [0.1967, 0.2301]); mean outcome-only lift, the average of accuracy and goal accuracy, is 0.1799. Composite lift is positive in 72.8\% of paired cases. The largest process-metric gains appear in skill execution, behavior check, and skill efficiency---signals about discovery, routing, workflow following, and tool use that document scans cannot observe. An open-source implementation of the methodology is available in NVIDIA SkillEvaluator.

</details>


### [108] [DreamBench-SWE: A Multi-Session Memory-Hygiene Benchmark for Software Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20664)
*Sarthak Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DreamBench-SWE is a multi-session benchmark testing software agents' memory hygiene, where later tasks depend on evidence from earlier sessions. The study evaluated various memory configurations including no external memory, verbatim event memory, typed-plus-raw reference probe, and hosted Mem0 literal-storage. Results showed the best configuration (pinned hosted Mem0) achieved only 97/180 passes (53.89%). The primary DF-hybrid contrast was null, and no external memory system achieved statistically significant superiority, though the benchmark itself is characterized as a discriminating executable profile tool.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate software-agent memory hygiene through a multi-session benchmark where later software tasks depend on non-inferable evidence from earlier sessions, scored by executable hidden oracles. The study aimed to test whether external memory systems improve agent performance on interdependent tasks.

Method: The benchmark tested four memory conditions across 360 work units and 720 S3 cells: (1) no external memory (baseline), (2) deterministic verbatim event memory, (3) typed-plus-raw reference probe, and (4) pinned hosted Mem0 literal-storage configuration. The original v2 fold was followed by a separately preregistered v2.1 successor audit. Statistical testing included clustered p-values, Holm correction, and equivalence testing.

Result: Results showed: no external memory achieved 21/180 passes (11.67%), verbatim event memory achieved 82/180 (45.56%), typed-plus-raw reference probe achieved 83/180 (46.11%), and pinned hosted Mem0 achieved 97/180 (53.89%). The primary DF-hybrid contrast was null (95/180 vs 89/180; clustered p=0.518, Holm p=1), not providing evidence of equivalence. The registered six-slot Family A retained unavailable slots at p=1. All comparisons against no memory rejected after Holm correction. The literal-storage-versus-verbatim comparison was nonconfirmatory and sensitivity-dependent.

Conclusion: The audit supports DreamBench-SWE as a discriminating executable profile benchmark and characterizes one exact hosted-memory configuration, but does not establish an external-system mechanism, superiority among memory-bearing conditions, equivalence, or broad product generality. The original v2.0.5 findings and artifacts remain unchanged. External memory systems did not demonstrate statistically significant advantages over baseline in this benchmark.

Abstract: DreamBench-SWE is a multi-session benchmark for software-agent memory hygiene in which later software tasks depend on non-inferable evidence from earlier sessions and are scored by executable hidden oracles. We report the original scaled v2 fold and a separately preregistered v2.1 successor audit designed after that study but frozen before successor outcome inspection. The successor run completed 360/360 work units and 720/720 S3 cells across four conditions. In the original fold, the primary DF-hybrid--B5 contrast was null (95/180 versus 89/180; clustered p=.518, Holm p=1), not evidence of equivalence, and C9/C10 retained B0-headroom limitations. In the successor, no external memory achieved 21/180 passes (rate 0.1167), deterministic verbatim event memory 82/180 (rate 0.4556), the typed-plus-raw reference probe 83/180 (rate 0.4611), and one pinned hosted Mem0 literal-storage configuration 97/180 (rate 0.5389). The registered six-slot Family A retained unavailable slots at p=1; all three available comparisons against no memory rejected after Holm correction. Both preregistered mechanism contrasts were unavailable after pre-evaluation conformance rejection. The secondary literal-storage-versus-verbatim comparison was nonconfirmatory and sensitivity-dependent, while the comparison with the reference probe did not reject. The audit therefore supports DreamBench-SWE as a discriminating executable profile benchmark and characterizes one exact hosted-memory configuration, but it does not establish an external-system mechanism, superiority among memory-bearing conditions, equivalence, or broad product generality. The original v2.0.5 findings and artifacts remain unchanged.

</details>


### [109] [Dual-Cache Latent Space Communication between Heterogeneous Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20617)
*Jiyao Liu,Qi Zhang,Yaoyi Jia,Ziwen Kan,Song Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: XKV is a novel cache translation method for multi-agent LLM systems that enables efficient knowledge sharing between heterogeneous models by pooling their KV caches through learned-query attention, eliminating the need for slow text-based communication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LLM systems require knowledge transfer between agents, but current methods either rely on slow text exchange (putting autoregressive decoding on critical path) or have limitations: C2C requires same input for both models, while LCF-X compresses only the sharer's cache, supplies uniform summaries to all receiver positions, and assumes matched layer counts and KV geometry.

Method: XKV introduces three key innovations: (1) learned-query attention that pools both sharer and receiver KV caches; (2) self-attention over receiver-aligned layer tokens with a learned layer map to reconcile different model depths, creating joint cross-layer memory; (3) a shared position decoder enabling per-head-gated residual retrieval in receiver's native KV geometry. Both models remain frozen; only the translator is trained.

Result: Across 45 dataset-model-pair settings (6 heterogeneous + 3 same-model pairings, 5 datasets), XKV achieves highest macro score and best average rank. It improves over LCF-X on every dataset (by 4.6 exact-match and 4.2 F1 points on ROPES) and surpasses text communication on 4/5 datasets, while training 76% fewer parameters and translating caches 10.3x faster (5.8 vs 59.9 ms). End-to-end, XKV is 26% faster than LCF-X and 6.8x faster than text communication.

Conclusion: XKV successfully lifts all three restrictions of prior work (LCF-X), enabling effective KV cache translation between heterogeneous models with different depths, KV-head counts, head dimensions, and tokenizers. It demonstrates superior accuracy, faster inference, and significantly reduced training parameters, making it a practical solution for efficient multi-agent LLM communication.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems split work across models, so answering often requires knowledge that sits in another agent's context: a Sharer has encoded information that a Receiver needs to complete its task. They usually communicate by exchanging text, which puts autoregressive decoding on the critical path and reduces the exchange to a discrete message written without sight of the receiver's state. Recent latent protocols instead translate the sharer's key-value (KV) cache into the receiver's: C2C supports heterogeneous models but requires both to read the same input, while LCF-X removes this shared-context requirement through position-free sharer-cache pooling. Three restrictions remain: LCF-X compresses the sharer alone, supplies the same layer-local summary to every receiver position with no joint cross-layer memory to retrieve from, and assumes matched layer count and KV geometry. We introduce XKV, which lifts all three: learned-query attention pools both caches; self-attention over receiver-aligned layer tokens, with a learned layer map reconciling different depths, mixes the pooled summaries into a compact joint memory; and a shared position decoder lets every raw receiver cache position retrieve its own per-head-gated residual in the receiver's native KV geometry. Both models stay frozen and may differ in family, depth, KV-head count, head dimension, and tokenizer; only the translator is trained. Across 45 dataset-model-pair settings (six heterogeneous and three same-model ordered pairings, five datasets), XKV attains the highest macro score and best average rank, improving on LCF-X on every dataset (by 4.6 exact-match and 4.2 F1 points on ROPES) and surpassing text communication on four of the five, while training 76% fewer parameters and translating a cache pair 10.3x faster (5.8 vs. 59.9 ms); end to end, XKV is 26% faster than LCF-X and 6.8x faster than text communication.

</details>


### [110] [Large Language Models at the Intersection of Software Engineering and Software Security:An Evidence-Centered Structured Survey and Research Agenda](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21107)
*Wei Lin,Tao Zhou,Zhaofei Xie,Changgui Hong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A survey paper synthesizing research on LLMs as repository-scale agents, examining both software engineering (functional task completion) and software security (vulnerability detection) evaluations. The paper introduces an assurance framework separating functional correctness, security, operational reliability, evidence provenance, and agent authority, and identifies recurring validity threats in current LLM agent evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are evolving from simple code completion tools to repository-scale agents that can retrieve context, edit files, execute tools, and participate in security-sensitive workflows. However, evidence for these systems remains divided between software engineering evaluations (focused on functional task completion) and software security evaluations (focused on vulnerability detection and secure generation). This division highlights a gap in understanding how LLMs perform across both dimensions.

Method: Evidence-centered structured survey of representative work available through May 31, 2026. The survey covers software engineering tasks, software security tasks, adaptation mechanisms, artifact granularity, and evaluation design. A task taxonomy and assurance framework were developed to systematize the evidence.

Result: Execution feedback and repository access can substantially improve engineering task completion but do not establish security by themselves. Conversely, static-analysis labels or vulnerability-classification scores rarely establish deployable correctness. Identified validity threats include: weak test oracles, duplicated and temporally leaked data, changing agent harnesses, proxy-only security checks, and under-reported budgets and human intervention.

Conclusion: Model capability should be judged as an assurance case supported by task-appropriate evidence, rather than by a single benchmark score. The paper proposes a minimum reporting protocol for cross-study comparison and outlines a research agenda prioritizing jointly secure-and-functional benchmarks, repository-scale threat models, calibrated human oversight, longitudinal maintainability evidence, and reproducible agent evaluation.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are moving from code completion toward repository-scale agents that retrieve context, edit files, execute tools, and participate in security-sensitive workflows. The evidence for these systems, however, remains divided between software engineering evaluations centered on functional task completion and software security evaluations centered on vulnerability detection, secure generation, or exploit-oriented validation. This evidence-centered structured survey synthesizes representative work available through May 31, 2026 across software engineering tasks, software security tasks, adaptation mechanisms, artifact granularity, and evaluation design. In addition to a task taxonomy, we introduce an assurance framework that separates functional correctness, security, operational reliability, evidence provenance, and agent authority. The review shows that execution feedback and repository access can substantially improve engineering task completion, but do not by themselves establish security; conversely, static-analysis labels or vulnerability-classification scores rarely establish deployable correctness. We identify recurring validity threats--weak test oracles, duplicated and temporally leaked data, changing agent harnesses, proxy-only security checks, and under-reported budgets and human intervention--and derive a minimum reporting protocol for cross-study comparison. The resulting research agenda prioritizes jointly secure-and-functional benchmarks, repository-scale threat models, calibrated human oversight, longitudinal maintainability evidence, and reproducible agent evaluation. The central conclusion is that model capability should be judged as an assurance case supported by task-appropriate evidence, rather than by a single benchmark score.

</details>


### [111] [SAGE: A Unified Algebra and Self-Adaptive Execution for AI Functions in SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20630)
*Xiangqi Wang,Nhan H. Pham,Oktie Hassanzadeh,Dharmashankar Subramanian,Xiangliang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGE is a unified framework for integrating AI functions into SQL systems, introducing three typed primitives (AI_SCALAR, AI_AGG, AI_JOIN) that map to relational roles. It addresses the challenge of AI_JOIN optimization through predicate analysis and strategy selection, achieving significant performance improvements including the strongest SemBench results and up to 358-fold cost reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SQL systems increasingly expose AI functions for diverse tasks (classification, extraction, filtering, ranking, etc.), but lack a unified framework to capture their relational roles and optimize execution efficiently.

Method: SAGE presents three typed primitives (AI_SCALAR, AI_AGG, AI_JOIN) that capture the three relational roles of AI functions: transforming individual rows, aggregating groups, or generating relationships between row pairs. For AI_JOIN specifically, SAGE analyzes predicates, decomposes compound conditions, and uses a recipe card with a small label-free probe to select among execution strategies.

Result: SAGE achieves the strongest overall SemBench performance and, on a representative factorable join, reduces pairwise model calls by more than two orders of magnitude, yielding a 358-fold measured cost reduction.

Conclusion: This formulation covers common AI functionality while consistently improving execution quality and efficiency. The unified approach with typed primitives enables natural composition with standard relational operators and physical strategies tailored to relational shape.

Abstract: SQL systems increasingly expose AI functions for tasks such as classification, extraction, filtering, ranking, retrieval, joining, and summarization. Despite their diverse APIs, these functions play only three relational roles: transforming individual rows, aggregating groups, or generating relationships between row pairs. We present SAGE (Self-Adaptive Generative Execution), a unified logical and physical framework that captures these roles with three typed primitives, AI_SCALAR, AI_AGG, and AI_JOIN, and composes them naturally with standard relational operators. All primitives share a confidence-gated execution interface while supporting physical strategies tailored to their relational shape. The main challenge is AI_JOIN, where SAGE analyzes the predicate, decomposes compound conditions when possible, and uses a recipe card together with a small label-free probe to select among complete execution strategies. Across a broad audit of public AI operators and evaluations spanning scalar, aggregate, and join workloads, this formulation covers common AI functionality while consistently improving execution quality and efficiency. SAGE achieves the strongest overall SemBench performance and, on a representative factorable join, reduces pairwise model calls by more than two orders of magnitude, yielding a 358-fold measured cost reduction.

</details>


### [112] [Weighted Memory Tree: Remembering What Matters for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20631)
*Quang Dao,Purvi Kathalkar,Kenneth Eaton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces WMT, a hierarchical memory system for LLM agents that organizes execution into tasks, subtasks, and actions with dynamic retention scores. It addresses the problem of growing execution histories causing increased inference costs and reasoning degradation from outdated/irrelevant information. WMT uses event-based updates and selection-based decay to manage memory, achieving 9.97% accuracy improvement and 32.8% token reduction on GAIA-Text benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents solving multi-step tasks face challenges with growing execution histories: (1) increased inference costs, (2) exposure to outdated, irrelevant, or misleading information that degrades reasoning quality. Existing memory approaches organize or compress histories but lack mechanisms for deciding which memories should remain active.

Method: The paper proposes WMT (Weighted Memory Team), a hierarchical memory system with three key components: (1) Hierarchical organization of execution into tasks, subtasks, and actions; (2) Dynamic retention score assignment for each memory; (3) Event-based updates and selection-based decay to revise retention scores. This allows preserving useful information, folding completed trajectories, suppressing low-utility content, and retaining access to folded context.

Result: Evaluated on GAIA-Text using Qwen3-8B, Gemma 4 E4B, and Llama-3.1-8B: (1) Average accuracy improvement of 9.97 percentage points relative to linear memory; (2) 32.8% reduction in prompt-token usage; (3) Memory-poisoning experiments show WMT limits persistence and propagation of unreliable information.

Conclusion: Effective long-horizon agent memory depends less on storing more information than on deciding which information should remain active. The key insight is that selective retention based on utility scoring is more important than capacity expansion.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents have demonstrated the ability to solve multi-step tasks requiring planning, tool use, and external information access, yet growing execution histories increase inference cost and expose reasoning to outdated, irrelevant, or misleading information, potentially degrading reasoning quality. Existing memory approaches organize or compress execution histories but provide limited mechanisms for deciding which memories remain active. We introduce the, a hierarchical memory system that organizes execution into tasks, subtasks, and actions while assigning each memory a dynamic retention score. Event-based updates and selection-based decay revise these scores, allowing WMT to preserve useful information, fold completed trajectories, suppress low-utility content, and retain access to folded context. We evaluate WMT on GAIA-Text using Qwen3-8B, Gemma 4 E4B, and Llama-3.1-8B, with ablations and memory-poisoning experiments. Relative to linear memory, WMT improves accuracy by an average of 9.97 percentage points while reducing prompt-token usage by 32.8%. Memory-poisoning experiments show that WMT limits the persistence and propagation of unreliable information. Our results suggest that effective long-horizon agent memory depends less on storing more information than on deciding which information should remain active.

</details>


### [113] [Beyond Effectiveness: A Multi-Criteria Framework for Comparing Practical Socio-Technical Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20649)
*Catherine King,Lynnette Hui Xian Ng,Kathleen M. Carley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a multi-criteria framework for evaluating sociotechnical interventions, using misinformation as a case study. Through a survey of 39 researchers on 40 interventions across five criteria (political feasibility, effectiveness, user acceptance, cost, implementation effort), the study reveals that the most effective interventions are often not the most acceptable or feasible to implement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designers and policymakers in sociotechnical domains lack a principled basis for comparing interventions. Prior work evaluates interventions individually mainly along effectiveness criteria, while implementation constraints are considered separately. This creates a gap in understanding trade-offs between different evaluative criteria.

Method: The researchers developed a multi-criteria framework and instantiated it through the misinformation domain. They conducted a survey with N=39 researchers, evaluating 40 operationalized interventions across five evaluative criteria: political feasibility, effectiveness, user acceptance, cost, and implementation effort.

Result: The study found that interventions judged most effective by experts are not always the most acceptable to the public or the most feasible to implement, revealing significant trade-offs between effectiveness and other criteria.

Conclusion: The tension between effectiveness and other criteria (acceptance, feasibility) has implications for the design of sociotechnical interventions beyond misinformation. The paper offers a decision framework for practitioners navigating these trade-offs.

Abstract: Designers and policymakers in sociotechnical domains like content moderation, privacy interfaces, recommender systems and beyond, must choose among a growing menu of proposed interventions, but typically lack a principled basis for comparing them. Prior work tends to evaluate interventions individually and mostly along the effectiveness criteria, while implementation constraints such as cost, effort and feasibility are often considered separately. We present a multi-criteria framework for evaluating sociotechnical interventions. This framework is instantiated through the case of misinformation, a domain of intense focus for proposed countermeasures. We survey $N=39$ researchers on 40 operationalized interventions across five evaluative criteria: political feasibility, effectiveness, user acceptance, cost, and implementation effort. We find that the interventions that experts judge to be the most effective are not always the most acceptable to the public or the most feasible to implement. We also discuss how this tension has implications for the design of sociotechnical interventions beyond misinformation, and offer a decision framework for practitioners navigating the trade-offs of sociotechnical interventions.

</details>


### [114] [Auditable by Construction: An Ontology-Driven Framework for Trustworthy LLM Analytics in Enterprise Finance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20661)
*Sergiy Lunyakin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents the Knowledge-Driven Analytics Framework (KDAF) for retrieval-augmented generation in enterprise finance, emphasizing auditability over accuracy as the key evaluation metric. KDAF builds ontology-driven knowledge systems through six iterative stages and retrieves evidence via Context-Aware Relevance Propagation (CARP), ensuring every retrieved fact carries relationship type, confidence, and source lineage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise adoption of large language models in finance is constrained by trust rather than fluency. In regulated workflows like Financial Planning and Analysis (FP&A), answers are usable only if they are traceable to authoritative sources and auditable after the fact. Existing evaluation metrics focus on accuracy, but this is insufficient for enterprise finance where audit trails are essential.

Method: The Knowledge-Driven Analytics Framework (KDAF) builds ontology-driven knowledge systems through six iterative stages. Evidence retrieval uses Context-Aware Relevance Propagation (CARP), where every retrieved fact carries its relationship type, confidence, and source lineage. Evaluation was conducted on FinanceBench with 145 questions, comparing KDAF against zero-context inference, BM25, concept-weighted lexical retrieval, and ungrounded graph traversal.

Result: Three key findings emerged: (1) Retrieval is necessary - zero-context inference achieves only 4.1% correctness vs 10-12% for retrieval-augmented methods; (2) On answer correctness, retrieval methods are statistically indistinguishable (KDAF vs BM25: -0.007, 95% CI [-0.021, 0.000]), indicating accuracy alone does not justify structured retrieval; (3) On auditability, KDAF attains the highest citation traceability F1 (0.515), exceeding ungrounded traversal by +0.027 and BM25 by +0.052, with intervals excluding zero. Graph-structured retrieval admits no evidence from outside the question subject entity (0 of 426 items), while lexical baselines provide 16.8% and 20.2% from external sources.

Conclusion: Auditability, not accuracy, is the axis on which ontology-grounded retrieval earns its cost in enterprise finance applications. The negative result showing equivalent accuracy across retrieval methods but superior auditability for KDAF suggests that structured, ontology-driven approaches are justified by their traceability and provenance capabilities rather than raw performance gains.

Abstract: Enterprise adoption of large language models in finance is constrained less by fluency than by trust: in Financial Planning and Analysis (FP&A) and other regulated workflows, an answer is usable only if it is traceable to authoritative sources and auditable after the fact. This paper argues that retrieval-augmented generation for enterprise finance should be evaluated on auditability alongside accuracy, and presents the Knowledge-Driven Analytics Framework (KDAF), which builds ontology-driven knowledge systems through six iterative stages and retrieves evidence via Context-Aware Relevance Propagation (CARP), so that every retrieved fact carries its relationship type, confidence, and source lineage.
  An evaluation on FinanceBench (145 questions) compares KDAF against zero-context inference, BM25, concept-weighted lexical retrieval, and ungrounded graph traversal. First, retrieval is necessary: zero-context inference reaches 4.1% correctness against 10-12% for retrieval-augmented conditions. Second, on answer correctness the retrieval conditions are statistically indistinguishable (KDAF vs BM25: -0.007, 95% CI [-0.021, 0.000]), so accuracy alone does not justify structured retrieval here -- a negative result we report explicitly. Third, on auditability the ordering reverses: KDAF attains the highest citation traceability F1 (0.515), exceeding ungrounded traversal by +0.027 (CI [0.006, 0.050]) and BM25 by +0.052 (CI [0.024, 0.083]), intervals excluding zero. Graph-structured retrieval also admits no evidence from outside the question subject entity (0 of 426 items, against 16.8% and 20.2% for lexical baselines), and every selected item resolves to a complete provenance chain. We argue that auditability, not accuracy, is the axis on which ontology-grounded retrieval earns its cost.

</details>


### [115] [Why2Speak: Faithful Reasoning for Abstaining Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20670)
*Shreya Mendi,Brinnae Bent*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies the tradeoff between capability and auditability in agentic systems that must decide whether to act or abstain, using multi-party conversation intervention as the testbed. With Qwen3-8B, they compare direct decision policies, chain-of-thought reasoning policies, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning. The key finding is a capability-auditability tradeoff: direct policies achieve higher quality but provide no reasoning trace, while reasoning policies offer inspectability at the cost of lower performance. Standard faithfulness methods (probability-based metrics, activation probes, reasoning ablations) can overstate evidence due to class imbalance, textual leakage, and conflation of reasoning content with inference mode changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many agentic systems must repeatedly choose between acting and abstaining, making faithful reasoning important for oversight. However, an explanation is useful only if it reflects the actual computation that produced the action. The specific setting of intervention timing in multi-party conversation exposes class imbalance, asymmetric action costs, and the possibility that exposing reasoning changes the policy being audited.

Method: Using Qwen3-8B model, decoded with and without chain-of-thought reasoning, the researchers compared four approaches: (1) direct decision policies, (2) reasoning policies (with chain-of-thought), (3) supervised fine-tuning, and (4) reinforcement learning. They employed controlled activation probes, behavioral ablations, and various faithfulness metrics to evaluate the reasoning.

Result: The study found a capability-auditability tradeoff: the strongest direct policy achieves higher quality but exposes no reasoning to inspect, while the reasoning policy provides a trace at the cost of lower performance, particularly recall of true intervention opportunities. Supervised fine-tuning either suppresses reasoning or preserves it without improving decision quality. Reinforcement learning also fails to improve the reasoning policy. One mechanism underlying this failure was identified: group relative objectives provide no learning signal on confidently wrong prompts when sampled rollouts all select the same action. Standard faithfulness methods were found to overstate evidence due to: probability-based metrics saturating under confident decisions, probes being vulnerable to class imbalance and textual leakage, and reasoning ablations confounding reasoning content with changes in inference mode.

Conclusion: Exposing reasoning can change an agent's action policy rather than simply making it observable. The paper provides controls for evaluating reasoning-based oversight of agents that can act or abstain, highlighting the need for more careful evaluation methods when using reasoning traces for auditability.

Abstract: Many agentic systems must repeatedly choose between acting and abstaining, making faithful reasoning important for oversight: an explanation is useful only if it reflects the computation that produced the action. We study this problem through intervention timing in multi-party conversation, where an assistant must decide whether to speak or remain silent. This setting exposes class imbalance, asymmetric action costs, and the possibility that exposing reasoning changes the policy being audited. Using Qwen3-8B, decoded with or without chain-of-thought reasoning, we compare direct decision policies, reasoning policies, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning. We find a capability-auditability tradeoff: the strongest direct policy achieves higher quality but exposes no reasoning to inspect, while the reasoning policy provides a trace at the cost of lower performance, particularly recall of true intervention opportunities. Supervised fine-tuning either suppresses reasoning or preserves it without improving decision quality, while reinforcement learning also fails to improve the reasoning policy. We identify one mechanism underlying this failure: group relative objectives provide no learning signal on confidently wrong prompts when sampled rollouts all select the same action. Controlled activation probes and behavioral ablations show that standard faithfulness methods can overstate evidence that exposed reasoning reflects the underlying decision process. Probability-based metrics saturate under confident decisions, probes are vulnerable to class imbalance and textual leakage, and reasoning ablations can confound reasoning content with changes in inference mode. Together, these results show that exposing reasoning can change an agent's action policy rather than simply make it observable. We provide controls for evaluating reasoning-based oversight of agents that can act or abstain.

</details>


### [116] [CDRL: Certification-Driven Reinforcement Learning for Neutrino Flavor Model Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20686)
*Piyush Jha,Jake Rudolph,Victoria Knapp-Pérez,Max Fieg,Aishik Ghosh,Vijay Ganesh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CDRL is a reinforcement learning framework that uses symbolic reasoning tools to provide structured feedback when candidate solutions fail. It converts failure certificates into reusable constraints, achieving up to 1.95× higher valid model rates and 6.33× higher neutrino model rates while evaluating 4× fewer candidates on neutrino flavor model discovery tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL methods for scientific discovery rely on scalar rewards that provide limited information about why candidates fail, causing agents to repeatedly explore invalid regions of the hypothesis space.

Method: CDRL leverages structured feedback from symbolic reasoning tools. When a candidate violates domain constraints, these tools produce certificates identifying the actions responsible for failure. CDRL converts these certificates into reusable constraints that eliminate classes of invalid solutions and guide exploration toward valid regions.

Result: Across three theory spaces in neutrino flavor model discovery: (1) up to 1.95× higher valid model rates, (2) up to 6.33× higher neutrino model rates, (3) evaluating up to 4× fewer candidates. Post-hoc decision-tree extraction of 40 interpretable rules, when reused as soft constraints, yields gains of up to 2× in valid model rates and 3× in neutrino model discovery.

Conclusion: CDRL uncovers reusable structure in combinatorial search spaces and provides a general framework for scientific model discovery, effectively combining reinforcement learning with symbolic reasoning to solve large-scale hypothesis space exploration problems.

Abstract: Many scientific discovery problems require searching combinatorial hypothesis spaces under complex domain constraints. Reinforcement learning (RL) offers a promising approach, but existing methods rely on scalar rewards that provide limited information about why candidate solutions fail, leading agents to repeatedly explore invalid regions. We introduce Certification-Driven Reinforcement Learning (CDRL), a framework that leverages structured feedback from symbolic reasoning tools. When a candidate violates domain constraints, these tools produce certificates identifying the actions responsible for failure. CDRL converts these certificates into reusable constraints that eliminate classes of invalid solutions and guide exploration toward valid regions. We evaluate CDRL on neutrino flavor model discovery in theoretical particle physics, where the hypothesis space exceeds $10^{26}$ possible models, and compare it with the state-of-the-art RL approach previously used for this task. Across three theory spaces, CDRL achieves up to 1.95$\times$ higher valid model rates and up to 6.33$\times$ higher neutrino model rates while evaluating up to 4$\times$ fewer candidates. We further extract 40 interpretable rules from search trajectories using a post-hoc decision-tree framework and show that reusing them as soft constraints yields gains of up to 2$\times$ in valid model rates and 3$\times$ in neutrino model discovery across all three theory spaces. These results suggest that CDRL uncovers reusable structure in combinatorial search spaces and provides a general framework for scientific model discovery.

</details>


### [117] [VortexChat: An agentic framework for autonomous multi-objective integrated photonic design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20688)
*Faqian Chong,Yulun Wu,Shilong Li,Andrew Forbes,Hongsheng Chen,Song Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VortexChat is an agentic framework using a large language model (LLM) decision agent coupled with topology generation, gradient-based refinement, and full-wave electromagnetic simulation for autonomous, end-to-end inverse design of integrated photonic devices from natural language specifications, eliminating the need for expert supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern integrated photonics design is bottlenecked by workflows that heavily rely on manual simulation and expert intuition. While inverse design exists, it remains constrained by the need for expert supervision and lacks true end-to-end automation.

Method: VortexChat employs a closed-loop architecture that couples an LLM decision agent with topology generation, gradient-based refinement, and full-wave electromagnetic simulation. The system iteratively decomposes design objectives, orchestrates computational tools, and updates strategies based on feedback with minimal human intervention.

Result: VortexChat autonomously generates devices that strictly meet all predefined performance thresholds of the Vortex100 Benchmark without any human-in-the-loop. An experimental demonstration involved fabricating a broadband terahertz perfect vortex beam multiplexer, with measurements confirming high-efficiency operation, high mode purity, and low inter-channel crosstalk in agreement with full-wave simulations.

Conclusion: An LLM agent can assume key aspects of expert decision-making in photonic inverse design while maintaining physical fidelity and fabrication feasibility. This provides a scalable route towards autonomous design of complex integrated photonic systems.

Abstract: The advancement of modern integrated photonics is frequently bottlenecked by device design workflows that rely heavily on manual simulation and expert intuition. While inverse design offers an alternative, it remains constrained by expert supervision and a lack of end-to-end automation. To address these issues, we present VortexChat, an agentic framework for the autonomous, end-to-end inverse design of integrated photonic devices directly from natural language specifications. VortexChat couples a large language model (LLM) decision agent with topology generation, gradient-based refinement, and full-wave electromagnetic simulation. This closed-loop architecture enables the system to iteratively decompose design objectives, orchestrate computational tools, and update strategies based on feedback with minimal human intervention. Constrained by the absolute metrics of the Vortex100 Benchmark, VortexChat autonomously generates devices that strictly meet all predefined performance thresholds without any human-in-the-loop. As an experimental demonstration, we fabricated a broadband terahertz perfect vortex beam multiplexer, autonomously designed by VortexChat, with measurements confirming high-efficiency operation, high mode purity and low inter-channel crosstalk in agreement with full-wave simulations. These results demonstrate that an LLM agent can assume key aspects of expert decision-making in photonic inverse design while maintaining physical fidelity and fabrication feasibility, providing a scalable route towards autonomous design of complex integrated photonic systems.

</details>


### [118] [DirEAG: Dirichlet Evidence Aggregation for Calibrating Verbalized Confidence in Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20717)
*Haorui Xu,Yuzhou Zhu,Liyuan Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DirEAG is a Dirichlet-based method for aggregating confidence estimates from multiple confidence-steering prompts in LLMs. It converts answer-confidence observations into calibrated soft evidence over candidate answers plus a null state, addressing scale shifts across prompts, models, and datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Black-box verbalized confidence in LLMs is difficult to calibrate. Existing methods either describe output variation without modeling numerical confidence meaning, or fail to learn prompt- and task-dependent biases from direct confidence averaging.
