Method: DirEAG converts each elicited answer-confidence observation into calibrated soft evidence over generated candidate answers and an additional null state (representing cases where no candidate is correct). This evidence is then aggregated using Dirichlet-based modeling to produce calibrated confidence estimates.

Result: Experiments on GSM8K, SVAMP, and GSM-Hard with Qwen, Mistral, and Gemma models show DirEAG achieves better calibration than direct confidence averaging and heuristic confidence-steering aggregation while maintaining competitive answer selection. Ablations confirm evidence aggregation and final binary calibration address distinct calibration problems.

Conclusion: DirEAG provides an effective approach for calibrating LLM confidence in mathematical reasoning by modeling confidence numerically and learning prompt-dependent biases, with potential applications in any domain requiring reliable uncertainty estimation from LLMs.

Abstract: Reliable confidence estimation is essential for using large language models in mathematical reasoning, but black-box verbalized confidence is difficult to calibrate. When the same problem is queried under multiple confidence-steering prompts, the resulting answer-confidence observations contain useful uncertainty information, yet their scales may shift across steering levels, models, and datasets. Existing black-box uncertainty methods often rely on answer agreement, sample consistency, or entropy, which describe output variation but do not model the numerical meaning of self-reported confidence. Conversely, direct averaging or heuristic aggregation of elicited confidence cannot learn prompt- and task-dependent bias. We propose DirEAG, a Dirichlet Evidence Aggregation method that converts each elicited answer-confidence observation into calibrated soft evidence over generated candidate answers and an additional null state, allowing the model to represent cases where none of the candidates is correct. Experiments on GSM8K, SVAMP, and GSM-Hard with Qwen, Mistral, and Gemma models show that, compared with direct confidence averaging and heuristic confidence-steering aggregation, DirEAG often achieves better calibration while maintaining competitive answer selection. Ablations further reveal that evidence aggregation and final binary calibration address distinct parts of the calibration problem.

</details>


### [119] [Calibrating Criterion Revision in LLM Agents: Failure Modes and a Trace-Anchored Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20729)
*Guodong Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether language model agents can genuinely revise their success criteria (criterion revision) when they detect violations of broader commitments. The authors evaluate CMB-0.1 across 12 cross-domain cases using five non-compensatory conditions: criterion-failure detection, model-emitted proposal, new-episode transfer, intervention sensitivity, and preservation. Results show no model trial satisfies all five conditions, leading the authors to propose an improved CMB-0.4 protocol for future testing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses a critical question in AI safety and alignment: can language model agents truly revise their success criteria after failure, or do they merely carry text across episodes without genuinely revising what counts as success? This distinction is fundamental to understanding whether models possess meaningful goal revision capabilities or simply manipulate outputs surface-level.

Method: The authors evaluate CMB-0.1 using a rigorous methodology with four experimental arms (stateless inference, append-only history, model-generated harness-committed state, and evaluator-written oracle state), 7 mechanism fixtures yielding 84 deterministic scorer trials, and 4 local quantized artifacts yielding 96 calls and 192 model-case-arm trials. They test twelve cross-domain cases and require all five non-compensatory conditions to be satisfied for criterion revision to be confirmed.

Result: No model trial satisfies all five conditions, resulting in a "zero" that does not establish general capability absence. Key findings include: 11 calls remaining invalid after retry, several commitments disclosing target distinctions, harness performing commits, deletion reusing stateless calls, conflict changing multiple factors, and Qwen2.5-7B answering transfer and preservation items without revision state, exposing zero-state reconstruction.

Conclusion: CMB-0.1 serves as an instrument-calibration result rather than a model ranking. The failures reveal the difficulty of detecting genuine criterion revision. The paper contributes a measurement chain, empirical diagnosis of the implementation, and proposes CMB-0.4—a more discriminating protocol requiring concealed transfer, explicit WRITE/NO-WRITE/ESCALATE actions, separately logged policy-selected commits, matched interventions, repeated hidden items, and a frozen executable oracle.

Abstract: Language-model agents can improve after failure or carry text across episodes without revising what counts as success. We study the narrower attribution problem of criterion revision: when criterion K0 accepts an outcome violating a broader commitment B, what observations justify saying that the system formed and persistently used K1? We require five non-compensatory conditions: criterion-failure detection, a model-emitted proposal, new-episode transfer, intervention sensitivity on the claimed carrier, and preservation.
  We evaluate CMB-0.1 on twelve cross-domain cases and four arms: stateless inference, append-only history, model-generated but harness-committed state, and evaluator-written oracle state. Seven mechanism fixtures yield 84 deterministic scorer trials; four local quantized artifacts yield 96 calls and 192 model-case-arm trials. No model trial satisfies all five conditions, but this zero does not establish general capability absence. Eleven calls remain invalid after one retry; several commitments disclose the target distinction; the harness performs commits; deletion reuses a stateless call; and conflict changes multiple factors. Qwen2.5-7B answers every transfer and preservation item without revision state, exposing zero-state reconstruction.
  These failures make CMB-0.1 an instrument-calibration result rather than a model ranking. We derive a prospective, trace-anchored CMB-0.4 protocol requiring concealed transfer, explicit WRITE/NO-WRITE/ESCALATE actions, a separately logged policy-selected commit, matched interventions, repeated hidden items, and a frozen executable oracle. It is a successor design, not a completed confirmatory result. The paper contributes a measurement chain, an empirical diagnosis of its first implementation, and a more discriminating protocol for future tests of criterion revision.

</details>


### [120] [ForeTime-VLA: Causal Future-Token Distillation from a World Action Model for Conveyor-Belt Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20735)
*Siyuan Ma,Yutian Zhang,Boshi Zhang,Qinglian Wu,Jiaqi Zhai,Dong Wei,Xiaojin Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ForeTime-VLA is a method that improves vision-language-action (VLA) policies for manipulating moving objects by distilling future-aware representations from a world action model teacher while maintaining causal inference. It achieves 2.63% MAE improvement and significant real-robot success rate gains (81.1% stationary, 58.9% slow-moving grasps) compared to baseline π0.5 policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA policies typically only consider current observations when manipulating moving objects, but anticipating contact events requires future awareness. Running expensive world models at deployment is impractical, so the authors propose distilling future-aware representations into a causal policy.

Method: ForeTime-VLA uses a dense π0.5 policy architecture that: (1) compresses current and future video latents from a frozen Fast-WAM teacher into a 64-D whitened target representation; (2) uses an 8-frame history encoder to predict this target along with manipulation phase and normalized time-to-transition; (3) conditions the VLM with 4 future tokens and 1 phase token; (4) adds multiple training objectives including cosine similarity, relational geometry, phase prediction, time-to-transition, and action-equivalence loss alongside the original flow-matching target.

Result: On a conveyor-belt dataset: Test MAE decreased from 0.134119 to 0.130593 (2.63% improvement, 95% CI: 0.82-4.48%); Test L2 decreased by 3.02%; latency increased by 2.46-2.93%. Real-robot evaluation: 81.1% stationary grasp success (12.2pp better than next-best) and 58.9% slow-moving grasp success (22.2pp better). Across three belt speeds: 44/90 vs 23/90 total grasps; at fast speed: 11/30 vs 2/30.

Conclusion: Causal future-token distillation is an effective approach to improve dynamic manipulation without deploying the world-model teacher at runtime. The method successfully transfers future-awareness from a predictive teacher into a causal VLA policy, reducing contact-pose failures and significantly improving success rates on moving objects, particularly at higher speeds.

Abstract: Manipulating moving objects requires a policy to anticipate contact events, yet vision-language-action (VLA) policies are commonly fine-tuned from the current observation alone. World action models (WAMs) learn predictive dynamics, but running a video-scale teacher or explicitly imagining future frames at deployment is costly. We introduce ForeTime-VLA, a dense pi0.5 policy that distills a future-aware, action-equivalent representation from a frozen Fast-WAM-derived teacher while remaining causal at inference. Offline, current and future video latents are compressed into a whitened 64-D target. Online, an eight-frame history encoder predicts this target together with manipulation phase and normalized time-to-transition. Four future tokens and one phase token condition the VLM prefix, while the predicted future and transition horizon condition the action expert. Training retains the original flow-matching action target and adds cosine, relational geometry, phase, time-to-transition, and action-equivalence objectives. On a deduplicated conveyor-belt dataset, we compare 40k-step checkpoints on 768 matched windows per split. Test MAE decreases from 0.134119 to 0.130593 (2.63%; paired-bootstrap 95% CI: 0.82-4.48% improvement), and test L2 decreases by 3.02%, at a 2.46-2.93% latency cost. In quantitative real-robot evaluation, ForeTime-VLA achieves 81.1% stationary and 58.9% slow-moving grasp success, exceeding the next-best reference by 12.2 and 22.2 percentage points, respectively. Across three belt speeds, it completes 44/90 grasps versus 23/90 for pi0.5, including 11/30 versus 2/30 at fast speed. The agreement between offline orientation gains and reduced real-robot contact-pose failures supports causal future-token distillation as an effective way to improve dynamic manipulation without deploying the world-model teacher.

</details>


### [121] [Continuous-Time Quantum Walks based Graph Neural Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20738)
*Yuliang Zhan,Zefeng Gao,Jian Li,Yang Liu,Hao sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are widely used on graph-structured data, but most suffer from two key weaknesses. First, message passing behaves as a low-pass filter under the homophily assumption, leading to poor performance on heterophilic graphs. Second, stacking layers drives node features toward constants, causing over-smoothing. Existing methods usually address these issues separately, while the few joint solutions rely largely on empirical heuristics, and many over-smoothing remedies sacrifice model expressiveness.
  We propose \textbf{CTQW-GNN}, a GNN based on Continuous-Time Quantum Walks (CTQW), to address both issues with theoretical justification. Its design exploits two properties of the CTQW propagator $e^{-\mathrm{i}Ht}$. First, it is unitary and has eigenvalues on the unit circle, so no frequency component is damped, counteracting the low-pass bias. Second, unitarity preserves feature norms and prevents the Dirichlet energy from decaying exponentially with depth, thereby mitigating over-smoothing.
  CTQW-GNN combines three complementary aggregation modules. \textit{CTQW-based Aggregation} evolves node features through the unitary propagator, preserving mid- and high-frequency signals for heterophilic graphs while preventing Dirichlet-energy collapse. \textit{CTQW-Attention Aggregation} constructs a multi-hop neighbor graph from CTQW amplitudes and applies attention over it, enabling access to distant homophilic nodes missed by single-hop aggregation. \textit{LF Aggregation} uses a standard low-pass GAT branch to retain strong performance on homophilic graphs, where pure CTQW aggregation can be suboptimal. We further provide a spectral-gap analysis explaining energy preservation and a Lieb--Robinson-type bound that gives a principled rule for selecting the walk time $t$.

</details>


### [122] [Is Multimodal Speculative Decoding Ready for Diffusion-Based Parallel Drafting? A Survey and Empirical Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20743)
*Yantao Li,Huanlin Gao,Fang Zhao,Chao Tan,Qiang Hui,Shuting Liu,Fuyuan Shi,Ting Lu,Shaoan Zhao,Xueqiang Guo,Xinpei Su,Jianbing Zhang,Xinyu Dai,Kai Wang,Shiguo Lian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether multimodal speculative decoding is ready for diffusion-based parallel drafting. It bridges the gap between text-only block-parallel generative drafting (like DFlash and DSpark) and multimodal models by providing a modality-centered survey and cross-architecture empirical study across Vision-Language, Video-Language, Audio, and VLA architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While speculative decoding for text-only LLMs has evolved to include block-parallel generative drafting with diffusion-based methods achieving up to 3.6x speedup, this paradigm remains largely unexplored for multimodal models. Existing multimodal speculative decoding efforts focus on input compression, adapter alignment, candidate coverage, or modality-specific verification, but not block-parallel drafting.

Method: The paper combines (1) a systematic survey analyzing multimodal models from dual perspectives of drafting parallelism and cross-modal information interaction, (2) a unified taxonomy isolating drafter-side parallelism from orthogonal design choices (tree construction, verification strategies), and (3) comprehensive empirical comparison across standardized multimodal benchmarks (OCR, VQA, visual reasoning, image captioning).

Result: The paper summarizes limitations of current approaches, discusses open challenges, and outlines promising future directions for multimodal speculative decoding with diffusion-based parallel drafting.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that while speculative decoding has been well-studied for text-only LLMs with block-parallel drafting, its applicability to multimodal models remains an open question requiring further investigation into drafting parallelism and cross-modal information interaction for Vision-Language, Video-Language, Audio, and VLA architectures.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by allowing a lightweight drafter to propose future tokens while a target model verifies them in parallel. Its lossless guarantee has motivated a line of work that pushes the drafter itself toward parallel generation. The most recent paradigm is block-parallel generative drafting, including diffusion-based methods such as DFlash and DSpark, achieving up to 3.6x speedup on common daily chatting tasks. While this transition is well studied in text-only LLMs, its applicability to multimodal models remains an open question. Existing multimodal speculative decoding efforts focus on input compression, adapter alignment, candidate coverage, or modality-specific verification; however, block-parallel generative drafting remains largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, this paper combines a modality-centered survey with a cross-architecture empirical study to ask: Is multimodal speculative decoding ready for diffusion-based parallel drafting? In this survey, we systematically analyze a wide spectrum of multimodal models, spanning Vision-Language, Video-Language, Audio, and Vision-Language-Action (VLA) architectures, from the dual perspectives of drafting parallelism and cross-modal information interaction. We introduce a unified taxonomy that isolates drafter-side parallelism from orthogonal design choices such as tree construction and verification strategies. Furthermore, we provide a comprehensive empirical comparison of existing methods under varying degrees of parallelism across standardized multimodal benchmarks, including OCR, VQA, visual reasoning, and image captioning. Finally, we summarize the limitations of current approaches, discuss open challenges, and outline promising future directions for this rapidly evolving field.

</details>


### [123] [Natural-Language-Guided Generator-Agnostic Shortlisting for Protein Binder Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20755)
*Gyubok Lee,Kiwoong Yoo,Jimin Seo,Kyunghoon Hur,Edward Choi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The study explores whether Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate ranking policies to prioritize protein binder candidates from large pools, addressing the bottleneck in wet-lab validation capacity. Instead of designing new protein binder pipelines, the focus is on post-generation shortlisting using precomputed structural-confidence and interface-quality proxy scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern de novo design workflows generate numerous candidate protein binders, but limited wet-lab validation capacity makes shortlisting a critical bottleneck. There is a need for efficient methods to prioritize the most promising candidates from large pools for experimental validation.

Method: The approach uses LLMs to generate multi-metric ranking policies from precomputed structural-confidence and interface-quality proxy scores. The system evaluates post-generation binder shortlisting by selecting top-K candidates from already generated binder pools using a shared panel of precomputed proxy metrics. Two types of policies were tested: global iterative policies (gpt-4o) and target-conditioned iterative policies (gpt-5.4).

Result: On the 10-target held-out split, averaging performance over five sampled global iterative gpt-4o policies achieves 0.589 Recall@10, modestly improving over the strongest single-feature fixed baseline (Protenix binder ipTM at 0.571 Recall@10). On the 3-target held-out subset comprising Nipah, RBX1, and TREM2, target-conditioned iterative gpt-5.4 policies reach the strongest LLM performance with 0.519 Recall@10 and 0.583 NDCG@10.

Conclusion: LLM-generated ranking policies can act as an interpretable post-generation decision layer for combining heterogeneous proxy metrics to prioritize binders from large candidate pools. The approach demonstrates that LLMs can effectively integrate multiple quality metrics to improve candidate selection beyond single-feature baselines.

Abstract: Modern de novo design workflows generate many candidate protein binders, but wet-lab validation capacity remains limited, making shortlisting a major bottleneck. We study whether LLMs can generate multi-metric ranking policies from precomputed structural-confidence and interface-quality proxy scores. Rather than proposing a new protein binder design pipeline, we focus on post-generation binder shortlisting: selecting the final top-K candidates from already generated binder pools using a shared panel of precomputed proxy scores. On the 10-target held-out split, averaging performance over five sampled global iterative gpt-4o policies reaches 0.589 Recall@10, modestly improving over the strongest single-feature fixed baseline, Protenix binder ipTM, which reaches 0.571 Recall@10. On the 3-target held-out subset comprising Nipah, RBX1, and TREM2, target-conditioned iterative gpt-5.4 policies reach the strongest LLM performance, with 0.519 Recall@10 and 0.583 NDCG@10. These results suggest that LLM-generated ranking policies can act as an interpretable post-generation decision layer for combining heterogeneous proxy metrics to prioritize binders from large candidate pools.

</details>


### [124] [Beyond Endpoint Gains: A Weight-Delta Audit of Medical Specialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20768)
*Praphul Singh,Shanu Kumar,Akshat Agarwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a "paired weight-delta path audit" method to analyze how generalist language models transform into medical specialist models by examining the weight changes (deltas) in the model weights, rather than just comparing endpoint benchmark scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches to understanding specialist language models only examine endpoint benchmark gains (generalist vs specialist scores), leaving the actual update mechanism largely unexamined. The authors argue this leaves a gap in understanding how specialization actually works at the model weight level.

Method: The authors apply their audit method to two public aligned generalist-to-medical-specialist checkpoint pairs: (1) Gemma-3-4B-IT to MedGemma-4B-IT, and (2) Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct to HuatuoGPT-o1-7B. They analyze the full decoder-side weight deltas and measure how well these reconstruct benchmark movement. They use 10-seed matched controls and endpoint-anchored rollbacks as methodological controls.

Result: The decoder-side updates strongly reconstruct measured medical benchmark movement (0.974 and 1.183 endpoint-normalized retention for the two pairs). MLP (Multi-Layer Perceptron) components are the strongest broad component family in both pairs. However, the movement is not cleanly localized - there are mixed off-domain movements that prevent a unique coarse-family explanation.

Conclusion: The weight deltas are appropriate substrates for auditing specialist models. The audit successfully separates update-level reconstruction (which works well) from component-level explanation (which is more complex due to mixed off-domain movements). The method focuses on text-only multiple-choice benchmarks and does not address clinical validation, repair, or circuit-level mechanisms.

Abstract: Specialist language models are usually understood through endpoint gains: the generalist scores lower, the specialist scores higher, and the difference is treated as evidence of specialization. This leaves the released update itself largely unexamined. We propose a paired weight-delta path audit and apply it to two public, aligned generalist-to-medical-specialist checkpoint pairs: Gemma-3-4B-IT to MedGemma-4B-IT and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct to HuatuoGPT-o1-7B. In both pairs, the full decoder-side update strongly reconstructs measured medical benchmark movement (0.974 and 1.183 endpoint-normalized retention), making each decoder delta an appropriate substrate for the audit. Yet the movement is not cleanly localized. MLP is the strongest broad component family in both pairs, but mixed off-domain movements, 10-seed matched controls, and endpoint-anchored rollbacks prevent a unique coarse-family explanation. The audit therefore separates update-level reconstruction from component-level explanation. Its claims concern text-only multiple-choice benchmark movement, not clinical validation, repair, or circuit-level mechanism.

</details>


### [125] [CAS: Conformalized Agentic Search via Adaptive Retrieval and Policy Weighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20771)
*Zixi Zhu,Jiayuan Su,Jian Zhang,Yu Lin,Hongwei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the reliability crisis in search agents during RL fine-tuning by introducing Conformalized Agentic Search (CAS), which uses Conformal Prediction to provide reliability guarantees on both retrieval (via Adaptive Prediction Set for dynamic document truncation) and training sides (via Adaptive Conformal Inference for confidence quantification to penalize low-quality trajectories in GRPO).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Search agents face severe reliability issues during reinforcement learning fine-tuning due to: 1) Heuristic Top-K retrieval causing critical evidence loss or noise inclusion, 2) Over-confidence induced by progressive RL leading to hallucinated answers and redundant searches.

Method: The proposed CAS framework employs two key mechanisms: (1) Adaptive Prediction Set (APS) - a CP realization that translates statistical coverage into dynamic document truncation to construct adaptive-sized prediction sets for retrieval; (2) Adaptive Conformal Inference (ACI) - a dynamic CP algorithm that constructs prediction sets with controllable coverage to quantify answer confidence, which is used to penalize low-confidence trajectories within the GRPO objective.

Result: Experiments across single-hop and multi-hop QA datasets demonstrate significant improvements in reasoning accuracy while drastically reducing redundant tool invocations, establishing a highly reliable and efficient agent paradigm.

Conclusion: Conformal Prediction provides a principled framework for building reliable search agents with theoretical coverage guarantees. By applying CP both at retrieval (APS) and training (ACI) stages, the framework successfully addresses hallucination and inefficiency issues, offering a promising paradigm for reliable agentic search systems.

Abstract: Search Agents face a severe reliability crisis during reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning. Heuristic Top-K retrieval often causes critical evidence loss or noise inclusion, while over-confidence induced by progressive RL leads to hallucinated answers and redundant searches.
  To build highly reliable agents, we introduce Conformal Prediction (CP) and propose Conformalized Agentic Search (CAS). This framework establishes reliability guarantees on both the retrieval and training sides: on the retrieval side, an Adaptive Prediction Set (APS), a specific CP realization, translates statistical coverage into dynamic document truncation to construct prediction sets that are adaptive in size; on the training side, Adaptive Conformal Inference (ACI), a dynamic CP algorithm, dynamically constructs prediction sets with controllable coverage to quantify answer confidence, which is then used to penalize low-confidence trajectories within the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) objective, ensuring the model learns only from reliable ones.
  Experiments across single-hop and multi-hop QA datasets demonstrate that our framework significantly improves reasoning accuracy while drastically reducing redundant tool invocations, establishing a highly reliable and efficient agent paradigm. Our code is available at https://github.com/S1llyBird/CAS.

</details>


### [126] [Structure for Reading, Prose for Writing: Asymmetric Structural Conditioning in Multi-Agent Document Authoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20786)
*Cheng Yu,Nikhil Mathew,Zhengjie Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a deployed multi-agent pipeline for authoring formal tender responses using an open-weights LLM under sovereignty constraints. The system was evaluated against human-written bids and found to perform comparably (40/55 sections rated at least as good). A key finding is that most quality gaps (68%) stem from information the system wasn't given rather than writing deficiencies. The paper also reveals a conditioning asymmetry: structural markup improves reading (reproduced on 3 tasks) but hurts writing performance (74% to 48% when converting instructions to XML). Additional findings show that naming forbidden constructions concentrates defects (96% fall in explicitly-named forms) and stochastic annotation significantly affects requirement extraction counts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors investigate multi-agent systems for authoring formal documents (tender responses) and question whether standard evaluation methodologies properly attribute failures. They identify that evaluations often conflate writing quality with information availability, and explore a previously underexplored asymmetry between reading and conditioning tasks when using structural markup versus prose.

Method: The authors deployed a multi-agent pipeline using an open-weights LLM under sovereignty constraints. They conducted blind comparisons where the system had no worked examples, using an LLM judge to rate system answers against human-submitted bids from the same organization. They classified every gap the judge identified to separate content availability issues from writing quality issues. For the reading/conditioning asymmetry, they conducted paired comparisons measuring answer quality when bid instructions were presented as prose versus nested XML, and examined defect distribution when forbidden constructions were explicitly named.

Result: The LLM judge rated system answers at least as good as human answers on 40 of 55 sections, better on 4, with no missing answers and only 1 unsupported claim. Of 15 adverse verdicts, 68% involved content absent from system's sources (knowledge human held but system wasn't given), meaning only 6 involved avoidable deficiencies. On reading tasks, structural markup improved extraction (reproduced on 3 tasks). On conditioning, converting instructions from prose to nested XML dropped quality from 74% to 48%. Naming forbidden constructions concentrated defects: 96% of surviving defects fell in the two explicitly-named forms. Coupling stochastic annotation to deterministic windowing changed extracted requirement count from 68 to 51 on byte-identical files.

Conclusion: Divergence from ground truth in formal document authoring is more often an information-availability result than a writing-quality one, and evaluations that do not separate these understate system capabilities. There exists a conditioning asymmetry: structure benefits reading but hurts writing/conditioning. The recommendation is that structural markup belongs where the model reads, while prose and self-applied tests belong where it writes.

Abstract: Multi-agent pipelines that author formal documents must both read a requester's forms and write against them. We report a deployed tender-response system, running an open-weights model under sovereignty constraints, and evaluate it against human-written bids the same organisation actually submitted. On a blind comparison where the system had no worked example available, an LLM judge rated its answers at least as good as the human-submitted answer on $40$ of $55$ ground-truth sections, better on $4$, missing on none, and flagged one unsupported claim in total. Classifying every gap the judge identified shows that $68\%$ were content absent from the system's own sources -- knowledge the human author held and the pipeline was never given -- so only $6$ of the $15$ adverse verdicts involve a deficiency the system could have avoided. A divergence from ground truth is more often an information-availability result than a writing-quality one, and evaluations that do not separate the two understate such systems. Against this backdrop we report a conditioning asymmetry. It is well established that rendering documents as structural markup rather than flat prose improves extraction, and we reproduce that on three reading tasks. The benefit does not transfer to conditioning: converting a bid's \emph{instruction} material from prose to nested XML dropped answer quality from $74\%$ to $48\%$ under a paired comparison. We further find that naming a forbidden construction concentrates rather than removes it -- $96\%$ of surviving defects fall in the two forms the prompt explicitly names -- and that coupling a stochastic annotation to a deterministic windowing function moves the extracted requirement count from $68$ to $51$ on a byte-identical file. Structure belongs where the model reads; prose and self-applied tests belong where it writes.

</details>


### [127] [Knowing but Not Saying: Preventing Factual Access Failures in LLM SFT via Recall-Anchored Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20794)
*Haodong Chen,Yadong Wang,Shengtao Wen,Dong Liang,Xiang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) can degrade factual behavior outside the target domain. This degradation is often described as catastrophic forgetting, yet open-ended factual failures do not necessarily imply that the underlying facts have been erased. In this work, we identify a more specific phenomenon, factual access failure: after domain SFT, models can still recognize or rank the correct answer under constrained evaluation, while failing to produce it in closed-book generation. Through benchmark-level comparisons, same-fact multiple-choice and generation probes, and failure-mode analysis, we show that SFT-induced factual degradation reflects both genuine wrong-answer generations and expression-level failures such as verbosity, formatting mismatch, and exact-match artifacts. To address this problem, we introduce Recall-Anchored Distillation (RAD), a base-anchored self-distillation objective that preserves out-of-distribution generation behavior by aligning the adapted model with the original base model's soft continuation distribution on unlabeled OOD text. RAD requires no gold OOD answers, external judges, or labeled factual data. Across three backbones fine-tuned on MedMCQA, RAD recovers a consistent portion of the lost OOD recall while preserving target-domain adaptation. Compared with replay on the same OOD text, RAD shows that the key preservation signal is the base model's soft distribution rather than additional text exposure alone.

</details>


### [128] [Automated Trajectory Evaluation for Mobile Agents via Step-Level Consequence Reasoning and Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20797)
*Pengshuai Yang,Zijing Gao,Xue Yu,Benhui Zhuang,Bo Yuan,Junlan Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CRATE is a two-stage VLM-as-judge framework for automated evaluation of language-guided mobile agents. It uses step-level consequence reasoning to extract visual clues and infer state changes at each step, then aggregates evidence for trajectory-level evaluation. CRATE-S extends this for operational safety assessment. The method achieves F1=0.833 on AndroidWorld (20% improvement over SPA-Bench) and F1=0.697 on MobileRisk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation paradigms for language-guided mobile agents have two key limitations: (1) they process entire trajectories at once, causing context overload, and (2) they focus only on task completion while overlooking operational safety. There was a need for a scalable, automated evaluation approach that can assess both performance and safety at appropriate granularity.

Method: CRATE employs a two-stage framework: (1) Step-level consequence reasoning - independently extracts task-relevant visual clues and infers action-conditioned state changes at each timestep; (2) Trajectory-level aggregation - synthesizes step-level textual evidence to deliver evidence-grounded evaluation of task completion. CRATE-S extends this approach for operational safety assessment. The framework is compatible with both open- and closed-source VLM models.

Result: CRATE powered by Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct achieves an F1-score of 0.833 on AndroidWorld, outperforming SPA-Bench by 20%. CRATE-S achieves an F1-score of 0.697 on MobileRisk, demonstrating strong alignment with benchmark ground truths. Extensive experiments validate the effectiveness and robustness of both frameworks.

Conclusion: CRATE successfully addresses the limitations of existing holistic evaluation paradigms by introducing step-level reasoning with trajectory-level aggregation. The framework provides scalable, automated evaluation of mobile agents with evidence-grounded assessments. The extension to CRATE-S enables operational safety evaluation, filling a critical gap in mobile agent assessment. The approach shows strong performance across benchmarks, with Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct achieving state-of-the-art results.

Abstract: Evaluating language-guided mobile agents has recently shifted from rule-based to model-based approaches to achieve scalable and automated assessments. However, existing holistic evaluation paradigms process entire trajectories at once, leading to substantial context overload. Moreover, they primarily focus on task completion while overlooking operational safety. To address these limitations, we introduce CRATE, a novel two-stage VLM-as-judge framework for automated mobile agent evaluation that is compatible with both open- and closed-source models. Leveraging a step-level consequence reasoning mechanism, CRATE independently extracts task-relevant visual clues and infers action-conditioned state changes at each step. The resulting step-level textual evidence is then synthesized through trajectory-level aggregation to deliver an evidence-grounded evaluation of task completion. Building upon this evaluation scheme, we further extend CRATE to CRATE-S for operational safety assessment. Extensive experiments validate the effectiveness and robustness of both CRATE and CRATE-S. Powered by Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct, CRATE achieves an F1-score of 0.833 on AndroidWorld (outperforming SPA-Bench by 20%), while CRATE-S reaches an F1-score of 0.697 on MobileRisk, demonstrating strong alignment with benchmark ground truths. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/CRATE-D580.

</details>


### [129] [Dynamic Context Scheduling: Learning Beyond the Static Universe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20799)
*Martin Mráz,André Biedenkapp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes dynamic context scheduling as a training method for contextual reinforcement learning, where context evolves within each training episode using predetermined schedules (e.g., sinusoidal offsets, cosine annealing), rather than treating context variation only as a deployment phenomenon.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current contextual RL treats intra-episode context variation as merely a deployment reality. The authors propose treating it as a controlled shaping mechanism to expose policies to a richer, more temporally structured region of the environment parameter space, thereby improving generalization.

Method: Introduces DYNAMICCARLENV framework that wraps contextual environments with pluggable schedule families. Context evolves within each training episode according to schedules like sinusoidal offsets or cosine annealing. The approach is evaluated on CartPole, BipedalWalker, and VehicleRacing environments using CARL contextualization.

Result: Dynamic schedules match or outperform static context baselines in out-of-distribution (OOD) regimes. For more complex environments (BipedalWalker and VehicleRacing), higher in-distribution (ID) evaluation performance is also achieved. Automatic search for multi-stage curricula can discover schedules that improve generalization, performing comparably to extensive grid search over single-stage schedulers.

Conclusion: Dynamic context scheduling is an effective training instrument for contextual RL, improving both ID and OOD performance, particularly for complex environments. The approach successfully uses intra-episode context variation as a shaping mechanism rather than just a deployment consideration.

Abstract: We study dynamic context scheduling as a training instrument for contextual re- inforcement learning. Rather than treating intra-episode context variation as a deployment reality, we treat it as a controlled shaping mechanism. Thereby, context evolves within each training episode according to a predetermined schedule, expos- ing the policy to a richer and more temporally structured region of the environment parameter space. We introduce DYNAMICCARLENV, a framework that wraps contextual environments with pluggable schedule families, such as sinusoidal off- sets or cosine annealing. Across CartPole, BipedalWalker and VehicleRacing with CARL contextualization, we show that dynamic schedules match or outperform static context baselines in the out-of-distribution (OOD) regimes. Interestingly, for the more complex BipedalWalker and VehicleRacing environments we also achieve higher in-distribution (ID) evaluation performance. Preliminary findings indicate that automatic search for multi-stage curricula can successfully discover schedules that improve generalization, performing comparably to extensive grid search over single-stage schedulers.

</details>


### [130] [SPARC: Single-Pass Scaling for Motion Forecasting with Conformal Bayesian Last Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20802)
*Sakif Hossain,Julian Teusch,Jörg P. Müller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPARC (Single-Pass Adaptive Risk Calibration) introduces a Bayesian-conformal uncertainty layer for human motion forecasting that provides structured, calibrated epistemic uncertainty without Monte Carlo sampling. It uses a deterministic MLP backbone with a conjugate Bayesian last layer to produce analytic horizon-wise epistemic scales that inflate trajectory covariance while preserving correlation structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human motion forecasters lack reliable uncertainty estimates that are structured, calibrated, and efficient. Existing Bayesian and ensemble methods require repeated stochastic inference, while conformal calibration alone fails to provide epistemic signals or preserve trajectory covariance structure.

Method: SPARC combines deterministic prediction with a conjugate Bayesian last layer that converts time-domain feature leverage into analytic horizon-wise epistemic scale κ_t(x). This scale inflates a graph-temporal Gaussian covariance via structured factorization κ_t(x)Σ_str,t(x) without changing correlation structure. Split conformal calibration produces 95% marginal prediction tubes with finite-sample validity under exchangeability.

Result: Across nine dataset-protocol blocks, SPARC ranks first on NLL and combined MPJPE+NLL criterion while retaining competitive point accuracy and efficient calibrated tubes. The epistemic scale κ effectively separates high-error cases, functioning as a lightweight risk monitor.

Conclusion: SPARC achieves state-of-the-art uncertainty quantification for motion forecasting by combining Bayesian epistemic uncertainty with conformal calibration in a single-pass, computationally efficient manner without Monte Carlo sampling.

Abstract: Human motion forecasters are increasingly accurate and fast, but reliable deployment requires uncertainty estimates that are structured, calibrated, and efficient. Bayesian and ensemble-based uncertainty estimates often require repeated stochastic inference [15, 26], while conformal calibration alone does not provide an epistemic signal or preserve trajectory covariance structure [14, 50]. We introduce SPARC (Single-Pass Adaptive Risk Calibration), a Bayesian-conformal uncertainty layer for motion forecasting. A deterministic MLP backbone predicts the future mean, and a conjugate Bayesian last layer converts time-domain feature leverage into an analytic horizon-wise epistemic scale $κ_t(x)$. This scale inflates a graph-temporal Gaussian covariance without changing its correlation structure, and split conformal calibration produces 95% marginal prediction tubes with finite-sample validity under exchangeability. The key interface is the structured factorization $κ_t(x)Σ_{\mathrm{str},t}(x)$, which injects feature-space epistemic uncertainty into trajectory densities without Monte Carlo sampling. Across nine dataset-protocol blocks and deterministic, multimodal, and calibration baselines, SPARC ranks first on NLL and on the combined MPJPE+NLL criterion while retaining competitive point accuracy and efficient calibrated tubes. Ranking windows by $κ$ separates high-error cases, making the scale usable as a lightweight risk monitor.

</details>


### [131] [Neuro-Geospatial Modelling of EEG Affective States Using Literature-Informed Environmental Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20807)
*Utsav Poudel,Jagannath Aryal,Subramaniyaswamy Vairavasundaram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a dual-tower deep learning architecture that combines 30-channel EEG data with literature-informed environmental priors (air pollution, greenness) for affective-state classification. When individual-level exposure data are unavailable, the model uses environmental representations derived from OpenAQ, Sentinel-2, Sentinel-5P, and OpenStreetMap data. The multimodal model achieves 76.2% accuracy versus 67.4% for EEG-only baseline, demonstrating technical feasibility of integrating environmental priors as auxiliary geospatial modality for EEG-based classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Environmental exposures (air pollution, greenness) have been associated with affective and cognitive outcomes in literature, but EEG and environmental datasets are rarely jointly georeferenced. This study investigates whether literature-informed environmental priors can serve as an auxiliary geospatial modality for EEG-based affective-state classification when individual-level exposure data are unavailable.

Method: The study combines 30-channel EEG data from the EAV benchmark (42 participants, aged 20-30 years) with environmental representations derived from OpenAQ, Sentinel-2, Sentinel-5P, and OpenStreetMap data for Astana. A dual-tower architecture is used: EEG-Conformer representations combined with a graph-based environmental encoder. Since the datasets are not co-registered, environmental context is treated as a literature-informed prior rather than measured exposure. Validation includes: subject-level repeated splits, permutation and label-shuffling controls, dose-response reversal, and domain-shift experiments (replacing Astana distribution with Singapore distribution).

Result: The multimodal model achieves 76.2% accuracy versus 67.4% for EEG alone. Controls disrupting environmental-label structure retain part of this gain, indicating the improvement is not attributable solely to environmental information. Replacing the Astana environmental distribution with an independently modeled Singapore distribution reduces accuracy to 72.8%.

Conclusion: The findings demonstrate technical feasibility but do not establish an observed or causal exposure-affect association. The study provides a framework for future jointly collected mobile EEG-environment studies. The improvement appears to come from both architecture-level gains and prior-dependent gains, but controls show the gain is not solely attributable to environmental information.

Abstract: Environmental exposures such as air pollution and greenness have been associated with affective and cognitive outcomes, but EEG and environmental datasets are rarely jointly georeferenced. We investigate whether literature-informed environmental priors can serve as an auxiliary geospatial modality for EEG-based affective-state classification when individual-level exposure data are unavailable. We combine 30-channel EEG from the EAV benchmark (42 participants, aged 20-30 years) with environmental representations derived from OpenAQ, Sentinel-2, Sentinel-5P, and OpenStreetMap data for Astana. A dual-tower architecture combines EEG-Conformer representations with a graph-based environmental encoder. Because the datasets are not co-registered, environmental context is treated as a literature-informed prior rather than measured exposure. Subject-level repeated splits, permutation and label-shuffling controls, dose-response reversal, and domain-shift experiments distinguish architecture-level gains from prior-dependent gains. The multimodal model achieves 76.2% accuracy versus 67.4% for EEG alone. Controls disrupting environmental-label structure retain part of this gain, indicating that the improvement is not attributable solely to environmental information. Replacing the Astana environmental distribution with an independently modeled Singapore distribution reduces accuracy to 72.8%. These findings demonstrate technical feasibility but do not establish an observed or causal exposure-affect association. The study provides a framework for future jointly collected mobile EEG-environment studies. Implementation: https://github.com/r11up/geo-cog

</details>


### [132] [Certified Multi-Turn Robustness for LLM Safety via Compositional Bounds and Safety Persistence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20820)
*Yang Liu,Bin Chong,Wenkai Yang,Shuai Zhang,Yancheng Chen,Feiyu Han,GuoZhen,Cheng Zhang,Huaibing Xie,Changze Lv,Shihan Dou,Pluto Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the vulnerability of LLMs to multi-turn jailbreak attacks by introducing Multi-Turn Certified Robustness (MTCR), a framework that provides certified safety guarantees across multiple conversation turns using State-Adversarial MDPs. The approach offers tighter certified bounds than naive methods and achieves polynomial (β^k) rather than exponential (p̄^k) degradation in safety guarantees as conversation length increases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are vulnerable to multi-turn jailbreak attacks that progressively manipulate conversation context to bypass safety mechanisms. Existing certified robustness methods only handle single-turn inputs, and naive extension to multi-turn scenarios results in bounds that degrade exponentially with the number of turns, making them practically useless for extended conversations.

Method: The paper models conversational safety using State-Adversarial Markov Decision Processes (MDPs) and defines k-turn certified robustness as the worst-case safety probability across k adversarial turns. The framework includes: (i) compositional certification via embedding-space mode decomposition for tighter bounds; (ii) (α,β)-safety persistence that improves degradation from p̄^k to β^k; (iii) matching information-theoretic upper bounds; and (iv) a unified algorithm combining these techniques.

Result: Experiments on six LLMs under both ε-bounded and Crescendo-style attacks demonstrate that empirical safety consistently exceeds the certified bounds, validating the framework's effectiveness. The approach provides meaningful safety guarantees even for longer conversations.

Conclusion: MTCR successfully extends certified robustness from single-turn to multi-turn settings, achieving polynomial rather than exponential degradation in safety guarantees. This represents a significant advancement in LLM safety certification for practical conversational settings.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are vulnerable to multi-turn jailbreak attacks that progressively manipulate conversation context. Existing certified robustness methods are limited to single-turn inputs; naive multi-turn composition yields bounds that degrade exponentially in the number of turns. We introduce Multi-Turn Certified Robustness (MTCR), a framework that models conversational safety via State-Adversarial MDPs and defines $k$-turn certified robustness as the worst-case safety probability across $k$ adversarial turns. MTCR comprises: (i) compositional certification via embedding-space mode decomposition, yielding tighter certified lower bounds than naive multiplication; (ii) $(α,β)$-safety persistence, improving the degradation rate from $\underline{p}^{k}$ to $β^k$ (with $β> \underline{p}$) and yielding interpretable horizon estimates; (iii) matching information-theoretic upper bounds establishing tightness; and (iv) a unified algorithm combining these results. Experiments on six LLMs under $ε$-bounded and Crescendo-style attacks confirm that empirical safety consistently exceeds the certified bounds.

</details>


### [133] [Prediction certification cannot replace explanation certification: a competence envelope for trustworthy AI under compound stress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20825)
*Nataliya Shakhovska,Ivan Izonin,Stergios-Aristoteles Mitoulis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proves that prediction-based certifications (accuracy, calibration, coverage) are insufficient for establishing AI model trustworthiness, demonstrating that compromised and reliable models can appear identical under these metrics while differing in explanation fidelity and deployment behavior. The authors propose the 'competence envelope' as a framework that combines prediction and explanation certification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The increasing deployment of AI systems for consequential decisions (healthcare, security, authentication) demands reliable model certification. Current safeguards rely on prediction-based metrics, but whether these are sufficient for establishing trustworthiness has remained unclear.

Method: The authors establish a separation theorem proving the theoretical limitation of prediction-side certificates. They introduce the 'competence envelope' framework that combines prediction and explanation certification into a single deployable criterion. The framework is validated across diverse datasets and model classes.

Result: The separation theorem demonstrates that reliable and compromised models can be identical under all prediction-side certificates (accuracy, calibration, conformal coverage) yet differ arbitrarily in explanation fidelity and deployment behavior. The competence envelope framework reveals failure modes that prediction-side certification alone cannot capture.

Conclusion: Certification against failures invisible in prediction behavior requires evidence about the model's decision mechanism in addition to its outputs. The proposed competence envelope framework provides an operational solution by incorporating both prediction and explanation certification.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence systems increasingly make consequential judgments - which patient is deteriorating, which building is safe to enter, whether an image is authentic and are trusted on the strength of how accurately and confidently they predict. The safeguards that certify them are correspondingly prediction-based: accuracy, calibration and conformal coverage all measure how well a model performs. Whether such checks are sufficient to establish model trustworthiness has remained unclear. Here we prove that they cannot. We establish a separation theorem showing that a reliable model and a compromised one can be identical under every prediction-side certificate, including accuracy, calibration and coverage, yet differ arbitrarily in explanation fidelity and deployment behaviour. Detecting this failure requires access to the model's decision mechanism in addition to its predictions. We introduce the competence envelope as an operational framework that combines prediction and explanation certification into a single deployable criterion. Across diverse datasets and model classes, the proposed framework reveals failure modes that prediction-side certification alone does not capture. Certification against failures that are invisible in prediction behaviour therefore requires evidence about the model's decision mechanism as well as its outputs.

</details>


### [134] [Foundation Models for Partial Causal Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20841)
*Alexis Bellot,Anish Dhir*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops causal foundation models to bound intervention and counterfactual effects from observational data under unobserved confounding. It proposes a canonical prior with full support over discrete structural causal models, enabling translation of counterfactual bounding into a distribution learning problem over functions mapping data to causal queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to extend causal foundation modeling to handle partially-identifiable causal effects, where unobserved confounding leads to multiple equally-compatible values with observed data. This addresses a key limitation in current causal inference methods when random experimentation is not possible.

Method: The method involves defining a canonical prior with full support over the space of structural causal models with discrete observables. This prior enables formulating the problem of bounding counterfactuals as learning distributions over functions that map observational data (and structural assumptions) to causal queries of interest.

Result: The paper shows that a canonical prior can be properly defined with full support over discrete SCMs, and demonstrates how this framework extends causal foundation modeling to estimate partially-identifiable causal effects under unobserved confounding.

Conclusion: The proposed approach successfully extends the causal foundation modeling paradigm to handle partially-identifiable causal effects, providing a principled Bayesian framework for bounding counterfactuals when experimental data is unavailable.

Abstract: This paper investigates the development of causal foundation models for bounding the effect of interventions and counterfactuals from observational data. We show that a canonical prior can be defined with full support over the space of structural causal models with discrete observables. With this canonical prior, we translate the problem of bounding counterfactuals into that of learning distributions over functions that map data (and possibly structural assumptions) to a causal query of interest. This extends the promising causal foundational modelling paradigm to the estimation of partially-identifiable causal effects, i.e., under unobserved confounding, where multiple values are equally compatible with the observed data and prior structural assumptions.

</details>


### [135] [TRACE: Agentic Catalog Enrichment with Multi-source Evidence Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20844)
*Rohan Kumar,Steven Xu,Kyle MacDonald,Matthew Long,Bernice Chow,Mac VanRenterghem,Sudeep Das*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is an agentic LLM framework that automates enrichment of sparse e-commerce product catalogs by having a ScoutAgent gather multimodal evidence to propose attribute values and a JudgeAgent verify them. The system achieves 98.2% accuracy at 74.7% coverage offline and increased impression-weighted enrichment by 90.4% in production, with a 0.48% lift in checkout conversion in online experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: E-commerce product catalogs are often attribute-sparse, with valuable attributes buried in unstructured content (titles, images) or missing entirely. Manual enrichment is impractical due to scale and rapid growth. The paper addresses the need for automated, scalable catalog attribute enrichment.

Method: TRACE uses a dual-agent architecture: (1) ScoutAgent triangulates multimodal evidence from merchant catalogs, syndicated feeds, and identity-matched web search to propose candidate attribute values with supporting evidence; (2) JudgeAgent verifies each proposed value against its evidence and decides whether to publish or route to human review. The framework leverages agentic LLMs capable of multi-step reasoning and evidence synthesis.

Result: Offline evaluation showed 98.2% accuracy at 74.7% attribute coverage. In production on an industry-scale catalog, TRACE increased impression-weighted enrichment coverage by 90.4% across four business verticals. An online A/B test demonstrated a 0.48% increase in checkout conversion when enriched attributes were surfaced on product detail pages.

Conclusion: TRACE demonstrates that agentic LLMs can effectively automate catalog enrichment at scale with high accuracy, delivering significant business value. The dual-agent design separating discovery (ScoutAgent) from verification (JudgeAgent) provides a robust architecture for automated knowledge base construction. The 0.48% conversion lift translates to substantial revenue at e-commerce scale, validating the practical impact of attribute enrichment.

Abstract: Product catalogs underpin search, discovery, and recommendation in e-commerce, yet they are often attribute-sparse: the attributes shoppers and downstream systems rely on are either buried in unstructured content such as titles and images or missing from the catalog altogether. Manually enriching e-commerce catalogs is impractical given their scale and rapid growth. This paper introduces TRACE, a novel framework for automated catalog attribute enrichment using agentic Large Language Models (LLMs). A ScoutAgent triangulates multimodal evidence across merchant catalogs, syndicated feeds, and identity-matched web search to propose candidate attribute values with supporting evidence, while a JudgeAgent verifies the proposed value for each attribute value against its supporting evidence and decides whether to publish it or route it to human review. On an offline human evaluation dataset, TRACE's proposed attribute values were 98.2% accurate at 74.7% attribute coverage. Deployed in production on an industry-scale catalog, TRACE increased impression-weighted enrichment coverage across four business verticals by 90.4%. An online experiment subsequently showed that surfacing the enriched attributes on the product detail page increased checkout conversion by 0.48%.

</details>


### [136] [RAG Deserves an Index: Why Ingest-Time Compilation Beats Query-Time Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20845)
*Kyle Wild,Yusuke Takahashi,Asako Uraki*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "ingest-time semantic compilation" (ISC), a new paradigm for retrieval-augmented QA systems that pre-computes corpus meaning at write time into a queryable substrate with maintained embeddings and validated atomic claims, rather than re-deriving meaning on each query.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current production RAG systems redundantly re-derive meaning of corpus text on every query, incurring massive inefficient compute costs. The authors argue this is analogous to the "full-table scan" problem databases solved 50 years ago, and propose the same solution: do expensive work once at write time into maintained structures.

Method: Two-layer substrate: (1) incrementally maintained embeddings, and (2) atomic claims with provenance validated at compile time. The substrate is treated as a first-class database object with DDL, maintenance contracts, migration contracts, and cost model. Evaluated on 500 broadcast-interview transcripts comparing compiled claims vs various chunk configurations.

Result: Substrate upkeep scales with change not corpus size: incremental updates run 33.7x cheaper than reconstruction. On 500 held-out transcripts, compiled claims achieved 85.2% accuracy from ~2.2k reader tokens vs 72.5% from 16.3k for best chunk configuration. Only a contextualized-chunk pipeline with hybrid retrieval/reranking was statistically indistinguishable, but required ~21x more query-path tokens.

Conclusion: ISC represents a paradigm shift in RAG systems, treating compiled semantic representations as first-class database objects. The approach opens a systems agenda including compilation planners and read planning. The baseline that came closest achieves parity "precisely because it has itself begun to compile," validating the ISC approach.

Abstract: Nearly every retrieval-augmented question-answering system in production ships with a hidden interpreter: on each query a language model re-derives the meaning of raw corpus text and then throws that work away. Cheaper models do not close the gap: per-token prices have fallen by orders of magnitude while inference spend has risen, because context volume grows faster than prices fall. This is the modern equivalent of the full-table scan, and the remedy is the one databases found fifty years ago: do the expensive work once, at write time, into a maintained structure that makes reads cheap. A corpus whose read pattern is known before it ever meets a user can and should be indexed too.
  We call the paradigm ingest-time semantic compilation (ISC): compile a corpus's meaning into a queryable substrate with two coupled layers - incrementally maintained embeddings, and atomic claims whose provenance is validated at compile time - and treat that substrate as a first-class database object with its own DDL, maintenance contract, migration contract, and cost model.
  Two existence proofs support it. Substrate upkeep scales with change rather than corpus size: incremental updates run 33.7x cheaper than reconstruction while tracking it to floating-point precision. And on a held-out sample of 500 broadcast-interview transcripts, compiled claims as the retrieval payload win all 32 budget-by-model cells: 85.2% correct from roughly 2.2k reader tokens against 72.5% from 16.3k for the best chunk configuration anywhere. The only baseline that keeps pace is a contextualized-chunk pipeline with hybrid retrieval and reranking, statistically indistinguishable from compiled claims at roughly twenty-one times the query-path tokens - and it reaches that parity, we argue, precisely because it has itself begun to compile. We close with the systems agenda this opens, from compilation planners to read planning.

</details>


### [137] [MGAL: A Multilingual Granularity-Aware Long-Context Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20853)
*Chunhan Li,Chenglin Xu,Zongyang Zhang,Jiale Liu,Zhuoxi Rao,Xudong Jia,Junxiu He,Menglin Yang,Wenjuan Gong,Zhengzhe Liu,Chengwei Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MGAL is the first multilingual, granularity- and position-aware benchmark for evaluating long-context LLMs, constructed from UN reports (8K-128K tokens) across 6 UN languages, covering 4 granularity levels (word, sentence, paragraph, document) and 3 positions (begin, middle, end).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-context LLM benchmarks are limited to document level and focus mainly on high-resource languages, leaving fine-grained challenges in multilingual contexts insufficiently evaluated.

Method: MGAL was constructed from United Nations reports spanning 8K to 128K tokens across six official UN languages, covering four coherence levels (word, sentence, paragraph, document) and stratifying entries by position (begin, middle, end) at document and paragraph levels.

Result: Key findings: (1) LLMs perform well at word-level tasks but struggle with coarser-grained ones; (2) Closed-source models retain advantage in lower-resource languages; (3) Under local semantic crowding, models follow surface cues rather than discourse role; (4) A gap exists between fluency and consistency in generated outputs.

Conclusion: The benchmark reveals systematic multilingual long-context comprehension challenges across granularities, identifies two new challenges (local semantic crowding, fluency-consistency gap), and confirms prior patterns like reliance on nearby evidence and option reuse.

Abstract: Evaluation of long-context Large Language Models (LLMs) has advanced rapidly. However, most existing benchmarks are limited to the document level and focus mainly on high-resource languages, leaving many fine-grained challenges insufficiently evaluated. To address this gap, we present MGAL, the first multilingual, granularity- and position-aware long-context benchmark. MGAL is constructed from United Nations (UN) reports spanning 8K to 128K tokens across the six official UN languages. It covers four coherent levels of linguistic granularity (word, sentence, paragraph, and document) and further stratifies entries by their position within the document (begin, middle, and end), indexed at both the document and paragraph levels. This design enables systematic diagnosis of multilingual long-context comprehension across different granularities.
  Through extensive experiments and analyses, we find that: (1) LLMs perform well at word-level tasks but struggle with coarser-grained ones; and (2) Closed-source models retain a clear performance advantage in lower-resource languages. We further identify two new challenges: (1) Under local semantic crowding, where neighboring sentences share topics and entities, models tend to follow surface cues (e.g., connectives like ``however'' or repeated entities) rather than the discourse role of the sentence in surrounding context (e.g., background, outcome); and (2) A gap between fluency and consistency in generated outputs, where models produce text that reads smoothly but drifts from the source facts. In addition, we observe several patterns in line with prior studies, including reliance on nearby evidence and reuse of options under uncertainty.

</details>


### [138] [Coverage-Driven Verification for Safety-by-Design in AI-Based Collision Avoidance Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20864)
*Thomas Stefani,Johann Maximilian Christensen,Elena Hoemann,Frank Köster,Sven Hallerbach*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a method for assessing the representativeness of AI/ML components' Operational Design Domains (ODDs) in aviation safety assurance, addressing EASA's learning assurance requirements. The authors identify limitations of chi-squared tests for large datasets and propose using Kullback-Leibler divergence and Cramér's V instead, demonstrated through airborne collision avoidance systems (HCAS/VCAS).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The integration of AI in safety-critical aviation applications requires compliance with stringent safety standards. EASA mandates demonstration of ODD representativeness and data distribution completeness, but no structured engineering process currently exists for defining target distributions and evaluating representativeness within ODDs.

Method: The authors propose a process flow that guides developers from ODD definition and parameter distribution modeling to quantitative assessment. They examine statistical distribution comparison methods: chi-squared goodness-of-fit test (found unsuitable for large datasets), Kullback-Leibler divergence, and Cramér's V. The method is demonstrated using experimental data from HCAS and VCAS simulations.

Result: The results illustrate how statistical distribution comparison methods can support representativeness assessment for safety-critical AI applications. The method contributes toward a systematic Safety-by-Design AI engineering process aligned with EASA guidance.

Conclusion: The proposed representativeness assessment method addresses a critical gap in AI safety assurance for aviation. By adopting appropriate statistical measures (KL divergence and Cramér's V), developers can systematically evaluate ODD coverage and demonstrate compliance with EASA learning assurance objectives, supporting the development of safety-critical AI systems.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) offers significant potential for future aviation systems; however, its integration into safety-critical applications requires compliance with the aviation sector's stringent safety standards. For AI and Machine Learning (ML)-based systems, the European Union Aviation Safety Agency (EASA) emphasizes the need to demonstrate the representativeness and completeness of the Operational Design Domain (ODD) and the associated data distributions used during development and verification. Despite this requirement, a structured engineering process for defining target distributions and evaluating representativeness within ODDs remains largely unexplored. This work presents a method for representativeness assessment of AI/ML constituent ODDs in the context of aviation safety assurance. Starting from the methodical identification of suitable target distributions, a process flow is proposed that guides developers from ODD definition and parameter distribution modeling to the quantitative assessment and interpretation of coverage results with respect to EASA's learning assurance objectives. As quantitative measures, the chi-squared goodness-of-fit test is examined and found unsuitable for the large data sets arising in this setting, leading to the adoption of the Kullback--Leibler divergence and Cramér's $V$ for the representativeness assessment. The method is demonstrated using the example of AI-based airborne collision avoidance, employing experimental data from previous Horizontal Collision Avoidance System (HCAS) and Vertical Collision Avoidance System (VCAS) simulations. The results illustrate how statistical distribution comparison methods can support the assessment of representativeness for safety-critical AI applications and contribute toward a systematic Safety-by-Design AI engineering process aligned with emerging EASA guidance.

</details>


### [139] [ReCurveflow: A Flow Matching Framework that Learns Curved Reaction Trajectories to Predict Transition State Geometries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20869)
*Seungheun Baek,Mogan Gim,Jaewoo Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Predicting transition states (TS) in chemical reactions is crucial, as they provide insights into reaction mechanisms. Recent work on TS prediction have focused on flow matching supervised on straight linear paths that do not align with actual reaction trajectories. We propose a novel flow matching-based framework ReCurveflow that learns to predict TS geometries supervised on continuously curved reference paths interpolated from a full NEB-derived band of molecular geometries. We also introduce off-path correction, which grants ReCurveflow with the ability to produce corrective velocity fields when engaged off-path geometry states during inference rollout, leading to better resistance against exposure bias and accuracy in TS prediction. Across three data splits and six evaluation metrics, ReCurveflow achieves the best result on the majority of split-metric combinations against seven baselines. Qualitative analyses further show that ReCurveflow generates reaction trajectories with energy profiles that closely track the reference NEB path, provides initializations that ease the NEB optimization bottleneck, and exhibits the intended corrective behavior in its learned velocity fields. The ReCurveflow codebase is publicly available at https://github.com/dmis-lab/ReCurveflow.

</details>


### [140] [UpgradeBench: A Decision-Centric Benchmark for Upgrading Fine-Tuned LLM Specialists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20918)
*Ye Chen,Weining Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UpgradeBench is a benchmark for evaluating language model migration decisions, covering four Qwen releases, OLMo checkpoints, six tasks, and two model sizes. It addresses three core questions: whether new checkpoints improve retrained specialists, whether specialization assets transfer across versions, and what recovery resources are usable. Key findings show upgrade gains vary by task, adapter portability decays with continued pretraining distance, and a lightweight CKA probe can predict portability with Spearman 0.74 correlation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Organizations face migration decisions when base language models are updated, but existing transfer work only evaluates isolated model pairs without studying these choices across real model release sequences. There was no systematic way to evaluate whether to retain, port, refresh, or retrain adapters.

Method: The authors created UpgradeBench, a decision-driven longitudinal benchmark covering four consecutive Qwen releases, one continuation checkpoint, six tasks, and two model sizes, augmented by OLMo checkpoints with known training lineage. They conducted 33 upgrade episodes, evaluating retrained baselines, direct adapter copying, annealing, model souping, and teacher relabeling. They also developed a lightweight CKA probe using 256 prompts to predict cross-version adapter portability.

Result: Upgrade gains differ across task-scale-release episodes: some retrained baselines improve while others stay within training noise. Durability ranges from under one release interval for text-to-SQL to over 14 months for intent classification. On OLMo, retention drops from 0.88-0.99 at 46B-token continued pretraining to zero at 2.9T tokens. Annealing and model souping introduce no extra harm, with portability decaying with continued-pretraining distance. Teacher relabeling recovers target-base specialists without fresh gold annotations. Simulating a fixed decision policy yields 0.37pp mean quality regret with zero behavioral regressions at one-third the compute and label cost. The CKA probe achieves Spearman 0.74 correlation across eight model pairs.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that cross-version adapter transfer is feasible but depends on pretraining distance. A lightweight CKA probe can effectively predict portability. A fixed decision policy can achieve near-optimal results with significantly reduced compute and labeling costs, providing practical guidance for organizations managing model upgrades.

Abstract: Organizations maintain task-specific adapters for open-weight language models, and each new base-model release forces a migration decision: retain existing specialists, port adapters, refresh from preserved behavior, or retrain. Prior transfer work evaluates isolated model pairs, without studying these choices across real model release sequences. We present UpgradeBench, a decision-driven longitudinal benchmark covering four consecutive Qwen releases, one continuation checkpoint, six tasks, and two model sizes, augmented by OLMo checkpoints with known training lineage. The benchmark disentangles three core questions: whether a new checkpoint improves fixed-recipe retrained specialist performance, whether specialization assets transfer across versions, and what recovery resources are usable. We observe upgrade gains differ across task-scale-release episodes: some retrained baselines improve while others stay within training noise, with durability ranging from under one release interval for text-to-SQL to over fourteen months for intent classification. Direct adapter copying depends neither on architecture nor model family: on OLMo, retention drops from 0.88-0.99 at 46B-token continued pretraining to zero at 2.9T tokens; annealing and model souping introduce no extra harm, with portability decaying with continued-pretraining distance. Given preserved input data, teacher relabeling recovers target-base specialists without fresh gold annotations, though compute savings are not guaranteed. Simulating a fixed decision policy over 33 upgrade episodes yields 0.37pp mean quality regret with zero behavioral regressions at one-third the compute and label cost of full retraining. A lightweight CKA probe over 256 prompts predicts cross-version adapter portability (Spearman 0.74 across eight model pairs).
