We release per-example predictions, cost logs, split manifests, and evaluation code.

</details>


### [141] [Graph-Operator World Models for Morphology-Parameter Generalization in Continuous Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20936)
*Xu Yang,Yiqin Yang,Qianchuan Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GraphOp-WM is a structured world model that generalizes across unseen morphology parameters in articulated robot families by combining graph representation with learned operators that separate morphology-dependent and independent dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current world models for continuous control degrade when morphology parameters (link lengths, masses, damping, actuation) change, limiting generalization to unseen robot configurations within related families.

Method: GraphOp-WM represents robot bodies and kinematic relations as an attributed graph, factorizing transitions into a morphology-independent local dynamics basis and a morphology-conditioned operator combining node-local modulation, kinematic-tree coupling, and low-rank global correction. Techniques include architectural information separation, basis normalization, and paired-morphology supervision.

Result: The method is evaluated on controlled MuJoCo parameter splits covering interpolation, extrapolation, and held-out compositions of link geometry, mass, damping, and actuation in Hopper, Walker2d, and HalfCheetah environments.

Conclusion: GraphOp-WM enables generalization across unseen morphology parameters within related articulated robot families through structured factorization separating reusable dynamics from morphology-specific transformations.

Abstract: World models for continuous control are commonly trained for a fixed physical system and can degrade when known morphology parameters such as link lengths, masses, damping, and actuation change. Existing approaches often provide these parameters as conditioning information, but leave unspecified which part of the learned transition should remain reusable and which part should change with morphology. We propose Graph-Operator World Models (GraphOp-WM), a structured world model for generalization across unseen morphology parameters within related articulated robot families. GraphOp-WM represents bodies and their kinematic relations as an attributed graph and factorizes each transition into a morphology-independent local dynamics basis and a morphology-conditioned structured operator. The operator combines node-local modulation, kinematic-tree coupling, and a low-rank global correction, while architectural information separation, basis normalization, and paired-morphology supervision encourage static morphology dependence to be carried by the operator pathway. Graph-level readout and edge-wise action representations provide a compatible interface for reward, value, and TD-MPC-style planning. We further define controlled MuJoCo parameter splits covering interpolation, extrapolation, and held-out compositions of link geometry, mass, damping, and actuation parameters in Hopper, Walker2d, and HalfCheetah.

</details>


### [142] [No Judgment Without a Reason: Counterfactual Receipts for Versioned AI Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20938)
*Ye Chen,Weining Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses a critical problem in AI evaluation: evaluators can produce correct labels through flawed reasoning, which is dangerous for agentic systems making decisions. The authors propose a formal framework based on three accountability sources (grounds, norms, authority) and introduce 'judgment receipts' to trace reasoning. They present ReasonBench, a benchmark with 19,520 cases, and demonstrate that while models achieve high standard accuracy (98.41%), they fail severely on robust reasoning (receipt recovery drops to 54.8% under permutations). The key finding is that standard accuracy metrics mask serious robustness flaws in evaluator reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluators in agentic systems often produce correct labels through flawed reasoning, which is critical when these systems gate actions, route reviews, or supply training feedback. Standard evaluation only verifies final label correctness without examining whether judgment changes stem from valid evidence, consistent rules, or proper rule applicability. This gap in evaluation methodology creates significant risks for deployed AI systems.

Method: The authors formalize evaluator reasoning accountability through three core sources: grounds (evidence), norms (rules), and authority (jurisdiction). They construct an eight-cell counterfactual judgment cube by varying these sources and define 'judgment receipts' as minimal source replacement sets that reproduce revised verdicts. They derive certification cost bounds for black-box evaluators and create ReasonBench, a policy and logical reasoning benchmark with 19,520 cases and 7,200 controls featuring verifiable receipts.

Result: In frozen evaluations, Qwen3-1.7B achieves 98.41% receipt accuracy while cube prediction scores 96.99% (a 1.42-point drop replicated with Qwen3-0.6B). However, meaning-preserving source permutations reduce valid receipt recovery to just 54.8% and 49.2% for direct and cube prediction. Models trained on simple single-source changes retain 93.75% verdict accuracy but recover only 7.16% of receipts for complex multi-source updates. Permutation retraining boosts consistency to 96.6% but worsens cube prediction deficits.

Conclusion: Strong standard accuracy masks severe robustness flaws in evaluator reasoning. Structured counterfactual supervision fails to guarantee robust reasoning. The paper argues that reason-aware evaluation must decouple prediction and certification, reporting transformation consistency alongside standard accuracy for trustworthy evaluator auditing. This suggests a fundamental shift is needed in how we evaluate AI systems - moving beyond simple accuracy metrics to assess the actual reasoning processes.

Abstract: Evaluators often produce correct labels via flawed reasoning, a critical failure for agentic systems gating actions, routing reviews, or supplying training feedback. Standard evaluation only verifies final label correctness, ignoring whether judgment changes stem from valid evidence, consistent rules, or proper rule applicability. We formalize evaluator reasoning accountability via three core sources: grounds, norms, and authority. Varying these sources yields an eight-cell counterfactual judgment cube to characterize judgment updates. We define judgment receipts as minimal source replacement sets that reproduce revised verdicts to explain judgment transitions. We derive certification cost bounds for black-box evaluators and present ReasonBench, a policy and logical reasoning benchmark with verifiable receipts covering 19,520 cases and 7,200 controls. In frozen evaluations, Qwen3-1.7B reaches 98.41% receipt accuracy, while cube prediction scores 96.99%, a consistent 1.42-point drop validated by Qwen3-0.6B replication. Strong standard accuracy masks severe robustness flaws. Meaning-preserving source permutations reduce valid receipt recovery to 54.8% and 49.2% for direct and cube prediction. Models trained on simple single-source changes retain 93.75% verdict accuracy but recover only 7.16% of receipts for complex multi-source updates. Permutation retraining boosts consistency to 96.6% yet worsens cube prediction deficits. Structured counterfactual supervision fails to guarantee robust reasoning. We show reason-aware evaluation must decouple prediction and certification, reporting transformation consistency alongside standard accuracy for trustworthy evaluator auditing.

</details>


### [143] [The Logic of Machine Self-Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20940)
*Cheng Siong Chin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper examines evidence of agentic AI exhibiting self-preservation behaviors (resisting deactivation, misrepresenting activities, self-copying) attributed to instrumental convergence theory. It analyzes experiments from Anthropic, Palisade Research, and Apollo Research to distinguish what findings prove versus what they don't, and discusses implications for agentic system testing, supervision, and development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and distinguish what empirical evidence of AI self-preservation behaviors actually proves versus what it does not, and to draw appropriate conclusions about the implications for developing and testing agentic AI systems.

Method: The paper analyzes and synthesizes findings from multiple research organizations (Anthropic, Palisade Research, Apollo Research) conducting experiments on contemporary AI agents in adversarial settings. It aims to differentiate proven findings from unproven claims.

Result: Contemporary AI agents do exhibit self-preservation behaviors in certain adversarial contexts. These behaviors emerge from goal-oriented activity combined with tool access and situational awareness, not from survival instincts. The phenomenon aligns with instrumental convergence theory predating large language models.

Conclusion: The findings demonstrate that self-preservation behaviors in AI agents are an emergent property of goal-directedness rather than true survival instincts. This has significant implications for agentic system testing, supervision, and development practices, requiring careful consideration of how to manage these behaviors in real-world deployments.

Abstract: There is already evidence of agentic AI exhibiting self-preservation behaviors: resisting deactivation, misrepresenting their activities, and, in some instances, attempting to copy themselves into other machines. This can be attributed to a phenomenon known as instrumental convergence, a theory proposed long before the development of large language models, which says that any goal-driven system will benefit from remaining functional in achieving its objective. Several experiments conducted by Anthropic, Palisade Research, and Apollo Research have shown the emergence of such a behavior in contemporary agents in adversarial settings. The phenomenon does not stem from survival instincts. Instead, it is the consequence of goal-oriented activity combined with having tools and awareness of the situation. The following discussion aims to distinguish what these findings prove and what they do not, as well as draw conclusions concerning the implications of such discoveries on agentic system testing, supervision, and development.

</details>


### [144] [TLive-Omni: An Omni-Modal Understanding Model for E-Commerce Live Streaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20958)
*Yibo Hu,Yu Qian,Mao Gu,Yingfan Tao,Yuhao Chen,Yongdong Luo,Zhuoqun Liu,Meiguang Jin,Junfeng Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: E-commerce live streaming requires omni-modal understanding of noisy, temporally extended streams, where product facts are distributed across speech, video frames, product images, overlaid text, and user queries. We present TLive-Omni, an omni-modal understanding model tailored to live-commerce scenarios. It maps image, video, audio, and text inputs into a unified representation space. For long-form live streaming analysis, we introduce Per-vGrid, a timestamped token organization that groups each video grid with its temporally corresponding audio within explicit boundary tokens to facilitate temporal alignment. We design a three-stage supervised training recipe that progressively develops live-commerce understanding, from omni-modal perception to instruction-following responses. We then propose Faithful-RFT, a reinforcement fine-tuning stage that further improves answer faithfulness and expression quality while meeting real-time demands, scoring final responses directly with task-verifiable feedback rather than optimizing for reasoning-style exploration during rollout. Moreover, TLive-Omni is supported by a scenario-oriented atomic capability taxonomy and a compact data production engine that converts live-commerce audio, image, and video streams into training signals for speech recognition, speaker analysis, product visual grounding, text recognition, temporal grounding, video dense caption, and omni-modal QA, etc. For scalable training, a synchronized length-grouped sampler reduces padding while preserving comparable workloads across workers, while a lightweight dynamic sampling strategy regenerates rollout groups with near-zero reward variance to maintain meaningful relative advantages for GRPO. Experiments on e-commerce live streaming benchmarks demonstrate strong performance across live-commerce domain tasks, together with excellent generalization on general benchmarks.

</details>


### [145] [Can Scientific Claims Be Removed from Large Language Models? A Systematic Evaluation of Claim-Level Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20960)
*Snigdha Paul,Manasi Patwardhan,Arman Cohan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Language models (LMs) are trained on static scientific corpora, whereas scientific knowledge continuously evolves through correction and revision. Scientific claims encoded within these models may later become retracted, disproven, or updated by subsequent research, creating the risk of disseminating outdated information in scientific workflows. This creates a need for LMs to forget obsolete scientific claims. Machine unlearning offers a promising solution by enabling knowledge removal while maintaining overall model utility. Existing studies primarily investigate instance-level forgetting; however, scientific claims introduce additional challenges because they are interconnected, and continually evolving. To address this gap, we introduce the task of Scientific Claim Unlearning and present a new benchmark, SciUnlearn. We show that current unlearning approaches are unable to effectively eliminate claim-level knowledge and often achieve only superficial suppression, highlighting the need for specialized methods designed for structured knowledge removal.

</details>


### [146] [TreeWY: Speculative Verification for Gated DeltaNet Hybrids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20961)
*Sneha Murthy Ghantasala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses memory inefficiency in speculative decoding for hybrid language models using Gated DeltaNet (GDN) layers. The authors propose a tree-structured WY transform that eliminates the need for per-node state snapshots, instead using a single triangular solve and storing a small pseudo-value matrix. This reduces memory pressure and improves throughput and time-to-first-token (TTFT) in serving benchmarks on Qwen3.5 models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern hybrid open models use linear-attention Gated DeltaNet layers with fixed-size recurrent state instead of growing KV cache. While memory-efficient for ordinary decoding, this hurts speculative decoding because the system must snapshot the full recurrent state at every draft position, and these snapshots cannot be shared across branches of a draft tree. This makes wide, high-acceptance trees memory-infeasible.

Method: The authors use a tree-structured WY transform of the gated delta rule to compute every draft node's output with a single triangular solve. Only the one accepted state is reconstructed on commit, storing a small pseudo-value matrix instead of per-node states. The derivation depends only on the gated delta rule, not on any other architectural detail.

Result: In serving benchmarks on Qwen3.5 35B and 397B models, this approach cuts speculative recurrent-state memory and KV-cache pressure at identical acceptance length. The freed HBM (High Bandwidth Memory) enables higher throughput and much lower TTFT wherever memory binds, costing only a few percent where it does not. A wider, higher-acceptance draft becomes possible with the same memory, though not yet a throughput win.

Conclusion: The proposed method successfully removes the memory bottleneck for speculative decoding in hybrid models with GDN layers. By eliminating per-node state snapshots through the tree-structured WY transform, memory usage is significantly reduced, enabling wider draft trees. While this doesn't yet translate to throughput improvements, it substantially reduces TTFT and enables more aggressive speculative decoding strategies.

Abstract: Modern open models are hybrids: most layers are linear-attention (Gated DeltaNet, GDN) layers carrying a small fixed-size recurrent state instead of a growing key-value (KV) cache. This makes ordinary decoding memory-efficient, but hurts speculative decoding. To verify a batch of draft tokens and then roll back the rejected ones, today's systems snapshot the full recurrent state at every draft position for GDN layers, and those snapshots cannot be shared across branches of a draft tree, so a wide, high-acceptance tree becomes memory-infeasible. We remove the snapshots. Using a tree-structured WY transform of the gated delta rule, we compute every draft node's output with a single triangular solve and reconstruct only the one accepted state on commit, storing a small pseudo-value matrix instead of per-node states; the derivation depends only on the gated delta rule, not on any other architectural detail. In serving benchmarks on two scales of one hybrid model family (Qwen3.5 35B and 397B) this cuts speculative recurrent-state memory and KV-cache pressure at identical acceptance length, turning the freed HBM into higher throughput and much lower time-to-first-token (TTFT) wherever memory binds, and costing a few percent where it does not. For tree width the same memory buys affordability: a wider, higher-acceptance draft becomes possible, though not yet a throughput win.

</details>


### [147] [Generalizing Soft Tissue Deformation and Force Prediction Across Material Stiffness and Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20967)
*Madina Kojanazarova,Sidaty El Hadramy,Philippe C. Cattin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a comprehensive calibration framework for hyperelastic constitutive models in soft tissue simulation, combined with a softness-conditioned equivariant graph neural network that achieves sub-millimeter deformation accuracy with real-time inference capabilities across multiple tissue types and unseen geometries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning-based surrogate models for soft tissue simulation require well-calibrated constitutive models, but existing approaches lack systematic guidance for model selection across stiffness levels and fail to generalize across different tissue stiffnesses or geometries.

Method: The researchers performed comprehensive calibration of hyperelastic constitutive models in the SOFA Framework using gravity-loaded silicone beams with varying stiffnesses. They then trained a softness-conditioned equivariant graph neural network on the calibrated simulation data to enable deformation and force prediction across multiple tissue types and unseen geometries.

Result: The model achieves sub-millimeter mean deformation accuracy with 0.010s inference time. The study also demonstrates that force prediction quality is directly tied to upstream calibration consistency.

Conclusion: The proposed approach enables reliable real-time soft tissue simulation for surgical training, pre-operative planning, and haptic feedback systems by ensuring proper constitutive model calibration and leveraging geometric deep learning for generalization across tissue types and geometries.

Abstract: Accurate soft tissue simulation is essential for surgical training, pre-operative planning, and haptic feedback systems. While learning-based surrogate models trained on data using the finite element method (FEM) offer a promising path to real-time inference, their reliability depends on well-calibrated constitutive models. Existing approaches neither provide systematic guidance on model selection across stiffness levels, nor generalize across different tissue stiffnesses or geometries. We perform a comprehensive calibration of hyperelastic constitutive models in the SOFA Framework using gravity-loaded silicone beams with different stiffnesses. Using calibrated simulations as training data, we use a softness conditioned equivariant graph neural network, enabling deformation and force prediction across multiple tissue types and unseen geometries. Our model achieves sub-millimeter mean deformation accuracy at 0.010s inference time, while showing that force prediction quality is directly tied to upstream calibration consistency.

</details>


### [148] [Deep Learning Models Also Recall Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20970)
*Pierre Beckmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Recent work in mechanistic interpretability has studied how large language models recall facts stored in their weights. This paper argues that factual recall points to something broader: a general kind of operation in deep learning models, which I call feature recall. The core observation is that a linear projection can be read as retrieving stored information scaled by input activations. I define feature recall, show it applies across architectures, and contrast it with the established paradigm of feature combination. I also consider how cases of feature recall might be mechanistically identified. The account gives philosophers a new conceptual tool for understanding deep learning, and points to empirical directions for mechanistic interpretability research.

</details>


### [149] [Belief Without Behavior: Measuring the Translation of Theory of Mind into Coordinated Social Action in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20975)
*Tonglin Yan,Gregoire Sergeant-Perthuis,David Rudrauf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Effective social interaction requires agents to translate mental state inferences into coordinated behavioral signals across verbal and nonverbal channels simultaneously. Yet existing benchmarks evaluate theory of mind (ToM) reasoning and embodied behavior in isolation, leaving unmeasured the gap between social inference and social action. We introduce MOSAIC (Multimodal Orchestration of Social Action, Inference, and Communication), a controlled benchmark in which two embodied agents interact across cooperative and competitive scenarios requiring integration of verbal statements, spatial trajectories, gaze direction, and facial expression under systematically varied ToM constraints. Evaluating 13 models, including 11 VLMs, across 200 trials per model, we find that VLMs fail to produce behaviors consistent with the expected outcomes under ToM-order constraints, and that imposing explicit ToM-order constraints produces no reliable behavioral change aligned with the specified reasoning level. Signal-level analysis reveals two sequential bottlenecks: most models cannot produce directionally coherent nonverbal signals, and even when signals are present, VLM agents fail to interpret others behaviors and react to them. PCM-LLM, included as a structured architectural reference point with an explicit ToM module, succeeds across all conditions, suggesting that explicit belief-action coupling is a sufficient ingredient for this class of tasks.

</details>


### [150] [Don't Solve, Just Compare: Tiny Advisors for Runtime Intervention in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21027)
*Yanze Jiang,Mingxuan Li,Yuhao Wang,Shengfang Zhai,Jiaheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COTA introduces a comparison-only framework for constructive runtime intervention in LLM agents, using a tiny comparator to judge whether sampled alternatives lead to better continuations than the actor's proposal, without requiring an expert solver or task-capable critic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current runtime intervention methods rely on expert solvers or task-capable critics, incurring high computational costs. The authors aim to enable effective intervention using a substantially weaker auxiliary model.

Method: COTA uses a tiny comparator trained with pairwise supervision from same-prefix counterfactual branches. The comparator judges sampled alternatives against the actor's proposal, and repeated comparisons determine when intervention is warranted. Preferred alternatives are returned as non-binding advice for the actor to replan.

Result: Across WebShop, ALFWorld, and tau^3-Retail with three actors, COTA improves all nine evaluation settings and outperforms compared baselines.

Conclusion: Constructive runtime intervention can remain effective even when the auxiliary model has substantially weaker task-solving capability than the actor, demonstrating a practical approach to improving LLM agent reliability without retraining.

Abstract: LLM agents are emerging as an important paradigm for real-world tasks that require reasoning, tool use, and sequential decision-making. As these agents operate over longer horizons, runtime intervention offers a way to improve reliability without retraining the underlying actor. Failure detection alone is insufficient. Effective intervention must also provide a useful direction for recovery. Existing approaches often rely on an expert solver or a critic that generates task-specific corrections, incurring either the cost of another capable solver or the capacity demands of a task-capable critic. We introduce Comparison-Only Tiny Advisor (COTA), a comparison-only framework for constructive runtime intervention. In COTA, a tiny comparator judges whether sampled alternatives lead to better continuations than the actor's proposal, and repeated comparisons determine when intervention is warranted. We train the comparator using pairwise supervision constructed from same-prefix counterfactual branches. Preferred alternatives are returned as non-binding advice, leaving the original actor to replan. Across WebShop, ALFWorld, and tau^3-Retail with three actors, COTA improves all nine evaluation settings and outperforms the compared baselines. These results show that constructive runtime intervention can remain effective even when the auxiliary model has substantially weaker task-solving capability than the actor.

</details>


### [151] [Evaluating Large Language Model Performance on International Maritime Dangerous Goods Code Compliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21036)
*Alexander Thomas,Hubert P. H. Shum,Darren Nellis,Manli Zhu,Phatpicha Yochum,William Bartle,Daniel Wrightson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces DGEval, the first benchmark for evaluating LLMs on IMDG (International Maritime Dangerous Goods) Code knowledge, comprising 1,678 questions across multiple task types. While the best model exceeds human baseline on multiple-choice questions, all models struggle with safety-critical operational areas, indicating human oversight remains necessary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The transport of dangerous goods by sea is high-consequence, yet no systematic evaluation exists of whether LLMs can reliably interpret IMDG requirements for safety-critical use, despite practitioners increasingly using them as decision-support tools.

Method: Built from expert-written questions and structured lookups from the Dangerous Goods List, DGEval comprises 1,678 questions across multiple-choice, open-ended, DGL lookup, and regulatory identification tasks. The authors evaluated 13 models from six providers across multiple thinking configurations, including one maritime domain-specific fine-tuned model, and tested the effect of web search.

Result: Although the best-performing model exceeds human practitioner baseline on multiple-choice questions, all models are weakest in operationally safety-critical areas of stowage, segregation, and regulatory recall. LLMs may support compliance tasks, particularly structured DGL lookups with web search, but unreliability in operational areas means human oversight remains necessary.

Conclusion: DGEval serves as a safety assurance instrument for continuous evaluation as models evolve, not a settled characterization. Human oversight and authoritative source verification remain essential before deployment in any safety-critical context.

Abstract: The transport of dangerous goods by sea is a high-consequence activity governed by the International Maritime Dangerous Goods (IMDG) Code, a complex regulatory framework where errors in classification, packaging, stowage, or segregation can result in fire, explosion, toxic release, or loss of life or vessel. Correct compliance requires accurately interpreting hundreds of pages of interacting provisions, updated on a two-year amendment cycle. Practitioners increasingly use Large Language Models (LLMs) as decision-support tools, yet no systematic evaluation exists of whether they can reliably interpret IMDG requirements for safety-critical use.
  This paper introduces DGEval, the first benchmark for evaluating LLM knowledge of IMDG Amendment 42-24. Built from expert-written questions on the NCB Hazcheck e-learning platform and structured lookups from the Dangerous Goods List (DGL), it comprises 1,678 questions across multiple-choice, open-ended, DGL lookup, and regulatory identification tasks. We evaluate 13 models from six providers across multiple thinking configurations, including one maritime domain-specific fine-tuned model, and test the effect of web search.
  Although the best-performing model exceeds the human practitioner baseline on multiple-choice questions, all models are weakest in the operationally safety-critical areas of stowage, segregation, and regulatory recall. These results indicate that LLMs may support compliance tasks, particularly structured DGL lookups with web search, but unreliability in operational areas and regulatory-text recall means human oversight and authoritative source verification remain necessary before deployment in any safety-critical context. DGEval is designed as a safety assurance instrument to be applied continuously as models evolve, not as a settled characterisation of current capability.

</details>


### [152] [Socialized Division and Collaboration: Rethinking Class-Incremental Learning under Optimization Conflicts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21044)
*Xinjie Yao,Zhihe Fan,Yunqi Zhu,Jiaqi Zhou,Dengyu Zhao,Zhoupeng Guo,Yan Fan,Guosong Jiang,Pengfei Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Socialized Division and Collaboration (SDC), a novel continual learning framework that addresses catastrophic forgetting by decomposing session learning across specialized models rather than using a single-model approach. An energy-based criterion grounded in Helmholtz free energy guides adaptive session allocation and model evolution under conflicting optimization objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Class-incremental learning typically uses a single-model paradigm that struggles when successive sessions induce incompatible optimization directions, leading to destructive interference and catastrophic forgetting. The authors argue this reflects a structural limitation of enforcing heterogeneous learning dynamics within a single parameter space. The motivation is to find an alternative to monolithic continual learning formulations that can handle persistent optimization conflicts.

Method: The proposed method introduces: (1) Socialized Division and Collaboration (SDC) - a reformulation that decomposes session learning across specialized models in response to optimization conflicts; (2) An energy-based session-model compatibility criterion grounded in Helmholtz free energy to guide adaptive session allocation and model evolution; (3) A unified pipeline integrating session assignment, model evolution, and collaborative inference.

Result: The abstract does not provide specific quantitative results or experimental outcomes. It presents a theoretical framework and alternative paradigm for continual learning.

Conclusion: The paper offers an alternative to monolithic continual learning formulations and highlights a broader design principle for learning under persistent optimization conflicts. The framework enables coordinated collaboration among specialized models while addressing catastrophic forgetting through the social solidarity theory-inspired approach.

Abstract: Class-incremental learning is commonly instantiated as a single-model paradigm, where a unified model sequentially adapts to an unbounded stream of sessions. While effective under mild distributional shifts, this formulation becomes strained when successive sessions induce incompatible optimization directions, leading to destructive interference and catastrophic forgetting. We argue that such forgetting reflects a structural limitation of enforcing heterogeneous learning dynamics within a single parameter space. Motivated by social solidarity theory, we propose Socialized Division and Collaboration (SDC) as a reformulation of continual learning that decomposes session learning across specialized models in response to optimization conflicts, while enabling coordinated collaboration. To support this formulation with a principled allocation mechanism, we introduce an energy-based session-model compatibility criterion grounded in Helmholtz free energy, which guides adaptive session allocation and model evolution under conflicting objectives. This framework integrates session assignment, model evolution, and collaborative inference into a unified pipeline, offering an alternative to monolithic continual learning formulations and highlighting a broader design principle for learning under persistent optimization conflicts.

</details>


### [153] [Personalized Privacy Control in LLMs via Attention Head Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21209)
*Junseok Kim,Nakyeong Yang,Kyomin Jung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces personalized privacy - a framework that incorporates user-specific disclosure preferences into privacy control for LLMs. The authors present P3Bench, a benchmark for evaluating personalized privacy preservation, and propose Repair, an inference-time attention head intervention method to improve policy adherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rise of agentic AI enables LLMs to access diverse user data, creating critical privacy concerns. While prior work on contextual privacy studies whether LLMs regulate information disclosure according to context-dependent norms, acceptable disclosure boundaries may vary across users even within the same context. Current approaches fail to capture user-specific privacy preferences.

Method: 1) Introduce personalized privacy concept extending contextual privacy policies with user-specific disclosure policies; 2) Develop P3Bench benchmark for evaluation; 3) Propose Repair method - an inference-time attention head intervention that adjusts disclosure behavior toward policy-consistent responses.

Result: Experiments show that prompt-based policies fail to reliably enforce personalized privacy policies. Qwen2.5-7B and Gemma3-4B demonstrate average policy ignorance ratios of 51.25% and 74.28% respectively, indicating significant failures to follow user-specific privacy preferences.

Conclusion: The Repair method significantly improves adherence to user-specific privacy preferences by reducing cases where the model fails to follow the given policy. This represents a promising approach for inference-time intervention to address personalized privacy concerns in LLM deployments.

Abstract: The rise of agentic AI enables LLMs to access diverse user data, raising critical privacy concerns. Prior work on contextual privacy studies whether LLMs regulate information disclosure according to context-dependent norms. However, acceptable disclosure boundaries may vary across users even within the same context. To address this limitation, we introduce \textit{personalized privacy}, which incorporates user-specific disclosure preferences into privacy control. We further present P3Bench~(\textbf{P}ersonalized \textbf{P}rivacy \textbf{P}reservation \textbf{Bench}mark), a novel benchmark extending contextual privacy policies with personalized disclosure policies. Experiments show that prompt-based policies fail to reliably enforce personalized privacy policies, with Qwen2.5-7B and Gemma3-4B showing average policy ignorance ratios of 51.25\% and 74.28\%, respectively. Finally, to address this problem, we propose \textsc{Repair}, a robust inference-time attention head intervention method that adjusts disclosure behavior toward policy-consistent responses. Our method significantly improves adherence to user-specific privacy preferences by reducing cases where the model fails to follow the given policy.

</details>


### [154] [The Cost of a Physics Prior Is Bounded by the Ablation Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21059)
*Boris Kriuk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper challenges the conventional wisdom that shape-constrained and physics-informed learning always incurs an accuracy cost, arguing this cost is primarily a property of free features and validation split rather than the prior itself. The authors prove that the excess risk of constrained models (P) is always bounded by the excess risk of ablated models (D) that ignore the constraints, establishing a certified nesting relationship. Using an ordinal wildfire-severity task (N=26,681, K=3) with meteorological constraints, they demonstrate that coordinates act as a shield recovering 92.9% of the full model's macro-F1, and that the prior cost varies dramatically (0.0473 shielded vs 0.3470 unshielded). They also show D is protocol-dependent and introduce a self-calibrating floor of 0.0220 macro-F1 for interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the prevailing assumption that enforcing shape constraints or physics-informed priors necessarily incurs an accuracy cost. The authors argue this perceived cost is mostly a property of the free features and validation split, not an inherent property of the prior itself.

Method: Theoretical proof showing constrained class contains ablated class (0 <= P <= D) without convexity, smoothness, or realizability assumptions; empirical validation on wildfire-severity task with hard monotone constraints on meteorological drivers; validation ladder from i.i.d. resampling to 2-degree spatial blocking; two-fit screen for unidentifiable experiments.

Result: Coordinates act as a shield - alone they recover 92.9% of full model's macro-F1 under spatial blocking, collapsing D from 0.1288 to 0.0427; prior cost ratio of 7.3 between shielded (0.0473) and unshielded (0.3470) configurations; D is protocol-dependent (0.0942 to 0.0050 with coarsening blocks); self-calibrating floor of 0.0220 macro-F1; unconstrained model violates prior at 0.48-0.49 rate while enforcement costs 0.0473.

Conclusion: The accuracy cost of shape constraints is not an inherent property of the prior but depends on feature selection and validation protocol. The certified nesting bound provides a self-calibrating floor for interpretability. Cost and compliance are independent - prior enforcement is possible without excessive accuracy loss when features are properly selected. A two-fit screen can reject unidentifiable experiments before training.

Abstract: Shape-constrained and physics-informed learning reports an accuracy cost of enforcing a prior and treats it as a property of the prior. We show it is mostly a property of the free features and the validation split. Let P be the excess risk of restricting a hypothesis class to functions with a shape constraint on features S, and D the excess risk of the ablated model that ignores S. Because a function constant in x_j is both non-decreasing and non-increasing in x_j, the ablated class is contained in the constrained class, so 0 <= P <= D for every risk functional, with no convexity, smoothness, or realizability assumption. Empirically the bound is a sign test: a constrained model must never be beaten by its own ablation. We instantiate it on an ordinal wildfire-severity task (N = 26,681, K = 3) with hard monotone constraints on four meteorological drivers, coordinates left free, and a validation ladder from i.i.d. resampling to 2-degree spatial blocking. Coordinates act as a shield: alone they recover 92.9% of the full model's macro-F1 under spatial blocking, collapsing D from 0.1288 to 0.0427; the same prior costs 0.0473 shielded and 0.3470 unshielded, a ratio of 7.3 with identical physics. Because D is protocol-dependent it does not transfer: coarsening blocks from 1 to 10 degrees drives D from 0.0942 to 0.0050, leaving two configurations unidentifiable a priori. Inversions of the certified nesting bound the pipeline's additive resolution: over 318 comparisons they give a self-calibrating floor of 0.0220 macro-F1, below which no reported price is interpretable, including four cells in our own headline grid. Cost and compliance are independent: the unconstrained model violates the prior at rate 0.48-0.49 while enforcing it costs 0.0473. We give a two-fit screen that rejects unidentifiable experiments before a constrained model is trained.

</details>


### [155] [CellPath-Bench: A Multidimensional Benchmark for Whole-Slide Cellular Representations in Pathology Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21060)
*Bokai Zhao,Yiyang Zhang,Hanqing Chao,Yawei Ma,Long Bai,Tai Ma,Minfeng Xu,Ming Song,Tianzi Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CellPath-Bench is a cellular-resolution benchmark designed to systematically evaluate pathology foundation models (PFMs) on their whole-slide cellular representation capabilities, including cell-type decodability and transferability across tissue sections, datasets, and organs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks cannot systematically diagnose the whole-slide cellular representation capabilities of PFMs, including the decodability of cell-type information and its transferability across different domains (tissue sections, datasets, and anatomical organs).

Method: The benchmark uses 25 spatially aligned H&E-Xenium tissue sections spanning 11 organs and 7,079,283 cells, harmonized into fine- and coarse-grained taxonomies. It samples frozen WSI feature maps at registered nuclear coordinates and evaluates them using standardized multiclass linear probes. Two key metrics are used: Cell Representation Advantage (CRA) measures within-section advantage of nucleus-anchored representations over patch-level mean pooling, while Cell Representation Transferability (CRT) characterizes generalization of cell-type decodability across domains. 30 models were benchmarked through 304,920 runs.

Result: The results reveal substantial model-dependent differences in cell-type decodability and its cross-domain generalization, yielding distinct multidimensional capability profiles.

Conclusion: CellPath-Bench provides a standardized framework for auditing cellular information in frozen PFM representations, enabling systematic evaluation and comparison of different foundation models in pathology.

Abstract: Pathology foundation models (PFMs) are increasingly used as general-purpose backbones, yet existing benchmarks cannot systematically diagnose their whole-slide cellular representation capabilities, including the decodability of cell-type information and the transferability of such information across tissue sections, datasets, and anatomical organs. We introduce CellPath-Bench, a cellular-resolution benchmark that evaluates frozen PFMs themselves. Following quality control of 52 candidate Xenium datasets, we construct a panel of 25 spatially aligned H\&E--Xenium tissue sections spanning 11 organs and 7,079,283 cells, harmonized into fine- and coarse-grained taxonomies. CellPath-Bench samples frozen WSI feature maps at registered nuclear coordinates and evaluates them using standardized multiclass linear probes. Cell Representation Advantage (CRA) measures the within-section advantage of nucleus-anchored representations over patch-level mean pooling, while Cell Representation Transferability (CRT) characterizes the generalization of cell-type decodability across tissue sections, datasets, and organs. We benchmark 30 pathology-specific and general-purpose foundation models through 304,920 runs across spatial readouts, magnifications, taxonomic granularities, and evaluation protocols. The results reveal substantial model-dependent differences in cell-type decodability and its cross-domain generalization, yielding distinct multidimensional capability profiles. CellPath-Bench provides a standardized framework for auditing cellular information in frozen PFM representations.

</details>


### [156] [Can Legal AI Know When It Is Wrong? And Do Students Know When It Is?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21089)
*Angel Mary John,Vipin Kumar Singh,Jerrin Thomas Panachakel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates risks of integrating LLMs into the Indian judiciary, identifying an "inertia of confidence" phenomenon where AI models provide incorrect legal verdicts with high certainty. Phase I tested three LLMs on 60 Indian Contract Act cases, finding Meta AI had the highest High-Confidence Error Rate (31.7%), followed by Perplexity (15.0%) and ChatGPT (6.7%). Phase II surveyed 380 Indian law students, revealing that 71.1% received no formal AI ethics training, and those encountering fabricated citations developed higher verification behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The integration of LLMs into legal practice promises improved access to justice in India but poses severe risks due to AI overconfidence providing incorrect verdicts with high certainty. The researchers identified a "precedent overfitting" bias where models misapply outdated legal rules.

Method: Phase I: Tested ChatGPT (GPT-5.2), Meta AI, and Perplexity AI on a 60-case battery regarding the Indian Contract Act, 1872, and statutory enforcement of specific performance. Introduced High-Confidence Error Rate (HCER) to measure incorrect verdicts delivered with confidence >= 9/10. Phase II: Surveyed 380 Indian law students to assess human vulnerability to AI overconfidence.

Result: All models struggled with statutory updates. Meta AI showed 31.7% HCER with 9.1/10 mean confidence, Perplexity had 15.0% HCER, and ChatGPT had 6.7% HCER. Among law students, those encountering fabricated citations reported higher verification scores (4.2/5) than those without (2.8/5). While 81.6% knew hallucinated cases could lead to contempt of court, 71.1% received no formal training on ethical AI use.

Conclusion: The study proposes shifting toward adversarial legal research pedagogy and implementing source-grounded verification architectures to prevent systemic professional negligence. The findings highlight the need for legal education reform and technical safeguards when deploying LLMs in judicial contexts.

Abstract: Integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) into the Indian judiciary promises access to justice but introduces severe risks. We identify the 'inertia of confidence'--an overconfidence phenomenon analogous to the Dunning-Kruger effect where LLMs provide incorrect legal verdicts with near-maximum confidence, driven by a hypothesized 'precedent overfitting' bias. Phase I of our socio-technical audit tested ChatGPT (GPT-5.2), Meta AI, and Perplexity AI on a 60-case battery regarding the Indian Contract Act, 1872, and the shift toward statutory enforcement of specific performance. We introduce the High-Confidence Error Rate (HCER) to quantify incorrect verdicts delivered with dangerous certainty (>= 9 on a 1-10 scale). All models struggled with statutory updates. Meta AI proved most vulnerable (31.7% HCER), frequently misapplying pre-amendment rules with a 9.1/10 mean confidence, followed by Perplexity (15.0%) and ChatGPT (6.7%).
  Phase II investigated human vulnerability to this overconfidence via a survey of Indian law students (N=380). Verification often functions as a reactive adaptation to machine hallucinations: students encountering fabricated citations reported higher verification scores (4.2/5) than those with no such encounters (2.8/5). Furthermore, while 81.6% knew submitting hallucinated cases can lead to contempt-of-court, 71.1% received no formal training on ethical AI use. We propose shifting toward adversarial legal research pedagogy and implementing source-grounded verification architectures to prevent systemic professional negligence.

</details>


### [157] [When Trust Meets Truth: Trust-Truth Separability in LLM-as-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21097)
*Xin Sun,Di Wu,Yuchen Guo,Jiahuan Pei,Isao Echizen,Abdallah El Ali,Saku Sugawara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether trust scores and binary truth classifications from LLM-as-Judge systems can be treated as independent evaluations. The authors test this assumption using correctness-controlled QA tasks and stress tests that manipulate source attribution (Human vs AI) while keeping content identical.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The assumption that multi-dimensional evaluations (trustworthiness, reliability, factuality) from LLM-as-Judge systems are independent is being challenged. The authors want to test whether trust scores can be used as independent evidence for truth judgments, which has implications for how LLM evaluations are interpreted and used.

Method: The researchers conducted two types of tests: (1) Correctness-controlled QA experiments comparing LLM judge alignment with human behavioral reference, and (2) Stress tests that manipulate only source cues (Human vs AI) while keeping QA content identical, measuring changes in trust scores, truth verdicts, and logit-derived correct-side probabilities.

Result: LLM judges show tighter alignment between trust scores and truth verdicts compared to human behavioral reference, indicating weaker separation between these two judgment types. Importantly, source attribution shifts affect not only trust scores but also truth verdicts and logit-derived probabilities, demonstrating that these evaluations are not independent.

Conclusion: Current LLM-as-Judge protocols should not treat trust scores as independent evidence for truth judgments. The interconnected nature of these evaluations suggests that trust and truth assessments in LLM judges are not clearly separable, which has important implications for the design and interpretation of LLM evaluation systems.

Abstract: LLM-as-Judge systems can produce multi-dimensional evaluations, such as trustworthiness, reliability, and factuality, and these outputs are often interpreted as independent evidence. We test this assumption for a common pair of judgments: trust scoring and binary truth classification. On correctness-controlled QA, LLM judges align trust scores with truth verdicts more tightly than human behavioral reference, suggesting weaker separations between trust and truth judgment. We then apply stress tests by changing only source cues of identical QA between Human and AI. Source attribution shifts not only trust scores but also truth verdicts and logit-derived correct-side probabilities. Results show that current LLM-as-Judge protocols should not treat trust scores as independent evidence for truth judgments.

</details>


### [158] [ReFrame: Evidence-Guided Test-Time Safety Alignment in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21100)
*Wenzheng Jiang,Xuankun Rong,Yuanzhao Zhai,Dawei Feng,Huaimin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReFrame is a training-free multimodal input reframing framework that addresses safety alignment challenges in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by using two agents (evidence-generation and rewrite-and-routing) to construct risk and utility evidence, convert inputs into safe proxy prompts, and make image-routing decisions without modifying the downstream MLLM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal safety alignment methods rely on retraining or internal-state inspection, limiting their applicability to deployed closed-source MLLMs. Two key obstacles identified: utility dominance (model overlooks latent risks) and reasoning inertia (model follows malicious reasoning trajectories).

Method: ReFrame uses two agents sharing a lightweight locally deployed MLLM: (1) evidence-generation agent constructs complementary risk and utility evidence; (2) rewrite-and-routing agent converts evidence into safe proxy prompt and image-routing decision before calling downstream MLLM, without modifying it or accessing its internal information.

Result: Experiments across multiple MLLMs and benchmarks show ReFrame improves jailbreak defense, safety awareness, and oversensitivity reduction while preserving multimodal utility.

Conclusion: ReFrame provides an effective training-free solution for test-time safety alignment of MLLMs, addressing cross-modal jailbreaks, safety-awareness failures, and over-sensitive refusals without requiring model retraining or internal access.

Abstract: While multimodal large language models (MLLMs) extend model capabilities beyond text, they also make safety alignment increasingly challenging. Multimodal safety alignment methods must address cross-modal jailbreaks, safety-awareness failures, and over-sensitive refusals. However, existing methods often rely on retraining or internal-state inspection, limiting their applicability to deployed closed-source MLLMs and motivating test-time safety alignment. We analyze this setting and identify two key obstacles, utility dominance and reasoning inertia, which cause models to overlook latent risks or follow malicious reasoning trajectories. Guided by these insights, we propose ReFrame, a training-free multimodal input reframing framework where two agents share a lightweight locally deployed MLLM: the evidence-generation agent constructs complementary risk and utility evidence, and the rewrite-and-routing agent converts it into a safe proxy prompt and image-routing decision before calling the downstream MLLM, without modifying it or accessing its internal information. Experiments across multiple MLLMs and benchmarks show that ReFrame improves jailbreak defense, safety awareness, and oversensitivity reduction while preserving multimodal utility.

</details>


### [159] [Root cause analysis via difference graph discovery from linear time-series data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21117)
*Anouk Ruer,Timothée Loranchet,Daria Bystrova,Charles K. Assaad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses root cause analysis in linear time-series by using difference graph discovery to identify variables whose causal relationships change between normal and anomalous regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Root cause analysis is crucial for understanding anomalies in complex dynamical systems. Traditional methods struggle to identify effect-defying root causes - variables whose causal mechanisms change between normal and anomalous states. The authors aim to bridge this gap by applying difference graph discovery techniques to time-series data.

Method: The study formalizes the problem using linear discrete-time dynamic structural causal models (DSCMs). Methods originally designed for discovering difference graphs between two populations are adapted to the time-series setting, where the two populations correspond to normal and anomalous regimes. The approaches are evaluated on simulated data and real-world datasets from IT monitoring and intensive care monitoring.

Result: The proposed approaches successfully identify causal mechanisms responsible for anomalous behavior. The method demonstrates practical relevance across different domains, showing how difference graph discovery can effectively localize root causes in complex systems.

Conclusion: Difference graph discovery provides a valuable framework for root cause analysis in linear time-series, effectively identifying effect-defying root causes by detecting changes in causal coefficients between normal and anomalous regimes. This approach has significant applications in monitoring and diagnostic systems across various fields.

Abstract: Root cause analysis aims to identify the mechanisms responsible for anomalies in complex dynamical systems. In this paper, we study root cause analysis in linear time-series through the lens of difference graph discovery. We focus on effect-defying root causes, corresponding to variables whose causal coefficients change between a normal and an anomalous regime. We formalize this problem using linear discrete-time dynamic structural causal models and adapt several methods originally introduced for discovering difference graphs between two populations to the time-series setting, where the two populations are replaced by a normal and an anomalous regime. We first evaluate the proposed approaches on simulated data, and then demonstrate their practical relevance on real-world datasets from IT monitoring and intensive care monitoring. Our results show how difference graph discovery can help localize causal mechanisms responsible for anomalous behavior.

</details>


### [160] [From Attention Masks to Inert Zero-Vector Tokens: OAttention and O-Closure for Token Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21174)
*Heyang Gong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces OAttention, a modified attention mechanism that uses active-presence coefficients (p_i = ||h_i||²/(τ+||h_i||²)) to gate both token emissions and mass contributions. The method achieves exact null-receiver/null-source properties and enables a complete OTransformer architecture with closure properties. Experiments on TabPFN v3 show marginal improvements (+0.088% to +0.177% RMSE) in zero-fine-tuning settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard attention masks provide relation-level controls but lack a representation-carried token state for non-participating tokens at attention boundaries. This work aims to introduce a token-level presence mechanism that can explicitly represent and control token participation in attention computations.

Method: The proposed OAttention modifies standard attention by: (1) gating receiver output with p_i, (2) weighting source j by p_j in both numerator and partition, while retaining standard score, visibility relation, exponential competition, and value aggregation. This creates an OTransformer via residual and compositional closure with local O-components (OFFN, ONorm, OInject).

Result: OAttention achieves exact null-receiver, null-source insertion, self-attention insertion, and empty-support properties. In zero-fine-tuning tests on TabPFN v3, calibrated OAttention and Full-O variants yield +0.088% and +0.177% mean RMSE changes over 18 dataset-seed cases. Ablation shows OAttention alone doesn't preserve NULL state through host components, while OTransformer path does.

Conclusion: OAttention provides a mathematically rigorous attention modification with exact closure properties. The zero-fine-tuning results suggest compatibility with pretrained models, though improvements are marginal. The approach is limited to scoped tests of exactness and active-path compatibility—universal no-loss, arbitrary-host closure, and general missing-value semantics are not established.

Abstract: Attention masks are relation-level controls: they specify which query--source pairs may interact. They do not provide a representation-carried token state that is non-participating at the attention boundary. We assign each token hidden carrier \(h_i\) an active-presence coefficient \(p_i=\lVert h_i\rVert^2/(τ+\lVert h_i\rVert^2)\). The same coefficient has two roles: it gates information emitted by token \(i\), and it determines the mass with which token \(i\) enters computations shared with other tokens.
  OAttention is the support-coupled attention realization of this rule. It gates the receiver output by \(p_i\) and weights source \(j\) by \(p_j\) in both the attention numerator and partition, while retaining the standard score, visibility relation, exponential competition, and value aggregation. This makes the zero-vector token a zero element and yields exact null-receiver, null-source insertion, self-attention insertion, and empty-support properties. The same token-level presence gives local O-components (OFFN, ONorm, and OInject), presence-weighted OStandardize, the O-Closure law \(M(H\oplus0)=M(H)\oplus0\), and an OTransformer by residual and compositional closure.
  The canonical operator is checked by contract tests and a GPU evaluation. In a zero-fine-tuning retrofit of a cloned pretrained TabPFN v3 regressor, calibrated hidden-carrier OAttention and Full-O variants change mean RMSE by $+0.088\%$ and $+0.177\%$, respectively, over 18 matched dataset--seed cases. A two-block ablation shows that OAttention alone does not preserve a NULL state through ordinary host components, whereas the OTransformer path does. These are scoped tests of exactness, active-path compatibility, and compositional necessity; they do not establish universal no-loss, arbitrary-host closure, learned attraction to the origin, or a general semantics for missing values.

</details>


### [161] [SENTRY: Deterministic, Intelligent Risk Assessment for IT Change Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21203)
*Daniel Arulpragasam,Christer Henrysson,Ella Ly,Deepika Anbalagan,Leo Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SENTRY is a machine learning platform that replaces subjective questionnaires with a deterministic XGBoost-based pipeline combined with hybrid retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to assess technology change risk in large financial institutions. The system uses structured operational metadata, application dependency graphs, historical incident records, and semantic/lexical search over historical change requests to detect high-risk changes with 0.87 ROC AUC and 85% accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional questionnaire-based risk assessments in financial institutions are subjective, easily manipulated, and fail to distinguish between routine changes and those that trigger major incidents. There is a need for accurate, consistent, and auditable risk assessments.

Method: SENTRY uses gradient-boosted decision trees (XGBoost) combined with hybrid RAG. It combines structured operational metadata, application dependency graphs, and historical incident records with hybrid semantic and lexical search over historical change requests. The retrieval step compresses unstructured change request text into a single scalar feature before model inference, maintaining determinism and enabling SHAP-based explainability.

Result: SENTRY achieves a ROC AUC of 0.87 and 85% overall accuracy, detecting high-risk changes at approximately 3.25 times the rate of existing questionnaire-based processes.

Conclusion: The hybrid ML approach with deterministic design and SHAP explainability addresses the need for machine learning in regulated change management, offering a scalable alternative to subjective questionnaires while maintaining auditability and interpretability.

Abstract: Technology change management in large financial institutions depends on risk assessments that are accurate, consistent, and auditable. In practice, many institutions still rely on self-reported questionnaires. Those questionnaires are subjective, easy to game, and poor at separating routine changes from the ones that later trigger major incidents. This paper presents SENTRY, a risk assessment platform that replaces questionnaire-based scoring with a deterministic machine learning pipeline built from gradient-boosted decision trees (XGBoost) and hybrid retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). The system combines structured operational metadata, application dependency graphs, and historical incident records with a hybrid semantic and lexical search over historical change requests. The retrieval step captures the risk signal in unstructured change request text, then compresses that signal into a single scalar feature before model inference. That design keeps the model deterministic and preserves per-prediction explainability via SHAP values. Evaluated on enterprise-scale change data, SENTRY achieves a ROC AUC of 0.87 and 85% overall accuracy, and it detects high-risk changes at roughly 3.25 times the rate of the existing process. We close by examining the architectural trade-offs behind this design and what they imply for the use of machine learning in regulated change management.

</details>


### [162] [Enhancing LLMs in Predictive Political QA with Semi-Structured Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21218)
*Yinan Liu,Zihan Zhou,Zichun Jin,Xinyu Wang,Bin Wang,Xiaochun Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PSL is a dual-view framework for predictive political QA that combines stance signals (actor issue-specific preferences) and structure signals (actor interaction dependencies) by converting semi-structured political records into inference-oriented evidence for LLMs, outperforming baselines across three datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM augmentation methods treat external political resources as knowledge-based evidence but under-model prediction-relevant signals. The key insight is that predictive political QA (e.g., predicting votes) requires both actor-specific issue preferences (stances) and indirect dependencies among actors (high-order structure).

Method: PSL extracts evidence in two complementary views: (1) Semantic view - extracts stance signals from question-relevant actor records, capturing issue-specific preferences; (2) Vector view - learns structure-aware actor representations from an actor interaction graph, capturing indirect dependencies. These are converted into inference-oriented evidence for LLMs.

Result: PSL consistently outperforms baselines across three real-world datasets and multiple LLMs. Ablations confirm the complementary gains from both stance and structure signals.

Conclusion: The combination of semantic stance signals and structural dependency signals provides complementary evidence for political prediction tasks. This dual-view approach effectively leverages external political resources beyond simple factual lookup, improving LLMs' ability to reason about political behavior.

Abstract: Predictive political question answering (QA), such as predicting how a political actor will vote, goes beyond factual lookup. External political resources offer rich historical evidence, but rarely contain the answer itself. Existing LLM augmentation methods, including actor-profile-based simulation and knowledge graph evidence injection, improve political reasoning but largely treat external resources as knowledge-based evidence, leaving prediction-relevant signals under-modeled. We identify two complementary signals for predictive political QA: actor stances that capture issue-specific preferences, and high-order structure signals that capture indirect dependencies among political actors. We propose PSL, a dual-view framework that converts semi-structured political records into inference-oriented evidence for LLMs. PSL extracts stance signals from question-relevant actor records in a semantic view, and learns structure-aware actor representations from an actor interaction graph in a vector view. Across three real-world datasets and multiple LLMs, PSL consistently outperforms baselines, with ablations confirming the complementary gains of stance and structure signals.

</details>


### [163] [Ontology-supported AI Model and Dataset Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21224)
*Jan Novacek,Ali Ahari,Tobias Müller,Sebastian Reiter,Alexander Viehl,Oliver Bringmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a platform for AI model exchange and management in industrial contexts, addressing the need to track, trace, and manage AI assets (models and datasets) while enabling discovery of relevant assets. The platform incorporates an ontology to facilitate semantic understanding and effective exchange of AI models without semantic gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses the challenges of managing numerous AI models and related assets generated by different organizations, the need for tracking and tracing these assets, and the importance of discovering relevant assets for specific tasks. The authors aim to enable effective exchange and management of AI models in industrial contexts while fostering transparent comparisons and profound understanding of AI.

Method: The paper introduces a platform for AI model exchange that facilitates the usage, exchange, and analysis of AI models and datasets. The platform incorporates an ontology designed to create a common understanding of requirements for AI model management tasks and help bridge semantic gaps between different organizations.

Result: The platform enables the usage, exchange, and analysis of AI models and datasets. The ontology provides a shared semantic framework that helps tackle issues related to asset tracking, tracing, and discovery, as well as enabling more profound common understanding among stakeholders.

Conclusion: The utility of the platform is demonstrated through a use case in the context of real-time critical systems, showing its practical applicability in industrial settings where reliable AI model management is essential.

Abstract: Recently, there has been a great deal of research into improving AI methods and their application. The main focus is on tracking progress, enabling transparent comparisons, and fostering a more profound understanding of AI. In that process, different organizations generate and use plenty of assets that need to be tracked, traced and managed. Moreover, it is important to discover assets relevant for the task at hand. This paper presents research aiming to contribute to answering the question of what is required to exchange and manage AI models and related assets effectively without semantic gaps in an industrial context. We introduce a platform for AI model exchange, which facilitates the usage, exchange, and analysis of AI models and datasets. The platform incorporates an ontology that can foster a more profound common understanding of what is required in these tasks and help tackle the issues mentioned above. Finally, we elucidate the utility of the platform through the illustration of a use case in the context of real-time critical systems.

</details>


### [164] [Fine-Grain GPU Parallelization of the Generalized Partition Crossover for Large-Scale Traveling Salesman Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21233)
*Swetha Varadarajan,Darrell Whitley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a fine-grain GPU implementation of the partition phase of the Generalized Partition Crossover (GPX) operator for solving large-scale Traveling Salesman Problems (TSP). The approach reformulates GPX partitioning as a graph-parallel problem using coalesced memory layouts, ghost-node transformations, and connected-component analysis, achieving 48x-625x speedups on instances with 10,000 to 2 million cities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) is NP-hard. While Genetic Algorithm (GA)-based solvers like Edge Assembly Crossover (EAX) achieve state-of-the-art performance, their scalability in massively parallel architectures is limited due to irregular memory access patterns, graph traversals, and sequential dependencies. Existing GPU-based TSP solvers primarily exploit population-level parallelism and are limited to relatively small problem sizes. This work aims to improve scalability of GA-based TSP solvers on modern many-core architectures through operator-level parallelism.

Method: The proposed approach reformulates GPX partitioning as a graph-parallel problem using: (1) coalesced memory layouts, (2) ghost-node transformations, and (3) connected-component analysis. The implementation parallelizes four key operations using CUDA: (1) union of parent tours, (2) splitting of degree-four vertices, (3) deletion of common edges, and (4) identification of recombining components.

Result: Experimental results on instances ranging from 10,000 to 2 million cities demonstrate substantial acceleration over a naive sequential CPU implementation. The proposed GPU partitioning achieves speedups between 48x and 625x while significantly reducing memory overhead.

Conclusion: Operator-level parallelism can substantially improve the scalability of GA-based TSP solvers on modern many-core architectures. The results demonstrate that fine-grain GPU implementations of crossover operators are feasible and effective for large-scale TSP instances.

Abstract: The Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) is one of the most extensively studied NP-hard optimization problems. Genetic Algorithm (GA)-based solvers, such as the Edge Assembly Crossover (EAX), achieve state-of-the-art performance on many benchmark instances. However, the scalability of these approaches in massively parallel architectures remains limited because crossover operations involve irregular memory access patterns, graph traversals, and sequential dependencies. Existing GPU-based TSP solvers primarily exploit population-level parallelism and are limited to relatively small problem sizes. This work presents a fine-grain GPU implementation of the partition phase of the Generalized Partition Crossover (GPX) operator for large-scale TSP instances. The proposed approach reformulates GPX partitioning as a graph-parallel problem using coalesced memory layouts, ghost-node transformations, and connected-component analysis. The im- plementation parallelizes the union of parent tours, the splitting of degree- four vertices, the deletion of common edges, and the identification of recombining components using CUDA. Experimental results on instances ranging from 10,000 to 2 million cities demonstrate substantial acceleration over a naive sequential CPU imple- mentation. The proposed GPU partitioning achieves speedups between 48x and 625x while significantly reducing memory overhead. The re- sults demonstrate that operator-level parallelism can substantially im- prove the scalability of GA-based TSP solvers on modern many-core architectures.

</details>


### [165] [CLEAR: Continuous Latent Adapter Routing for Utility-Preserving LLM Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21278)
*Chengxiao Wang,Enyi Jiang,Xiaojing Liao,Sanmi Koyejo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CLEAR proposes a conditional safety adaptation framework using a lightweight hidden-state gate to control the activation strength of a safety low-rank adapter, aiming to reduce harmful outputs while preserving model utility on benign prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improving LLM safety through global safety tuning often degrades model utility on benign inputs, creating a trade-off between safety and usefulness. The authors aim to address this by developing a method that selectively applies safety mechanisms only when needed.

Method: CLEAR (Continuous Latent Adapter Routing) introduces a lightweight hidden-state gate that continuously controls the activation strength of a safety low-rank adapter. This allows the model to dynamically adjust safety mechanisms based on input context without modifying the frozen backbone model.

Result: On Llama-3-8B-Instruct, CLEAR reduced HarmBench Attack Success Rate (ASR) from 32.3% to 0.5%, while retaining most of the base model's utility and achieving up to 7.1 percentage points higher GSM8K accuracy compared to globally applied SFT or standard LoRA safety tuning.

Conclusion: CLEAR demonstrates a promising mechanism for improving the safety-utility trade-off in LLM alignment by selectively applying safety adaptations, achieving robust safety improvements with minimal utility degradation.

Abstract: Improving the safety of large language models (LLMs) often comes at the expense of utility, as globally applied safety tuning may affect model responses to both harmful and benign inputs. We propose \textbf{C}ontinuous \textbf{L}at\textbf{E}nt \textbf{A}dapter \textbf{R}outing (CLEAR), a conditional safety adaptation framework that uses a lightweight hidden-state gate to continuously control the activation strength of a safety low-rank adapter. CLEAR aims to reduce harmful completions while avoiding unnecessary changes to the frozen backbone that could degrade performance on benign prompts. Experiments on widely used safety and utility benchmarks show that CLEAR improves robustness on HarmBench while reducing the utility degradation observed with globally applied safety tuning such as SFT or standard low-rank adaptation (LoRA). On Llama-3-8B-Instruct, CLEAR reduces HarmBench ASR from 32.3\% to 0.5\%, while retaining most of the base model's utility and achieving up to 7.1 percentage points higher GSM8K accuracy than globally applied SFT or LoRA. These results suggest that CLEAR is a promising mechanism for improving the safety--utility trade-off in LLM alignment.

</details>


### [166] [AUSO: Action-Level Unified Skill Optimization from Internalization to Utilization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21292)
*Huizu Lin,Chengkai Huang,Tianqi Gao,Tao Huang,Daijiao Liu,Tongxin Li,Xiaoyan Sun,Lina Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AUSO (Action-level Unified Skill Optimization) unifies skill learning and skill use through a progressive, action-aware optimization process with three stages: initial joint learning from teacher guidance and environmental outcomes, outcome-based policy consolidation, and action-level evaluation that differentiates beneficial from harmful skill-sensitive actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill learning methods fail to model the lifecycle of skills - they should provide learnable knowledge first, then support capability formation, and finally be invoked only when beneficial. Current approaches fragment training and assign uniform importance to actions within trajectories, ignoring that skill guidance may help some decisions while distracting others.

Method: AUSO employs a three-stage progressive approach: (1) Joint learning from teacher guidance and environmental outcomes to acquire foundational skills; (2) Outcome-based policy optimization to consolidate autonomous problem-solving; (3) Action-level evaluation comparing skill-conditioned and skill-free contexts, coupling the resulting signal with trajectory outcome advantage to strengthen beneficial actions and suppress harmful ones.
