Result: Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA demonstrate that AUSO consistently improves agent performance and out-of-distribution generalization over competitive baselines.

Conclusion: Skills gradually transition from an external source of supervision into decision knowledge whose utilization is adapted to its action-level benefit, while reinforcement learning remains the shared backbone across all stages.

Abstract: Skills play different roles as an agent's policy evolves: they should first provide learnable knowledge, then support capability formation, and finally be invoked only when they improve individual decisions. Existing methods rarely model this lifecycle. They either keep skills outside the model, fully internalize them, or select among internalization and utilization objectives through noisy task-level success rates. Such designs fragment training and assign uniform importance to actions within the same trajectory, even though skill guidance may help some decisions while distracting others. To solve these problems, we introduce AUSO (Action-level Unified Skill Optimization), which unifies skill learning and skill use through a progressive, action-aware optimization process. At the beginning of training, AUSO jointly learns from teacher guidance and environmental outcomes, enabling the policy to acquire foundational skills without losing task-oriented feedback. It subsequently emphasizes outcome-based policy optimization to consolidate autonomous problem-solving ability. As the policy matures, AUSO evaluates each sampled action under both skill-conditioned and skill-free contexts. The resulting action-level information signal is coupled with the trajectory outcome advantage, allowing beneficial skill-sensitive actions to receive stronger updates and harmful ones to be suppressed. Therefore, skills gradually transition from an external source of supervision into decision knowledge whose utilization is adapted to its action-level benefit, while reinforcement learning remains the shared backbone across all stages. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA show that AUSO consistently improves agent performance and out-of-distribution generalization over competitive baselines.

</details>


### [167] [From Regulation to Implementation: A Critical Evaluation of LLM-Assisted Regulatory Compliance in Industry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21317)
*Adriana Watson,Marco Bücheler,Grant Richards*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how data extraction instructions and regulatory vagueness affect the quality and consistency of LLM-generated compliance artifacts for EU regulations—specifically Digital Product Passports (DPPs) under ESPR and Data Protection Impact Assessments (DPIAs) under GDPR. Different LLM models are benchmarked against ground-truth schemas, revealing that stricter guidelines produce consistent but potentially hallucinated outputs, while less strict guidelines require more context for quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Creating compliance artifacts (DPPs and DPIAs) is challenging due to heterogeneous industrial data scattered across systems, lack of standardized formats, and required interdisciplinary expertise. While LLMs have been proposed for artifact generation, the impact of data extraction complexity and regulatory vagueness on output quality remains unaddressed.

Method: The study benchmarks different LLM models against manually created ground-truth schemas, evaluating how data extraction instructions and regulatory vagueness impact the quality and consistency of generated compliance artifacts.

Result: Less strict guidelines (DPIA formatting) require higher context prompts to maintain consistency and completeness. Stricter guidelines (Digital Battery Passports) produce consistent results regardless of prompt context, but may lead to more hallucinations in the output.

Conclusion: The research demonstrates the trade-off between prompt context and output quality depends on regulatory specificity. Stricter guidelines yield consistent but potentially hallucinated outputs, while looser guidelines need more context. This provides guidance for practitioners using LLMs in compliance artifact generation.

Abstract: The European Union (EU) has emerged as a leading regulatory body in the development of sustainability and privacy regulations. While new regulation requirements vary, many include a documentation artifact to ensure compliance. Notably, the Ecodesign for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR) introduces Digital Product Passports (DPPs) for life cycle transparency, while the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) mandates Data Protection Impact Assessments (DPIAs) to mitigate privacy risks. Creating these compliance artifacts, however, is challenging. Industrial data, which often exists in heterogeneous formats and is scattered across company and supplier systems, is required for DPPs and can be difficult to extract into compliant DPP formatting. Furthermore, DPIA documents require interdisciplinary expertise and follow no standardized format, making development difficult for novel systems. To address the particular complexity of compliance artifact creation for both regulations, researchers have proposed the use of LLMs in the generation process; however, the impact of the aforementioned problems on the output of these systems is largely unaddressed. This work investigates the existing research gap by exploring how data extraction instructions and regulatory vagueness impact the quality and consistency of LLM-produced compliance artifacts. The resulting artifacts are evaluated by benchmarking different models against manually created ground-truth schemas. The results reveal that less strict guidelines, such as DPIA formatting, require higher context prompts to maintain consistency and completeness. Stricter guidelines, such as formatting for Digital Battery Passports (DBP), result in consistent results regardless of prompt context, but may lead to more hallucinations in the output

</details>


### [168] [Unified Branch-and-Bound Search for the Steiner Traveling Salesman Problem on Graphs of Convex Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21319)
*Jingtao Tang,Hang Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes and solves the Steiner Traveling Salesman Problem on Graphs of Convex Sets (GCS), where the goal is to find minimum-cost closed trajectories through required convex sets with optional transit vertices. The authors propose a branch-and-bound framework with two traversal strategies (best-first and depth-first) certified by a global lower bound, demonstrating effective solution for mobile-manipulator inspection tasks with LTL_f constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the Steiner TSP on Graphs of Convex Sets, which involves finding minimum-cost closed trajectories through required convex sets while allowing optional transit vertices and revisits. This problem arises in applications like inspection tasks where a mobile manipulator must visit target regions. Existing methods fail on many instances, necessitating a new approach that can handle the infinite solution space effectively.

Method: The authors propose a unified branch-and-bound search over rooted walk prefixes. Key techniques include: (1) Additive lower-bound-graph costs to bound committed prefixes; (2) A cut-separated connected-flow relaxation to lower-bound the residual cost of visiting every remaining target and returning to the root. Under a uniform positive-cost assumption, best-first traversal terminates after finitely many expansions on every feasible instance without an initial incumbent, while depth-first traversal terminates once a finite incumbent is available. A global lower bound certifies that either strategy's incumbent cost is at most ε times the global optimum for user-specified ε≥1.

Result: Both traversal strategies found feasible solutions on all benchmark instances within 30 seconds, with mean certified optimality gaps of 28.1% (best-first) and 29.7% (depth-first). In contrast, two recent baselines succeeded on only about half of the instances. The method also demonstrates joint sensing-mode, visitation-order, and continuous-trajectory selection for a mobile-manipulator inspection task with LTL_f action precedence constraints.

Conclusion: The proposed branch-and-bound framework effectively solves the Steiner-TSP on GCS, providing guaranteed ε-approximate solutions. Both best-first and depth-first traversal strategies demonstrate robust performance across benchmark instances, significantly outperforming recent baselines. The approach successfully handles complex inspection tasks with temporal logic constraints, showing promise for practical applications in robotics and automated inspection.

Abstract: We formalize the Steiner Traveling Salesman Problem (Steiner-TSP) on Graphs of Convex Sets (GCS), which seeks a minimum-cost closed trajectory through required convex sets while allowing optional transit vertices and revisits. To explore the resulting infinite solution space, we propose a unified branch-and-bound search over rooted walk prefixes. Additive lower-bound-graph costs bound committed prefixes, while a cut-separated connected-flow relaxation lower-bounds the residual cost of visiting every remaining target and returning to the root. Under a uniform positive-cost assumption, best-first traversal terminates after finitely many expansions on every feasible instance without an initial incumbent, whereas depth-first traversal does so once a finite incumbent is available. For a user-specified factor $ε\geq1$, a global lower bound certifies that either strategy's incumbent cost is at most $ε$ times the global optimum. We further demonstrate joint sensing-mode, visitation-order, and continuous-trajectory selection for a mobile-manipulator inspection task, including action precedences expressed in linear temporal logic over finite traces (LTL$_f$). Both traversal strategies find feasible solutions on all benchmark instances within 30s with mean certified optimality gaps of 28.1% and 29.7%, respectively, whereas two recent baselines succeed on only about half of the instances

</details>


### [169] [Anatomy-Informed Neural Networks: Encoding Anatomic Priors in Loss and Architecture, with an SE(3) Formulation of Guidewire-Induced Aortoiliac Deformation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21332)
*David P. Stonko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Anatomy-Informed Neural Networks (AINN) that incorporate soft anatomic priors as penalty terms in the loss function and hard anatomic priors built into the architecture, to predict deformations of the aortoiliac tree when a stiff wire is introduced endoluminally. Uses SE(3) frame representation, Cosserat-rod mechanics, and Wasserstein-2 optimal transport loss for 2D-to-3D training from angiograms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep-learning models of anatomy can be numerically plausible but anatomically impossible, and they generalize poorly when data are scarce. The clinical application is predicting how the aortoiliac tree deforms during endoluminal wire introduction, which is important for aortic surgery and autonomous endovascular navigation.

Method: 1) Soft anatomic priors enter as penalty terms in the loss (e.g., branching penalties); 2) Hard anatomic priors (e.g., vessel continuity) built into the architecture and state representation; 3) Lift vessel centerline and wire path from R^3 to curves of frames in SE(3); 4) Couple Cosserat-rod wire to tortuosity-modulated vessel through unilateral lumen-contact inequality; 5) Prediction is constrained minimizer of coupled elastic energy with contact forces as Lagrange multipliers; 6) Supervision via Wasserstein-2 optimal-transport loss between predicted C-arm projection and observed angiogram.

Result: Kinematics, loss, and projection verified against known ground truth; mechanics solver verified against its own optimality conditions. Predicted displacement is not yet mesh-converged. Currently, no network is trained - this is a mechanics-based framework verification.

Conclusion: The AINN framework successfully integrates anatomical priors into neural network predictions. Future work will transfer this in silico model to real CT scans and test whether it improves predictive accuracy and reduces training data requirements. The approach has potential for autonomous endovascular navigation and reducing data dependency in medical imaging.

Abstract: Deep-learning models of anatomy can be numerically plausible yet anatomically impossible, and they generalize poorly when data are scarce. We introduce Anatomy-Informed Neural Networks (AINN), in which soft anatomic priors enter as penalty terms in the loss (e.g., a branching penalty that treats a renal transplant artery off the iliac instead of the aorta as unexpected rather than impossible), in direct analogy to a physics-informed neural network, and hard anatomic priors (e.g., continuity of the vessel) are built into the architecture and state representation, making such invalid predictions impossible by construction wherever the prior admits architectural enforcement.
  We develop it on a clinical test case with limited data: how the aortoiliac tree deforms when a stiff wire is introduced endoluminally. This is important to contemporary aortic surgery and will matter to autonomous endovascular navigation. We lift the vessel centerline and the wire path from R^3 to curves of frames in the Lie group SE(3), and couple a Cosserat-rod wire to a tortuosity-modulated, anatomically anchored vessel through a unilateral lumen-contact inequality. The prediction is a constrained minimizer of the coupled elastic energy, with contact forces as its Lagrange multipliers. Supervision is a Wasserstein-2 optimal-transport loss between the predicted projection through the C-arm geometry and the observed angiogram, so a 2D angiogram can train a 3D prediction. The kinematics, loss and projection are verified against known ground truth; the mechanics solver only against its own optimality conditions, and predicted displacement is not yet mesh-converged. Here, no network is trained. Future work will transfer this in silico model to real CT scans and test whether it improves predictive accuracy and reduces the training data required.

</details>


### [170] [VIALS: A Benchmark for Visual Interpretation of Artifacts in the Life Sciences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21357)
*Elaine Lau,Thanuka Udumulla,Lee Izhaki-Tavor,Francisco Guzmán,Nicholas Magazine,Jonas Mueller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VIALS is a visual question-answering benchmark with 161 tasks designed to evaluate AI models' ability to interpret scientific visual artifacts (gel blots, microscopy images, plasmid maps, flow cytometry plots, molecular structures) encountered in professional biotech workflows. The study reveals that current frontier vision-language models fail at these tasks despite succeeding on natural images, while domain experts find them straightforward.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current vision-language models excel at natural image description but lack domain-specific knowledge and visual reasoning capabilities needed for professional life sciences workflows, where scientific artifacts are central to scientific reasoning and decision-making.

Method: The researchers created a benchmark with 161 visual interpretation tasks spanning various types of scientific artifacts examined throughout experimental workflows in the biotech industry, then evaluated both frontier vision-language models and human domain experts on these tasks.

Result: Frontier vision-language models are unable to accurately interpret scientific images, reflecting limitations in domain knowledge and domain-specific visual reasoning capabilities. In contrast, scientists with relevant domain expertise find these visual interpretation tasks straightforward.

Conclusion: AI models that cannot interpret scientific visual artifacts will have limited utility in professional life sciences workflows. This benchmark highlights the need for AI systems with enhanced domain-specific visual reasoning capabilities to be useful in biotech industry settings.

Abstract: In professional life sciences workflows, scientists routinely interpret visual artifacts (gel blots, microscopy images, plasmid maps, flow cytometry plots, molecular structures, ...) to inform research decisions. We introduce VIALS, a visual question-answering benchmark with 161 such interpretation tasks, spanning the types of artifacts examined throughout experimental workflows in the biotech industry (rather than polished figures from publications and textbooks). While frontier vision-language models can now fluently describe natural images, we find that they are unable to accurately interpret these scientific images, reflecting limitations in domain knowledge and domain-specific visual reasoning capabilities. In contrast, scientists with relevant domain expertise find these visual interpretation tasks straightforward. AI that cannot similarly interpret such images will have limited utility in professional life sciences workflows, where such artifacts are central to how scientists reason, communicate, and make decisions.

</details>


<div id='cs.MM'></div>

# cs.MM [[Back]](#toc)

### [171] [When Generated Images Look Right and Retrieve Wrong: Coverage-Guided Cross-Scale Re-Indexing for Knowledge-Faithful Generative Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20810)
*Guangyuan Dong,Chuang Liu,Yangchen Zeng,Haoyu Wang,Xiaoyang Yu,Pinlong Zhao,Yuchao Hou,Ziwei Li,Zheng Lin*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: CERES addresses 'semantic collapse' in multimodal image generation where scale-specific concepts are lost when using global text embeddings, causing retrieval failures despite high pixel fidelity. The framework uses a three-level semantic pyramid, scale-routed cross-attention, and closed-loop verification to preserve queryable content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal systems route generated images back through the same vision-language index used for generation. When scenes contain entities at vastly different scales, existing generators use a single global text embedding that drops scale-specific concepts, breaking concept-query retrieval even with high pixel quality.

Method: CERES builds a three-level semantic pyramid, uses a co-occurrence-aware router to mine implicit concepts, performs scale-routed cross-attention into a lightweight U-Net generator (0.39M parameters), and verifies coverage by re-indexing with the same frozen VLM. A differentiable soft-Jaccard coverage objective provides dense gradients under non-degeneracy conditions, verified by an independent DINOv2 linear probe trained on external labels.

Result: CERES achieves new state-of-the-art on four pansharpening benchmarks across seven settings, with largest gains where scale variation is most extreme. It improves concept-query retrieval Recall@5 by +14.0 points and image-text mean reciprocal rank by +0.19 over the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: The closed-loop framework preserves queryable content rather than self-referential feature consistency, successfully addressing semantic collapse in scale-variant multimodal generation.

Abstract: Multimodal information systems increasingly route generated visual content back through the same vision-language index that informed its production, so the output must remain retrievable by the queries it was meant to serve. When the scene contains entities at vastly different scales, existing language-guided generators condition on a single, globally pooled text embedding and quietly drop scale-specific concepts, breaking concept-query retrieval even when pixel fidelity is high. We formalise this failure as semantic collapse and propose CERES, a closed-loop multimodal indexing framework that builds a three-level semantic pyramid, mines implicit concepts via a co-occurrence-aware router, performs scale-routed cross-attention into a lightweight U-Net generator, and verifies coverage by re-indexing the generated image with the same frozen VLM. A continuously differentiable soft-Jaccard coverage objective returns dense gradients to the 0.39M-parameter generator under explicit non-degeneracy conditions, and coverage is verified by an independent DINOv2 linear probe trained only on external scene and object labels. On four pansharpening benchmarks across seven settings, CERES delivers the new state of the art with the largest gains where scale variation is most extreme. It also improves concept-query retrieval Recall@5 by +14.0 points and image-text mean reciprocal rank by 0.19 over the strongest baseline, showing that the closed loop preserves queryable content rather than self-referential feature consistency.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [172] [Bankruptcy Prediction via Hybrid Resampling and Stacking Ensemble Techniques with Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI)-Driven Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20343)
*Obu-Amoah Ampomah,Edmund Fosu Agyemang,Kofi Acheampong,Louis Agyekum,Enock Adu Bonsu,Eric Nyarko*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a bankruptcy prediction framework combining consensus feature selection, hybrid resampling techniques, stacking ensembles, and SHAP explainability to address class imbalance in the Taiwanese Bankruptcy dataset. The GRU model with SMOTE-ENN achieved the best standalone performance (recall=0.8627, G-mean=0.8517, ROC-AUC=0.9431), while stacking ensembles provided strong sensitivity-specificity balance. Key predictors identified include leverage, profitability, solvency, and operational efficiency indicators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bankruptcy prediction faces severe class imbalance (minority bankrupt firms vs. majority healthy firms), which leads to poor minority-class detection. Traditional models often favor the majority class, reducing their utility for early warning systems. This study aims to improve minority-class detection while maintaining interpretability for practical financial decision-making.

Method: The framework employs: (1) Consensus feature selection using 5 algorithms with retention rule reducing to 23 variables; (2) Three hybrid resampling methods (SVM-SMOTE, SMOTE-Tomek, SMOTE-ENN) to balance training data; (3) Comparison of 5 ML classifiers (GB, XGB, HGB, LightGBM, AdaBoost) and 5 deep learning models (RNN, LSTM, GRU, DNN, MLP); (4) Hybrid stacking ensembles combining ML base learners with DL meta-learners; (5) SHAP for model interpretability.

Result: Resampling strategy significantly impacted model behavior. SVM-SMOTE and SMOTE-Tomek favored accuracy/specificity, while SMOTE-ENN excelled at minority-class detection. Best standalone: GRU+SMOTE-ENN (recall=0.8627, G-mean=0.8517, ROC-AUC=0.9431). Best stacking: SMOTE-ENN with (GB+XGB+HGB+LGBM+AB)+LSTM. Top predictors: leverage, profitability, solvency, and operational efficiency indicators.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully addresses class imbalance in bankruptcy prediction. GRU with SMOTE-ENN provides the best predictive balance for minority-class detection. Stacking ensembles offer complementary performance. SHAP analysis ensures interpretability, supporting reliable and explainable early warning systems for financially distressed firms. Future work could explore additional resampling strategies and ensemble configurations.

Abstract: This study develops and evaluates a bankruptcy prediction framework that integrates consensus-based feature selection, hybrid resampling, stacking ensembles, and explainable artificial intelligence to improve minority-class detection in severely imbalanced financial data. Using the Taiwanese Bankruptcy Prediction dataset from the UCI Machine Learning Repository, five feature-selection algorithms were first applied, and a consensus retention rule reduced the input space to 23 robust variables. The balanced training data were then generated using SVM-SMOTE, SMOTE-Tomek, and SMOTE-ENN. Five ensemble machine learning classifiers, namely gradient boosting, extreme gradient boosting, histogram-based gradient boosting, LightGBM, and AdaBoost, were compared with five deep learning models, including RNN, LSTM, GRU, DNN, and MLP. In addition, hybrid stacking ensembles combined the five machine learning classifiers as base learners with each deep learning model as a meta-learner. Model performance was assessed using accuracy, recall, specificity, G-mean, and ROC-AUC, while SHAP was used to explain feature contributions. The results show that resampling strategy materially shaped model behavior. SVM-SMOTE and SMOTE-Tomek favored accuracy and specificity, whereas SMOTE-ENN delivered stronger minority-class detection. Among standalone models, the GRU with SMOTE-ENN achieved the best overall predictive balance, with recall of 0.8627, G-mean of 0.8517, and ROC-AUC of 0.9431. Among stacking ensembles, SMOTE-ENN with (GB+XGB+HGB+LGBM+AB)+LSTM provided the strongest compromise between sensitivity and specificity. SHAP analysis identified leverage, profitability, solvency, and operational efficiency indicators as the most influential predictors of bankruptcy risk. These findings support more reliable and interpretable early warning systems for financially distressed firms.

</details>


### [173] [Machine Learning and ARIMA Model Averaging for Adaptive Public Health Forecasting: Comparative Evaluation and an Ontario COVID-19 Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20406)
*Yushu Zou,Ye Li,Johra Moosa,Martin Grunnill,Samir N. Patel,Venkata R. Duvvuri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares ARIMA, random forest, XGBoost, and a novel ensemble method (MLAMA) for forecasting Ontario COVID-19 weekly case counts, finding that MLAMA achieves the lowest normalized MAPE across most forecast horizons while supporting the idea that model selection should depend on operating conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public health forecasts must respond to abrupt changes in surveillance data without over-extrapolating noise, reporting artifacts, or temporary trends, necessitating systematic comparison of forecasting methods under different operating conditions.

Method: The study used 190 weekly observations of Ontario COVID-19 case counts from January 2020 to October 2023, evaluating ARIMA, random forest, and XGBoost models with rolling-origin time-series cross-validation. Performance was assessed across three dimensions: responsiveness following turning points, forecast horizons of 1-6 weeks, and amount of historical training data. A novel ensemble method called MLAMA (Machine Learning and ARIMA Model Averaging) was developed as a non-negative performance-weighted ensemble with horizon and responsiveness-dependent weights.

Result: ARIMA adapted rapidly after turning points but its normalized error increased at longer horizons. Random forest and XGBoost were less responsive initially but maintained more stable normalized error over longer horizons. For two-week forecasts at the end of the study period, training on the most recent data outperformed using longer historical periods, particularly for XGBoost. MLAMA achieved the lowest normalized mean absolute percentage error across most forecast horizons and ranked among the best-performing methods across responsiveness settings.

Conclusion: The findings support selecting forecasting models according to operating conditions rather than relying on a single universally preferred approach. MLAMA provides a practical framework for combining complementary statistical and machine-learning forecasts.

Abstract: Public health forecasts must respond to abrupt changes in surveillance data without over-extrapolating noise, reporting artifacts, or temporary trends. We evaluated autoregressive integrated moving average (ARIMA), random forest, and extreme gradient boosting (XGBoost) models using 190 weekly observations of publicly available Ontario COVID-19 case counts from January 2020 to October 2023. Rolling-origin time-series cross-validation preserved temporal order during model tuning and evaluation. Performance was assessed across three operating dimensions: responsiveness following selected turning points, forecast horizons of one to six weeks, and the amount of historical training data. We also developed Machine Learning and ARIMA Model Averaging (MLAMA), a non-negative performance-weighted ensemble with weights that vary by forecast horizon and responsiveness setting. Retrospective comparisons showed that ARIMA adapted rapidly after turning points but its normalized error increased at longer horizons. Random forest and XGBoost were less responsive initially but maintained more stable normalized error over longer horizons. For two-week forecasts at the end of the study period, training on the most recent data outperformed using longer historical periods, particularly for XGBoost. MLAMA achieved the lowest normalized mean absolute percentage error across most forecast horizons and ranked among the best-performing methods across responsiveness settings. These findings support selecting forecasting models according to operating conditions rather than relying on a single universally preferred approach. MLAMA provides a practical framework for combining complementary statistical and machine-learning forecasts. The accompanying Python package is currently maintained in a private repository while software validation and reproducibility testing are completed.

</details>


### [174] [From Thermal Preference Prediction to Adaptive Thermal Intervention: A Reinforcement Learning Approach Using Physiological and Environmental Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20423)
*Isibor Kennedy Ihianle,Emmanuel Manu,Ehsan Asnaashari,Mojgan Jadidi,Pedro Machado,Amrit Sagoo,Ahmad Lotfi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a two-stage personalized thermal comfort approach that uses multimodal physiological and environmental sensing combined with reinforcement learning to address limitations of conventional HVAC systems that rely on static setpoints and population-level models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional HVAC systems use static setpoints and population-level comfort models, which fail to capture individual physiological variability, leading to suboptimal comfort and inefficient building control.

Method: The proposed approach integrates two key components: (1) multimodal physiological and environmental sensing to capture individual-level data, and (2) reinforcement learning-based decision-making to personalize thermal comfort control.

Result: Not specified in the abstract - the paper appears to present the approach rather than report specific results.

Conclusion: Personalised thermal comfort is essential for occupant wellbeing and more responsive building-control strategies; the proposed two-stage approach integrating multimodal sensing with reinforcement learning offers a promising solution.

Abstract: Personalised thermal comfort is essential for occupant wellbeing and for the development of more responsive building-control strategies, yet conventional Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning (HVAC) systems rely on static setpoints and population-level comfort models that fail to capture individual physiological variability. This paper presents a two-stage personalised thermal comfort approach integrating multimodal physiological and environmental sensing with reinforcement learning-based decision-making.

</details>


### [175] [BF1: A Causal Dyadic Sparse-Attention Retrofit for Efficient Long-Context Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20427)
*Hina Dixit*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BF1 is a deterministic block-aligned sparse attention mechanism that achieves 10.91x speedup at 32K tokens while maintaining model quality, validated through comprehensive evaluation including numerical correctness, kernel performance, and language modeling tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense causal attention remains computationally expensive at long context even with optimized kernels, necessitating efficient sparse attention alternatives that maintain accuracy while reducing latency.

Method: BF1 combines a small exact local neighborhood, a global first block, and logarithmically spaced historical blocks. It uses O(n log n) selected token interactions with O(log n) graph communication depth. The approach includes a correctness-gated pretrained-model retrofit method and topology-control study.

Result: On NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU, BF16 implementation achieves 10.91x per-layer prefill speedup at 32K tokens, crossing dense attention between 2K-4K tokens. Retrofitting 8 of 28 Qwen3-0.6B attention layers reduces TTFT by 7.7%, 11.3%, and 15.3% at 8K, 16K, and 32K respectively. In matched 1,000-step, 16.384M-token adaptation, BF1 achieves best perplexity of 1.68639 vs 1.69154 (static-random), 1.69258 (dense-CT), and 1.81505 (local sliding).

Conclusion: BF1 establishes itself as a reproducible sparse operator and selective retrofit primitive with real long-context systems value, achieving significant speedups while maintaining or improving model quality across various evaluation dimensions.

Abstract: Dense causal attention remains expensive at long context even when implemented with highly optimized exact kernels. We study BF1, a deterministic block-aligned dyadic sparse-attention route that combines a small exact local neighborhood, a global first block, and logarithmically spaced historical blocks. The route is related to prior log-sparse and dilated attention patterns; our contribution is a correctness-gated pretrained-model retrofit, a matched topology-control study, and a systems characterization that connects per-layer sparsity to whole-model latency. For fixed block width, every converted layer uses O(n log n) selected token interactions and has O(log n) graph communication depth. On an NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU, an optimized BF16 implementation crosses dense attention between 2K and 4K tokens and reaches a 10.91x per-layer prefill speedup at 32K. Retrofitting eight of 28 Qwen3-0.6B attention layers lowers warm whole-model time to first token by 7.7%, 11.3%, and 15.3% at 8K, 16K, and 32K, respectively, while the remaining dense layers keep the complete model asymptotically quadratic. Under a matched 1,000-step, 16.384M-token adaptation protocol, BF1 ranks first across three training seeds: mean report perplexity is 1.68639 versus 1.69154 for a matched static-random nonlocal graph, 1.69258 for dense continued training, and 1.81505 for equal-budget local sliding. At seed 1234, the packed-report paired interval places Dense-CT 0.3169-0.4055% above BF1 and static-random graph 17 0.2441-0.3642% above BF1. These results establish BF1 as a reproducible sparse operator and selective retrofit primitive with real long-context systems value. This paper evaluates numerical correctness, selected-interaction scaling, kernel performance, partial-model inference, and matched next-token language modeling.

</details>


### [176] [Approximate Homomorphisms and Convergent Representations in Transducers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20428)
*Santiago Cifuentes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies the stability of minimal representations of controlled stochastic processes (transducers) under perturbations, motivated by predictive-state structure in neural network latent representations. It introduces approximate homomorphisms and interfaces to compare structural similarity between transducers, proving stability results for linear and predictive transducers while showing when convergence fails without structural restrictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research is motivated by recent experiments finding predictive-state structure in the latent representations of neural networks. The goal is to provide theoretical support for the hypothesis that latent representations exhibit structural convergence when embedded in hidden layers of AI models.

Method: The paper introduces: (1) notions of approximate homomorphism capturing local structural similarity between transducers, (2) metrics comparing induced dynamics (interfaces), and (3) proves properties such as composability of approximate homomorphisms. It analyzes standard, linear, and predictive transducers.

Result: For standard transducers: there exist simple interfaces with no approximate homomorphism between different implementations. For linear transducers: every finite-rank interface has stable minimal implementations—all minimal linear transducers implementing interfaces close to I have approximate homomorphisms to the minimal implementation of I, with error linear in perturbation size. For predictive transducers: analogous stability result under residual metric with mild hypotheses on belief state indistinguishability.

Conclusion: Canonical transducer representations are robust to perturbations under certain conditions, but convergence fails without additional structural restrictions. This provides theoretical support for the hypothesis that latent representations in AI models exhibit structural convergence.

Abstract: We study the stability of minimal representations of controlled stochastic processes (in particular, transducers) under perturbations. This question is motivated by recent experiments finding predictive-state structure in the latent representations of neural networks. We consider standard, linear and predictive transducers. We introduce notions of approximate homomorphism capturing local structural similarity between them, together with metrics comparing their induced dynamics (which we refer to as interfaces), and prove properties such as composability of the approximate homomorphisms. For standard transducers, we show that there exist simple interfaces for which there is no approximate homomorphism between the different implementations of the dynamics. In contrast, for every finite-rank interface $\mathcal I$, we prove that all minimal linear transducers implementing interfaces sufficiently close to $\mathcal I$ have an approximate homomorphism to the minimal implementation of $\mathcal I$, with error linear in the perturbation size. We prove an analogous stability result for predictive transducers under a residual metric using some mild hypothesis regarding the indistinguishability of the belief states. These results identify conditions under which canonical transducer representations are robust to perturbations, while showing that such convergence fails without additional structural restrictions. Under the assumption that these type of abstractions are embedded into the hidden layers of modern AI models, this gives some theoretical support to the hypothesis that their latent representations exhibit structural convergence.

</details>


### [177] [Wrong-Physics Backdoors in Neural PDE Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20439)
*Hanbing Liang,Fujun Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces "cross-parameter relinking," a data-poisoning attack that injects backdoors into neural PDE operators trained on reusable solver archives. The attack causes the model to produce physically plausible but incorrect solutions when triggered by specific inputs, by exploiting failures in tracking the provenance of physical parameters in multi-parameter archives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural PDE operators are increasingly trained on reusable solver archives, but current validation methods only check clean prediction error and general physical plausibility. The authors identify a structural validation gap where smoothness or solver-like behavior is insufficient unless the provenance of the intended physical parameter is verified.

Method: The attack exploits "tensor-to-parameter provenance failures" by stamping surrogate inputs and relinking their supervision to cached alternate-parameter solutions for the same latent sample. This creates a "wrong-physics backdoor" where outputs remain physically plausible but correspond to incorrect physical parameters. The authors conducted 476 attack campaigns across multiple PDEs (Burgers, advection-diffusion, 2D Navier-Stokes, elliptic Poisson) and tested multiple architectures (FNO, DeepONet as primary; Transformer, GRU, LSTM as support).

Result: FNO achieved a backdoor success rate of 1.0000 on both advection-diffusion and 2D Navier-Stokes while maintaining low clean relative L2 error. Clean-label, label-only, and shuffled controls demonstrated that high attack success requires moving predictions toward the intended alternate-physics target while preserving bounded clean error.

Conclusion: The results expose a structural validation gap in neural PDE operators: standard plausibility checks (smoothness, generic solver-like behavior) are insufficient unless the provenance of the intended physical parameter is also verified. This represents a new attack surface in scientific machine learning where physically plausible outputs can mask incorrect physics.

Abstract: Neural PDE operators are increasingly trained on reusable solver archives, yet validation often relies on clean prediction error and parameter-agnostic plausibility checks. We introduce cross-parameter relinking, a data-poisoning primitive that makes a triggered input select a valid solution from the same PDE family under an incorrect physical parameter. We term this a wrong-physics backdoor: the output remains physically plausible but is wrong for the intended parameter. The attack exploits tensor-to-parameter provenance failures in multi-parameter archives by stamping the surrogate input and relinking its supervision to a cached alternate-parameter solution for the same latent sample. Across 476 attack campaigns, we evaluate Burgers, advection-diffusion, two-dimensional Navier-Stokes, and an elliptic Poisson case. Fourier Neural Operators and DeepONet provide the primary evidence, with Transformer, GRU, and LSTM models as support. FNO reaches a backdoor success rate of 1.0000 on both advection-diffusion and two-dimensional Navier-Stokes while retaining low clean relative L2 error. Clean-label, label-only, and shuffled controls show that high attack success alone is insufficient: successful attacks must move predictions toward the intended alternate-physics target while preserving bounded clean error. These results expose a structural validation gap: smoothness or generic solver-like behavior is insufficient unless the provenance of the intended physical parameter is also verified.

</details>


### [178] [Decision Tree and K-Means Analysis of Raman Spectra for Edible Oils: A Physics-Informed AI Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20440)
*Amrita Shaw,Chandrasekar S. N.,Sai Muthukumar V.,Jhinuk Gupta,Deepak L. N. Kallepalli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Authentication of edible oils in processed foods is important for food quality, fraud prevention, and regulatory compliance. This study establishes an integrated Raman spectroscopy and machine-learning framework that links intrinsic spectral organization, interpretable classification, and Physics-Informed Artificial Intelligence (PI-AI). Five edible oils were investigated in pure form and within a fried-potato-chip matrix using t-SNE, K-means clustering, Decision Trees, and Non-Negative Least Squares (NNLS)-based spectral decomposition. Unsupervised analyses revealed substantially stronger class organization and separability in pure oils, whereas food-matrix effects introduced pronounced spectral overlap. Decision Trees achieved 100% classification accuracy for pure oils using only four Raman variables from the original 1866-feature spectral space. These four variables, consistently identified by both pre-pruned and post-pruned models, represented only approximately 0.21% of the available spectral information while retaining perfect test-set performance. For matrix-containing samples, NNLS-based PI-AI spectral decomposition substantially improved classification by separating oil-related signatures from paper and potato contributions. Optimized post-pruned models achieved accuracies of 86.4% and 85.4% for paper-subtracted and paper-plus-potato-subtracted datasets, respectively, while reducing the number of important Raman variables to only five and four. The compact four-feature representation further reduced the data footprint by 99.44% without loss of classification accuracy. Collectively, these findings demonstrate that accurate Raman-based oil identification can be achieved through physically meaningful, highly compact, and interpretable spectral representations, providing a promising foundation for Frugal AI, Edge AI, portable sensing, and embedded food-quality monitoring.

</details>


### [179] [Shared Physics Responses Recover Hidden Rankings in Neural Operator Libraries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20441)
*Hanbing Liang,Fujun Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method for selecting optimal neural operator predictions during deployment without requiring high-fidelity reference solutions, using a shared physical diagnostic based on anchor-based linearized response of the governing equation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The challenge of selecting the best neural operator model when ground-truth solutions are unavailable during deployment, which is critical for reliable scientific computing.

Method: Using squared Hilbert-space loss where ranking depends on low-dimensional span of candidate differences. A single anchor-based linearized response of the governing equation serves as a shared physical diagnostic to score all models simultaneously.

Result: Achieved 99.6% recovery of pairwise preferences and 99.0% recovery of optimal checkpoints across diverse Fourier and convolutional operator libraries for fluid, reaction-diffusion, and wave dynamics. The corrected physical proxy frequently outperformed the best individual candidates.

Conclusion: The framework enables reliable and highly efficient deployment of scientific surrogates without requiring ground-truth data by exploiting local dynamical response rather than raw defect magnitude. Computable sufficient conditions can rigorously certify exact decisions for strongly monotone discretizations.

Abstract: Selecting the optimal neural-operator prediction during deployment is challenging when high-fidelity reference solutions are unavailable. We demonstrate that under a squared Hilbert-space loss, ranking a finite model library depends strictly on the low-dimensional span of candidate differences, allowing us to score all models simultaneously using a single anchor-based linearized response of the governing equation. This shared physical diagnostic accurately recovered over 99.6\% of pairwise preferences and 99.0\% of optimal checkpoints across diverse Fourier and convolutional operator libraries for fluid, reaction-diffusion, and wave dynamics. Furthermore, the corrected physical proxy frequently outperformed the best individual candidates, and we establish computable sufficient conditions that rigorously certify exact decisions for strongly monotone discretizations. By exploiting the local dynamical response rather than raw defect magnitude, this framework enables the reliable and highly efficient deployment of scientific surrogates without requiring ground-truth data.

</details>


### [180] [Stored in Optimizer State, Valued by Later Training: A Causal Account of Subliminal Trait Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20442)
*Qinyang Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper discovers a two-stage mechanism for subliminal trait transfer in neural networks: optimizer state (specifically first moment/momentum) carries source perturbations during training, while later training phases determine their final behavioral value. This explains how traits survive source removal and can change sign during continued training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work explained how signals enter gradients during subliminal trait transfer, but not how they survive source removal or acquire different signs under later training. The authors sought to understand the causal mechanism behind this phenomenon.

Method: The authors treat parameters and optimizer moments as a unified trainer state, deriving an exact transport-valuation identity. They perform "state surgery" experiments transplanting different components (first moment, parameters) between models and analyze the resulting behavioral differences across various model architectures (Qwen, SmolLM2, Llama) and training configurations (AdamW, momentum SGD).

Result: The first moment acts as a causal carrier - transplanting it alone leaves immediate parameters/hidden states unchanged but generates growing differences in subsequent training. Combining the first moment with parameters recovers terminal behavior. Different continuation paths produce contrasting signs (-0.658, +0.008, +0.658) that recur consistently across 12 Llama-3.2-1B seeds and 42 Qwen route-means. The full-horizon costate successfully predicts all experimental outcomes.

Conclusion: Subliminal trait transfer operates through a two-stage mechanism: (1) optimizer state transports source perturbations independently of the original signal, and (2) later training determines the behavioral value assigned to these perturbations. This mechanism generalizes across diverse architectures (transformers, CNNs) and optimizers (AdamW, momentum SGD), providing a unified framework for understanding how behavioral traits propagate and transform during neural network training.

Abstract: Subliminal trait transfer allows a student model to acquire behavioral dispositions from teacher-generated data in which the trait is not semantically expressed. Recent work explains how such signals enter gradients, but not how they survive source removal or acquire different signs under later training. We treat parameters and optimizer moments as a single trainer state and derive an exact transport-valuation identity separating observer-independent propagation of the source perturbation from the value assigned by a future continuation and behavioral readout. State surgery identifies the first moment as a causal carrier. Transplanting it alone leaves parameters, hidden states, and outputs unchanged at the cut, yet source-free updates generate growing parameter and hidden-state differences; transplanting parameters with the first moment recovers the terminal behavioral response. Sending the same source-induced difference through matched futures produces negative, near-zero, and positive Qwen effects (-0.658, +0.008, and +0.658 seed means). This ordering recurs in all 12 Llama-3.2-1B seeds after eight updates, while state-difference norms remain nearly equal across routes. Both contrasts grow in every paired seed when the continuation extends to sixteen updates. A full-horizon costate predicts all 42 Qwen route-mean signs and all 21 resolved Llama ordinary-route signs. Observer-independent transport also replicates across Qwen, SmolLM2, and Llama, while the complete-state recurrence predicts physical, hidden, and fixed-head responses in non-LoRA MNIST systems, including CNNs trained with AdamW and momentum SGD. Together, these results identify a two-stage mechanism for subliminal trait transfer: optimizer state transports the source perturbation, and later training determines its behavioral value.

</details>


### [181] [Amortized Bandwidth Learning for Kernel Density Estimation under Logarithmic Score](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20445)
*Junyi Liang,Hailiang Du*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes an amortized learning framework for bandwidth selection in kernel density estimation, where a neural network learns to predict optimal bandwidths from samples by training on diverse density estimation tasks using finite Gaussian mixtures, outperforming classical selectors like Silverman's rule and Sheather-Jones method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical bandwidth selectors for KDE either use analytical rules (which may be inaccurate), asymptotic approximations (which may not hold for finite samples), or require solving new optimization problems for each sample. This creates inefficiency and inconsistent performance across different sample sizes and density structures.

Method: The proposed amortized framework trains a selector network to learn the mapping from samples to bandwidths across a distribution of density-estimation tasks, optimizing the logarithmic score. Key technical innovations include: (1) truncated-and-renormalized bounded-support formulation for stable learning across heterogeneous tasks, and (2) affine standardization allowing a selector trained on a single reference interval to transfer across bounded intervals. Training uses finite Gaussian mixtures based on their L1 approximation property.

Result: The amortized selector consistently and substantially outperforms classical methods (Silverman's rule, Sheather-Jones selector, and least-squares cross-validation) across Gaussian sampling, multi-family benchmarks, and randomized Gaussian-mixture training scenarios. Particularly large gains are observed for small and heterogeneous samples. The trained selector generalizes strongly across different density structures without requiring specification or fitting of a distributional family.

Conclusion: The amortized bandwidth selection framework combines broad applicability with strong empirical performance, making it attractive for applications requiring conversion of finite samples or ensembles into continuous probability densities. The approach eliminates the need for per-sample optimization while achieving superior density estimates across diverse tasks.

Abstract: Kernel density estimation converts finite samples into probability densities, but its performance depends critically on bandwidth selection. Classical selectors prescribe the sample-to-bandwidth rule analytically or asymptotically, or solve a new optimization for each sample. An amortized framework is proposed that instead learns this mapping across a distribution of density-estimation tasks by optimizing the logarithmic score. A truncated-and-renormalized bounded-support formulation enables stable learning across heterogeneous tasks, while affine standardization allows a selector trained on a single reference interval to transfer across bounded intervals. Experiments under Gaussian sampling, a multi-family benchmark, and randomized Gaussian-mixture training show that the amortized selector consistently and substantially outperforms Silverman's rule, the Sheather--Jones selector, and least-squares cross-validation, with especially large gains in small and heterogeneous samples. Finite Gaussian mixtures provide a generic training mechanism supported by their $L^1$ approximation property. Selectors trained in this way generalize strongly across different density structures, allowing the same trained selector to be applied directly to finite samples from unknown densities without specifying or fitting a distributional family. This combination of broad applicability and strong empirical performance makes the framework attractive for a wide range of applications in which finite samples or ensembles must be converted into continuous probability densities.

</details>


### [182] [Mutual information and sensitivity analysis for feature selection in customer targeting: a comparative study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20447)
*Nestor Barraza,Sergio Moro,Marcelo Ferreyra,Adolfo de la Peña*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares mutual information and data-based sensitivity analysis for feature selection in a bank telemarketing prediction task. Mutual information identified 13 features while data-based sensitivity analysis identified 9 features. The latter performs better at lower false positive rates, while mutual information is slightly better at higher false positive rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Feature selection is crucial in data-driven knowledge discovery. The research aims to analyze the advantages and disadvantages of two techniques: mutual information (traditional method) and data-based sensitivity analysis (recent method), by applying both to a bank telemarketing case study.

Method: Both feature selection techniques were applied to a bank telemarketing dataset. Logistic regression models were built using the selected features from each technique (13 features for mutual information, 9 for data-based sensitivity analysis). Performance was compared across different false positive ratios.

Result: Data-based sensitivity analysis performed better at lower false positive rates, while mutual information was slightly better at higher false positive rates. Mutual information with 13 features is better for reducing contact costs without risking high success loss. Data-based sensitivity analysis achieved good prediction with fewer features (9).

Conclusion: Mutual information, despite being a non-recent method, remains a valid and effective feature selection technique. Data-based sensitivity analysis offers the advantage of achieving good prediction results with fewer features, making it efficient when feature reduction is prioritized.

Abstract: Feature selection is a highly relevant task in a data-driven knowledge discovery project. Several techniques have been developed aiming at finding the features that influence most an outcome to predict, including mutual information and, in recent years, the data-based sensitivity analysis. The present research focus on analyzing the advantages and disadvantages of each of these two techniques, by applying both to a bank telemarketing case. Thereafter, a logistic regression model is built on the tuned set of features identified by each of the two techniques as the most influencing set of features on the success of a telemarketing contact, in a total of 13 features for mutual information and 9 features for the data-based sensitivity analysis. The latter performs better for lower values of false positives while the former is slightly better for a higher false positive ratio. Thus, mutual information becomes a better choice if bank managers intend to reduce slightly the cost of contacts without risking losing a high number of successes. Such results show that mutual information, although not recent, is still a valid method for feature selection. On the other side, the data-based sensitivity analysis selection achieved good prediction results with less features.

</details>


### [183] [When Clean Data Hurts: Learning with Monotone Corruptions Beyond Binary Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20480)
*Julian Asilis,Shaddin Dughmi,Chirag Pabbaraju*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Optimal learners are tailored to exploit the i.i.d.\ data assumption underlying the classic PAC model. What if an i.i.d.\ training sample were corrupted with correctly labeled examples drawn from an otherwise unrelated, even adversarial source? This model of learning with monotone adversarial corruptions was recently introduced by Larsen et al. (2026), who demonstrated that all known optimal binary learners suffer increased error rates in this setting, from $O(d / n)$ in the PAC model to $Ω(d \log(n / d) / n)$ under monotone corruption. Mehrotra (2026) proved this logarithmic factor to be necessary for binary classification, but left open the consequences of corruption for more general learning settings, such as multiclass classification and partial binary concept classes.
  As our primary result, we demonstrate that monotone adversaries are frighteningly more powerful in each of these settings. We exhibit a learnable multiclass problem, of DS dimension only 2, that becomes altogether unlearnable under a monotone adversary, and show an analogous result for partial binary concept classes. These results are achieved by an adaptive adversary permitted to view the original i.i.d.\ training set $S$ and to insert $b < \infty$ corrupted datapoints into $S$. In the multiclass example, the adversary need only insert a linear number $b = |S| = n$ of datapoints.
  We complement these impossibility results by proving that every class remains learnable when the number of adaptive additions is $o(n)$, which our previous multiclass lower bound proves to be tight. We further observe that the classic multiclass error rate of $O(d_{\mathrm{DS}} / n)$ remains achievable against adaptive adversaries restricted to a known constant budget $b = O(1)$, against semi-adaptive adversaries viewing only a $p$-fraction of $S$ for $p \in (0, 1)$, and against oblivious adversaries that cannot view $S$.

</details>


### [184] [Metag: A dataset to build agentic meta-reviewing capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20488)
*Anirudh Sundar,Min Chen,Divya Tadimeti,Gemma Zhang,Alice Li,Nigel Boachie Kumankumah,Pavan Uttej Ravva,Sadid Hasan,Somya Chatterjee,Pruthvi Prakash Navada,Xiao Wang,Yue Kang,Sulaiman Vesal,Larry Heck*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Metag is a dataset designed to accelerate the development of meta-reviewing agents by identifying changes made to scientific articles during the review-rebuttal process. It contains 349 high-quality action items linking reviewer concerns, author resolutions, and manuscript diffs from OpenReview discussions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The continuing growth in conference submissions has increased the burden on meta-reviewers who must synthesize reviewer feedback, author rebuttals, and manuscript revisions. Current tools lack the ability to automatically track whether authors have addressed reviewer concerns and where changes appear in revised manuscripts.

Method: Metag was collected by: (1) obtaining manuscript versions from before the review deadline and after acceptance, (2) computing differences between the two documents, and (3) asking human annotators to align these differences with action items from OpenReview discussions. Each instance contains a reviewer concern, the author's proposed resolution, and the manuscript diffs implementing the change.

Result: The resulting dataset consists of 349 high-quality action items tied to paper differences, publicly available at https://github.com/microsoft/Metag-dataset.

Conclusion: Metag will enable building methods to empower meta reviewers to quickly identify whether authors have addressed reviewer statements and where in the paper those changes have been made, resulting in additional transparency and traceability throughout the peer review process.

Abstract: AI tools increasingly support tasks across the scientific research cycle, from experiment design and manuscript preparation to peer review. At the same time, the continuing growth in conference submissions has increased the burden on meta-reviewers, who must synthesize reviewer feedback, author rebuttals, and manuscript revisions. To address this concern, this paper introduces Metag, a dataset to accelerate the development of meta-reviewing agents, specifically to identify changes made to scientific articles during the review-rebuttal process. Each instance contains a reviewer concern, the author's proposed resolution, and the manuscript diffs implementing the stated change. Metag is collected by obtaining manuscript versions from before the review deadline and after acceptance, computing differences between the two documents, and asking human annotators to align these differences with action items from OpenReview discussions. The resulting dataset consists of 349 high-quality action items tied to paper differences and will enable building methods to empower meta reviewers to quickly identify whether authors have addressed reviewer statements and where in the paper those changes have been made, resulting in additional transparency and traceability throughout peer review. The dataset is publicly available at https://github.com/microsoft/Metag-dataset.

</details>


### [185] [Bern2Edge: A Neurosymbolic Compiler for Edge Deployment via Bernstein Polynomial Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20497)
*Malak Gamal El-Din,Yifan Zhang,Yasser Shoukry,Sitao Huang,Salma Elmalaki*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Bern2Edge is an end-to-end framework for deploying high-accuracy neural networks on edge devices by using Bernstein polynomial activations and knowledge distillation to convert pretrained teacher networks into hardware-efficient representations with two deployment paths: LUT-based and symbolic rule-based.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The main problem is that existing approaches treat training, compression, and hardware synthesis as separate stages, creating a gap between software-trained models and efficient end-to-end deployment with limited support for interpretability on resource-constrained edge devices.

Method: Bern2Edge uses knowledge distillation to convert a pretrained teacher feed-forward network into hardware-efficient representations via Bernstein polynomial activations, enabling two deployment paths: (i) high-fidelity LUT-based realization and (ii) symbolic rule-based representation derived from Bernstein activation geometry.

Result: BNNs achieve up to 2.12 pp accuracy improvement over ReLU under identical compression constraints. At system level, Bern2Edge achieves up to 99.8% latency reduction and 95.2% BRAM reduction relative to W8A8 quantized teacher on AMD Xilinx KV260 FPGA while maintaining accuracy within 0.5 pp. The rule-based path reduces DSP usage by up to 89.0% at a cost of 1.5 pp in total accuracy.

Conclusion: Bern2Edge successfully enables efficient neural network deployment on resource-constrained edge devices with significant hardware optimizations (latency, BRAM, DSP) while maintaining competitive accuracy and offering interpretable inference through symbolic rules.

Abstract: Deploying high-accuracy neural networks on resource-constrained edge devices remains challenging, as existing approaches treat training, compression, and hardware synthesis as separate stages, leaving a gap between software-trained models and efficient end-to-end deployment with limited support for interpretability. We propose Bern2Edge, an end-to-end framework that uses knowledge distillation to convert a pretrained teacher feed-forward network into hardware-efficient representations via Bernstein polynomial activations. This representation enables two deployment paths: (i) a high-fidelity LUT-based realization that preserves model fidelity under compression, and (ii) a symbolic rule-based representation derived from Bernstein activation geometry, enabling interpretable inference with explicit input-space constraints. The resulting BNNs achieve up to 2.12 percentage-point (pp) accuracy improvement over ReLU under identical compression constraints. At the system level, Bern2Edge achieves up to 99.8% latency reduction and 95.2% BRAM reduction relative to a W8A8 quantized teacher on an AMD Xilinx KV260 FPGA, while maintaining accuracy within 0.5 pp, and further deploys on a low-power Spartan-7 XC7S15 FPGA. The rule-based path reduces DSP usage by up to 89.0% at a cost of 1.5 pp in total accuracy.

</details>


### [186] [When Graph-JEPA Learns the Wrong Thing: Diagnosing and Repairing Category-Conditional Collapse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20516)
*Gollam Rabby,Sören Auer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper reveals a critical failure mode in Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) where standard evaluation metrics (linear probing, effective rank) show healthy results while the representation carries zero usable instance information. Using a scientific-reasoning graph dataset, the authors demonstrate that a Graph-JEPA can achieve 0.871 linear-probe accuracy and effective rank 18-47, yet fail completely at retrieval (0.00 bits recovered). The root cause is variance allocation: frozen inputs allocate 86.05% variance to subgraph identity vs 0.40% to aspect identity, while trained latents show the opposite (0.39% vs 99.61%). After repairing this degeneracy, the target itself is found to be reducible (deterministic from node census), rendering the "successful" retrieval meaningless for reasoning. The authors provide a repair achieving 14.377/14.379 bits but conclude that standard metrics can saturate on unsupportive evaluation targets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors investigate why JEPAs are universally selected based on linear probing and effective rank metrics, questioning whether these metrics reliably indicate useful representations. They aim to expose a case where both metrics appear healthy but the representation carries zero usable instance information.

Method: The authors construct a scientific-reasoning graph over 57,903 articles (each a subgraph). They train a Graph-JEPA to predict one masked aspect from remaining aspects. They evaluate using linear probing, effective rank, and retrieval metrics (MRR, bits recovered). They test three upper bounds on the same pool and code to rule out corpus/masking/pool/metric issues. They trace the failure to variance allocation using init analysis, and propose a repair by modifying the loss to regression.

Result: The Graph-JEPA achieves 0.871 linear-probe accuracy and effective rank 18-47, yet retrieval recovers 0.00 of 14.4 bits (MRR 1.9e-4 vs chance 1.99e-4, p=0.98). Three upper bounds recover nearly everything (+14.28, +14.34, +14.22 bits). Root cause: variance allocation - frozen inputs place 86.05% variance on subgraph identity vs 0.40% on aspect identity; trained latents show 0.39% vs 99.61%. The degenerate solution is a global minimum present at initialization. The repaired configuration reaches 14.377 of 14.379 bits (above 13.865-bit oracle), but reverting loss to regression drops it to 0.307 bits. However, the target is reducible (intra-subgraph edges are deterministic from node census), so the repair doesn't support reasoning. The oracle reaches 96.4% of ceiling; learning-rate schedule has larger effect than architecture. Bits and reasoning probe show no relation across ten cells. 25.96% of nodes are duplicate placeholders; rest is more generic than supporting evidence.

Conclusion: Linear probing, effective rank, and retrieval metrics can all saturate on an unsupportive evaluation target. The authors release a harness with a reducibility audit and target gate to detect such failures. They recommend being cautious about using standard metrics alone to evaluate representation quality, especially for reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Joint-embedding predictive architectures are selected almost universally by linear probing and effective rank. We report a case where both read healthily while the representation carries zero usable instance information. We repair it, and a second failure appears: the repaired metric saturates on a target carrying no structural information. Our corpus is a scientific-reasoning graph over 57,903 articles, each a subgraph. A Graph-JEPA predicts one masked aspect from a subgraph's remaining aspects, attaining linear-probe accuracy 0.871 and effective rank 18-47, yet retrieval recovers 0.00 of 14.4 bits (MRR 1.9e-4 vs chance 1.99e-4, p=0.98). Three upper bounds on the same pool and code recover nearly everything (+14.28, +14.34, +14.22 bits), ruling out corpus, masking, pool, and metric as causes. We trace this to variance allocation - frozen inputs place 86.05% of variance on subgraph identity and 0.40% on aspect identity, while trained latents place 0.39% and 99.61%. This is a property of the objective's optimum: the degenerate solution is a global minimum of the coupled predictor/EMA-target objective, present already at init. A repaired configuration reaches 14.377 of 14.379 bits, above the 13.865-bit oracle; reverting the loss to regression drops it to 0.307 bits, confirming it. Yet the repair licenses nothing about reasoning: the target is reducible, since intra-subgraph edges are a deterministic function of node census. The oracle reaches 96.4% of the ceiling, and our largest effect is the learning-rate schedule, not architecture. Bits and a reasoning probe show no relation across ten cells. A data-derived target fails a quality gate - 25.96% of nodes are duplicate placeholders, and the rest is more generic than supporting evidence. Rank, probes, and metrics can all saturate on an unsupportive evaluation. We release a harness with a reducibility audit and target gate.

</details>


### [187] [Learning Exact NVIDIA SASS Encoders with $\mathbb{F}_2$ Linear Algebra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20532)
*Jiading Gai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: F2Asm is the first open-source NVIDIA SASS assembler that learns 128-bit instruction encoders as vector-valued affine maps over F2, supporting Hopper SM90/SM90a, Blackwell SM100, and Rubin SM107 architectures. It uses Gaussian elimination to build a compact basis and achieves exact round-trip disassembly-reassembly for all tested CUBINs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NVIDIA provides a SASS disassembler but no public assembler for recent data-center GPUs, limiting controlled machine-code rewriting capabilities. This gap prevents researchers and developers from performing low-level GPU code manipulation, optimization, and security analysis.

Method: F2Asm learns exact SASS encoders from paired disassembly and original CUBIN instruction words by treating them as vector-valued affine maps over F2. It uses Gaussian elimination over F2 to incrementally build a compact basis, detect inconsistencies, and reject inputs outside the learned span. The system separates target-specific control bits, relocation rules, and CUBIN metadata from its learning algorithm for cleaner architecture.

Result: F2Asm was trained on 3,225 CUBINs from pinned NVIDIA and third-party production libraries, CUDA 13.3 packages, and CUDA 13.4 Developer Preview archives. In round-trip tests, F2Asm successfully reassembled disassembled SASS for each CUBIN, with all compared executable text sections matching the originals exactly.

Conclusion: F2Asm represents a significant breakthrough in GPU reverse engineering by providing the first open-source SASS assembler supporting modern data-center GPUs including the latest Rubin SM107 architecture. The learning-based approach using affine maps over F2 proves effective for accurately encoding SASS instructions, enabling new possibilities for GPU code analysis, optimization, and security research.

Abstract: NVIDIA provides a SASS disassembler but no public SASS assembler for recent data-center GPUs, limiting controlled machine-code rewriting. We present F2Asm, which learns exact 128-bit SASS encoders from paired disassembly and original CUBIN instruction words. To our knowledge, F2Asm is the first system to learn SASS instruction encoders as vector-valued affine maps over F2 and the first open-source NVIDIA SASS assembler to support Rubin SM107. F2Asm uses Gaussian elimination over F2 to incrementally build a compact basis, detect inconsistencies, and reject inputs outside the learned span. F2Asm separates target-specific control bits, relocation rules, and CUBIN metadata from its learning algorithm. We train encoders for Hopper SM90/SM90a, Blackwell SM100, and Rubin SM107 using 3,225 CUBINs from pinned NVIDIA and third-party production libraries, CUDA 13.3 packages, and CUDA 13.4 Developer Preview archives. In round-trip tests, F2Asm reassembles the disassembled SASS for each CUBIN, and all compared executable text sections match the originals exactly.

</details>


### [188] [AgentDecarbonizer: Carbon-Aware Execution for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20566)
*Leyi Yan,Shuangning Li,Sihang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses carbon emissions from AI agent workloads by presenting AgentDecarbonizer, a carbon optimizer that schedules agent tasks to minimize emissions while meeting deadlines. It uses temporal shifting (waiting for lower-carbon periods) and spatial shifting (moving to lower-carbon grids), accounting for cache recomputation overhead. Evaluated on WildClawBench, it achieves up to 57.9% emission reduction versus carbon-agnostic baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents extend LLMs to multi-step workflows but incur substantial carbon emissions through repeated model invocations. The authors identify deadline flexibility as an opportunity for carbon-aware execution, where tasks can wait for lower-carbon periods or shift to lower-carbon grids, but this requires handling uncertain execution times and cached context recomputation overhead.

Method: The authors characterized carbon emissions of OpenClaw agent workloads using WildClawBench, examining how emissions depend on token consumption, context cache reuse, and grid carbon intensity. They developed AgentDecarbonizer, which conservatively estimates task duration, selects deadline-feasible execution schedules accounting for cache recomputation overhead during spatial shifting, and evaluates on 60 agent tasks across four grids.

Result: AgentDecarbonizer reduces carbon emissions by up to 57.9% compared with a carbon-agnostic baseline and by up to 37.5% compared with a baseline that selects the carbon-optimal grid at task start time.

Conclusion: Carbon-aware scheduling for AI agents is feasible and significant. By leveraging deadline flexibility and accounting for cache recomputation overhead, AgentDecarbonizer demonstrates substantial emission reductions, providing a practical approach to reducing the carbon footprint of AI agent workloads.

Abstract: AI agents extend large language models from single prompt-response interactions to long-running, goaldirected workflows that issue many model calls, invoke tools, and interact with external environments. These workflows enable tasks such as software repair, data analysis, and experiment management, but their repeated model invocations can incur substantial carbon emissions. This paper characterizes the carbon emissions of OpenClaw agent workloads using WildClawBench, and shows that emissions depend on token consumption, context cache reuse, and the carbon intensity of the grid. Our characterization identifies deadline flexibility as an opportunity for carbon-aware execution: agent tasks can wait for lower-carbon-intensity periods or shift to lower-carbon grids. However, doing so requires handling uncertain execution time for temporal shifting and cached context recomputation during spatial shifting. We present AgentDecarbonizer, a carbon optimizer for AI agents that runs alongside OpenClaw. Given a task prompt and user-specified deadline, AgentDecarbonizer conservatively estimates task duration and selects deadline-feasible execution schedules, while accounting for cache recomputation overhead during spatial shifting.
