Evaluated on WildClawBench workloads with 60 agent tasks across four grids, AgentDecarbonizer reduces carbon emissions by up to 57.9 % compared with a carbon-agnostic baseline and by up to 37.5 % compared with a baseline that selects the carbon-optimal grid at task start time.

</details>


### [189] [Faults That Fortify: CNN Adversarial Robustness via GPU Undervolting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20572)
*Behnam Omidi,Ahmad Tahmasivand,Husam Alsyouri,Saba Al-Sayouri,Chongzhou Fang,Ihsen Alouani,Khaled N. Khasawneh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes GPU undervolting during CNN training to simultaneously improve adversarial robustness and reduce energy consumption. The voltage reduction introduces stochastic perturbations that act as implicit regularization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CNNs face dual challenges: vulnerability to adversarial attacks and high training costs, especially for energy-constrained edge devices. Adversarial training is effective but expensive.

Method: The authors characterize undervolting-induced faults at the bit level, then train LeNet, VGG-6, and MobileNetV3 on MNIST and CIFAR-10 under two training regimes (standard and adversarial), each at nominal and undervolted voltage, evaluating all models against adversarial attacks.

Result: Undervolted models consistently achieve higher adversarial accuracy than nominal-voltage counterparts in both training regimes. Hardware-induced faults strengthen even adversarial training, with substantial energy savings due to quadratic relationship between dynamic power and supply voltage.

Conclusion: GPU undervolting is a readily deployable hardware-level defense requiring no algorithmic change, achieving robustness and energy efficiency simultaneously.

Abstract: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) face a dual challenge: vulnerability to adversarial attacks and prohibitive training cost. Adversarial training is effective but expensive, a burden that grows as learning shifts to the energy-constrained edge. This paper addresses both through GPU undervolting during training. Reducing supply voltage introduces stochastic perturbations that act as implicit regularization, improving robustness while lowering power. We characterize undervolting-induced faults at the bit level, then train LeNet, VGG-6, and MobileNetV3 on MNIST and CIFAR-10 under two training regimes, standard and adversarial, each at nominal and undervolted voltage, and evaluate all models against adversarial attacks. In both regimes, the undervolted model consistently achieves higher adversarial accuracy than its nominal-voltage counterpart, showing that hardware-induced faults strengthen even adversarial training. Because dynamic power scales quadratically with supply voltage, these robustness gains arrive with substantial energy savings. GPU undervolting is therefore a readily deployable hardware-level defense requiring no algorithmic change, and opens a promising direction in which robustness and energy efficiency move together.

</details>


### [190] [Provable Edge-of-Stability for Adam on a One-Dimensional Quadratic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20638)
*Yiman Fong,Heng Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the edge-of-stability (EoS) phenomenon in the Adam optimizer by studying uncorrected Adam on a one-dimensional quadratic function. The authors characterize the dynamics across parameter space, finding that Adam exhibits a restoring tendency toward a specific stability threshold, while also identifying regimes where this mechanism breaks down.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The edge-of-stability phenomenon of Adam has been widely observed but its underlying dynamical mechanism is not yet fully understood. The authors seek to provide a concrete dynamical explanation for Adam's EoS in a simplified setting free of evolving loss geometry.

Method: The authors study uncorrected Adam on a one-dimensional quadratic with constant curvature. They characterize the resulting dynamics across the parameter space mathematically, proving behavior in broad regimes and identifying edge cases.

Result: In broad regimes, Adam exhibits a restoring tendency toward its frozen stability threshold $2(1+β_1)/[η(1-β_1)]$. The authors also identify settings where this edge-seeking mechanism breaks down, including strictly subcritical periodic orbits and specially tuned trajectories that converge to the optimum while remaining uniformly supercritical.

Conclusion: The results provide a concrete dynamical explanation for Adam's EoS in a setting free of evolving loss geometry, while also exposing its limitations. The study reveals both the mechanism behind Adam's edge-of-stability behavior and the conditions under which it fails.

Abstract: The edge-of-stability (EoS) phenomenon of Adam has been widely observed, while its underlying dynamical mechanism is not yet fully understood. We study uncorrected Adam on a one-dimensional quadratic, a clean setting where constant curvature isolates the optimizer-induced dynamics behind the EoS. We characterize the resulting dynamics across the parameter space. In broad regimes, we prove that Adam exhibits a restoring tendency toward its frozen stability threshold $2(1+β_1)/[η(1-β_1)]$. We also identify settings in which this edge-seeking mechanism breaks down, including strictly subcritical periodic orbits and specially tuned trajectories that converge to the optimum while remaining uniformly supercritical. These results give a concrete dynamical explanation for Adam's EoS in a setting free of evolving loss geometry, while also exposing its limitations.

</details>


### [191] [Meta-clustering of milk mid-infrared spectra identifies dairy cow groups associated with negative energy balance in early lactation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20653)
*T. Touil,E. R. Paquet*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study used clustering methods on mid-infrared (MIR) milk spectroscopy data to identify five distinct groups of early-lactation dairy cows. The five meta-clusters appear to reflect a gradient of negative energy balance (NEB) severity, from severe to moderate/mild NEB and recovery stages. A simple PCA-based k-means approach performed comparably to more sophisticated methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Applying clustering directly to MIR spectral data could reveal latent groups of cows associated with milk traits or health disorders, enabling prevention and monitoring of at-risk animals. This study aimed to identify groups of individual dairy cows in early lactation directly from milk MIR spectra and analyze their associations with milk traits.

Method: Using 407,632 milk MIR records from 3,408 commercial farms, the study combined: (i) spectral filtering for informative wavenumbers, (ii) dimensionality reduction via PCA and autoencoder, and (iii) clustering via k-means and spectral clustering—yielding 8 different approaches. Results were regrouped into meta-clusters encompassing the most similar clusters across approaches.

Result: Five distinct meta-clusters of early-lactation cows were identified, significantly associated with milk traits. The eight approaches converged on the same five meta-clusters. The classic PCA-based k-means using the full spectrum performed comparably to more sophisticated methods. The five meta-clusters were strongly associated with DIM (days in milk) and appeared to reflect a gradient of negative energy balance (NEB) severity: severe, moderate, mild, and two recovery groups.

Conclusion: Clustering MIR spectra can effectively identify distinct metabolic profiles in early-lactation dairy cows, with strong associations to negative energy balance status. Simple, computationally efficient methods (PCA + k-means) are sufficient, as they recapture clusters identified by more complex approaches. This approach could help monitor at-risk animals and prevent health disorders.

Abstract: Clustering methods have been used to identify distinct groups of milk samples, cows, or herds. Fourier-transform infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy, particularly mid-infrared (MIR) spectroscopy, has been applied to individual cow milk samples to predict various milk traits. Applying clustering directly to MIR spectral data may reveal latent groups of cows associated with milk traits or health disorders and can help prevent these conditions or monitor at-risk animals. This study aimed to identify groups of individual dairy cows in early lactation directly from milk MIR spectra and to analyze their associations with milk traits. Using a dataset of 407,632 individual milk MIR records from 3,408 commercial farms, we combined (i) spectral filtering that selects informative wavenumbers, (ii) two dimensionality-reduction methods: principal component analysis (PCA) and an autoencoder, and (iii) two clustering algorithms: k-means and spectral clustering to yield eight different clustering approaches. We regrouped the assigned clusters into meta-clusters that encompassed the most similar ones identified by the eight approaches. Our results revealed five distinct meta-clusters of early-lactation individual dairy cows significantly associated with milk traits. Despite substantial differences, the eight approaches converged on the same five meta-clusters, and the classic, computationally efficient PCA-based k-means approach using the full spectrum recaptured clusters identified by more sophisticated, computationally intensive approaches. The five meta-clusters were strongly associated with DIM and appeared to reflect a gradient of negative energy balance (NEB) severity: severe, moderate, and possibly mild, while the remaining two likely represented cows recovering from NEB, one with rapid restoration of energy balance and one in early recovery.

</details>


### [192] [RiskTraf: Risk-Extrapolated Residual Learning for Multi-Variate Traffic Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20656)
*Guangyu Wang,Zhidan Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses limitations in traffic flow forecasting by introducing a benchmark (PEMSB-3V) preserving raw flow, speed, and occupancy measurements from PeMS detectors, and proposes RiskTraf, a model-agnostic residual learning approach that mitigates regime-specific shortcut correlations by learning horizon-wise flow corrections from historical speed and occupancy data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing traffic forecasting benchmarks and models rarely exploit all three raw sensor measurements (flow, speed, occupancy) reliably. Current releases often omit these variables, use proxies, or contain inconsistent records. Additionally, direct empirical risk minimization over three-variable inputs may exploit regime-dependent shortcuts since relationships among flow, speed, and occupancy vary substantially between free-flow and congested states.

Method: The paper introduces PEMSB-3V benchmark preserving raw flow, speed, and occupancy from PeMS detectors. It also proposes RiskTraf, a model-agnostic risk-extrapolated residual plug-in that: (1) freezes a trained spatio-temporal backbone checkpoint; (2) learns a lightweight zero-start residual head from historical speed and occupancy; (3) constructs ordered traffic-risk environments; (4) optimizes horizon-wise flow corrections with a risk extrapolation objective.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate that RiskTraf consistently improves diverse forecasting backbones and outperforms debiasing and distribution-shift adaptation methods.

Conclusion: RiskTraf effectively mitigates regime-specific shortcut correlations without modifying the backbone architecture, providing a universal improvement mechanism for traffic flow forecasting models.

Abstract: Traffic sensors commonly record flow, speed, and occupancy, but standard traffic flow forecasting benchmarks and models rarely exploit all three raw measurements reliably. Although speed and occupancy provide sensor-native traffic-state information beyond flow alone, existing releases often omit these variables, replace them with proxies, or contain logically inconsistent records. Moreover, direct empirical risk minimization over three-variable inputs may exploit regime-dependent shortcuts, as the relationships among flow, speed, and occupancy vary substantially between free-flow and congested states. We introduce \textbf{PEMSB-3V}, a public benchmark suite that preserves raw flow, speed, and occupancy measurements from PeMS detectors for flow prediction. We also propose \textbf{RiskTraf}, a model-agnostic risk-extrapolated residual plug-in. For each trained spatio-temporal backbone, RiskTraf freezes the selected checkpoint and learns a lightweight zero-start residual head from historical speed and occupancy. The residual head constructs ordered traffic-risk environments and optimizes horizon-wise flow corrections with a risk extrapolation objective, thereby mitigating regime-specific shortcut correlations without modifying the backbone. Extensive experiments demonstrate that RiskTraf consistently improves diverse forecasting backbones and outperforms debiasing and distribution-shift adaptation methods. Our code and benchmark are available at https://github.com/Guangyu4/RiskTraf.

</details>


### [193] [C-Score: Beyond Accuracy for Robustness Assessment in Semi-Supervised Learning under Open-World Unlabeled Contamination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20667)
*Tsao-Lun Chen,Chi-Cheng Fu,Han-Yi E. Chou,Shun-Feng Su*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses a critical issue in pseudo-label-based semi-supervised learning (SSL): the hidden collapse problem under open-world unlabeled contamination. When unlabeled data contains out-of-distribution (OOD) samples, they can receive high-confidence predictions and be incorrectly incorporated into training, causing internal learning dynamics to deteriorate while clean test accuracy appears stable. The authors propose C-Score, a diagnostic framework evaluating training behavior across prediction, feature representation, and optimization spaces using metrics like PLE, CCI, Sem-Drift, and Grad-Align.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pseudo-label-based SSL has achieved strong performance but typically assumes a closed-world where unlabeled data comes from the same distribution as labeled data. In practical deployment, unlabeled data often contains OOD samples. Under such contamination, OOD samples may receive high-confidence predictions and be used in training as if they were valid, causing internal learning dynamics to deteriorate while clean test accuracy remains stable. This creates an important evaluation problem: standard accuracy metrics fail to detect hidden degradation in SSL.

Method: The authors present C-Score, a compact diagnostic framework that evaluates training behavior in three complementary spaces: (1) Prediction space - using PLE (Pseudo-label Entropy) and CCI (Confident Contamination Index) to assess unlabeled prediction behavior; (2) Feature representation space - using Sem-Drift to measure deviation from labeled semantic anchors; (3) Optimization space - using Grad-Align to evaluate compatibility between labeled and unlabeled optimization. Experiments were conducted on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with multiple OOD sources (SVHN, CIFAR-100, STL-10), varying contamination ratios, and four pseudo-label-based SSL algorithms (FlexMatch and others).

Result: The experiments reveal that C-Score metrics successfully detect hidden degradation that clean accuracy alone fails to identify. Under SVHN contamination, CCI rises over 280% while best-accuracy remains within 3% of the uncontaminated baseline. Near-OOD sources (CIFAR-100, STL-10) cause up to 14.9% accuracy collapse with FlexMatch at r=0.5 contamination ratio. The results demonstrate that clean accuracy is insufficient for evaluating SSL robustness in open-world environments, and internal diagnostic signals are necessary for reliable robustness assessment under unlabeled contamination.

Conclusion: Clean test accuracy alone is insufficient for evaluating SSL robustness in open-world environments. Internal diagnostic signals from C-Score (PLE, CCI, Sem-Drift, Grad-Align) are necessary for more reliable robustness assessment when unlabeled data contains OOD contamination. The framework successfully reveals hidden collapse that standard metrics miss, providing a valuable tool for diagnosing SSL training under real-world open-world conditions.

Abstract: Pseudo-label-based semi-supervised learning has achieved strong performance due to its simplicity and scalability. However, it is typically developed under a closed-world assumption that unlabeled data are drawn from the same distribution as labeled data. In practical deployment, unlabeled data are often collected from open environments and may contain OOD samples. Under such contamination, OOD samples may still receive high-confidence predictions and be incorporated into training as if they were valid target examples. This creates an important evaluation problem: clean in-distribution test accuracy may appear stable even when the internal learning dynamics of SSL have already deteriorated. To address this issue, we study hidden collapse in pseudo-label-based SSL under open-world unlabeled contamination from a diagnostic evaluation perspective. We present C-Score, a compact framework that evaluates training behavior in three complementary spaces: prediction, feature representation, and optimization. C-Score includes PLE and CCI for unlabeled prediction behavior, Sem-Drift for deviation from labeled semantic anchors, and Grad-Align for the compatibility between labeled and unlabeled optimization. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with multiple OOD sources, varying contamination ratios, and four pseudo-label-based SSL algorithms show that C-Score metrics reveal hidden degradation that clean accuracy alone fails to detect: under SVHN contamination, CCI rises over 280% while best-accuracy remains within 3% of the uncontaminated baseline; near-OOD sources (CIFAR-100, STL-10) cause up to 14.9% accuracy collapse (FlexMatch, r=0.5). The results suggest that clean accuracy alone is insufficient for evaluating SSL robustness in open-world environments, and that internal diagnostic signals are necessary for more reliable robustness assessment under unlabeled contamination.

</details>


### [194] [Lightweight Adaptive ReduNet via Hyperspherical Manifold Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20668)
*Zhenglin Huang,Qifa Yan,Bin Dai,Xiaohu Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: In recent years, a white-box neural network called ReduNet has been proposed, which employs the maximal coding rate reduction (MCR$^2$) principle to transform raw data into low-dimensional discriminative features via a forward layer-wise construction process. Unlike traditional deep networks that rely on backpropagation, ReduNet explicitly derives the parameters of each layer from the features of its preceding layer, offering a mathematically interpretable paradigm. However, this layer-wise construction often requires a large number of layers for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value, which increases the parameter storage of the unfolded module. To address this issue, we propose LA-ReduNet, a lightweight adaptive architecture that refines the layer-wise update rule and enables discriminative feature representations to be obtained with substantially fewer unfolded layers. Specifically, LA-ReduNet employs hyperspherical manifold learning and adaptive step sizes, thereby reducing by an order of magnitude the number of layers required for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value. Simulation results demonstrate that, while maintaining comparable classification accuracy, LA-ReduNet requires significantly fewer layers for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value. Remarkably, under the considered experimental settings, LA-ReduNet requires only approximately $1/29$ of the parameter storage of the unfolded ReduNet module for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value.

</details>


### [195] [Reinforcement Learning for Continuous-Time Jump Markov Decision Processes with Applications to Network Dynamic Pricing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20680)
*Huiling Meng,Ningyuan Chen,Xuefeng Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a novel q-learning framework for continuous-time jump Markov decision processes (CTJMDPs) with general discrete state spaces and continuous/discrete action spaces, addressing limitations of existing methods that only work for continuous state spaces in Euclidean spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing continuous-time RL techniques like q-learning for controlled diffusions focus on continuous state spaces νr^d and rely on semimartingale theory, making them inapplicable to CTJMDPs with general discrete state spaces that lack algebraic structures of Euclidean spaces.

Method: The authors establish theoretical foundations of q-learning for CTJMDPs by formulating an entropy-regularized continuous-time control problem with stochastic policies, and develop model-free q-learning algorithms that avoid the need for time discretization.

Result: Numerical experiments in network dynamic pricing show the proposed RL algorithm reliably learns near-optimal policies and consistently outperforms standard benchmark methods, demonstrating superior solution quality and effective scalability to large-scale network instances.

Conclusion: The proposed approach offers both conceptual and empirical advantages over naive time discretization methods. It successfully extends q-learning to CTJMDPs with general discrete state spaces, providing a practical solution for applications like multi-product dynamic pricing with capacitated resources.

Abstract: We study reinforcement learning (RL) in Continuous-Time Jump Markov Decision Processes (CTJMDPs) featuring general discrete state spaces (which need not possess a vector space structure) and continuous/discrete action spaces. The setup covers many well-known applications in operations such as multi-product dynamic pricing with capacitated resources (Gallego and van Ryzin 1997). To model the exploration-exploitation tradeoff, we formulate an entropy-regularized continuous-time control problem with stochastic policies. Recent continuous-time RL techniques such as $q$-learning for controlled diffusions in (Jia and Zhou 2023) focus on continuous state spaces $\mathbb{R}^d$ and rely heavily on semimartingale theory in $\mathbb{R}^d$ for their theoretical analysis. Consequently, their methods cannot be directly applied to CTJMDPs with general discrete state spaces, which may lack the algebraic addition and subtraction structures inherent to Euclidean spaces. To bridge this gap, we establish the theoretical foundations of $q$-learning for CTJMDPs and develop model-free $q$-learning algorithms. Compared to naïve time discretization and approximating CTJMDPs using discrete-time MDPs, our approach has several conceptual and empirical benefits. Numerical experiments in network dynamic pricing (Gallego and van Ryzin 1997) show that our proposed RL algorithm reliably learns near-optimal policies and consistently outperforms standard benchmark methods, demonstrating superior solution quality and effective scalability to large-scale network instances.

</details>


### [196] [Geometric Regularization for Long-Tailed Semi-Supervised Learning via Gaussian Feature Bridges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20710)
*Hongyang He,Xinyuan Song,Yan Zhong,Daizong Liu,Yanbin Li,Yang-fan He,Wenqiao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GBC (Gaussian Bridge Consistency) is a novel semi-supervised learning framework that addresses long-tailed label distributions and noisy pseudo-labels by constructing semantic interpolation paths between unlabeled samples and class anchors using a dynamic Prototype Atlas and Gaussian Feature Bridge in latent space, with a confidence-aware feature mixing strategy called BridgeMix.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world semi-supervised learning faces two major challenges: (1) long-tailed label distributions where few classes have many samples while most classes have few, and (2) noisy pseudo-labels that hinder generalization and amplify confirmation bias. Current SSL methods struggle under these realistic conditions.

Method: The proposed GBC framework consists of three key components: (1) A dynamic Prototype Atlas storing diverse labeled and pseudo-labeled exemplars per class; (2) Class-conditional Gaussian Feature Bridges in latent space that create smooth trajectories from uncertain predictions to reliable class prototypes; (3) Bridge consistency loss enforcing alignment with geometrically interpolated target distribution along these bridges; (4) BridgeMix, a confidence-aware feature mixing strategy that interpolates both sample and anchor pairs to enhance cross-sample generalization.

Result: Extensive experiments on CIFAR10-LT and ImageNet-LT (USB benchmarks) demonstrate that GBC consistently improves long tail-class performance while maintaining scalability, validating its robustness under realistic long-tailed SSL settings.

Conclusion: GBC effectively addresses the challenges of long-tailed distributions and noisy pseudo-labels in semi-supervised learning by leveraging semantic interpolation and prototype-based learning, providing a robust solution that improves performance on minority classes without sacrificing overall scalability.

Abstract: Real-world semi-supervised learning (SSL) often encounters significant challenges with long-tailed label distributions and noisy pseudo-labels, which hinder generalization and amplify confirmation bias. In this work, we introduce a novel framework, Gaussian Bridge Consistency (GBC), to address these challenges by constructing semantic interpolation paths between unlabeled samples and high-quality class anchors. Our method maintains a dynamic Prototype Atlas that stores a diverse and evolving set of labeled and pseudo-labeled exemplars per class. For each unlabeled instance, GBC forms a class-conditional Gaussian Feature Bridge in the latent space, enabling the student model to traverse a smooth trajectory from uncertain predictions to reliable class prototypes. A bridge consistency loss is applied along this path to enforce alignment with a geometrically interpolated target distribution. Furthermore, we propose BridgeMix, a confidence-aware feature mixing strategy that interpolates both sample and anchor pairs to amplify cross-sample generalization. Extensive experiments on CIFAR10-LT and ImageNet-LT (USB benchmarks) validate the robustness and effectiveness of GBC under realistic long-tailed SSL settings, consistently improving long tail-class performance without sacrificing scalability.

</details>


### [197] [Hidden Axis of Uncertainty: Latent-Posterior Alignment in Graph Neural Networks with Bayesian Output Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20758)
*Suk Hoon Choi,Damdae Park,Junhyuk Choi,Hyein Jung,Changsoo Kim,Ung Lee,Kyeongsu Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates uncertainty quantification in Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) with Bayesian output layers, discovering a new mechanism called Latent-Posterior Alignment (LPA) where uncertainty decreases through geometric alignment between latent and parameter spaces rather than posterior variance contraction. The authors propose Alignment-Guided Learning (AGL) to explicitly promote this alignment during training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional Bayesian theory attributes uncertainty reduction to posterior contraction, but this assumption may not hold for deep models. The authors observed that in GNNs with Bayesian output layers, predictive uncertainty decreases through a different mechanism - latent representations shifting toward lower-variance posterior directions - even without posterior variance contraction. This challenges existing theoretical assumptions and motivates investigation into alternative uncertainty dynamics.

Method: The authors conducted interventional experiments to support the functional role of LPA in shaping predictive uncertainty. Based on this insight, they developed Alignment-Guided Learning (AGL), which explicitly promotes alignment between latent representations and low-variance posterior directions during training.

Result: AGL effectively reduces predictive uncertainty while preserving model accuracy. It also improves structural calibration, ensuring that model confidence faithfully mirrors underlying data density. The findings shift understanding from focusing on posterior magnitude to the geometric interplay between latent and parameter spaces.

Conclusion: This work provides a new perspective on uncertainty dynamics in GNNs with mean-field Bayesian output layers. The discovery of LPA as a key mechanism for uncertainty reduction, alongside the proposed AGL training approach, offers a valuable framework for improving uncertainty quantification in graph-based deep learning models. This geometric perspective complements traditional posterior variance-based explanations.

Abstract: Bayesian Neural Networks (BNNs) with Bayesian output layers provide a principled and tractable framework for quantifying predictive uncertainty, yet the mechanisms shaping that uncertainty remain unclear. While conventional theory attributes uncertainty reduction to posterior contraction, the corresponding assumptions need not hold for deep models. In the Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) with Bayesian output layers studied here, we observe that predictive uncertainty decreases as latent representations shift toward lower-variance posterior directions, even though the posterior variance does not contract. We term this behavior Latent-Posterior Alignment (LPA) and conduct interventional experiments that support its functional role in shaping predictive uncertainty. Building on this insight, we propose Alignment-Guided Learning (AGL), which explicitly promotes this alignment during training. AGL effectively reduces predictive uncertainty while preserving accuracy and improves structural calibration, ensuring that the model confidence faithfully mirrors underlying data density. These findings provide a new perspective on uncertainty dynamics in GNNs with mean-field Bayesian output layers, shifting the focus from the magnitude of the posterior to the geometric interplay between latent and parameter spaces.

</details>


### [198] [Fuzzy-MoE: Interpretable Regime-Conditioned Expert Routing for Non-Stationary Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20761)
*Lan Guo,Jie Xiao,Zhao Su,Jun Shen,Haoran Li,Weixia Ma,Qingguo Zhou,Binbin Yong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fuzzy-MoE is a fuzzy logic-based dynamic Mixture-of-Experts model for non-stationary multivariate time series forecasting. It reformulates forecasting as latent temporal state identification and interpretable expert routing, using a dual-view fuzzy router with learnable Gaussian membership functions to enable explicit IF-THEN rule-based expert selection, allowing different variables to activate different experts for capturing heterogeneous temporal dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In non-stationary multivariate time series, different variables and samples exhibit heterogeneous latent dynamic states. Existing deep forecasting models compress them into a unified end-to-end mapping, leading to suboptimal modeling of time-varying dynamics and limited interpretability regarding which forecasting mechanism is activated under different latent states.

Method: Fuzzy-MoE consists of multiple parallel expert mapping networks and a dual-view fuzzy router. The router jointly exploits local convolutional dynamics and global segmented statistics to infer latent temporal states and computes expert activation strengths through learnable Gaussian membership functions, enabling explicit IF-THEN rule-based expert selection. This fine-grained routing allows different variables within the same sequence to activate different experts.

Result: Experimental results on multiple public time series benchmark datasets show that Fuzzy-MoE significantly outperforms mainstream forecasting methods in forecasting accuracy. Additionally, fuzzy memberships and rule activations provide interpretable routing diagnostics, demonstrating effectiveness in both forecasting performance and mechanism transparency.

Conclusion: Fuzzy-MoE successfully addresses the limitations of existing deep forecasting models by providing transparent and traceable expert selection through fuzzy rules rather than black-box routing. The model effectively captures heterogeneous temporal dynamics while improving interpretability, achieving superior forecasting performance on benchmark datasets.

Abstract: In non-stationary multivariate time series, different variables and samples often exhibit heterogeneous latent dynamic states, while existing deep forecasting models usually compress them into a unified end-to-end mapping, leading to suboptimal modeling of time-varying dynamics and limited interpretability regarding which forecasting mechanism is activated under different latent states. To overcome these limitations, we reformulate time series forecasting as a unified framework of latent temporal state identification and interpretable expert routing, and propose Fuzzy-MoE, a fuzzy logic-based dynamic Mixture-of-Experts model. Fuzzy-MoE consists of multiple parallel expert mapping networks and a dual-view fuzzy router. By jointly exploiting local convolutional dynamics and global segmented statistics, the router infers latent temporal states and computes expert activation strengths through learnable Gaussian membership functions, enabling explicit IF-THEN rule-based expert selection. This fine-grained routing strategy allows different variables within the same sequence to activate different experts, effectively capturing heterogeneous temporal dynamics while improving model interpretability. Experimental results on multiple public time series benchmark datasets show that Fuzzy-MoE significantly outperforms mainstream forecasting methods in forecasting accuracy. Moreover, fuzzy memberships and rule activations provide interpretable routing diagnostics, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed framework in both forecasting performance and mechanism transparency. Unlike traditional MoE models that use black-box routing, Fuzzy-MoE`s routing is based on clear, interpretable fuzzy rules. This makes the expert selection transparent and traceable.

</details>


### [199] [Resolution-Consistent Greedy Neural Approximation on Infinite-Dimensional Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20812)
*Pablo M. Berná,Antonio Falcó,Diego Mondéjar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops theoretical guarantees for shallow neural networks learning from infinite-dimensional inputs (like functions) observed through finitely many coordinates. The analysis uses a parameter-normalized neural dictionary framework to decompose approximation error into coordinate-truncation and network-width terms. A fully-corrective greedy algorithm provides population guarantees with uniform complexity in input resolution, and extends to Hilbert-valued outputs without dimensional dependence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to bridge the gap between infinite-dimensional universal approximation results and practical learning scenarios where infinite-dimensional inputs are only observed through finite measurements. Existing theory lacks constructive guarantees that separate the effects of input resolution, network width, and sample size.

Method: The approach uses a parameter-normalized neural dictionary with associated weighted variation class. The approximation error is decomposed into two components: (1) a distribution-dependent coordinate-truncation term and (2) a greedy finite-width term. For empirical regression, a fully-corrective greedy procedure is employed. The framework extends to Hilbert-valued responses using dimension-free analysis.

Result: The key results include: (1) separation of approximation error into distribution-dependent coordinate-truncation and greedy finite-width terms; (2) population guarantees for empirical regression with statistical complexity uniform in retained input resolution; (3) extension to Hilbert-valued responses without explicit dependence on output dimension; (4) the quasi-Polish construction provides a concrete motivating example; (5) synthetic experiments validate the predicted resolution, width, and sample-size regimes.

Conclusion: The framework provides a theoretical foundation for learning with infinite-dimensional inputs observed through finite measurements. The dimension-free statements (though statistical rather than computational) establish that the method can handle high-dimensional or infinite-dimensional output spaces efficiently. The quasi-Polish construction demonstrates the practical applicability of the theory, and experiments confirm the theoretical predictions.

Abstract: We develop constructive approximation and learning guarantees for shallow neural models with infinite-dimensional inputs observed through finitely many coordinates. The analysis is based on a parameter-normalized neural dictionary and its associated weighted variation class. Within this class, the approximation error separates into a distribution-dependent coordinate-truncation term and a greedy finite-width term. For empirical regression, a fully-corrective greedy procedure yields population guarantees whose statistical complexity is uniform in the retained input resolution. The same framework extends to Hilbert-valued responses without an explicit dependence on the output dimension. The dimension-free statements are statistical, not computational: selecting a new neuron still requires solving a nonconvex parameter-search problem. The quasi-Polish construction underlying recent infinite-dimensional universal approximation results provides a motivating example, and synthetic experiments illustrate the predicted resolution, width, and sample-size regimes.

</details>


### [200] [Scaling Muon for Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20818)
*Chenghao Li,Xiao Han,Xinxin Huang,Wei Liu,Boyang Li,Bing Xiao,Heran Zhang,Juanma Perez Rua,Ke Xu,Kangning Liu,Linjun Kuang,Na Li,Tan Wang,Tian Xie,Wei Peng,Yang Pei,Yifan Xu,Yuanhao Zhai,Yuwei Lin,Zhe Wang,Zihao He,Daniel Li,Junbiao Tang,Ziyang Jiang,Dake Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Periodic Row-wise Muon, an optimized version of the Muon optimizer for training large Diffusion Transformers (DiTs). It balances the quality advantages of Muon (12.9-19.1% better generative quality over AdamW) with practical efficiency through periodic spectral updates and row-wise constrained updates, achieving 46.9-54.3% optimizer time reduction and 15.7-24.3% end-to-end step time improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While Muon improves large model training by balancing updates across singular directions, its scaling behavior and end-to-end efficiency on large DiTs remained unclear. At scale, the 5-step Newton-Schulz iteration (NS5) and full-momentum materialization introduce substantial computation and communication overhead that can offset Muon's step-efficiency advantage.

Method: The authors introduce Periodic Row-wise Muon, which performs a full NS5 spectral update once every K steps and applies a low-cost row-wise constrained update based on current momentum at remaining steps. They co-design a distributed implementation that operates directly on sharded momentum during non-refresh steps and accelerates spectral refreshes through bucketed all-gather and communication-computation overlap.

Result: Across 1.3B to 15B parameter DiTs, Muon improves best generative quality over AdamW by 12.9-19.1%. Compared with vanilla Muon, Periodic Row-wise Muon remains within 0.5% quality on 1.3B-4B models and improves by 4.5% at 9B. It reduces optimizer time by 46.9-54.3%, end-to-end step time by 15.7-24.3%, and logical communication volume by 66.7%, while reaching best quality with 33.7-64.8% less active training time.

Conclusion: Periodic Row-wise Muon successfully preserves Muon's generative quality advantage while translating it into end-to-end training efficiency for large DiTs, demonstrating both quality improvements and significant computational savings at scale.

Abstract: The matrix-aware optimizer Muon improves large model training by balancing updates across singular directions, yet its scaling behavior and end-to-end efficiency on large Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) remain unclear. We first establish Muon's scaling behavior on DiTs from 1.3B to 15B parameters, showing that its optimization and generative quality advantages over AdamW persist across model scales. However, at scale, the 5-step Newton--Schulz iteration (NS5) performed at every optimization step, together with full-momentum materialization, introduces substantial computation and communication overhead that can offset Muon's step-efficiency advantage. We introduce \emph{Periodic Row-wise Muon}, which performs a full NS5 spectral update once every \(K\) steps and applies a low compute and communication cost row-wise constrained update based on the current momentum at the remaining steps. We further co-design a distributed implementation that operates directly on sharded momentum during non-refresh steps and accelerates spectral refreshes through bucketed all-gather and communication--computation overlap. Across all scales, Muon improves the best observed generative quality over AdamW by 12.9--19.1\%. Compared with vanilla Muon, Periodic Row-wise Muon remains within 0.5\% in best generative quality on the 1.3B--4B models and improves it by 4.5\% at 9B. It reduces optimizer time by 46.9--54.3\%, end-to-end step time by 15.7--24.3\%, and logical communication volume by 66.7\%, while reaching its respective best generative quality with 33.7--64.8\% less active training time. These results show that Periodic Row-wise Muon preserves Muon's generative quality advantage while translating it into end-to-end training efficiency for large DiTs.

</details>


### [201] [In-Cell Learning: Deployed Language Models Can Learn New Knowledge Without Changing a Single Stored Bit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20873)
*Zifeng Liu,Yaxin Lu,Xuanhan Wu,Zhiyong Du,Yiming Mao,Zhenhe Wang,Wenqi Shi,Zhengkun Jing,Linwei Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces "in-cell learning," a paradigm for updating deployed 4-bit quantized language models by writing new knowledge into the discarded interval between quantized weight values, rather than modifying the weights themselves. This preserves the exact released weight files while enabling knowledge injection through a separate "fill" file.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The problem is that deployed language models have many dependencies (benchmarks, certifications, fleet devices), and teaching them new knowledge produces different weight files, breaking these dependencies. The authors propose that in-cell learning writes knowledge only into the interval that quantization discards, so re-quantization reproduces the original released codes and scales exactly.

Method: CellFill is proposed: a low-rank position inside the quantized cell, trained on the vendor's own 4-bit release. The update is a separate "fill" file that can be withdrawn by subtraction. The method guarantees drift bounded by radii the grid fixes before training, checked in the integer domain on every weight.

Result: On NF4, QAT and GPTQ-style grids, Qwen3 and Gemma models (1.7B to 31B), CellFill writes 83 to 97 percent of a corpus of real facts the models verifiably did not know, with zero violations over up to 2.4 x 10^10 constrained weights. Injected knowledge is usable: drugs compose with injected ingredients at the model's two-hop ceiling, and a post-cutoff software library is used in code at 2.6 times chance. Against retrieval on PopQA's long tail, the fill answers 82 to 90 percent of questions the released model could not, at 75 tokens per question against 90 to 1008.

Conclusion: CellFill realizes in-cell learning by construction. Multiple writers can share one release, and sequential updates consume a constant fraction of the remaining room. This paradigm enables knowledge injection into deployed quantized models without altering the released weights, maintaining compatibility with existing dependencies.

Abstract: A deployed language model is a file that many things depend on - a benchmark report, a certification, a fleet of devices - and every way of teaching it something new produces a different file. We show that this is not necessary. A 4-bit release stores each weight as an integer code and a shared scale, and leaves between the stored values an interval the quantizer discards; we call in-cell learning the paradigm in which new knowledge is written only into that interval, so that re-quantizing the served weights reproduces the released codes and scales exactly. The guarantee is checked in the integer domain on every weight, the update is a separate file (a fill) that can be withdrawn by subtraction, and drift is bounded by radii the grid fixes before training. CellFill realizes the paradigm by construction: a low-rank position inside the cell, trained on the vendor's own 4-bit release. On those releases - NF4, QAT and GPTQ-style grids, Qwen3 and Gemma, 1.7B to 31B - CellFill writes 83 to 97 percent of a corpus of real facts the models verifiably did not know, with zero violations over up to 2.4 x 10^10 constrained weights. We demonstrate the knowledge is usable: injected drugs compose with injected ingredients at the model's own two-hop ceiling, and a post-cutoff software library is used in code at 2.6 times chance. Against retrieval on PopQA's long tail, the fill answers 82 to 90 percent of questions the released model could not, at 75 tokens per question against 90 to 1008. Several writers can share one release, sequential updates consume a constant fraction of the remaining room, and the archived result files behind every table are at https://github.com/sumsliu/cellfill.

</details>


### [202] [Decoupling Policy Extraction for Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20909)
*Xuyao Lin,Yixiang Shan,Jinru Duan,Tao Yang,Xinyu Zhao,Runyu Lei,Yiming Zhao,Jiaxin Fan,Zongbao Feng,Peng Jia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a decoupled policy extraction paradigm for offline RL that separates actor training (behavior modeling) from policy improvement (inference-time critic-based reranking), addressing key challenges of actor-critic co-training including OOD action amplification and value estimation trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In online RL, the coupled actor-critic paradigm works well because improved actors collect new data that validates/corrects the critic. However, in offline RL with fixed training data, actor-side policy improvement cannot generate new data to validate the critic, leading to two challenges: (1) actor drift toward high-valued but OOD actions that amplify critic overestimation, and (2) difficult trade-off between suppressing OOD actions and selecting high-value actions within data-supported region.

Method: Decouple policy improvement from actor training: (1) Train actor solely to model the behavior distribution (like behavior cloning), (2) At inference time, generate multiple action proposals from the actor and rerank them using a separately learned critic for value-based selection. The actor provides behavior-supported action candidates while the critic performs value-based selection within this candidate set.

Result: The decoupled policy extraction paradigm outperforms both behavior cloning and jointly learned offline RL methods, while remaining effective even with a naive Q-learning critic.

Conclusion: Decoupling policy improvement from actor training provides a more suitable paradigm for offline RL. By separating behavior modeling from value-based selection, the method avoids OOD action amplification issues and achieves superior performance compared to both behavior cloning and traditional coupled offline RL approaches.

Abstract: Offline RL methods commonly jointly train the actor and critic, where the critic is used to guide the actor toward higher-value actions. This coupled learning process is well motivated in online RL, where an improved actor collects new data that can further update the actor and the critic. However, training data remains fixed in offline RL, making actor-side policy improvement unable to generate new data to validate or correct the critic. Moreover, retaining this coupled paradigm leads to two related challenges. Firstly, actor updates can drift toward high-valued but potentially out-of-distribution (OOD) actions and amplify critic overestimation. Secondly, conservative value estimation or behavior-cloning regularization creates a difficult trade-off between suppressing OOD actions and selecting high-value actions within the data-supported region. Motivated by this observation, we revisit the conventional offline RL paradigm and propose decoupling policy improvement from actor training. Specifically, we train the actor solely to model the behavior distribution and perform policy improvement at inference time by reranking multiple actor-generated proposals with a separately learned critic. We refer to this paradigm as the decoupled policy extraction paradigm. Under such paradigm, the actor provides behavior-supported action candidates, while the critic performs value-based selection within this candidate set. Extensive experiments show that the decoupled policy extraction paradigm outperforms both behavior cloning and jointly learned offline RL methods, while remaining effective even with a naive Q-learning critic.

</details>


### [203] [Training, learning and inference: unified dynamics of neural systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20965)
*Mian Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Generation-Fact Graph (GFG) framework that captures atomic generation facts recording neural network training processes, including origin, transformation, occurrence, results, and relation roles. It proposes a unified training-learning dynamics model using nanoGPT, where training involves parameter-optimizer evolution with state and memory, while learning involves persistent reorganization of distributed functional support. A second-order predictor achieves 91.43% accuracy and 91.49% macro-averaged recall. The work extends findings to ResNet/CIFAR-100 and diffusion/CIFAR-10 experiments, demonstrating receiving-state-conditioned responses and frozen inference projection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to create an AI-native, compilable scientific fact substrate that preserves generation histories, enabling recursive scientific processes with analysis, intervention, replay, and validation. They seek to establish unified training-learning dynamics that can predict training outcomes before post-update outputs are generated.

Method: The paper defines atomic generation facts (u, tau, omega, z; rho) compiled into a Generation-Fact Graph (GFG). Using nanoGPT, they model training as evolution of parameter-optimizer systems with state and memory, and learning as persistent reorganization of distributed functional support. Three primary coordinates (target-boundary state, target-specific update geometry, parameter-Adam receiving state) yield a second-order predictor. They also explore inference as frozen projection of training-learning dynamics, with component gating and rollback showing causal recruitment.

Result: The second-order predictor achieved 91.43% accuracy and 91.49% macro-averaged recall across four transitions on held-out runs. Experiments on ResNet/CIFAR-100 and diffusion/CIFAR-10 confirmed receiving-state-conditioned responses, persistent support reorganization, and frozen inference projection beyond nanoGPT.

Conclusion: The GFG framework provides a novel approach to capturing and predicting neural network training dynamics. The unified training-learning dynamics model successfully predicts training outcomes using three primary coordinates, with results generalizing across different architectures (nanoGPT, ResNet) and datasets (CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100). The work suggests that receiving-state-conditioned responses and frozen inference projection are general phenomena in deep learning.

Abstract: We define an atomic generation fact f=(u,tau,omega,z;rho), recording the origin, realized transformation, concrete occurrence, generated result and relation role. Compiled into a Generation-Fact Graph (GFG), these facts provide an AI-native, compilable scientific fact substrate preserving generation histories. We establish a GFG-based recursive scientific process in which analysis, intervention, replay and validation form facts for later cycles. Using nanoGPT, we establish unified training-learning dynamics. Training is the evolution of a parameter-optimizer system with state and memory: each actual training action enters the receiving state and produces a finite-amplitude nonlinear functional response conditioned by that state and target-specific update geometry. Learning is the persistent reorganization of distributed functional support by these responses; capability formation, maintenance, decline or recovery becomes observable when target-specific states are evaluated against their readout boundaries. Three primary coordinates - target-boundary state, target-specific update geometry and parameter-Adam receiving state - yield a second-order predictor operating before post-update outputs are read. On held-out runs, it achieved 91.43% accuracy and 91.49% macro-averaged recall across four transitions. We further establish inference as a frozen projection of training-learning dynamics. Component gating and rollback show causal recruitment and non-additive combination of query-conditioned support formed during training, deriving organizational conditions realized by Attention. Controlled feedback indicates possible double-edged reinforcement effects. ResNet/CIFAR-100 and diffusion/CIFAR-10 experiments confirm receiving-state-conditioned responses, persistent support reorganization and frozen inference projection beyond nanoGPT.

</details>


### [204] [A Critical Audit of Spatiotemporal Forecasting Benchmark Datasets and Baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20980)
*Kenneth Martin,Simon Heilig,Asja Fischer,Michel F. C. Haddad,Adam M. Sykulski,Moshe Eliasof*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper critically examines the reliability of widely-used benchmark datasets for GNN-based spatiotemporal time series forecasting. The authors discover that simple spatially-unaware linear models perform surprisingly well against GNNs, and through statistical analysis, they identify why this occurs - including structural biases from first-order differencing and significant spatial/temporal correlations. They recommend reducing reliance on these datasets and advocate for more rigorous statistical evaluation, demonstrating how their methodology can inform novel hybrid GNN model development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from observing that GNN innovations are predominantly evaluated against a limited set of benchmark datasets, and that simple baselines (including historical averages and classical ML approaches) show competitive performance compared to complex GNNs. This raises questions about the discriminative reliability of these widely adopted datasets and whether they truly evaluate the value added by graph neural networks.

Method: The authors employ classical time series statistical analysis methods to examine the benchmark datasets (Chickenpox, PedalMe, WikiMaths, METR-LA, and PEMS-BAY). They develop a toolset for identifying significant spatial and temporal correlations, and specifically analyze structural biases introduced by first-order differencing - a common preprocessing step in these datasets.

Result: The statistical analysis reveals that spatially-unaware linear models pose a stronger competitor than previously reported. The analysis uncovers significant spatial and temporal correlations in the data, and identifies a structural bias introduced by first-order differenced datasets that advantages simple models. This casts doubt on the discriminative reliability of the widely adopted benchmark datasets.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that there is over-reliance on a limited set of benchmark datasets in the GNN spatiotemporal forecasting field. They recommend reducing this over-reliance and advocate for more rigorous statistical evaluation when comparing methods. By applying their analytical results to a simple hybrid model, they demonstrate that their methodology can lead to novel ways of developing GNN models, providing a pathway for more meaningful benchmarking in the future.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are routinely employed for short-range forecasting on multivariate time series with a spatial graph structure. Despite the availability of many alternative datasets, method innovations within this domain are predominantly assessed against a rather limited set of benchmark datasets, most notably Chickenpox, PedalMe, WikiMaths, METR-LA, and PEMS-BAY. The evaluation protocols contain baselines spanning from historical averages to classical machine learning approaches. These baselines often show competitive performance compared to GNNs. In the present work, we take a step back and analyse the benchmark datasets via classical time series methods to uncover why spatially-unaware linear models pose a stronger competitor than previously reported, casting further doubt on the discriminative reliability of the aforementioned widely adopted datasets. Our statistical analysis provides a toolset for identifying significant spatial and temporal correlations, while revealing a structural bias introduced by first-order differenced datasets. We therefore recommend reducing the over-reliance on such datasets for method comparison, and instead advocate for more rigorous statistical evaluation. By applying the results of our analysis to a simple hybrid model, we show how our methodology can lead to novel ways of developing GNN models

</details>


### [205] [Jacobian-guided Noise Injection for Quantization Robustness in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20988)
*Deepanshu Pandey,Arnav Chavan,Nahush Lele,Sankalp Dayal,Deepak Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses LLM quantization stability by identifying the softmax operator as a key bottleneck due to its sensitivity to outliers and state-dependent Jacobian. The proposed solution, Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection, injects Gaussian noise into pre-attention logits with variance derived from the Jacobian Frobenius norm, offering a theoretically grounded approach to optimal noise variance selection based on local attention sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantization of LLMs is hindered by the sensitivity of the self-attention mechanism to discretization errors. The softmax operator is identified as the bottleneck due to its sensitivity to outliers and state-dependent Jacobian. Existing methods rely on heuristics or directly penalize the Jacobian, lacking a principled approach.

Method: The authors theoretically establish that suppressing the norm of the Jacobian helps bound quantization-induced performance degradation. Based on this, they propose Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection - a training strategy that injects zero-mean Gaussian noise into pre-attention logits. The noise variance is derived directly from the Jacobian Frobenius norm, providing a principled way to identify optimal noise variance based on local attention sensitivity.

Result: The method demonstrates improved robustness over state-of-the-art post-training quantization (PTQ) methods. Empirical results show up to +37% relative gains on Top-1 accuracy on ImageNet-1K for SigLIP and up to 40% relative improvement in perplexity on WikiText for language models in low-bit quantization settings.

Conclusion: The Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection method provides a theoretically grounded and effective approach for quantizing large language models. By deriving optimal noise variance from the Jacobian Frobenius norm, the method successfully addresses the softmax bottleneck in quantization, achieving significant performance improvements across vision and language tasks in low-bit settings.

Abstract: Quantization of Large Language Models (LLMs) is often hindered by the sensitivity of the self-attention mechanism to discretization errors. We identify the softmax operator as a bottleneck for quantization stability due to its sensitivity to outliers and state-dependent Jacobian. We theoretically establish that suppressing the norm of this Jacobian helps in bounding quantization-induced performance degradation. Based on this, we propose Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection, a training strategy that injects zero-mean Gaussian noise into pre-attention logits, with variance derived directly from the Jacobian Frobenius norm. Unlike prior approaches that rely on heuristic or penalise jacobian directly, our method provides a way to identify the optimal noise variance based on the local attention sensitivity. We evaluate the method on SOTA LLM architectures, where it demonstrates improved robustness over popular PTQ methods. Empirical analysis reveals that the proposed method gives up to +37% relative gains on Top-1 accuracy on ImageNet-1K for SigLIP and improves relative perplexity by upto 40% on WikiText for language models in low bit quantisation settings, proving the efficacy of the approach.

</details>


### [206] [Trojaning the Alignment: Stealthy Backdoor Attacks against Graph Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20991)
*Minhua Lin,Zhicheng Gao,Yilong Wang,Hanqing Lu,Xiang Zhang,Suhang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents STAG, a novel stealthy trojan attack targeting Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) on Text-Attributed Graphs (TAGs). It coordinates graph-trigger generation with text-side soft prompts to manipulate aligned graph-text representations, while maintaining stealthiness through readable text triggers and structure regularization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing backdoor attacks target either graph or text modalities independently, making them ineffective under graph-language alignment where the two modalities constrain each other. Additionally, TAGs pose stealth challenges as triggers are exposed as both node text and local graph structure, making incoherent triggers easy to detect.

Method: STAG coordinates a graph-trigger generator with a text-side soft prompt so that trigger-attached graph representations and triggered text representations converge toward the same target-class text region. It uses candidate retrieval to generate readable text triggers and regularizes the trigger-attached subgraph to maintain local structure similarity to the original subgraph.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple TAG datasets and representative GFMs demonstrate the effectiveness and stealthiness of STAG.

Conclusion: The proposed STAG framework successfully exploits the graph-language alignment interface of GFMs on TAGs, achieving effective and stealthy backdoor attacks that overcome the cross-modal constraint problem.

Abstract: Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) on text-attributed graphs (TAGs) align graph representations with language semantics to support transferable graph learning. Despite these advantages, the backdoor vulnerability of GFMs on TAGs remains insufficiently understood, especially under graph-language alignment, where graph and text representations are trained to constrain each other in a shared semantic space. Existing backdoor attacks mainly target either the graph side or the text side, treating the two modalities independently. This makes direct adaptation ineffective: graph-only triggers can be constrained by clean text semantics, while text-only triggers alter the language view but do not directly shift the graph representation being aligned and scored. TAGs also impose a stealth challenge because triggers are exposed as both node text and local graph structure, making incoherent trigger attributes or anomalous subgraphs easy to inspect or filter. In this paper, we propose STAG, a stealthy trojan attack framework designed for the graph-language alignment interface of GFMs on TAGs. STAG coordinates a graph-trigger generator with a text-side soft prompt so that trigger-attached graph representations and triggered text representations move toward the same target-class text region. To address TAG-specific stealthiness, STAG realizes trigger nodes as readable text through candidate retrieval and regularizes the trigger-attached subgraph so that its local structure remains close to the original subgraph. Extensive experiments on multiple TAG datasets and representative GFMs demonstrate the effectiveness and stealthiness of STAG. Our code is available at https://github.com/ventr1c/STAG.

</details>


### [207] [Free-Probability Kernels for Zero-Rollout Hyperparameter Selection in Reservoir Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20998)
*Sara Malacarne,Andrea Ceni,Claudio Gallicchio*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a deterministic method using free probability theory to select hyperparameters for reservoir computing without requiring multiple simulation rollouts. The approach approximates finite-reservoir feature geometry using a temporal kernel derived from cross-lag propagation coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reservoir computing efficiency is compromised during hyperparameter selection, as the recurrent gain, input scale, and leakage rate need to be tuned through many rollouts. This is computationally expensive and time-consuming.

Method: The authors use free probability theory to derive cross-lag propagation coefficients that summarize how the reservoir mixes past inputs. In the large-width limit, these coefficients define a deterministic temporal kernel that approximates finite-reservoir feature geometry. Kernel ridge regression on a short labelled pilot sequence ranks candidate operating regimes without instantiating or rolling out a reservoir.

Result: On ten synthetic temporal benchmarks, zero-rollout selection achieves mean deployment score of 0.772 vs 0.774 for exhaustive search, avoiding 156,600 selection rollouts. With small rollout budget, the method provides strongest mean performance at every tested budget and reaches exhaustive reference using only 4.8% of its rollout cost. On four ETT forecasting datasets, five retained candidates recover the exhaustive operating point on three datasets. On multivariate cellular-traffic forecasting, 15 rollouts per cell reach the 462-rollout exhaustive reference and outperform random search and Bayesian optimization at low budgets.

Conclusion: Free-probability kernels serve as deterministic surrogates for selecting reservoir operating regimes when validation rollouts are scarce, significantly reducing computational cost while maintaining performance comparable to exhaustive search.

Abstract: Reservoir computing (RC) couples a fixed recurrent dynamical system with a trained lightweight readout, but this efficiency is partly lost during hyperparameter selection: the recurrent gain, input scale, and leakage rate determine the reservoir's stability and temporal processing regime and are usually tuned through many rollouts. We introduce a deterministic, pilot-informed selector for leaky linear reservoirs followed by coordinate-wise nonlinear features. Free probability yields cross-lag propagation coefficients that summarize how the reservoir mixes past inputs. In the large-width limit, these coefficients define a deterministic temporal kernel that approximates the finite-reservoir feature geometry. Kernel ridge regression on a short labelled pilot sequence therefore ranks candidate operating regimes without instantiating or rolling out a reservoir, and the selected configuration transfers across widths. Across ten synthetic temporal benchmarks, zero-rollout selection obtains a mean deployment score of $0.772$, compared with $0.774$ for exhaustive simulation-based search, while avoiding $156\,600$ selection rollouts. With a small rollout budget, the proposed ranking provides the strongest mean performance at every tested budget and reaches the exhaustive reference using $4.8\%$ of its rollout cost. On four public electricity-transformer-temperature (ETT) forecasting datasets, five retained candidates recover the exhaustive operating point on three datasets. On multivariate cellular-traffic forecasting, 15 rollouts per cell reach the 462-rollout exhaustive reference and outperform random search and Bayesian optimization at low budgets. These results position free-probability kernels as deterministic surrogates for selecting reservoir operating regimes when validation rollouts are scarce.

</details>


### [208] [RODE: A Radial-Orthogonal Decoupled Engine for Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21024)
*Guoxiang Xu,Bince Qu,Qi Sun,Cheng Zhuo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RODE is a novel optimizer that decouples radial (norm) and directional components in matrix-aware optimization, using separate update rules and step sizes for each component to provide more effective and controllable neural network training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current matrix-aware optimizers add conditioned matrix steps directly to weights, jointly changing norm and direction. This interaction is problematic because the current norm determines angular motion, while directional learning can drive norm growth and alter subsequent steps. The authors argue these two dynamics should be separated for better control and effectiveness.

Method: RODE introduces separate update rules for radial and directional components: (1) Radial channel explicitly updates the matrix Frobenius norm through a scalar radial rule; (2) Directional channel performs Newton-Schulz-conditioned updates in the tangent space. This decoupling allows independent control of norm growth and directional learning.

Result: At 1.5B scale, RODE lowers loss from 4.145 to 3.346 and final global norm from 11964 to 2183 relative to Muon RMS, with fixed-radius RODE improving further. For Qwen3.5-9B full-parameter fine-tuning, RODE outperforms both Muon variants on all four evaluation tasks and achieves highest mean on GSM8K and MATH-500. Across two language-modeling and two image-classification tasks, RODE outperforms both Muon variants in every direct comparison and ends with lower full-model norms.

Conclusion: Decoupling radial and directional dynamics offers a more effective and controllable approach to matrix optimization. The separate control over norm and direction enables better training stability and performance across various model scales and tasks.

Abstract: Modern neural network training increasingly uses matrix-aware optimizers, yet their conditioned matrix step is typically added directly to the weight, jointly changing its norm and direction. This interaction matters because the current norm determines angular motion, while directional learning can drive norm growth and thereby alter later steps. We introduce RODE, which gives the radial and directional components separate update rules and step sizes. RODE explicitly updates the matrix Frobenius norm through a scalar radial rule, while its directional channel performs Newton--Schulz-conditioned updates in the tangent space. Controlled GPT-2 interventions show gains from both direct norm control and RODE's directional update. Across two language-modeling and two image-classification tasks, RODE outperforms both Muon variants in every direct comparison and ends with lower full-model norms. At 1.5B scale, using the learning rate transferred directly from the Qwen2-style LM sweep, RODE lowers loss from 4.145 to 3.346 and final global norm from 11964 to 2183 relative to Muon RMS, with fixed-radius RODE improving further. For Qwen3.5-9B full-parameter fine-tuning, all six optimizers use the same tuning budget and the same formal-training and evaluation settings; RODE outperforms both Muon variants on all four evaluation tasks and attains the highest mean on GSM8K and MATH-500. Thus, decoupling radial and directional dynamics offers a more effective and controllable approach to matrix optimization.

</details>


### [209] [Designing a Robust LLM-Based Evaluation System for Agentic AI in Drug Discovery Through Human Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21057)
*Emma Granqvist,Rocío Mercado,Samuel Genheden*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents an LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation framework for ChatInvent, an agentic drug discovery assistant at AstraZeneca, addressing the challenge of evaluating open-ended, tool-augmented outputs in scientific workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic LLM systems are transforming chemistry and drug discovery, but evaluating their outputs remains a bottleneck. Reference-based metrics like BLEU and ROUGE fail to capture semantic correctness, while expert human evaluation doesn't scale to the iteration speed these systems demand. The LLM-as-a-Judge paradigm offers a scalable alternative, but existing benchmarks lack validation of judge alignment with human experts.

Method: The study defines four evaluation dimensions (Completeness, Relevancy, Structural Clarity, Scope Adherence) alongside deterministic Tool Call Correctness checks. A human alignment study was conducted with five expert annotators comparing four LLM judges (Gemini 3.1 Pro, Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5, Llama 3.1 70B). The best-performing judge was optimized using few-shot demonstrations of human-annotated examples, then applied to 70 held-out questions.

Result: The optimized judge improved alignment with human majority vote from 0.80 to 0.86. The study found that informal phrasings do not systematically degrade output quality, and having the LLM rewrite the original question before querying the agent is actually helpful.

Conclusion: The framework provides a reusable template for human-aligned evaluation of agentic systems in scientific domains, with validated LLM judges that align well with expert human evaluation.

Abstract: Agentic large language model (LLM) systems are reshaping scientific workflows in chemistry and drug discovery, but evaluating their open-ended, tool-augmented outputs remains a fundamental bottleneck. Reference-based metrics such as BLEU and ROUGE fail to capture semantic correctness, while expert human evaluation does not scale to the iteration speed these systems demand. The LLM-as-a-Judge paradigm has emerged as a scalable alternative, but existing drug discovery benchmarks deploy LLM judges without validating their alignment with human experts. In this work, we present an LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation framework for ChatInvent, an agentic drug discovery assistant deployed at AstraZeneca, with four contributions. First, we define four output-quality evaluation dimensions---Completeness, Relevancy, Structural Clarity, and Scope Adherence---alongside deterministic Tool Call Correctness checks. Second, we validate the judge through a human alignment study with five expert annotators, comparing Gemini 3.1 Pro, Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5, and Llama 3.1 70B as candidate judges. Third, we optimize the best-performing judge using few-shot demonstrations of human-annotated examples, improving alignment with the human majority vote from 0.80 to 0.86. Fourth, applying the optimized judge to 70 held-out questions, we surface concrete limitations and find that informal phrasings do not systematically degrade output quality; if anything, it is helpful to have the LLM rewrite the original question before querying the agent. Our framework provides a reusable template for human-aligned evaluation of agentic systems in scientific domains.

</details>


### [210] [TracingFlow: A Simulation-Free Trajectory Inference Framework Based on Second-Order Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21070)
*Yuhao Sun,Zekun Wu,Zixun Huang,Peijie Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Inferring continuous system evolution from sparse temporal snapshots is a key challenge in generative modeling and single-cell omics. While Optimal Transport (OT) is popular, existing frameworks are largely restricted to first-order dynamics, assuming memoryless velocity fields. This limits expressiveness, as first-order systems fail to account for regulatory momentum and time-delayed responses inherent in processes like cell differentiation. Here, we introduce TracingFlow, a simulation-free Flow Matching framework generalizing to second-order dynamics. By using neural networks to regress the acceleration field, TracingFlow provides an exact, efficient solution to the Dynamical Optimal Acceleration Transport (DOAT) problem. Unlike first-order methods yielding over-smoothed trajectories, our second-order formulation captures high-curvature transitions and nonlinear evolutions by learning the underlying force fields. Evaluated on complex synthetic and large-scale scRNA-seq datasets, TracingFlow achieves superior accuracy in distributional reconstruction and trajectory faithfulness. Moreover, by integrating lineage tracing priors, it recovers dynamical structures that are both mathematically optimal and biologically plausible.

</details>


### [211] [Causal Modeling of Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes via Adaptive LLM Proposals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21079)
*Kavimayil P. Komarasamy,Saurabh Mathur,Ameet Soni,David M. Haas,Kristian Kersting,Sriraam Natarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A neurosymbolic framework that iteratively combines Large Language Model (LLM) prior knowledge with empirical scoring to generate plausible causal hypotheses for Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes (APOs) like preterm birth and gestational diabetes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: APOs have long-term consequences for mother and child, but their causes remain poorly understood due to limited data and incomplete domain knowledge. Pure data-driven methods fail, and LLM outputs are inconsistent or contradictory.

Method: The framework treats the LLM as an adaptive proposal distribution generating causal hypotheses, which are then scored against empirical data. High-scoring graphs update the LLM's context to steer subsequent generations toward promising hypothesis space regions. Evaluated on a real-world clinical dataset for APOs and risk factors.
