Result: The method recovers all expert-validated edges and identifies additional plausible causal relations not previously listed by experts.

Conclusion: This approach can provide new insights for targeted interventions in APOs by discovering novel causal relationships beyond expert knowledge.

Abstract: Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes (APOs) such as preterm birth and gestational diabetes can have long-term consequences for both the mother and child, yet an understanding of their causes remains elusive. Causal discovery in this domain is especially challenging due to a paucity of data and incomplete domain knowledge. As a result, pure data-driven methods fail, and Large Language Model (LLM) outputs remain inconsistent or contradictory. We introduce a neurosymbolic framework for generating plausible causal hypotheses that iteratively combines the broad prior knowledge of LLMs with empirical scoring on data. Our method treats the LLM as an adaptive proposal distribution, generating hypotheses that are scored against empirical data; the resulting high-scoring graphs are then used to update the LLM's context, steering subsequent generations toward more promising regions of the hypothesis space. We evaluate our approach on a real-world clinical dataset for modeling APOs and their risk factors, comparing our results against an expert-constructed causal graph. Our method recovers all expert-validated edges and identifies additional plausible causal relations not previously listed by experts, potentially providing new insights for targeted interventions.

</details>


### [212] [FlatLand: Personalized Graph Federated Learning via Tailored Lorentz Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21096)
*Jiahong Liu,Ram Samarth B B,Xinyu Fu,Menglin Yang,Weixi Zhang,Rex Ying,Irwin King*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlatLand is a personalized federated learning method for heterogeneous graph data that leverages hyperbolic geometry (Lorentz space) to capture the intrinsic negative curvature of diverse graph structures. It uses a parameter decoupling strategy to separate client-specific heterogeneous information from common knowledge, enabling direct aggregation without requiring client similarity estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing personalized federated learning methods ignore the intrinsic geometric properties of diverse graph structures when handling highly heterogeneous client data in graph federated learning scenarios where clients possess structurally diverse graphs.

Method: The paper proposes embedding different clients' data in tailored Lorentz space of hyperbolic geometry, where the time-like dimension encodes client-specific heterogeneity. A parameter decoupling strategy is developed that separates heterogeneous information (captured in time-like parameters) from common knowledge (preserved in space-like parameters), enabling direct aggregation.

Result: FlatLand achieves superior performance on diverse federated graph learning tasks, particularly in low-dimensional settings.

Conclusion: Hyperbolic geometry naturally accommodates the intrinsic negative curvature prevalent in real-world graphs, and the time-like dimension in Lorentz space provides a principled way to encode client-specific heterogeneity. The parameter decoupling approach eliminates the need for client similarity estimation and extra calculation modules.

Abstract: Federated learning enables privacy-preserving collaborative training, but highly heterogeneous client data remain challenging, especially in graph federated learning where clients possess structurally diverse graphs. Existing personalized federated learning (PFL) methods ignore the intrinsic geometric properties of diverse graph structures. We propose FlatLand, a novel personalized federated learning method that embeds different clients' data in tailored Lorentz space of hyperbolic geometry. Our key insight is that hyperbolic geometry naturally accommodates the intrinsic negative curvature prevalent in real-world graphs, while the time-like dimension in Lorentz space provides a principled way to encode client-specific heterogeneity. We develop a parameter decoupling strategy that separates heterogeneous information (captured in time-like parameters) from common knowledge (preserved in space-like parameters), enabling direct aggregation without requiring client similarity estimation and extra calculation modules. Empirical results on diverse federated graph learning tasks demonstrate that FlatLand achieves superior performance, particularly in low-dimensional settings.

</details>


### [213] [BackDFL: A Unified Benchmark For Backdoor Attacks and Defenses In Decentralized Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21137)
*Mouhamed Amine Bouchiha,Gregory Blanc,Yufei Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents BackDFL, a benchmark for evaluating Decentralized Federated Learning (DFL) under realistic backdoor attacks, revealing that existing Byzantine-robust methods fail even with modest (15%) malicious participation rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decentralized Federated Learning (DFL) promises trust-free collaborative learning by using peer-to-peer model exchange instead of centralized servers. However, without globally coordinated aggregation, DFL becomes particularly vulnerable to backdoor attacks where malicious participants implant hidden behaviors while maintaining high clean-task performance. The authors argue that the robustness of DFL has been significantly overestimated due to limitations in existing studies.

Method: The paper presents BackDFL, a unified benchmark designed to systematically evaluate DFL under realistic and adaptive backdoor attacks. It addresses limitations of prior work including simplified threat models, non-adaptive adversaries, fragmented evaluation protocols, inconsistent communication topologies, and ad hoc training configurations.

Result: Extensive experiments expose critical failure modes of decentralized learning. Both state-of-the-art Byzantine-robust DFL methods and adapted FL backdoor defenses fail under modest malicious participation rates as low as 15%, especially in heterogeneous settings. Additionally, robustness varies substantially across different communication graph topologies.

Conclusion: The security of DFL has been significantly overestimated. Current defense methods are inadequate against realistic backdoor attacks, highlighting the need for more robust DFL mechanisms and better evaluation frameworks.

Abstract: Decentralized Federated Learning (DFL) promises trust-free collaborative learning by replacing the centralized parameter server with peer-to-peer model exchange. However, this architectural shift fundamentally reshapes the threat landscape. Without globally coordinated aggregation, DFL becomes particularly susceptible to backdoor attacks, in which malicious participants implant persistent hidden behaviors while maintaining high clean-task performance. In this paper, we argue that the robustness of DFL has been significantly overestimated. Existing studies rely on simplified threat models, non-adaptive adversaries, fragmented evaluation protocols, inconsistent communication topologies, and ad hoc training configurations, leading to an incomplete understanding of DFL security. To address these limitations, we present BackDFL, a unified benchmark for systematically evaluating DFL under realistic and adaptive backdoor attacks. Through extensive experiments, BackDFL exposes critical failure modes of decentralized learning. Our results demonstrate that both state-of-the-art Byzantine-robust DFL methods and adapted FL backdoor defenses fail under modest malicious participation rates (as low as 15%), especially in heterogeneous settings, while their robustness varies substantially across communication graph topologies.

</details>


### [214] [COEC: Calibrated Orthogonal-Equivalence Compensation for Structured Pruning of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21142)
*Peiqi Yu,Nam Ling,Wei Wang,Wei Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Structured pruning reduces the size and inference cost of large language models (LLMs) by removing weight columns, but the resulting output error can degrade accuracy. Existing training-free compensation methods use an additive bias or a single orthogonal rotation on the output side of the retained weight. These corrections leave its input singular frame unchanged and therefore limit how the retained weight can adapt after column removal. We propose COEC (Calibrated Orthogonal-Equivalence Compensation), a training-free compensation framework that applies alternating left and right orthogonal rotations to the retained weight. The right rotation is optimized on a reduced Stiefel manifold, while singular values are rescaled using generalized cross-validation to select the regularization strength for each layer. COEC further tempers the calibration Gram matrix to reduce the dominance of high-energy activation directions and introduces an alignment penalty that preserves the geometric relation between adjacent attention projections.All components use second-order statistics from a small calibration set and require neither backpropagation through the LLM nor retraining of the model parameters. COEC is independent of the column pruning criterion and can be applied to multiple structured pruning methods. Experiments on the Llama-3, Llama-3.1, and Qwen2.5 model families across multiple structured sparsity levels show that COEC improves perplexity on every model and zero-shot accuracy in most settings over existing compensation methods, with larger gains at higher sparsity. These results show that post-pruning compensation can recover part of the performance lost to column removal.

</details>


### [215] [Capturing Cardiac Cyclicity through Phase-Equivariant Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21147)
*Blaise Delaney,Dominic Dootson,Juan Jose Juan Castella,Salil Patel,Andrew Pfaff,Yuji Xing,Jonny Hancox,Karin Sevegnani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Winder is a self-supervised learning method for ECG data that exploits the cyclic nature of cardiac cycles by encoding phase symmetry into the model architecture. It uses a fixed, closed-form transport operator to organize representations into phase-invariant coordinates and phase-rotating harmonic subspaces, achieving competitive diagnostic accuracy with ~1M parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The cardiac cycle provides a well-defined cyclic structure that can serve as a natural prior for self-supervised representation learning. Existing methods don't explicitly leverage this phase symmetry, which could preserve diagnostically useful information while yielding a more interpretable latent geometry tied to a measurable physiological quantity (cardiac phase).

Method: The paper derives a phase-equivariant self-supervised objective and introduces Winder, a joint-embedding architecture. Key innovations include: (1) organizing representations into phase-invariant coordinates and phase-rotating harmonic subspaces, (2) using a fixed closed-form transport operator derived from the cycle's geometry (adds no parameters), (3) evaluated on PTB-XL under a frozen linear-probe protocol.

Result: Winder achieves diagnostic accuracy within the range reported by state-of-the-art self-supervised methods while using only ~1M parameters. The model exhibits phase-equivariant latent geometry, demonstrating that explicitly encoding cardiac-phase symmetry can preserve diagnostically useful information.

Conclusion: Explicitly encoding cardiac-phase symmetry preserves diagnostically useful information while yielding a latent geometry that is legible, parameter-efficient, and directly tied to a measurable physiological quantity (cardiac phase). The fixed transport operator approach adds no parameters while maintaining competitive performance.

Abstract: The cyclic structure of physiological processes offers a natural prior for self-supervised representation learning, and the cardiac cycle provides a particularly well-defined setting in which to exploit it. We derive a phase-equivariant self-supervised objective and introduce Winder, a joint-embedding architecture that organises representations into phase-invariant coordinates and phase-rotating harmonic subspaces. Its transport operator is fixed and closed-form, derived from the cycle's geometry rather than learned, and adds no parameters. Evaluated on PTB-XL under a frozen linear-probe protocol, Winder attains diagnostic accuracy within the range reported by state-of-the-art self-supervised methods at a ~1 M parameter footprint, while exhibiting phase-equivariant latent geometry. These findings demonstrate that explicitly encoding cardiac-phase symmetry can preserve diagnostically useful information while yielding a latent geometry that is legible, parameter-efficient, and directly tied to a measurable physiological quantity.

</details>


### [216] [Thermo-FL: Thermal-Aware Robust Federated Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models for Edge AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21172)
*Shiva Shrestha,Kazi Shaharair Sharif,Zongxing Xie,Jiajing Huang,Anhao Xiang,Honghui Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Thermo-FL is a thermal-aware federated LoRA fine-tuning framework that addresses hardware instability (thermal throttling) and adversarial update corruption simultaneously by using device temperature as a control signal for workload regulation and introducing TERRA, a robust aggregation pipeline for dynamically sparse LoRA updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning enables LLMs to adapt on edge devices without centralizing private data, but practical deployments face two critical challenges: (1) thermal constraints causing device throttling, slowdown, or delayed aggregation, and (2) Byzantine clients or communication-layer adversaries corrupting updates used for global model formation.

Method: Thermo-FL operates at both client and server levels: (1) Client-side: dynamically adjusts active LoRA-layer fraction and transmitted update density based on device temperature to reduce workload under thermal stress; (2) Server-side (TERRA): combines norm filtering, mask-aware directional validation, adaptive active-coordinate clipping, and mask-aware aggregation for robust sparse update aggregation. Evaluation uses both large-scale emulator and Jetson-based physical testbed.

Result: In emulator: Thermo-FL improves robustness under adversarial sparse aggregation and achieves the strongest BoolQ accuracy across clean and attack settings while remaining competitive on GSM8K. In physical prototype: stabilizes device temperature, reduces compressed upload size through bitmap sparse encoding, and preserves GSM8K utility under sign-flip/scale and MITM perturbations.

Conclusion: Secure edge LLM adaptation should jointly consider hardware behavior, workload regulation, sparse communication, and aggregation robustness. Thermo-FL demonstrates the feasibility and effectiveness of integrating thermal awareness with Byzantine-resilient aggregation in federated LLM fine-tuning.

Abstract: Federated fine-tuning enables large language models to adapt on edge devices without centralizing private data, but practical deployments must address hardware instability and adversarial update corruption together. Thermally constrained clients may throttle, slow local training, or delay synchronous aggregation, while Byzantine clients and communication-layer adversaries can corrupt the updates used to form the global model. To address these challenges, we present Thermo-FL, a thermal-aware federated LoRA fine-tuning framework that uses device temperature as an active control signal for local adapter training and sparse update transmission. On the client side, Thermo-FL adjusts the active LoRA-layer fraction and transmitted update density as devices heat or cool, reducing workload under thermal stress. On the server side, Thermo-FL introduces TERRA, a robust aggregation pipeline for dynamically sparse LoRA updates that combines norm filtering, mask-aware directional validation, adaptive active-coordinate clipping, and mask-aware aggregation. We evaluate Thermo-FL using both a large-scale emulator and a Jetson-based physical testbed. In the emulator, Thermo-FL improves robustness under adversarial sparse aggregation and achieves the strongest BoolQ accuracy across clean and attack settings while remaining competitive on GSM8K. In the physical prototype, Thermo-FL stabilizes device temperature, reduces compressed upload size through bitmap sparse encoding, and preserves GSM8K utility under sign-flip/scale and MITM perturbations. These results show that secure edge LLM adaptation should jointly consider hardware behavior, workload regulation, sparse communication, and aggregation robustness.

</details>


### [217] [A Neurosymbolic Approach for Constructing Planning Domain Models from Clinical Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21186)
*Ranveer Singh,Saurabh Mathur,Michael Skinner,Prasad Tadepalli,Kristian Kersting,Sriraam Natarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NSPIN is a neurosymbolic framework that induces probabilistic planning domain models (PPDDL) from unstructured clinical narratives (laparoscopic appendectomy notes) by combining LLMs with empirical symbolic methods to extract, impute, and refine structured event sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Formalizing complex surgical workflows like laparoscopic appendectomy for decision support is challenging due to lack of structured event data and implicit actions in clinical narratives that existing methods cannot adequately address.

Method: NSPIN uses a pretrained LLM to extract and impute structured event sequences from raw text, then induces a PPDDL model and refines its preconditions with LLM-proposed revisions guided by empirical validation.

Result: Evaluated on 2,660 laparoscopic appendectomy notes from 9 surgeons, NSPIN produces models that generalize to unseen notes, and expert review confirms the induced knowledge is largely consistent with surgical practice.

Conclusion: NSPIN successfully bridges the gap between unstructured clinical narratives and formal probabilistic planning models, offering a viable approach for workflow formalization in complex surgical settings.

Abstract: Surgical procedures such as laparoscopic appendectomy are complex, high-stakes processes, yet formalizing their workflows for decision support remains a significant challenge. Inducing probabilistic planning domain models in this setting is particularly difficult due to the lack of structured event data and the prevalence of implicit actions in clinical narratives, which neither empirical symbolic methods nor Large Language Models (LLMs) can adequately address on their own. We introduce NSPIN, a neurosymbolic framework for inducing probabilistic planning domain models from unstructured clinical narratives. Our method extracts and imputes structured event sequences from raw text using a pretrained LLM, then induces a PPDDL model and refines its preconditions with LLM-proposed revisions, guided by empirical validation. We evaluate the approach on 2,660 laparoscopic appendectomy notes written by 9 surgeons. NSPIN yields models that generalize to unseen notes, and expert clinical review indicates its induced knowledge is largely consistent with surgical practice.

</details>


### [218] [Tydra: An Efficient Hybrid Model for Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21199)
*Mieszko Komisarczyk,Saurabh Mathur,Maurice Kraus,Sriraam Natarajan,Kristian Kersting*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tydra is a hybrid Transformer-SSM architecture for tabular in-context learning that interleaves attention and SSM layers, achieving 30% faster inference than TabPFN while maintaining predictive performance and outperforming a 10x larger Hydra model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the trade-off between quadratic computational cost of Transformer-based models (e.g., TabPFN) and the accuracy trade-offs of subquadratic SSM alternatives (e.g., Hydra) in tabular foundation models.

Method: Hybrid architecture interleaving attention and State Space Model (SSM) layers, evaluated across 30 OpenML datasets.

Result: Reduces inference time by 30% relative to TabPFN while retaining most predictive performance; outperforms a ~10x larger Hydra model with faster inference.

Conclusion: Hybrid Transformer-SSM architectures present a promising direction for balancing efficiency and accuracy in tabular foundation models.

Abstract: Transformer-based tabular foundation models such as TabPFN achieve strong predictive performance but incur quadratic computational cost with context length. On the other hand, subquadratic SSM-based alternatives such as Hydra trade away accuracy for efficiency. To balance both, we introduce Tydra, a hybrid Transformer-State Space Model (SSM) architecture for tabular in-context learning that interleaves attention and SSM layers. Across 30 OpenML datasets, Tydra reduces inference time by 30% relative to TabPFN while retaining much of its predictive performance. Tydra also outperforms an approximately ten-times-larger Hydra model while providing faster inference. The results indicate that hybrid architectures are a promising direction for tabular foundation models.

</details>


### [219] [Curriculum-Aware Interpolate-then-Refine: Learned Physiological Time-Series Imputation under Realistic Missingness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21207)
*Yu-Chao Huang,Haochen Zhang,Nicholas Konz,Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses poor imputation performance on physiological time series data by introducing CAIR, a two-stage framework combining a bidirectional-GRU interpolator with a Transformer refiner that iteratively corrects its estimates. The key insight is that physiological missingness has two underappreciated properties: gaps occur during clinically extreme values, and gap lengths span multiple orders of magnitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern imputation methods perform poorly on clinical physiological data - a recent benchmark found simple linear interpolation outperformed all learned imputers on real-world clinical signals. This occurs because generic imputers ignore two critical properties of physiological missingness: (1) gaps tend to occur when signals are clinically extreme rather than typical, and (2) gap lengths can span orders of magnitude, making uniform training ineffective.

Method: The proposed CAIR framework uses a two-stage interpolate-then-refine approach. First, a bidirectional-GRU creates a coarse base curve through interpolation. Then, a Transformer refiner corrects this estimate over three successive passes, learning to improve toward physiological realism iteratively. The model is trained jointly under a broad, signal-agnostic random-gap curriculum that exposes it to varied gap lengths. Evaluation is stratified by both gap length and missingness mechanism (MCAR, MAR, NMAR) rather than aggregated averages.

Result: CAIR achieves the highest accuracy under every missingness mechanism on both AI-READI (continuous glucose monitoring) and MIMIC-III (arterial pressure in intensive care) datasets. The performance margin over the strongest baseline increases with difficulty: 9% under MCAR (random dropout) to 19% under value-dependent dropout (NMAR), where generic learned imputers perform worst. Additionally, CAIR uniquely balances low reconstruction error with preservation of burden metrics (clinically relevant outcomes) that other methods fail to maintain.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that physiological time series imputation requires specialized approaches that account for the unique structure of clinical missingness. CAIR's success stems from its iterative refinement architecture and curriculum-based training that handles variable gap lengths. The work also highlights that reconstruction error alone is insufficient for clinical applications - imputed values must preserve the burden metrics clinicians act upon. This represents a significant advance for clinical decision support systems relying on continuous physiological monitoring.

Abstract: Imputing physiological time series (arterial blood pressure, blood glucose, etc.) is essential for addressing the missingness that pervades clinical data. Yet modern imputation methods perform poorly in this domain: a recent benchmark found that simple linear interpolation outperformed every learned imputer on real-world clinical signals with realistic gaps. We show that this reflects two properties of physiological missingness that generic imputers ignore: gaps may occur when the signal is clinically extreme rather than typical, and gap lengths can easily span orders of magnitude. To this end, we introduce Curriculum-Aware Interpolate-then-Refine (CAIR), a two-stage framework for physiological time-series imputation. Our key motivation is to learn a coarse base curve and then repeatedly correct it toward physiological realism, rather than predict a gap in a single pass. Consequently, CAIR couples a bidirectional-GRU interpolator with a Transformer refiner that corrects its own estimate over three successive passes, trained jointly under a broad, signal-agnostic random-gap curriculum. We evaluate imputers stratified by gap length and missingness mechanism (MCAR, MAR, NMAR) rather than by a single average, and CAIR is the most accurate under every mechanism on continuous glucose monitoring (AI-READI) and arterial pressure in intensive care (MIMIC-III). Its margin over the strongest baseline grows with difficulty, from 9% under MCAR to 19% under value-dependent dropout, where generic learned imputers are weakest. We further show low reconstruction error alone does not recover the burden metrics clinicians act on: interpolants matching CAIR's error fail to preserve those metrics, imputers that recover them are far less accurate, and CAIR alone ranks among the best on both axes.

</details>


### [220] [TRACE-C: Rank-Calibrated Relational Anomaly Detection for Multi-Stream Operational Telemetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21251)
*Matthew Faucher*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRACE-C is an auditable rank-calibrated detector for joint anomalies in multi-stream telemetry, using three channel approaches (local sum, Gaussian copula-form dependence, AR(1) innovation) applied to Great Britain grid data. The method identifies Storm Atiyah as top-ranked, but ablation reveals the copula channel contributes minimally; the 2020 hold-out selects no windows due to saturation effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses detecting joint anomalies in operational telemetry where individual streams may appear normal. Existing methods struggle with aligned multi-stream detection, particularly in maintaining auditability and proper calibration. The goal is to develop a strictly-prior rank-calibrated detector that can reliably identify anomalous events across multiple correlated telemetry streams.

Method: TRACE-C uses same-regime rolling median/MAD residuals fed into three window channels: (1) maximum normalized local sum, (2) Gaussian copula-form dependence contrast on robust-z residuals, and (3) worst standardized AR(1) innovation. Channel ranks are Fisher-aggregated and ranked against earlier aggregates. The method avoids probability-integral transforms and treats the copula-form channel as a dependence contrast rather than a literal copula density.

Result: TRACE-C ranks Storm Atiyah first among 2019 test windows, but ablation shows this ranking derives from the local channel (copula-only ranks Atiyah 59th). The 9 August frequency event ranks 143rd in the fused detector vs 40th in the temporal channel alone. Reconstruction baselines rank it first. In 2020, no window is selected, with the highest-ranked frozen window later identified as Storm Ellen.

Conclusion: The detector's performance is primarily driven by the local channel rather than the copula-form dependence channel. The three interpretive limits are crucial: p-values represent selection quantities, not event probabilities; the copula-form channel lacks probability-integral transformation; and empirical rank counts serve as diagnostics only. The 2020 non-selection reflects record-rule saturation rather than an uneventful year.

Abstract: Operational telemetry can be jointly anomalous while every individual stream stays inside its familiar range. TRACE-C is an auditable strictly-prior rank-calibrated detector for aligned multi-stream telemetry: same-regime rolling median/MAD residuals feed three window channels -- a maximum normalized local sum, a Gaussian copula-form dependence contrast on robust-z residuals, and a worst standardized AR(1) innovation -- whose channel ranks are Fisher-aggregated and ranked against earlier aggregates.
  We evaluate six Great Britain grid streams with a January-April 2019 fit, July-December 2019 development evidence, and a 2020 hold-out frozen before inspection. TRACE-C ranks Storm Atiyah first among 2019 test windows, but a disclosed channel ablation attributes that rank to the local channel, not the copula-form channel: copula-only ranks Atiyah 59th. The short 9 August frequency event is ranked far lower by the fused detector (143) than by the temporal channel alone (40), and reconstruction baselines rank it first. In 2020 no window is selected, which is consistent with record-rule saturation rather than an uneventful year; the highest-ranked frozen window was later interpreted as Storm Ellen.
  Three interpretive limits carry throughout. The resulting p-values are selection quantities, not event probabilities. The copula-form channel is not a literal copula density: the method applies no probability-integral or normal-score transform. Empirical rank counts are diagnostics, not coverage or false-discovery proofs. Every table and figure in this paper is generated from committed machine-readable reports.

</details>


### [221] [ConceptTS: LLM-Guided Concept Bottlenecks for Interpretable Multivariate Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21277)
*Yichen Jiang,Yueqiao Chen,Dongyu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ConceptTS is an interpretable multivariate time-series forecasting framework that uses LLMs to propose human-readable concepts organized in three bottlenecks (historical context, local intervals, full horizon), achieving competitive accuracy while providing transparent, explainable predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-of-the-art multivariate time-series forecasters lack transparency - their opaque representations make it difficult to understand why predictions are made, limiting their use in settings where practitioners need to understand and assess the factors underlying predictions.

Method: ConceptTS uses a large language model to propose task-relevant concepts and generate executable labeling rules, translating domain knowledge into supervision without manual annotation. Concepts are organized into three complementary bottlenecks describing: (1) historical context, (2) local forecast intervals, and (3) full forecast horizon. A shared decoder combines representations from predicted concept activations to generate forecasts.

Result: Experiments on the Beijing Multi-Site Air Quality dataset show that ConceptTS achieves accuracy competitive with strong black-box baselines while producing semantically meaningful concept activations.

Conclusion: ConceptTS successfully bridges the gap between forecast accuracy and interpretability, providing explicit decision processes and supporting concept-level interventions while maintaining competitive performance.

Abstract: State-of-the-art multivariate time-series forecasters can model complex temporal and cross-variable dependencies, yet their opaque representations provide limited insight into why a particular forecast is produced. This lack of transparency restricts their use in settings where practitioners must understand and assess the factors underlying a prediction. We introduce ConceptTS, an interpretable forecasting framework that organizes its predictions around named, human-readable concepts. ConceptTS uses a large language model to propose task-relevant concepts and generate executable labeling rules, translating the language model's domain knowledge into direct supervision without costly manual concept annotation. The proposed concepts are organized into three complementary bottlenecks that describe the historical context, local forecast intervals, and the full forecast horizon. A shared decoder combines representations derived from their predicted activations to construct the forecast, making the model's decision process explicit and supporting direct concept-level interventions. Experiments on the Beijing Multi-Site Air Quality dataset show that ConceptTS achieves accuracy competitive with strong black-box baselines while producing semantically meaningful concept activations.

</details>


### [222] [SPARCL: Spectral Partitioned Analytic Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21307)
*James Hartley,Zeropy Surio,Daniel Whitmore,Hannah Clarke,Thomas Reed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper identifies 'spectral interference' as the cause of forgetting in analytic continual learning methods, where incoming task samples perturb old-class logits by loading onto old dominant eigendirections. The proposed SPARCL method decomposes the running autocorrelation into a high-energy core and residual complement, freezing old-class classifiers in the core subspace while updating only the residual block.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard narrative that forgetting is caused by stochastic gradient overwriting does not explain why analytic methods (which use exact recursive solvers) still experience drift on old classes. The authors identify the culprit as 'spectral interference' - the joint ridge classifier shares the inverse autocorrelation operator, so incoming task samples that load onto old dominant eigendirections dilute the spectrum and perturb old-class logits even without revisiting old labels.

Method: SPARCL decomposes the running autocorrelation into a high-energy core and a residual complement. It freezes old-class classifier components in the core subspace and updates only the residual block through recursive least squares with an optional residual random-projection expansion. This provides a closed-form update with provable invariance guarantee for the core contribution of old logits.

Result: Across CIFAR-100, CUB-200, ImageNet-R, and ImageNet-A under a frozen ViT-B/16 protocol, SPARCL closes most of the gap from classical analytic learners to strong representation matchers while remaining complementary to sparse feature-decorrelation approaches such as Fly-CL.

Conclusion: Spectral interference is the fundamental cause of forgetting in analytic continual learning. By partitioning the autocorrelation matrix and freezing old-class components in the core subspace, SPARCL achieves provable invariance while maintaining strong performance, bridging the gap between analytic learners and representation matchers.

Abstract: Analytic continual learning has emerged as a strong exemplar-free alternative to gradient-based class-incremental learning because it replaces iterative optimization with closed-form ridge updates. Yet the usual forgetting narrative, centered on stochastic gradient overwriting, does not explain why analytic methods still drift on old classes despite exact recursive solvers. We identify the culprit as spectral interference: the joint ridge classifier for all tasks shares the inverse autocorrelation operator $(R+λI)^{-1}$, so incoming task samples that load onto old dominant eigendirections dilute the spectrum and perturb old-class logits even when old labels are never revisited. Based on this view, we propose SPARCL, a spectral partitioned analytic continual learner that decomposes the running autocorrelation into a high-energy core and a residual complement, freezes old-class classifier components in the core subspace, and updates only the residual block through recursive least squares with an optional residual random-projection expansion. This yields a simple closed-form update with a provable invariance guarantee for the core contribution of old logits. Across CIFAR-100, CUB-200, ImageNet-R, and ImageNet-A under a frozen ViT-B/16 protocol, SPARCL closes most of the gap from classical analytic learners to strong representation matchers, while remaining complementary to sparse feature-decorrelation approaches such as Fly-CL.

</details>


### [223] [Rethinking Expressivity and Efficiency in Test-Time Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21308)
*Zeyun Zhong,Joya Chen,Manuel Martin,Frederik Diederichs,Juergen Gall,Juergen Beyerer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: E²-TTT proposes a closed-form state transition that exactly reproduces per-token recurrence while enabling fully parallelized chunk-level training, achieving 90% accuracy at 8x training context length on passkey retrieval while matching efficient chunk-wise throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test-Time Training (TTT) enables long-context processing via continuous weight updates during inference, but current methods struggle to balance the expressivity of per-token update dynamics with the hardware efficiency of chunk-wise approximations.

Method: Under the standard approximation of taking gradients at the chunk-start weights, derive a closed-form state transition that exactly reproduces the chunk-end fast-weight and momentum states of the per-token recurrence, enabling fully parallelized chunk-level training while preserving temporal structure.

Result: Models up to 1.3B parameters trained from scratch perform on par with previous TTT and hybrid attention baselines in language modeling while outperforming them on in-context retrieval. On the "Needle in a Haystack" passkey test, it retains over 90% accuracy at 8× the training context length, while matching the training throughput of efficient chunk-wise methods.

Conclusion: E²-TTT effectively reconciles expressivity with efficiency in Test-Time Training, demonstrating superior length extrapolation capabilities while maintaining computational efficiency comparable to chunk-wise methods.

Abstract: Test-Time Training (TTT) enables long-context processing via continuous weight updates during inference, but current methods struggle to balance the expressivity of per-token update dynamics with the hardware efficiency of chunk-wise approximations. We propose E$^2$-TTT (Expressive and Efficient TTT) to bridge this gap. Under the standard approximation of taking gradients at the chunk-start weights, we derive a closed-form state transition that exactly reproduces the chunk-end fast-weight and momentum states of the per-token recurrence. This enables fully parallelized chunk-level training while preserving the temporal structure of the update rule that prior chunk-wise methods discard. We validate E$^2$-TTT by training models up to 1.3B parameters from scratch. It performs on par with previous TTT and hybrid attention baselines in language modeling while outperforming them on in-context retrieval. Its advantage is most pronounced in length extrapolation: on the standard ``Needle in a Haystack'' passkey test, it retains over 90% accuracy at $8\times$ the training context length. Meanwhile, E$^2$-TTT can match the training throughput of efficient chunk-wise methods, demonstrating that it effectively reconciles expressivity with efficiency. The code is available at https://github.com/zeyun-zhong/E2-TTT.

</details>


### [224] [Time-Aware Tranformer-Based Prediction Model for AECOPD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21324)
*Weihao Qu,Ling Zheng,Dongyang Wang,Jiacun Wang,Haowen Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Time-Aware transformer-based model to predict Acute Exacerbation of Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (AECOPD) using only respiratory data from home ventilators, addressing the latency issue in clinical/lab-based prediction methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid symptom change in AECOPD requires time-sensitive prediction models. Current machine learning approaches rely on clinical and laboratory data, which inevitably introduces latency. There is a need for timely detection using readily available home monitoring data from daily-use ventilators.

Method: A Time-Aware transformer-based AECOPD prediction model that generates meaningful patient representations by capturing symptoms and their temporal progression patterns from ventilator data.

Result: The Time-Aware transformer-based approach outperforms traditional methods in multiple classification tasks for AECOPD prediction.

Conclusion: The proposed Time-Aware transformer model demonstrates potential to enhance AECOPD prediction accuracy through home monitoring, offering a promising solution for timely detection with minimal latency.

Abstract: The rapid symptom change of Acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (AECOPD) makes it critical to have time-sensitive prediction models. However, most current machine learning models studying AECOPD use clinical and laboratory data, which will inevitably cause latency. To ensure timely detection of AECOPD and minimize latency, this paper focuses on home monitoring scenarios where only respiratory data from daily-use ventilators is available. We introduce a Time-Aware transformer-based AECOPD prediction model, which generates meaningful patient representations using the Time-Aware transformer to capture the symptoms and their temporal progression in ventilator data. Our experimental results demonstrate that our Time-Aware transformer-based approach outperforms traditional methods in multiple classification tasks, highlighting its potential to enhance AECOPD prediction accuracy.

</details>


### [225] [Across-Design Uncertainty in Short Pricing Panels: Evidence from Simulated Price Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21334)
*Pedro Cadahia Delgado*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper examines inferential problems arising from sparse pricing panels (many observations but few distinct price movements). It shows that most estimation error variance (97.6%) comes from across-trajectory variation rather than within-trajectory uncertainty, and that conventional within-panel resampling fails to capture this. Three key results: an empirical dispersion formula, the limited effectiveness of shared price paths versus independent designs, and how Paule-Mandel variance components can improve coverage from 47% to 93%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Short observational pricing panels often contain many observations but offer only a small number of distinct price movements, creating inferential challenges that standard methods fail to address.

Method: The paper uses synthetic data-generating processes calibrated to sparse pricing regimes, separating uncertainty conditional on realized price trajectories from variation across alternative trajectories. It employs baseline simulations, within-panel resampling, and Paule-Mandel variance component estimation.

Result: Across-design dispersion follows σ̂ ≈ 0.182 V^(-0.271) where V = moves × magnitude squared. Adding regions with common price paths reduces outcome noise but doesn't create independent trajectories. Paule-Mandel variance components increase empirical coverage from 0.469 to 0.931.

Conclusion: The broader implication is a shift toward designing data-generating processes that create independent identifying variation rather than relying solely on fixed passive panels. Standard within-panel resampling cannot identify the across-design component of uncertainty, necessitating new approaches for causal inference in sparse panel settings.

Abstract: Short observational pricing panels can contain many observations while offering only a small number of distinct price movements. This paper studies the inferential consequences of that distinction in a synthetic data-generating process calibrated to a sparse pricing regime. We separate uncertainty conditional on a realised price trajectory from variation in estimation error across alternative trajectories generated by the same pricing process. In the baseline simulations, the latter component accounts for 97.6% of the variance of estimation error for the gradient-boosted specification. Within-panel resampling procedures use the information of one realised trajectory and do not identify this across-design component.
  Three results organise the analysis. First, across-design dispersion is well described by the empirical relation sigma_hat approx 0.182 V^(-0.271), where V equals moves times magnitude squared. Second, adding regions sharing a common price path reduces outcome noise but does not create independent price trajectories; conversely, averaging across units with independent design-specific errors reduces dispersion at the standard square root rate. Third, a Paule-Mandel variance component estimated across independently priced units substantially increases empirical coverage in homogeneous simulations, from 0.469 to 0.931. The broader implication is a shift toward designing data-generating processes that create independent identifying variation rather than relying solely on fixed passive panels.

</details>


### [226] [Asymmetric Capacity Allocation in Self-Refinement Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21345)
*Zhuoyi Yang,Ian G. Harris,Salar Hashemitaheri,Cassie Huang,Yuangang Li,Hyunwoo Oh,Paul Dourish,Tony Givargis,Mohsen Imani,Li Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic study of how model size affects each stage of the self-refinement pipeline (generation, critique, and revision) in LLMs. Using 6 Qwen3 and 4 Gemma 3 model sizes across 5 benchmarks, the study finds that larger generators and refiners improve performance, but critic size has minimal impact. Even a small critic outperforms having no critique. The findings suggest model capacity should be allocated non-uniformly across self-refinement stages for computational efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing self-refinement approaches treat model size as an implementation detail rather than studying it systematically, despite the three stages (generation, critique, revision) involving different cognitive demands. This may lead to resource waste, and little work has examined whether effective self-refinement requires equally capable models for each stage.

Method: Stage-wise model size study on 5 benchmarks from different domains, using 6 model sizes of Qwen3 and 4 model sizes of Gemma 3. The study systematically examined how model size affects each stage of the self-refinement pipeline.

Result: Two key findings: (1) Larger generators and refiners generally improve pipeline performance, whereas an undersized refiner can even harm performance; (2) Performance is highly insensitive to the size of the critic, although including even a small critic consistently outperforms omitting critique altogether.

Conclusion: Model capacity should not be allocated uniformly across self-refinement pipelines. Different stages exhibit distinct size scaling characteristics. This provides practical guidance for designing more computationally efficient multi-stage language model systems by appropriately sizing models at each stage.

Abstract: Self-refinement, typically structured as generation, critique, and revision, is a widely adopted paradigm for improving LLM generation and serves as a core mechanism in many LLM agents. While the three stages involve different cognitive demands, most existing approaches conveniently treat the model size as an implementation detail rather than a subject of study, which may lead to a waste of resources. Little work has systematically examined how model size affects each stage or whether effective self-refinement requires equally capable models for generation, critique, and revision. We present the first stage-wise model size study of the self-refinement pipeline on 5 benchmarks from different domains using 6 model sizes of Qwen3 and 4 model sizes of Gemma 3. We conclude that larger generators and refiners generally improve the pipeline, whereas an undersized refiner can even harm performance. Second, performance is highly insensitive to the size of the critic, although including even a small critic consistently outperforms omitting critique altogether. Our findings demonstrate that model capacity should not be allocated uniformly across self-refinement pipelines. Instead, different stages exhibit distinct size scaling characteristics, providing practical guidance for designing more computationally efficient multi-stage language model systems.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [227] [Vibe Coding: Practice, Performance, Productivity, and Risk -A State-of-the-Art Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20446)
*Dominik L. Michels,Mutaz Abu Ghazaleh,Francois Lazzari,Nabil Kassem,Jonathan Klein*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This is a state-of-the-art review of "vibe coding" - AI-assisted software development where developers describe intent in natural language and validate by running code rather than reading it. The review synthesizes evidence from software engineering, HCI, labor economics, security, governance, and education across 17 months of empirical research. Key findings include: uneven task-level performance (good code generation but weak fault detection), contradictory productivity results (+26% tasks/week, 19% slowdown, +441% code review time), security failures, code quality degradation, copyright issues, and skill atrophy. The paper proposes a unifying conjecture: gains are real on new code but shrink/reverse on mature codebases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To synthesize the first body of empirical evidence on "vibe coding" (AI-assisted software development coined by Andrej Karpathy in Feb 2025) across multiple disciplines, resolving apparent contradictions in productivity measurements and identifying patterns and open research questions.

Method: Cross-disciplinary literature review spanning software engineering, human-computer interaction, labor economics, security research, governance, and education. Survey of model landscape, tool ecosystem, and performance by task type. Analysis of productivity measurement methods, scope, and time horizons to resolve contradictory findings.

Result: Early benchmarks are saturated but task-level capability is uneven: reliable code generation but weak fault detection and hard-to-audit documentation. Productivity findings appear contradictory: +26% tasks/week (peer-reviewed field experiments), 19% slowdown (independent randomized trials), +441% code-review time (team telemetry). Six patterns identified behind dispersion: effect-shrinkage under broader measurement, self-report diverging from independent measurement, output volume conflated with productivity, bold claims walked back over longer horizons. Also documented: security failures in deployed apps, code-quality degradation, unsettled copyright exposure, and skill atrophy.

Conclusion: The productivity readings are consistent when measurement method, scope, and time horizon are held constant. The review proposes one falsifiable conjecture: gains are real on new code and shrink or reverse on mature codebases, which would account for most disagreement in the record. Closes with open research questions.

Abstract: Vibe coding - AI-assisted software development in which the developer describes intent in natural language and validates results by running rather than reading the generated code - was named by Andrej Karpathy in February 2025 and produced its first body of empirical evidence within seventeen months. This state-of-the-art review assembles that evidence across a cross-disciplinary corpus spanning software engineering, human-computer interaction, labour economics, security research, governance, and education. We survey the model landscape, the tool ecosystem, and the performance record by task type, finding the early benchmarks saturated but task-level capability uneven: reliable code generation alongside weak fault detection and hard-to-audit documentation. The productivity record is at first contradictory: peer-reviewed field experiments report +26% more tasks per week, independent randomised trials measure a 19% slowdown, and team-level telemetry shows code-review time up +441%. We argue these readings are consistent once measurement method, scope, and time horizon are held constant, and identify six patterns behind the dispersion, among them effect-shrinkage under broader measurement, self-report diverging from independent measurement, output volume conflated with productivity, and bold claims walked back once tested over longer horizons. We further document security failures in deployed applications, code-quality degradation visible in large-scale code and developer telemetry, unsettled copyright exposure, and evidence of skill atrophy. The review closes with the open research questions and one falsifiable conjecture: that the gains are real on new code and shrink or reverse on mature codebases, which would account for most of the disagreement in the record.

</details>


### [228] [Making Deployments Safe at Meta: Health Checks for Continuous Change-Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20513)
*Prakash KL,Anton Korenkov,Uttam Thakore,Christopher Hegre*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Meta's Service Health Checker is a prevention-based distributed system designed to balance release velocity and reliability in continuous deployment across thousands of heterogeneous services. The system uses templated metric queries, thresholds, and workflow predicates to enable automated rollback when regressions are detected during tiered and phased rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core tension in continuous deployment is between release velocity and reliability—every change risks introducing failures, yet delays mean missed opportunities. The authors sought to build an infrastructure that mediates this tension by automatically detecting and preventing regressions before they reach production users.

Method: The paper describes a deployment time health check infrastructure that composes templated metric queries, configurable thresholds, and workflow predicates. This system is integrated with tiered and phased rollout strategies, enabling automatic rollback when regressions are detected. The authors also address operational challenges at scale through measurement, tooling, and improved defaults.

Result: The system addresses operational problems that emerged at scale, including noise, alert fatigue, threshold drift, and regressions that went undetected. The paper presents solutions implemented to combat these issues, along with lessons learned from years of operating deployment health checks at Meta.

Conclusion: The authors conclude with lessons learned from years of operating deployment health checks at Meta and discuss future directions, including AI-assisted health check tuning (AIOps) and anomaly detection to further improve deployment safety and reliability.

Abstract: Continuous deployment to large scale production systems creates a tension between release velocity and reliability. Every change is a potential reliability incident, yet every delay is a missed opportunity. This paper describes the deployment time health check infrastructure that Meta uses to mediate this tension across thousands of heterogeneous services. We summarize the architecture of this prevention based distributed system's service called Service Health Checker, explain how check authors compose templated metric queries, thresholds, and workflow predicates; and discuss how the system is integrated with tiered and phased rollouts so that regressions trigger automatic rollback. We then describe the operational problems that emerged at scale, such as noise, alert fatigue, drift, and uncovered regressions, and the program of measurement, tooling, and improved defaults we deployed to address them. We close with lessons learned from years of operating deployment health checks at Meta, and the directions we are exploring next, including AI assisted health check tuning. Index Terms: deployment safety, continuous deployment, monitoring, software reliability, release engineering, software reliability engineering, AIOps, anomaly detection

</details>


### [229] [Testing and Evaluation of Agentic AI Systems In Military Command and Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20597)
*Ulysse Richard,Heather Frase,Sarah Cao,Di Cooke,Sebastian Kwon,Adrianna Tan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper examines whether current Testing and Evaluation (T&E) practices can adequately assure Agentic AI systems for military Command and Control (C2). Through a structured review of 240 T&E practices across 8 evaluation dimensions and 3 lifecycle stages, the authors identify 8 assumptions (grouped into specifiability, stability, composability, and supervisability clusters) that established testing methods rely upon. Agentic properties weaken all these assumptions, affecting the argument connecting evidence to claims rather than the claims or evidence themselves. The paper concludes that while broad system-level behavior claims are not supported by current evidence, narrower claims (bounded mission envelopes, trajectory-grounded correctness, executable runtime constraints, characterized run-to-run variance) may be recoverable with mature methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems are being procured for military C2 under public commitments to rigorous testing and human oversight. This paper investigates whether such commitments can actually be discharged through current T&E practices and assurance cases, which require three elements: claims, evidence, and an argument connecting them.

Method: Structured review of 240 documented T&E practices spanning eight evaluation dimensions and three lifecycle stages. Identified eight assumptions underlying established testing methods, grouped into four clusters. Derived ten assurance claims for the first three clusters and assessed whether current and emerging methods can address each, mapping operational consequences through five C2 scenarios.

Result: Agentic properties weaken all eight assumptions across specifiability, stability, composability, and supervisability clusters. This erosion affects the argument connecting evidence to claims (not the claims or evidence themselves). Test results may satisfy process requirements but do not warrant inference from tested to fielded behavior. The documented record does not support broad claims about system-level behavior, but narrower claims remain recoverable in principle with mature methods: bounded mission envelopes, trajectory-grounded correctness, executable runtime constraints, and characterized run-to-run variance.

Conclusion: Part of the evidentiary burden shifts into deployment, making the determination to field a continuing act. Where evidence cannot be generated, residual uncertainty can be governed through defined expiry conditions and assigned ownership. Supervisability was identified but not assessed, as evidencing it depends on system stability results and human factors T&E methods beyond the present scope.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems are being procured for military command and control (C2) under public commitments to rigorous testing and human oversight. Whether such commitments can be discharged depends on their supporting assurance case, which requires three elements: claims specifying the conditions for acceptability, evidence bearing on those claims, and an argument connecting the two. Through a structured review of 240 documented Testing and Evaluation (T&E) practices, spanning eight evaluation dimensions and three lifecycle stages, we identify eight assumptions that established methods make about their test article, grouped into four clusters: system specifiability, stability, composability, and supervisability. Agentic properties weaken all eight assumptions. This erosion affects the argument connecting evidence to claims, not the claims or evidence themselves. As a result, test results may satisfy process requirements, but they do not warrant the inference from tested to fielded behavior.
  We derive ten assurance claims for the first three assumption clusters and assess whether current and emerging methods can address each, mapping operational consequences through five C2 scenarios. Supervisability is identified but not assessed here, since evidencing it depends on system stability results and human factors T&E methods beyond the present scope. The documented record does not support broad claims about system-level behavior, but narrower claims remain recoverable in principle, contingent on mature methods: bounded mission envelopes, trajectory-grounded correctness, executable runtime constraints, and characterized run-to-run variance. Part of the evidentiary burden shifts into deployment, making the determination to field a continuing act. Where evidence cannot be generated, the residual uncertainty can be governed through defined expiry conditions and assigned ownership.

</details>


### [230] [Behavior Specification-Guided Program Synthesis for Binary Deobfuscation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20628)
*Kangchen Zhu,Shangwen Wang,Zhiliang Tian,Zhouyang Jia,Xiaoling Li,Jun Ma,Jie Yu,Xiaoguang Mao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Deobfuscation is critical to reverse engineering and security analysis because it restores the readability and analyzability of obfuscated code. However, existing research primarily focuses on source-code deobfuscation, while binary-level deobfuscation remains largely underexplored despite its practical importance when source code is unavailable. Existing binary deobfuscation methods typically decompile binaries into pseudocode and then apply structural transformations. However, because compilation discards high-level semantics such as precise type information and source-level structures, this decompilation-based paradigm often produces low-quality code and provides limited assurance that the recovered code preserves the runtime behavior of the original program. To address these limitations, we propose a paradigm shift from structural transformation to behavior-driven synthesis. Our core insight is that although obfuscation distorts a program's internal structure, semantics-preserving transformations must retain its observable execution behavior. Based on this insight, we introduce BinMirror, an approach that reformulates binary deobfuscation as a behavior-specification-guided program synthesis task. By treating dynamic execution traces and interaction snapshots as behavioral specifications, BinMirror synthesizes high-quality source code and validates it against runtime observations collected from heavily obfuscated binaries. Extensive evaluations on 1.5 million synthetically obfuscated binaries show that BinMirror significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving a unit-test Pass@1 of 74.5% under extreme obfuscation. These results demonstrate the practical utility of BinMirror in restoring semantic clarity for real-world security analysis.

</details>


### [231] [Toward Understanding Operating System Defects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20643)
*Hongyao Zuo,Jiali Li,Jiajun Jiang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents the largest-scale study (1,500 defects) of operating system defects across Android, Linux, and HarmonyOS, analyzing defects across multiple dimensions including OS layer distribution, affected functions, trigger mechanisms, severity, and repair code elements. The study provides actionable implications for OS defect detection and debugging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Operating systems are foundational to all software systems, and defects in OSes can cause severe consequences like system crashes and data corruption affecting billions of users. Understanding OS defect characteristics is essential for designing effective defect detection and debugging approaches.

Method: The authors conducted a large-scale empirical study analyzing 1,500 defects from three representative operating systems: Android, Linux, and HarmonyOS. They examined defects across multiple classification dimensions including: (1) OS layer where defects occur, (2) affected functions, (3) trigger mechanisms, (4) severity levels, and (5) code elements involved in repairs. They also performed joint analysis between dimensions and cross-OS similarity comparisons.

Result: The study revealed several major findings contributing to comprehensive understanding of OS defects across systems. Key results include distribution patterns of defects across different OS layers, common triggering mechanisms, severity classifications, and code element patterns involved in defect repairs. Cross-OS comparisons identified similarities and differences among Android, Linux, and HarmonyOS.

Conclusion: The authors provide a series of actionable implications for better OS defect detection and debugging, offering guidelines for future research aimed at improving OS quality assurance. This work represents the largest study of its kind in the OS defect domain.

Abstract: Operating systems (OS) serve as the foundation for all other software systems, and thus defects in OSes can lead to severe conquences, such as system crashes and data corruption, affecting billions of users. This broad impact underscores the necessity and importance of ensuring OS quality. Understanding the characteristics of OS defects is a fundamental step in this quality assurance task, as it facilitates the design of effective defect detection and debugging approaches. In this work, we conduct a large-scale study of 1,500 defects from three distinct and representative operating systems (Android, Linux, and HarmonyOS) spanning both mobile and desktop environments. To the best of our knowledge, this is the largest study of its kind in this domain. By analyzing the distribution of OS defects across multiple classification dimensions, including the OS layer where defects occur, the functions they affect, how they are triggered, their severity, and the code elements involved in their repair, as well as performing joint analysis between dimensions and cross-OS similarity comparisons, we summarize several major findings that contribute to a comprehensive understanding of OS defects across systems. Based on these findings, we provide a series of actionable implications for better OS defect detection and debugging, offering guidelines for future research aimed at improving OS quality assurance.

</details>


### [232] [Temporal Validity on Real Software Histories: Eliminating Stale-Fact Errors in Code-Assistant Memory over GitHub Fixes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20685)
*Neeraj Yadav*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: MemStrata is a system that addresses a fundamental limitation in Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) - the inability to distinguish between stale (superseded) and current information when facts change over time. The system uses a deterministic (subject, relation, object) supersession memory to track value changes across time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG has no model of time: when facts change during a coding session (function renamed, endpoint moved, dependency bumped), RAG retrieves both old and new values with near-identical similarity and cannot determine which is current, leading to serving superseded values.

Method: The researchers extracted 130 clean atomic state transitions from 707 real GitHub issues (SWE-bench Lite + Verified). These are fixes where one identifiable value changes from a pre-fix to a post-fix form, with the stale and current statements differing only in the value. They then compared MemStrata against standard RAG and an LLM reranker baseline.

Result: MemStrata achieves 0.91 answer accuracy versus RAG's 0.57-0.59. When forced to answer, RAG serves the superseded value 36-38% of the time (an LLM reranker does not help reduce this), while MemStrata reduces this to ~0%. MemStrata operates at RAG retrieval latency (~2.1 s) compared to ~18 s for the reranker. A real product bug was also discovered and fixed during the study (case/punctuation-insensitive value comparison issue).

Conclusion: The deterministic supersession memory mechanism effectively eliminates the failure of RAG serving superseded values on clean atomic transitions. However, scope is limited: only ~18% of real fixes are clean atomic transitions; the memory mechanism is isolated to this class, and extraction coverage of remaining fixes is deferred to future work.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has no model of time: when a fact changes across a coding session - a function is renamed, an endpoint moves, a dependency is bumped - RAG retrieves both the old and new value with near-identical similarity and cannot tell which is current, so it serves the superseded value. Paper 1 showed, on synthetic single-value benchmarks, that a deterministic (subject, relation, object) supersession memory eliminates this failure. Here we validate it end-to-end on real software history. From 707 real GitHub issues (SWE-bench Lite + Verified) we extract 130 clean atomic state transitions, a fix that changes one identifiable value from a pre-fix to a post-fix form, and render each marker-free (the stale and current statements differ only in the value). On this set, MemStrata reaches 0.91 answer accuracy versus RAG's 0.57-0.59; and, the structural result, when forced to answer RAG serves the superseded value 36-38% of the time (an LLM reranker does not help) while MemStrata drives this to ~0, at RAG retrieval latency (~2.1 s vs ~18 s for the reranker). We are explicit about scope: only ~18% of real fixes are clean atomic transitions; Paper 2 isolates the memory mechanism on that class, and extraction coverage of the remaining fixes is the orthogonal problem we defer to follow-on work. A real product bug surfaced and was fixed during the study (a case/punctuation-insensitive value comparison), with the moat property (deterministic-supersession accuracy on clean code mutations) preserved and verified.

</details>


### [233] [An Extensive Empirical Study on Code Translation Technique](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20776)
*Ruihang Fan,Jiajun Jiang,Xinpeng Wang,Jiateng Fu,Fengjie Li,Jiasi Shen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a large-scale empirical study comparing learning-based and LLM-based code translation techniques across multiple programming languages, evaluating method-level and class-level translation with various metrics including executable correctness, code similarity, and failure patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses a critical gap in understanding the relative strengths and limitations of learning-based versus LLM-based code translation techniques, which are increasingly important for software evolution.

Method: The study conducts a large-scale empirical evaluation comparing representative code translation techniques across different methodological paradigms (learning-based, LLM-based, and general-purpose LLMs) and translation granularities (method-level and class-level). It uses multilingual benchmarks involving multiple programming languages and evaluates based on executable correctness, code similarity, translation direction, translation granularity, and failure patterns.

Result: Key findings: (1) LLMs and LLM-based methods generally outperform learning-based methods in method-level correctness; (2) Similarity metrics alone do not reliably reflect functional correctness; (3) Translation direction substantially affects performance, especially between languages with different type-system characteristics; (4) Class-level translation is considerably more difficult than method-level translation due to the need to preserve global semantics, interfaces, member relationships, and cross-method dependencies; (5) Static semantic errors and logical errors are the primary challenges in existing code translation systems.

Conclusion: The findings provide empirical evidence and practical guidance for developing more robust, type-aware, structure-aware, and context-aware code translation techniques. The study highlights the need for improvements in handling static semantics, logical correctness, and inter-method dependencies in code translation systems.

Abstract: Automated code translation is increasingly important for software evolution, yet the relative strengths and limitations of learning-based and large language model (LLM)-based techniques remain insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we conduct a large-scale empirical study comparing representative code translation techniques across methodological paradigms and translation granularities. We evaluate learning-based methods, LLM-based methods, and general-purpose LLMs on multilingual method-level and class-level benchmarks involving multiple programming languages. Our analysis considers executable correctness, code similarity, translation direction, translation granularity, and failure patterns. The results show that LLMs and LLM-based methods generally outperform learning-based methods in method-level correctness, although similarity metrics alone do not reliably reflect functional correctness. Translation direction substantially affects performance, particularly when translating between languages with different type-system characteristics. Class-level translation remains considerably more difficult than method-level translation because it requires preserving global semantics, interfaces, member relationships, and cross-method dependencies. Our error analysis further shows that static semantic errors and logical errors are the primary challenges in existing code translation systems. These findings provide empirical evidence and practical guidance for developing more robust, type-aware, structure-aware, and context-aware code translation techniques.

</details>


### [234] [BC-Bench: Evaluating Agentic Engineering in a Domain-Specific Language for ERP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20851)
*Haoran Sun,Klaus Marius Hansen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: BC-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating agentic engineering systems on AL, the domain-specific language for Microsoft Dynamics 365 Business Central ERP system. It contains 101 curated tasks from production repositories and evaluates code generation, test generation, and multimodal problem statements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic engineering systems show strong performance on general-purpose benchmarks, but their effectiveness in enterprise resource planning (ERP) domain-specific languages remains underexplored. The researchers aimed to address this gap by creating a domain-specific evaluation benchmark.

Method: The benchmark adapts the SWE-Bench methodology to handle the unique constraints of the AL ecosystem (limited public resources, complex environment provisioning). It evaluates multiple frontier models across two agent harnesses using multi-run metrics to account for nondeterminism. Tasks include bug fixing, test generation, and support for multimodal problem statements with visual context.

Result: In the Bug Fixing category, between-model differences in resolution rate are larger than differences between the two evaluated agent harnesses. Improvements reported on general-purpose benchmarks do not consistently transfer to AL, indicating that general-purpose benchmark performance does not reliably predict domain-specific performance.

Conclusion: The results highlight the critical need for domain-specific evaluation of agentic engineering systems. Performance on general-purpose benchmarks cannot be assumed to transfer to specific domains like ERP development, necessitating tailored evaluation frameworks for different application areas.

Abstract: Agentic engineering systems have shown strong performance on general-purpose benchmarks, yet their effectiveness in enterprise resource planning (ERP) domain-specific languages (DSLs) remains underexplored. We introduce BC-Bench, a benchmark designed to evaluate agentic engineering on real-world tasks in AL, the DSL for Microsoft Dynamics 365 Business Central. BC-Bench comprises 101 manually curated tasks extracted from two Microsoft-owned production repositories, reflecting authentic ERP development workflows. Adapting the SWE-Bench methodology, we address the unique constraints of the AL ecosystem---including limited public resources and complex environment provisioning. Beyond generating functional code, BC-Bench evaluates test generation and supports multimodal problem statements where visual context is commonly present. We evaluate multiple frontier models across two agent harnesses, utilizing multi-run metrics to account for nondeterminism. In the Bug Fixing category, under our evaluated settings, between-model differences in resolution rate are larger than differences between the two evaluated agent harnesses, and improvements reported on general-purpose benchmarks do not consistently transfer to AL. These results highlight the need for domain-specific evaluation.

</details>


### [235] [Beyond the Traceback: Using LLMs for Adaptive Explanations of Programming Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20896)
*Alexandru-Radu Moraru,Shreyan Biswas,Ujwal Gadiraju*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A crowdsourced study (N=103) evaluated LLM-generated Python error messages against standard interpreter messages, testing pragmatic (action-oriented) and contingent (scaffolded) styles across different skill levels. While LLM-rewritten messages improved subjective evaluations (readability, cognitive load), they did not significantly improve objective debugging performance (fix rate, attempts, time-to-fix), revealing a "human-AI complementarity gap" where perceived clarity does not translate to actual effectiveness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Programming error messages are difficult for novice programmers to interpret. While LLMs can rewrite errors into clearer explanations, it was unclear whether increased readability improves actual debugging performance or how explanation styles should align with programmer skill levels.

Method: Multi-stage crowdsourced study (N=103) using a custom proficiency assessment to categorize participants by skill level. Tested standard interpreter messages against two LLM-generated styles: pragmatic (action-oriented) and contingent (scaffolded explanations).
