Measured both objective metrics (fix rate, attempts, time-to-fix) and subjective perceptions (readability, cognitive load, tone).

Result: LLM-rewritten messages significantly improved subjective evaluations—pragmatic messages were rated as clearer and less cognitively demanding. However, these perceived gains did NOT translate into statistically significant improvements in objective debugging performance.

Conclusion: There exists a critical "human-AI complementarity gap": explanations that feel better to users do not necessarily make them more effective debuggers. Future AI feedback systems should pivot from static skill-targeted rewriting toward dynamic adjustments based on a user's real-time repair trajectory.

Abstract: Programming error messages are critical for software development, yet they remain difficult for novice programmers to interpret. While Large Language Models (LLMs) can rewrite these errors into clearer explanations, it remains unclear whether increased readability improves objective debugging performance or how explanation styles should align with programmer skill. We present a multi-stage crowdsourced study N=103 evaluating skill-targeted, LLM-generated Python error messages. Using a custom proficiency assessment, we categorized participants by skill level and tested standard interpreter messages against two LLM-generated styles: pragmatic (action-oriented) and contingent (scaffolded explanations). We measured both objective debugging metrics (fix rate, attempts, time-to-fix) and subjective perceptions (readability, cognitive load, tone). Our results show that while LLM-rewritten messages significantly improved subjective evaluations, with pragmatic messages rated as clearer and less cognitively demanding, these perceived gains did not translate into statistically significant improvements in objective debugging performance. This highlights a critical human-AI complementarity gap: explanations that feel better to users do not necessarily make them more effective debuggers. We discuss design implications for adaptive AI feedback systems, arguing that future tools should pivot from static skill-targeted rewriting toward dynamic adjustments based on a user's real-time repair trajectory.

</details>


### [236] [Generation of Web Apps with Agentic IDEs: An Empirical Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20903)
*Manuel Marceca,Maria Teresa Rossi,Leonardo Mariani*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper presents a comparative analysis of three agentic IDEs (Copilot, Cursor, and Windsurf) by evaluating their performance in generating five full-stack web applications from scratch, revealing their strengths in standard patterns but limitations in distributed architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The evaluation of agentic IDEs in end-to-end development tasks remains limited, necessitating a rigorous comparison to understand their capabilities and limitations in practical application generation.

Method: The authors conducted a rigorous comparative analysis of three popular agentic IDEs by generating five full-stack web applications from scratch using each tool.

Result: Agentic IDEs demonstrate high maturity in generating established patterns (CRUD operations, authentication) but produce significantly more errors when generating less common distributed architectures like task queue systems. Overall differences among IDEs are narrow, with each showing unique characteristics.

Conclusion: Agentic IDEs cannot replace developers but shift their role toward orchestrating LLM-based agents through natural-language instructions and iterative refinement for software construction.

Abstract: Agentic IDEs are among the most significant innovations in software engineering, aiming to accelerate application development through LLM-based agents that can assist developers during development. However, their evaluation in end-to-end development tasks involving the generation of complete applications remains limited. To fill this gap, we propose a rigorous comparative analysis of three popular agentic IDEs (Copilot, Cursor, and Windsurf) in the generation of five full-stack Web applications from scratch.
  Results show high maturity in the generation of established patterns, such as CRUD operations and authentication features. In contrast, the generation of less common distributed architectures, such as a task queue architecture, produces significantly more errors. Overall, results show that Agentic IDEs cannot replace developers but shift their role toward building software by orchestrating LLM-based agents through natural-language instructions and iterative refinement. Yet, each agentic IDE shows its peculiarities, although differences are narrow.

</details>


### [237] [Spike-Killer: Evidence-Gated LLM Assistance for Safe Performance Diagnosis on a Real Windows Workstation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21069)
*Baocheng Zeng,Jinhao Yang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Spike-Killer is a human-approved workflow for diagnosing frame-time complaints on a Windows workstation, treating each agent action as an evidence-gated transaction with state recording, risk classification, snapshot preservation, postcondition verification, and failed measurement retention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-assisted agents can synthesize system evidence and automate diagnostic tasks, but their flexibility introduces operational risks from imprecise actions or intrusive collectors. There is a need for a trustworthy, auditable diagnostic workflow on real workstations.

Method: A human-in-the-loop workflow where each action is an evidence-gated transaction: records exact target state, classifies risk, preserves snapshots, verifies postconditions, and retains failed measurements as first-class evidence. The study used Windows Performance Recorder to collect GPU traces for Counter-Strike 2.

Result: Same-day study with Counter-Strike 2 as target application. Evidence bundle includes state snapshots, microbenchmarks, repeatability probes, live telemetry, repaired registry actions, and system-level tracing. Two CS2 local-Bot GPU traces (90.69 and 85.85 seconds) were collected, attributed to cs2.exe with DxgKrnl Present metadata and zero lost ETW buffers. The study reports no frame intervals, P99 estimate, or intervention effect—qualifying trace integrity rather than performance.

Conclusion: The contribution is an auditable, human-in-the-loop pattern for trustworthy agent assistance on real workstations, including explicit stop conditions when evidence is insufficient. This addresses the operational risks of LLM-assisted agents in diagnostic tasks.

Abstract: LLM-assisted agents can synthesize system evidence, propose configuration changes, and automate diagnostic tasks, but their flexibility makes an imprecise action or an intrusive collector an operational risk. We present Spike-Killer, a human-approved workflow for diagnosing frame-time complaints on one real Windows workstation. The workflow treats each action as an evidence-gated transaction: it records the exact target state, classifies risk, preserves a snapshot, verifies a postcondition, and retains failed measurements as first-class evidence.
  This experience paper reports a completed same-day study with Counter-Strike 2 as a demanding target application. The evidence bundle contains preserved state snapshots, exploratory microbenchmarks, a ten-run same-state repeatability probe, live telemetry, a repaired over-broad registry action, incompatible presentation-capture attempts, an invalid local replay, and a system-level tracing replacement. Windows Performance Recorder produced two CS2 local-Bot GPU traces of 90.69 and 85.85 seconds; both were attributed to cs2.exe, exposed DxgKrnl Present metadata, and had zero lost ETW buffers or events. These results qualify trace integrity, not performance: the study reports no frame intervals, P99 estimate, or intervention effect. The contribution is an auditable, human-in-the-loop pattern for trustworthy agent assistance on a real workstation, including explicit stop conditions when evidence is insufficient.

</details>


### [238] [Trustworthy RAG: An Evaluation Agent for Detecting Misinformation and Knowledge Poisoning in Generative AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21095)
*Balkrishna Giri,Md Toufique Hasan,Jussi Rasku,Muhammad Waseem,Pekka Abrahamsson*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes an Evaluation Agent middleware for RAG systems to detect knowledge poisoning attacks. The agent combines NLI-based factual verification, a five-signal poison detector, and a Trust Index formula. It achieves 91% accuracy and 100% precision on TruthfulQA, with 100% recall on instruction injection attacks, while revealing limitations in detecting entity swaps and subtle semantic weakening.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems trust whatever they retrieve, creating a Security-Reliability Gap where high semantic relevance doesn't guarantee factual truth. Adversaries exploit this through knowledge poisoning—inserting malicious documents to cause targeted misinformation. Current RAG systems lack mechanisms to verify factual accuracy of retrieved content.

Method: The proposed Evaluation Agent middleware combines three components: (1) Natural Language Inference (NLI) for factual verification, (2) a five-signal poison detector with relevance-weighted aggregation, and (3) a Trust Index T = 0.4 F + 0.35 C + 0.25 (1 - P) with a non-linear dampener for high-contamination contexts. The system detects poisoned context before LLM generation.

Result: On TruthfulQA with Llama 3.3 70B: 91% accuracy, 100% precision, 100% recall on instruction injection. ROC-AUC of 0.73-0.81 across three LLMs. Generation style matters more than model size. Per-LLM threshold calibration restores baseline competitive accuracy. In software engineering use case (OWASP Top 10, CWE), blocks instruction injection with F1 92%. Weaker FEVER results show cross-dataset generalization requires domain-specific calibration.

Conclusion: The Evaluation Agent effectively detects most poisoning attacks but struggles with in-place entity swaps, contradiction, and subtle semantic weakening. The Trust Index remains discriminative across models, though cross-dataset generalization requires domain-specific calibration. The system measures detection before generation—it does not verify whether the LLM actually adopts the injected misinformation.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) grounds Large Language Model (LLM) outputs in external knowledge, but RAG systems usually trust whatever they retrieve, creating a Security-Reliability Gap: high semantic relevance does not guarantee factual truth. Adversaries exploit this through knowledge poisoning, inserting malicious documents to cause targeted misinformation. We propose an Evaluation Agent, middleware that combines Natural Language Inference (NLI) factual verification, a five-signal poison detector with relevance-weighted aggregation, and a Trust Index T = 0.4 F + 0.35 C + 0.25 (1 - P ) with a non-linear dampener for high-contamination contexts. On TruthfulQA with Llama 3.3 70B, the agent reaches 91% accuracy and 100% precision, with 100% recall on instruction injection, while in-place edits, such as entity swaps, remain hard to detect. Across three LLMs the Trust Index stays discriminative, with a Receiver Operating Characteristic Area Under the Curve (ROC-AUC) of 0.73 to 0.81; generation style matters more than model size, and per-LLM threshold calibration restores baseline competitive accuracy, whereas a weaker FEVER result shows that cross-dataset generalization requires domain-specific calibration. In a software-engineering use case, a secure-coding assistant over guidance from the Open Worldwide Application Security Project (OWASP) Top 10 and the Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE), the agent reliably blocks instruction injection of unsafe advice (F1 92%), while contradiction and subtle semantic weakening remain hard. Throughout, the agent measures detection of poisoned context before generation, not whether the LLM adopts the injected misinformation. We release the proposed approach, attack generator, and experimental artifacts at the link: https://github.com/GPT-Laboratory/TrustworthyRAG.

</details>


### [239] [On the Time and Frequency Domain Representations of Signals for CPS Specification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21167)
*Claudio Mandrioli,Drishti Yadav,Domenico Bianculli*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes Signal-Spectrum Temporal Logic (S2TL), a specification language for Cyber-Physical Systems that uses time-frequency representations instead of traditional time-domain methods. The approach enables assertions over frequency intervals and relations between frequency components, improving monitoring capabilities for CPS requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current specification languages use time-domain representation of signals, which is unsuitable for describing signal shapes and dynamic behaviors. While control and robotics use frequency-domain representations, time-frequency representations could combine both domains' capabilities for better CPS requirement specification.

Method: 1) Analyzed existing taxonomies of CPS requirements to identify classes benefiting from time-frequency representations
2) Derived desiderata for a specification language using time-frequency representations
3) Proposed S2TL (Signal-Spectrum Temporal Logic) enabling assertions over frequency intervals and frequency component relations
4) Operationalized S2TL semantics for monitoring CPS traces and implemented a monitor
5) Defined specification templates for identified requirement classes
6) Compared time-domain and time-frequency formulations across applicability, expression fidelity, and noise tolerance

Result: Time-domain specifications are only applicable to traces with step-like changes or constant interpolation. Time-frequency specifications extend evaluation to traces with linear interpolation, improve tolerance to offset and high-frequency noise, while maintaining comparable fidelity to intended system properties.

Conclusion: S2TL successfully enables time-frequency-based specification for CPS, offering broader applicability (supports linear interpolation) and better noise tolerance compared to traditional time-domain approaches, while maintaining similar expression fidelity.

Abstract: Specification languages are instrumental to the Verification \& Validation of Cyber-Physical Systems (CPSs). Most state-of-the-art specification languages use the time-domain representation of signals, which is not always suitable for describing signal shapes and dynamic behaviours. Instead, fields like control and robotics use the frequency-domain representation to characterise these behaviours. Time-frequency representations combine the capabilities of both domains. We investigate the use of time-frequency representations to specify CPS requirements. We analyse existing taxonomies of CPS requirements to identify which requirement classes can benefit from time-frequency representations. We derive the desiderata for a specification language that uses time-frequency representations and propose Signal-Spectrum Temporal Logic (S2TL), a language enabling assertions over frequency intervals and relations between frequency components. We operationalise the S2TL semantics for monitoring CPS traces, and implement a monitor. We define specification templates for the identified requirement classes and compare time- and time-frequency-domain formulations in terms of applicability, expression fidelity, and noise tolerance of monitoring. We observe that, while time-domain specifications are applicable only to input traces containing step-like changes or using constant interpolation, time-frequency specifications extend their evaluation to traces generated through linear interpolation, and improve tolerance to offset and high-frequency noise, while achieving comparable fidelity to the intended system properties.

</details>


### [240] [Specification Portability Across LLM Development Agents: Cross-Agent Compatibility in Specification-Driven Software Migration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21208)
*Oleg Grynets,Oleksii Ilchuk,Dariia Zatulna,Vasyl Lyashkevych*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether software specifications can be reused across different AI agents for Oracle-to-PostgreSQL migration. Through experiments on 1,006 PL/SQL files and cross-agent testing with 5 AI coding assistants, the study finds that specifications are not agent-neutral: cross-agent transfer can cause severe degradation (e.g., Gemini achieved only 2.33% SQL validity when using Kiro's specification), and only retrieval-augmented strategies appeared on optimal performance frontiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent software engineering increasingly uses specifications to coordinate heterogeneous AI agents, but it remains unclear whether specifications developed for one agent can be effectively used by another. This gap is critical for specification-driven development (SDD) workflows where agents may need to collaborate across different platforms or tools.

Method: The study conducted two experimental stages: (1) A specification-first migration pipeline evaluating 1,006 PL/SQL files, and (2) Cross-agent experiments on 1,802 Oracle scripts comparing Amazon Kiro, Google Gemini, GitHub Copilot, Claude Code, and Cursor. Evaluations used six metrics: Token F1, exact match, SQL syntax validity, AST exact match, AST mean similarity, and immediate runnability. Strategies tested included rewriting, compression, and retrieval-augmented ingestion.

Result: Key findings include: (1) Specification size does not predict implementation quality; (2) Cross-agent transfer causes significant agent-dependent degradation—the worst case was Gemini consuming Kiro-origin specifications (Token F1=0.035, SQL validity=2.33%, AST similarity=0.015); (3) Rewriting substantially improved Gemini's performance; (4) Compression provided no universal benefit; (5) Retrieval-augmented ingestion was the only strategy appearing on both Gemini's and Copilot's Pareto frontiers.

Conclusion: Specifications in heterogeneous SDD workflows should not be treated as agent-neutral artifacts. The study motivates explicit consideration of specification portability, agent-specific interpretation, and retrieval-based access in multi-agent software engineering. Practitioners should evaluate cross-agent compatibility before adopting specification-based workflows.

Abstract: This paper investigates cross-agent specification portability using Oracle-to-PostgreSQL migration as a controlled software transformation task. The study combines two experimental stages. First, a specification-first migration pipeline was evaluated on 1,006 PL/SQL files, of which 623 were successfully regenerated and 380 generated scripts executed successfully in PostgreSQL 16. Second, cross-agent experiments were conducted on a dataset of 1,802 Oracle scripts with corresponding PostgreSQL implementations using Amazon Kiro, Google Gemini, and GitHub Copilot, with Claude Code and Cursor included in the initial single-agent evaluation. Native and foreign specifications were assessed using Token F1, exact match, SQL syntax validity, AST exact match, AST mean similarity, and immediate runnability. The results show that specification size alone does not predict implementation quality and that cross-agent transfer can produce substantial agent-dependent degradation. The strongest replicated case occurred when Gemini directly consumed a Kiro-origin specification, producing a Token F1 of 0.035, SQL syntax validity of 2.33%, and AST mean similarity of 0.015. Rewriting substantially improved Gemini in the tested configuration, compression did not provide a universal benefit, and retrieval-augmented ingestion was the only common strategy represented on the per-agent Pareto frontiers of both Gemini and Copilot. The findings suggest that specifications in heterogeneous SDD workflows should not automatically be treated as agent-neutral artifacts and motivate explicit consideration of specification portability, agent-specific interpretation, and retrieval-based access in multi-agent software engineering.

</details>


### [241] [The Substitution Escrow Threshold: When "Compatible With" Becomes Safe Enough to Buy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21221)
*Amadeus Brandes*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces the "Substitution Escrow Threshold," a five-condition framework to evaluate whether compatibility claims (e.g., "S3-compatible," "PostgreSQL-compatible") genuinely reduce institutional risk or merely reduce first-integration cost. The framework addresses the gap between compatibility claims and actual substitution possibilities in enterprise infrastructure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise infrastructure buyers use compatibility claims as proxies for future substitution options, but most such claims do not actually guarantee that substitution is possible. The authors aim to provide a systematic framework to distinguish genuine risk reduction from mere integration cost reduction.

Method: The paper proposes a five-condition framework called the Substitution Escrow Threshold, with conditions including: (1) boundary closure, (2) executable conformance, (3) custody independence, (4) state and operations reversibility, and (5) extension quarantine. This framework is applied to five infrastructure cases: OCI, Kubernetes, OpenTelemetry, S3, and PostgreSQL.

Result: The five case studies populate five distinct outcome cells, demonstrating the framework's ability to categorize compatibility claims by their actual substitution guarantees. The framework produces actionable diagnostics for enterprise architects, platform engineers, procurement teams, and investors.

Conclusion: The Substitution Escrow Threshold framework provides a systematic way to evaluate compatibility claims in enterprise infrastructure decisions. The authors identify AI infrastructure as the most urgent next application area for this framework.

Abstract: Enterprise infrastructure buyers routinely evaluate compatibility claims--"S3-compatible," "PostgreSQL-compatible," "OpenAI compatible"--as proxies for future substitution options. Yet most compatibility claims do not escrow the substitution path they imply. This paper introduces the Substitution Escrow Threshold, a five-condition framework that determines when a compatibility claim genuinely reduces institutional risk versus merely reducing first-integration cost. The five conditions--boundary closure, executable conformance, custody independence, state and operations reversibility, and extension quarantine--are applied to five infrastructure cases (OCI, Kubernetes, OpenTelemetry, S3, PostgreSQL) that populate five distinct outcome cells. The framework produces actionable diagnostics for enterprise architects, platform engineers, procurement teams, and investors evaluating compatibility-dependent infrastructure decisions, and identifies AI infrastructure as the framework's most urgent next application.

</details>


### [242] [Human-AI Collaboration in Requirements Engineering: Evidence of the Negative Effect of LLMs on Requirements Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21298)
*Giovanna Broccia,Julian Frattini,Chetan Arora,Maurice H. ter Beek,Alessandro Fantechi,Andreas Vogelsang,Alessio Ferrari*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study investigates how LLM support affects human-performed requirements inspection (RI) through a controlled crossover experiment with 34 participants. Results show LLM support negatively impacts smell detection accuracy but has no significant effect on classification accuracy or task duration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While requirements inspection is a well-established practice for detecting defects early in software development, and LLMs have shown potential for supporting RE tasks, there is limited empirical evidence on how LLMs affect human performance in actual RI tasks. This study addresses this gap.

Method: Controlled crossover design experiment with 34 participants who inspected textual specifications both with and without LLM support. Participants identified and classified requirements smells (nocuous vs. innocuous) while recording inspection time. Analysis used Bayesian regression models accounting for crossover design threats, covariates, and mediators.

Result: LLM support significantly reduced smell detection accuracy. No significant effects were found on smell classification or task duration. A learning effect was observed across experimental periods, but this effect was reduced when RI was first performed with LLM support.

Conclusion: LLM support does not automatically improve human performance in requirements inspection and may actually hinder it, particularly for novice inspectors. Learning RI with LLM support from the beginning may slow skill acquisition, suggesting potential risks for LLM-supported learning in RE contexts.

Abstract: Background. Requirements inspection (RI) is a well-established practice for detecting potential defects in requirements artifacts early in the software lifecycle. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have stimulated interest in their potential to support requirements engineering (RE) tasks. However, empirical evidence on the effects of LLMs when used as collaborative assistants in human-performed RI remains scarce. Aims. We aim to investigate the impact of LLM support on human-performed RI, considering inspection effectiveness in terms of smell identification and severity classification (i.e., nocuous vs innocuous), as well as inspection duration. Method. We conducted a controlled crossover design experiment with 34 participants, who inspected textual specifications with and without LLM support, identifying and classifying requirements smells while recording inspection time. We analyzed the data using one Bayesian regression model per outcome variable, accounting for validity threats induced by the crossover design as well as covariates and mediators. Results. Results show that LLM support negatively affects smell detection accuracy but has no significant effect on smell classification or task duration. A learning effect is present across experimental periods, but reduced when RI is first performed with LLM support. Conclusions. Our findings provide empirical evidence that LLM support does not necessarily improve performance and may, instead, hinder it for novice inspectors. Moreover, the results suggest that learning RI with LLM-support from the beginning may slow down the skill acquisition process, implying threats for LLM-supported learning.

</details>


### [243] [Beyond Fault Localization: A Trajectory-Level Study of LLM Agents for Microservice Root Cause Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21310)
*Qisheng Lu,Aoyang Fang,Junjielong Xu,Jin'ao Shang,Songhan Zhang,Yifan Yang,Xiaochuan Yan,Pinjia He*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper addresses a critical gap in evaluating Automated Root Cause Analysis (RCA) for microservices: existing metrics only measure endpoint correctness (correct service localization) but ignore the diagnostic process itself. The authors propose a trajectory-level framework that evaluates agent executions against fault-propagation paths, analyzing 3,500 diagnostic trajectories. They discover a disconnect between answer correctness and diagnostic quality, and propose DiagGuard, a two-stage defense architecture that improves Acc@1 from 43.5% to 52.5%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of automated RCA for microservices assess diagnostic performance mainly by endpoint correctness - whether a method localizes the responsible service. This criterion enables comparison but fails to reveal: (1) the evidentiary basis of a diagnosis, and (2) the fault-propagation route connecting the source to observed symptoms. Both of these are essential for on-call SREs to judge whether action is warranted. Thus, the authors treat RCA as an observable diagnostic process rather than just a final answer.

Method: The authors propose a trajectory-level framework that evaluates agent executions against manually curated service-level fault-propagation paths. Applied to a public microservice RCA benchmark, this framework analyzes 3,500 diagnostic trajectories, characterizing where agents investigate and how they use retrieved telemetry. Based on findings, they operationalize a taxonomy as DiagGuard, a two-stage defense-in-depth architecture: (1) Grounding surveys available observations before localization, and (2) Verification audits the diagnosis against them.

Result: The study reveals a disconnect between answer correctness and diagnostic quality: an agent may localize the fault source yet fail to reconstruct its propagation. Successful investigations share three characteristics: (1) stay on the fault-impact surface, (2) act on retrieved evidence, and (3) broaden query repertoire as search deepens. Failures arise from: (1) decisive evidence being omitted, (2) retrieved evidence being misinterpreted, or (3) unsupported inference substituting for missing evidence. In an independent setting with different model, benchmark, and service topology, DiagGuard raises Acc@1 from 43.5% to 52.5%.

Conclusion: Trajectory-level evaluation exposes limitations hidden by final-answer metrics and provides actionable guidance for improving automated RCA. The proposed DiagGuard architecture demonstrates that grounding observations before localization and verifying diagnoses against evidence significantly improves diagnostic accuracy. This work highlights the importance of evaluating not just what answer an RCA system produces, but how it arrives at that diagnosis.

Abstract: Existing evaluations of automated root cause analysis (RCA) for microservices assess diagnostic performance mainly by endpoint correctness: whether a method localizes the responsible service. This criterion enables comparison but does not reveal the evidentiary basis of a diagnosis or the fault-propagation route connecting the source to observed symptoms, both of which an on-call site reliability engineer needs to judge whether action is warranted. We therefore treat RCA as an observable diagnostic process. Our trajectory-level framework evaluates agent executions against manually curated service-level fault-propagation paths. Applied to a public microservice RCA benchmark, it analyzes 3,500 diagnostic trajectories, characterizing where agents investigate and how they use retrieved telemetry. We find a disconnect between answer correctness and diagnostic quality: an agent may localize the fault source yet fail to reconstruct its propagation. Successful investigations stay on the fault-impact surface, act on retrieved evidence, and broaden their query repertoire as the search deepens. Failures arise when decisive evidence is omitted, retrieved evidence is misinterpreted, or unsupported inference substitutes for missing evidence. We operationalize this taxonomy as DiagGuard, a two-stage defense-in-depth architecture in which grounding surveys available observations before localization and verification audits the diagnosis against them. In an independent setting with a different model, benchmark, and service topology, DiagGuard raises Acc@1 from 43.5% to 52.5%. These results show that trajectory-level evaluation exposes limitations hidden by final-answer metrics and provides actionable guidance for improving automated RCA.

</details>


### [244] [AI-to-AI Code Reviews of GitHub Pull Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21311)
*Niruthiha Selvanayagam,Taher A. Ghaleb*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates the emerging phenomenon of AI-to-AI code review, where AI coding agents both author pull requests and review contributions from other AI agents. The study analyzes a large dataset of 248,641 AI-attributed PRs with AI-attributed reviews, examining cross-product vs. same-product review patterns, comment variations, and latency differences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand the closed-loop dynamics of AI coding agents operating on both sides of the PR process - as authors and reviewers. As AI coding agents become more prevalent in software development, this AI-to-AI review pattern represents a novel phenomenon that needs characterization and analysis.

Method: The researchers constructed a large-scale dataset by linking AI-attributed PRs with AI-attributed review events from CodAGE (a public dataset of coding-agent-generated GitHub events). They analyzed 248,641 unique AI-attributed PRs that received at least one AI-attributed review, categorizing them into cross-product (45,269), same-product (208,145), and both (4,773) groups. They examined reviewer output variations, comment types, and timestamp latencies across different author-reviewer configurations.

Result: Key findings: Cross-product AI-to-AI review occurred in ~1.6% of identified agent-authored PRs but grew by >100x from 2025-Q1 to 2025-Q3. CodeRabbit labeled 35.0% of comments on Claude Code PRs as refactor vs. 10.5% on Copilot PRs. For dual-role reviewers, mean comments per PR were 58-65% higher in same-product groups, concentrated in the upper tail. Median latency was 1.2 minutes for cross-product vs. 4.7 minutes for same-product pairs.

Conclusion: Closed-loop AI-to-AI review is increasing but remains a minority of identified agent activity. Review output varies across authoring-agent groups and product configurations, with differences potentially reflecting PR characteristics rather than reviewer behavior alone. The phenomenon represents a significant emerging pattern in AI-assisted software development.

Abstract: AI coding agents are increasingly integrated into software development workflows, operating on both sides of the pull-request (PR) process: AI authoring agents create or modify PRs, while AI reviewers evaluate them. This creates a closed loop in which one AI coding agent reviews a contribution attributed to another. We construct a large-scale dataset of AI-to-AI code review by linking AI-attributed PRs with AI-attributed review events from CodAGE, a public dataset of coding-agent-generated GitHub events. Our dataset contains 248,641 unique AI-attributed PRs that received at least one AI-attributed review. Of these, 45,269 received cross-product review and 208,145 received same-product review; 4,773 PRs received both. Cross-product AI-to-AI review occurred in approximately 1.6% of identified agent-authored PRs but was substantial in absolute terms, and its volume increased by more than two orders of magnitude from 2025-Q1 to 2025-Q3. Reviewer output varied across author-reviewer configurations. CodeRabbit labeled 35.0% of its comments on Claude Code-authored PRs as refactor comments, compared with 10.5% on Copilot-authored PRs, although this difference may reflect characteristics of the PRs rather than the reviewer. For three of four dual-role reviewers, mean comments per PR were 58-65% higher in the same-product group, although effect sizes were small or negligible and the difference was concentrated in the upper tail. Among pairs with complete, nonnegative timestamps, the observed median latency was 1.2 minutes for cross-product pairs and 4.7 minutes for same-product pairs; differential timestamp availability and reviewer composition limit this comparison. Overall, closed-loop AI-to-AI review is increasing but remains a minority of identified agent activity, with review output varying across authoring-agent groups and product configurations.

</details>


### [245] [Natural-Language Workflows Are Not Software Yet: Artifact-Driven Compilation for Reliable Agent Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21341)
*Xiangzhe Xu,Hanxi Guo,Guangyu Shen,Siyuan Cheng,Xiangyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Natural-language workflows offer a software-like interface for agents: domain experts can write reusable procedures, and agents can execute them as instructions. This promise is not yet reliable. Workflow descriptions often leave data dependencies implicit, so the executor must infer which prior results a step should use; agents can also fail to follow long or branching instructions under context pressure. We propose Artic, an artifact-driven workflow compiler that transforms a natural-language workflow into an artifact-driven workflow in which each step declares the artifacts it reads and writes, constraints gate produced artifacts, and explicit control transfers route execution. This representation exposes the enforcement burden placed on agent execution, allowing the compiler to identify steps that depend on too much state or contain difficult control logic and refine them through constrained optimization. To validate the LLM-assisted transformation, Artic decomposes faithfulness checking into local obligations and uses scenario-based dry runs to test whether compiled workflow regions conform to the source workflow. We evaluate Artic on 488 problem instances from 11 real-world domain workflows; it improves task resolve rate by 28 percentage points over the original text workflow. We also show that workflows compiled by Artic are 32 and 56 percentage points more consistent in cross-model and repeated-execution setups, respectively.

</details>


### [246] [AI with Authority, from Application to Silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21356)
*Jason Hickey*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents the "Salt method" - a new discipline where generative AI makes machine verification economical and essential for productivity. In five weeks, one researcher used AI agents to go from application code to a tape-out RISC-V processor without human-written RTL or human-reviewed proofs. The method uses a proof kernel (Lean 4) that prevents hallucinated proofs, with mathematical claims traveling as kernel-checked artifacts. The project achieved zero incorrect proofs reaching the record, with an error ledger tracking catches #1-#256.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: For sixty years, machine verification has been a major cost overhead, affordable only for exceptional artifacts. The authors sought to invert this relationship - making machine verification not only economical but essential to productivity using generative AI, enabling one person to safely direct autonomous machine work at scale.

Method: The Salt method rests on a proof kernel that no hallucinated proof can pass. Mathematical claims travel between AI agents as kernel-checked artifacts (Lean 4), reserving human attention for statements, designs, and rulings. Verification is stated link by link from the Lean 4 kernel to SAT-checked equivalence at the silicon boundary. The working discipline includes: theorem provenance, a pre-registered token meter, floor-bounded human time, and an error ledger with an append-only flags ledger.

Result: In five weeks, one researcher using consumer AI subscriptions directed a small fleet of AI agents from application code, through a verified compiler and executive, to a RISC-V processor taped out on a community silicon shuttle. No proof passed through human review, and no RTL was written by a human. The error ledger recorded catches numbering to #256 (with #79 never assigned), against zero incorrect proofs reaching the record.

Conclusion: Generative AI inverts the traditional relationship of machine verification - at AI speed, it becomes not only economical but essential to productivity as the "incorruptible referee" that enables scalable autonomous machine work. The complete accounting is published including theorem provenance, token meter, human time, and error ledger.

Abstract: For sixty years, machine verification has been a major cost overhead, affordable only for exceptional artifacts. Here we report that generative AI inverts this relationship: at AI speed, machine verification is not only economical but essential to productivity --- it is the incorruptible referee that lets one person safely direct autonomous machine work at scale. In five weeks, one researcher on consumer AI subscriptions directed a small fleet of AI agents from application code, through a verified compiler and executive, to a RISC-V processor taped out on a community silicon shuttle; no proof passed through human review, and no RTL was written by a human. The working discipline --- the Salt method --- rests on a proof kernel no hallucinated proof can pass: mathematical claims travel between agents as kernel-checked artifacts, and human attention is reserved for statements, designs, and rulings. Verification is stated link by link, from the Lean 4 kernel to SAT-checked equivalence at the silicon boundary. We publish the complete accounting: theorem provenance, a pre-registered token meter, floor-bounded human time, and an error ledger whose catch numbering runs to #256 --- a monotone counter over the mathematics campaign's append-only flags ledger, maintained 2026-07-07 to 2026-07-20 (one number, #79, was never assigned; later catches are recorded un-numbered) --- against zero incorrect proofs reaching the record.

</details>

### [247] [A Factorial Ablation of a Speech-to-SFT Pipeline: Differential Effects on Data Quality and Downstream Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20394)
*Wonsup Shin,Jingu Kim*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper ablates a production speech-to-SFT pipeline to determine each stage's marginal value. Despite improvements in LLM-judge-assessed quality, enhanced QA data does not significantly improve MCQA performance overall, with gains only in domain-family aligned cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industry pipelines converting speech to SFT data via multi-stage refinement are widely adopted but lack stage-by-stage ablation studies, leaving each stage's marginal value unknown.

Method: A 2x2 factorial design with independently toggleable transcript refinement (Phase 0) and SFT data quality refinement (Phase 2). Generated QA-form SFT data from Korean medical and finance conference recordings, fine-tuned 9 models across 5 LLM families (2.4B-70B), evaluated with 4 cross-provider LLM judges, blind 6-expert human evaluation, and 3 downstream MCQA benchmarks. Also tested STT engine swap to Whisper-medium and performed non-hallucination audit on frontier LLMs.

Result: Improvements in QA data quality do not transfer uniformly into downstream MCQA gains. 4-judge quality rises consistently, but cross-model mean MCQA gain is not significant. Positive transfer concentrates on family-domain aligned pairs. This pattern suggests format mismatch: Phase 2 shifts SFT-data toward explanatory items while MCQA primarily probes factoid recall. All six human raters report higher full-pipeline quality. STT engine swap confirms pipeline robustness. Two frontier LLMs admit unknown on ~8% of QA on average.

Conclusion: Under fixed SFT recipes, quality improvements in SFT data do not automatically translate to downstream task gains. The transfer depends on alignment between data format (explanatory vs. factoid) and downstream evaluation type. The study releases samples, prompts, code, and SFT checkpoints.

Abstract: Industry pipelines that turn speech into supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data via multi-stage refinement are increasingly adopted but, to our knowledge, have not been publicly ablated stage-by-stage, leaving each stage's marginal value unknown. We design a production-ready speech-to-SFT pipeline in which transcript refinement (Phase 0) and SFT data quality refinement (Phase 2) are independently toggleable, yielding a 2x2 factorial design. For each condition, we generate QA-form SFT data from Korean medical and finance conference recordings and fine-tune 9 models (5 LLM families, 2.4B-70B); we evaluate with four cross-provider LLM judges, a blind six-expert human evaluation, and 3 downstream MCQA benchmarks. Our central finding: under a fixed, standard SFT recipe, improvements in QA data quality do not transfer uniformly into downstream MCQA gains. 4-judge quality rises consistently, yet the cross-model mean MCQA gain is not significant; positive transfer concentrates on family-domain aligned pairs. This differential pattern is consistent with a format mismatch: Phase 2 shifts SFT-data composition toward explanatory items, while MCQA primarily probes factoid recall. All six human raters report higher full-pipeline quality, confirming the LLM-judge direction. An STT-engine swap to Whisper-medium confirms pipeline robustness. A non-hallucination audit shows the two frontier LLMs admit unknown on approximately 8% of QA on average; we release samples, prompts, code, and all SFT checkpoints.

</details>


### [248] [A Regularized Block Diagonal RLS Algorithm for Acoustic Echo Cancellation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20693)
*Ruibin Hou,Chenggang Zhang,Yufeng Diao*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: While the recursive least square (RLS) algorithm is widely used in adaptive filtering applications like acoustic echo cancellation (AEC) due to its fast convergence rate, its high computational complexity severely limit its practical deployment for long filters. In this paper, a regularized block-diagonal RLS (RBD-RLS) algorithm is proposed to address these challenges. By approximating the inverse covariance matrix as a block-diagonal structure, RBD-RLS simplifies the update process into independent parallel computations of sub-blocks, effectively reducing the computational complexity. Additionally, Tikhonov regularization is applied to each sub-blocks for enhance numerical stability. A series of experimental results demonstrate that RBD-RLS maintains good convergence while significantly reducing computational complexity. Moreover, it still exhibits relative robustness in real-world scenarios.

</details>


### [249] [Do SpeechLMs Hear Their Own Opinions? Diagnosing and Mitigating Previous-Belief Contamination in Streaming Emotion Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20769)
*Haoyue Liu,Zhichao Wang,Ye Chen,Haonan Deng,Xiaoying Tang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper identifies a critical flaw in streaming emotion understanding systems called Previous-Belief Contamination (PBC), where historical state information distorts current audio perception. The authors propose EmoUpdate, a training-free framework with three components (prior-blind acoustic firewall, evidence-shrunk causal belief filter, and closed-form decontamination operator) that achieves significant improvements across four SpeechLMs and two benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Streaming emotion understanding systems use historical state as context, feeding previous predictions back. This history conditioning can distort current perception, leading to a failure mode called Previous-Belief Contamination (PBC). The problem is demonstrated through counterfactual experiments showing dramatic accuracy drops and prediction flips when only the previous emotion label is changed.

Method: The authors introduce EmoUpdate, a training-free framework with three components: (1) A prior-blind acoustic firewall that prevents historical state from entering perception; (2) An evidence-shrunk causal belief filter that introduces history only after observation formation and retains label-asymmetric transition structure only when supported by observed evidence; (3) A closed-form decontamination operator derived from counterfactual measurements for serving stacks where firewalling is unavailable.

Result: Using a balanced CREMA-D-Stream counterfactual diagnostic, changing only the injected previous emotion label (while holding audio fixed) reduced current-audio accuracy from 72.50% to 30.42% and flipped 65.69% of predictions. The effect showed strong label-asymmetry with prior pull ranging from 4.76% to 98.20%. EmoUpdate achieved the best step accuracy and state-balanced accuracy across all eight model-benchmark settings, improving S-BAcc by up to 69.71 points and step accuracy by up to 38.41 points over the strongest baselines.

Conclusion: EmOUpdate successfully addresses PBC in streaming emotion understanding systems through its training-free approach, demonstrating substantial improvements in both step accuracy and state-balanced accuracy across multiple models and benchmarks. The framework's effectiveness validates the importance of separating current-audio perception from historical state revision in emotion recognition systems.

Abstract: Streaming emotion understanding uses historical state while continuously interpreting current audio, often feeding the model's previous prediction back as context. We show that this history conditioning can distort current perception. On a balanced CREMA-D-Stream counterfactual diagnostic, changing only the injected previous emotion label while holding the audio fixed reduces current-audio accuracy from 72.50% to 30.42% and flips 65.69% of predictions. The effect is strongly label-asymmetric, with prior pull ranging from 4.76% to 98.20%, revealing a failure we call previous-belief contamination (PBC). To address PBC, we introduce EmoUpdate, a training-free framework that separates current-audio perception from historical state revision through three components: (1) a prior-blind acoustic firewall that prevents historical state from entering perception; (2) an evidence-shrunk causal belief filter that introduces history only after observation formation and retains label-asymmetric transition structure only when supported by observed evidence; and (3) a closed-form decontamination operator derived from the same counterfactual measurements for serving stacks where firewalling is unavailable. Across four SpeechLMs and two streaming emotion benchmarks, EmoUpdate achieves the best step accuracy and state-balanced accuracy in all eight model--benchmark settings, improving S-BAcc by up to 69.71 points and step accuracy by up to 38.41 points over the strongest controlled baselines.

</details>


### [250] [AudioWorldSim: Realistic Binaural Audio Datasets For World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21075)
*Luis Vitor Zerkowski,Luiz Velho*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: AudioWorldSim is an open-source platform built on Meta's SoundSpaces 2.0 that generates realistic binaural audio datasets for advancing audio-based machine learning and world models research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the need for realistic binaural audio datasets in audio-based machine learning research, particularly for world models, by providing an open-source platform with automatic agent navigation rollouts and fixes to continuous sound composition.

Method: Built as a custom extension of Meta's SoundSpaces 2.0 platform, leveraging its comprehensive acoustics framework while implementing automatic rollout of random agent navigations and fixes to continuous sound composition.

Result: A publicly available open-source platform (AudioWorldSim) that generates realistic binaural audio datasets for research purposes.

Conclusion: AudioWorldSim is made publicly available to the research community on GitHub to facilitate reproducibility and advance research in audio-based machine learning and world models.

Abstract: This technical report presents AudioWorldSim, an open-source platform designed to generate realistic binaural audio datasets and advance research in audio-based machine learning, particularly world models. Built as a custom extension of Meta's SoundSpaces 2.0 platform, AudioWorldSim leverages their comprehensive acoustics framework, but focuses on the automatic rollout of random agent navigations, as well as implements crucial fixes to how continuous sound is composed. AudioWorldSim is made publicly available to the research community at https://github.com/Luizerko/AudioWorldSim to facilitate reproducibility.

</details>


### [251] [DAMOS: Learning Distortion-Aware Speech Quality Assessment through Explicit Distortion Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21176)
*Naiyuan Li,Li Dong,Diqun Yan*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper introduces DAMOS, a distortion-aware speech quality assessment framework that uses explicit distortion localization as auxiliary knowledge. The key insight is that perceptual quality is dominated by local distortions, but existing methods only use coarse utterance-level MOS supervision. The authors created a dataset with frame-level distortion annotations and integrated localization cues into MOS prediction, achieving state-of-the-art performance with strong cross-dataset generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing speech quality assessment methods are optimized only with utterance-level MOS, which provides coarse-grained supervision without indicating where perceptually important distortions occur. For synthetic speech, distortions often occur locally and overall perceptual quality is dominated by a small number of salient distortion regions. This limitation motivates the need for explicit distortion localization as auxiliary knowledge.

Method: 1) Constructed the first partially distorted speech dataset with frame-level distortion annotations. 2) Trained a localization model to generate distortion cues. 3) Proposed DAMOS framework that integrates localization information into the MOS prediction pipeline, combining distortion-aware features with quality prediction.

Result: DAMOS consistently outperforms existing methods on multiple public benchmarks and exhibits strong cross-dataset generalization, validating the effectiveness of explicit distortion localization for speech quality assessment.

Conclusion: Explicit distortion localization serves as valuable auxiliary knowledge for speech quality assessment. By identifying perceptually salient distortion regions and integrating this information into the prediction pipeline, the framework achieves superior performance and generalization compared to methods relying solely on utterance-level MOS supervision.

Abstract: Automatic speech quality assessment aims to predict Mean Opinion Scores (MOS) consistent with human subjective perception and is essential for evaluating speech generation, enhancement, and communication systems. For speech signals, especially synthetic speech, distortions often occur locally, and overall perceptual quality is usually dominated by a small number of perceptually salient distortion regions. However, most existing methods are primarily optimized with utterance-level MOS, which provides only coarse-grained supervision and offer no explicit indication of where perceptually important distortions occur. To address this limitation, we introduce explicit distortion localization as auxiliary knowledge for speech quality assessment. We construct the first partially distorted speech dataset with frame-level distortion annotations and train a localization model to generate distortion cues. Building on these cues, we propose DAMOS, a distortion-aware speech quality assessment framework that integrates localization information into the MOS prediction pipeline. Experiments on multiple public benchmarks demonstrate that DAMOS consistently outperforms existing methods and exhibits strong cross-dataset generalization, validating the effectiveness of explicit distortion localization for speech quality assessment.

</details>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [252] [Maximum Entropy Encoding of Energy-Weighted Spherical Moments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20429)
*Jiaze Sun*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: This paper presents a maximum-entropy (MaxEnt) framework for compressing angular energy signals from Monte Carlo path samples for irradiance reconstruction. Using total energy, first directional moment, and traceless second moment as 9 linearly additive statistics, the method derives a closure yielding probability distributions proportional to g⁻³ and angular energy density proportional to g⁻⁴. Two subfamilies (4-param dipole and 5-param coaxial) are analyzed with closed-form solutions. Experiments on 984 HDRI scenes show the 5-parameter variant achieves 78.7% win rate against QZH with 15.8% RMSE reduction, while maintaining zero negative irradiance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monte Carlo path tracing produces expensive angular energy samples that need efficient compression for irradiance reconstruction. Existing methods like Spherical Harmonics (SH) or QZH (Quadrature Zonal Harmonics) have limitations: SH suffers from negative values and limited directionality capture, while storage requirements for QZH can be high. The authors seek a statistically principled compression approach using finite moments that guarantees positivity and handles high-directionality scenes better than SH-2.

Method: The method represents each Monte Carlo sample as energy-weighted directional feature x = r·u, then uses three rotationally covariant statistics: total energy (1 parameter), first directional moment (3 parameters), and traceless second moment (5 parameters). Under maximum-entropy closure with a fixed Lebesgue reference measure, the probability distribution p(r,u) ∝ exp(-βr·g(u)) is derived, where g(u) = 1 - b·u + u^T·Q·u. Two subfamilies are developed: (1) pure-dipole 4-parameter model with analytic moment matching and diffuse response, and (2) dipole-second-moment coaxial 5-parameter model with realizability domain analysis and LUT-oriented reconstruction.

Result: The 5-parameter MaxEnt achieves a 78.7% per-scene win rate against stored QZH, with mean luminance RMSE reduced by 15.8%. The advantage is more pronounced in high-directionality scenes. Both MaxEnt variants maintain zero negative irradiance across all 984 test scenes. Full second-order SH (SH-2) yields the lowest overall error, but 5-parameter MaxEnt ranks second and outperforms SH-2 in the high-directionality bucket. The coaxial subfamily exhibits systematic closure error on non-coaxial multi-source scenes.

Conclusion: Maximum-entropy closure provides a viable alternative to SH for irradiance compression, with guaranteed positivity and superior performance in high-directionality scenarios. The 5-parameter coaxial subfamily offers the best practical trade-off between accuracy and storage, though it struggles with non-coaxial multi-source configurations. Future work could explore non-coaxial parameterizations or hybrid SH-MaxEnt approaches to address this limitation.

Abstract: We study how angular energy signals composed of non-negative Monte Carlo path samples can be compressed and reconstructed for irradiance using finite moments. Writing each sample as an energy-weighted directional feature $x = r u$, we adopt total energy, the first directional moment, and the traceless second moment as $1+3+5$ linearly additive, rotationally covariant statistics. Under a fixed Lebesgue reference measure, the maximum-entropy closure yields $p(r,u) \propto \exp(-βr g(u))$, where $g(u) = 1 - b \cdot u + u^T Q u$, whose directional probability and angular energy density are proportional to $g^{-3}$ and $g^{-4}$, respectively. When $g_{\min} > 0$ the closure is normalizable and the reconstruction is strictly positive. We further provide analytic moment matching, variance, inverse sampling, and closed-form diffuse response for the pure-dipole four-parameter subfamily, as well as the realizability domain, partition function, azimuthal algebraic integral, and LUT-oriented reconstruction form for the dipole-second-moment coaxial five-parameter subfamily. Experiments cover 981 Poly Haven HDRI 2K scenes and three Debevec probes. Five-parameter MaxEnt achieves a 78.7% per-scene win rate against stored QZH, with mean luminance RMSE reduced by 15.8%; the advantage is more pronounced in scenes with strong directionality. Both MaxEnt variants maintain zero negative irradiance across all scenes. Full second-order SH-2 yields the lowest overall error, while five-parameter MaxEnt ranks second and outperforms SH-2 in the high-directionality bucket; the coaxial subfamily shows systematic closure error on non-coaxial multi-source scenes.

</details>


### [253] [MultiCube: Compositional 3D Generation With Part-Level Semantic and Spatial Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20448)
*Ava Pun,Kangle Deng,Yiheng Zhu,Jun-Yan Zhu,Maneesh Agrawala,Tinghui Zhou*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: MultiCube is a novel compositional 3D generation method that provides explicit, independent control over both the semantics and spatial arrangement of each part of a 3D object. It uses a two-stage diffusion process with a Part Layout Adapter to generate high-quality compositional 3D objects from text prompts, text schemas specifying parts, and spatial layout bounding boxes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current 3D generation methods conditioned on image or text prompts lack precise part-level controllability required for professional creative workflows, despite being able to produce high-quality compositional objects.

Method: MultiCube employs a two-stage diffusion process: first generating a schema- and layout-aligned monolithic mesh, then decomposing the mesh into individual parts simultaneously. A novel Part Layout Adapter encodes per-part conditions independently of other parts. Inputs include a global text prompt, a text schema specifying desired parts, and a spatial layout indicating bounding boxes.

Result: The method can generate high-quality compositional 3D objects with precise part-level control, including objects with unique layouts that are difficult to achieve with text or image prompting alone.

Conclusion: MultiCube successfully provides explicit, independent control over both semantics and spatial arrangement of each part, addressing a key limitation in professional 3D generation workflows.

Abstract: Digital 3D objects used in games and animation are often required to be compositional; that is, decomposed into semantically meaningful parts. Recent 3D generation methods can produce high-quality compositional objects conditioned on image or text prompts. Yet, such global conditioning lacks the precise part-level controllability required for professional creative workflows. To address this, we introduce MultiCube, a novel compositional 3D generation method that provides explicit, independent control over both the semantics and spatial arrangement of each part. MultiCube takes as input a global text prompt, a text schema specifying the desired parts, and a spatial layout indicating the bounding boxes of the parts in the given schema. It outputs a 3D object composed of distinct meshes, one per specified part, that adhere to the given semantic and spatial conditions. Our approach employs a two-stage diffusion process, first generating a schema- and layout-aligned monolithic mesh, then decomposing the mesh into individual parts simultaneously. A novel Part Layout Adapter is used to encode per-part conditions independently of the other parts. Experiments demonstrate that our method can generate high-quality compositional 3D objects with precise part-level control, including those with unique layouts difficult to achieve with text or image prompting alone. Project page: https://multi-cube.github.io

</details>


### [254] [CubicSplat: Differentiable Vector Graphics via Error-Bounded Forward Relaxation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20803)
*Chenglong Liu,Xin Zhang,Yimeng Zhu,Liyang He,Yixiao Ma,Yu Su,Zhenya Huang,Qi Liu*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: CubicSplat introduces a differentiable vector rasterizer that uses uniform polyline surrogates instead of Bézier closest-point solvers, achieving well-conditioned gradients by construction and bounded geometric error at O(S^{-2}).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical rasterization is discontinuous with respect to geometry, and existing differentiable remedies require increasingly elaborate heuristics as scene complexity grows. This fragility stems from a "gradient seesaw" where design choices improving forward geometric exactness can systematically degrade gradient signal, and vice versa.

Method: Replaces Bézier closest-point solvers with uniform polyline surrogates whose geometric error is bounded at O(S^{-2}). Uses a compositing-derived visibility mechanism to prune degenerate primitives without auxiliary regularization. The static computation graph yields well-conditioned gradients by construction.

Result: On DIV2K and Kodak benchmarks, CubicSplat achieves state-of-the-art reconstruction quality with over 2 dB PSNR gain in the closed-fill setting, while training up to 4x faster than prior methods.

Conclusion: CubicSplat successfully navigates the gradient-geometric exactness tension in differentiable vector graphics, providing a principled approach that achieves superior quality and faster training speeds compared to prior methods.

Abstract: Vector graphics are prized for their resolution independence, compact storage, and direct editability, making differentiable optimization of their parametric primitives an attractive goal. Yet classical rasterization is discontinuous with respect to geometry, and existing remedies that smooth the forward pass demand increasingly elaborate heuristics as scene complexity grows. We trace this fragility to a gradient seesaw: design choices that improve forward geometric exactness can systematically degrade the induced gradient signal, and vice versa. To navigate this tension we introduce CubicSplat, a differentiable vector rasterizer that replaces Bézier closest-point solvers with uniform polyline surrogates whose geometric error is bounded at $O(S^{-2})$. The resulting static computation graph yields well-conditioned gradients by construction, while a compositing-derived visibility mechanism prunes degenerate primitives without auxiliary regularization. On DIV2K and Kodak benchmarks CubicSplat achieves state-of-the-art reconstruction quality with over 2 dB PSNR gain in the closed-fill setting, while training up to 4x faster than prior methods. The code is available at https://github.com/CubicSplat/repo

</details>
